&quot; Light has come into the world, hut to this poor peasant it has come in vain. For six thousand years the Sons of Adam, in sleepless effort, have been devising, doing, dis&not; covering; in mysterious indissoluble communion, warring, a little band of brothers, against the great black empire of Necessity and Night ; they have accomplished such a conquest and conquests : and to this man it is all as if it had not been. The four-and* twenty letters of the Alphabet are still Runic enigmas to him. He passes by on the other side ; and that great Spiritual Kingdom, the toil-won conquest of his own brothers, all that his brothers have conquered, is a thing non-extant for him. An invisible empire ; he knows it not, suspects it not. And is it not his withal ? the conquest of his own brothers, the lawfully acquired possession of all men?&rdquo;&mdash; Carlyle .
PREFACE.,The Old Year coming to an end, among other works needful to be done, the bookbinder has to bind up the numbers of our Magazine,&mdash;bind our twelve little sheaves into one greater sheaf ; and our readers expect a word from us.,Let us hope that this our Volume П. is not unworth the binding, that it is worth putting on our shelves and reading. Insignificant in circulation, slender in bulk, holding together only short scattered letters and &ldquo; minutes&rdquo; or &ldquo; essays,&rdquo; in common prose, it yet seems to me reviewing it, to deserve kindly acceptance from those who have at heart the welfare of the Working Men of England. To such, I think, it bears a voice of Hope. Yes, Hope ! for however faintly, it speaks seriously,&mdash;of a work to be done, of a work doing ; and so invites us all to more strenuous efforts and closer union.,The more specific purpose of our Magazine is to promote &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; ; to make each speak to the other, College to College, teacher to teacher and student, and student to student and teacher. By monthly record and communion we hoped to make each College more ef&not; fective in spirit, range, and method, and, if possible, to generate other Colleges, to carry the like work into fresh towns, cities, and neighbour&not; hoods. But herein we are perpetually reminded of our larger, our largest purposes; how we are engaged in an experiment, how at every step &amp;amp; we need to be learners; nor can we think too often or too seriously upon these things. 1,It is astonishing, when we reflect upon it, that up to this century, the Working Men of England lived without intellectual instruction, lived and died ignoiant, even of the Alphabet :&mdash;as is so touchingly described in the passage opposite, which I have chosen for motto. &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;, who come to our^ &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, are happily not in the forlorn condition of that &ldquo; P oor peasant ;&rdquo; yet they should remember that that &ldquo; poor peasant &rdquo; exists still, ay, hundreds of such still are in England, and thousands To all of us, moreover, is not much of the Kingdom of Knowledge still an invisible kingdom? Then came Children&rsquo;s Schools, and Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes: the schools are going on bravely, the institutes, more doubtfully, but not, I hope, doing otherwise than well. Now, quite in late years, come &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. Our work is peculiar in many ways. As distinguished from schools, we have no boys with shining morning faces, creeping unwillingly to school, but men, with beards grown or growing, who, the day&rsquo;s labour done, come willing to learn ; as distinguished from Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes, we teach in class requiring solid effort on the part of our students; as distinguished generally from other places of education, we teach working men fat least, as such they come to us), and undertake to teach them all the humane learning we ourselves possess humane learning as distinct &iacute;rom professional accomplishment,&mdash;what of that is ours, shall be theirs l&iacute; _they will have it. Thus we find ourselves in a new realm ; new moun&not; tains, rivers, and forests around us. Questions such as these beset us &middot; How shall our Colleges be constituted, what share in the government in ihe administration of them, shall these men-students have, shall we have
prizes, examinations, certificates, how shall we teach our new subjects ? Now on these and many other matters we want all the mutual help we can get, all the best thoughts and experiences of our brother-Colleges. Especially, however, we want information upon our new subjects, which of them are most needed, and how they may best be taught. A good method of teaching classics and mathematics may be ascertained, for they have been taught for generations ; so perhaps also of modern languages ; but we teach, or hope to teach, history, literature, logic, the natural sciences, music, drawing ; nay, even politics and morals ; all that a man should be&not; lieve and know, so far as we ourselves have knowledge, howsoever acquired.,Then we call ourselves Colleges. I do not battle for the name of Col&not; lege, though the name is a right good one, but the thing we mean is in&not; deed important. We mean this: We would have our students more than successful students,&mdash;they ш-е more than students, they are Men ; we would have them wise and happy and helpful men, and have all the learning and life at the College contribute to this. Especially do we wage war with the vulgar vanity of learnedness, and the silly mean ambition that covets learning, and even labours for learning, only to go and sell it for money ; and with that pride of Mammon that makes a man dumb to his brother and blind to the glad light of heaven. In our College life we pledge ourselves, and we entreat all who join us, to strive by simplicity, by honest respectful afiectionateness, by looking each other plainly in the face, and by cheerful giving and receiving, to cultivate fellowship between class and class and friendship between man and man.,How much of this, or how little, have &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; done ? Think rather how much there is to do. I can only speak of one College : so far as I know that, no teacher, no student, but has cause to be grateful for his experiences there, and is grateful; and the oldest and most faithful owe it most and love it best.,This little volume testifies to some of these things. It contains papers by teachers on sundry subjects, Latin, English Literature, Translations from the Classics, History, Chemistry ; use of a Geological Museum ; papers or letters on prizes and examinations, social meetings, College rifle corps, the education of women (a subject this, that is very near and should be very dear to us) ; a history of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, a letter from Mr. Maurice to Hughes on the world-famed prizefight ; records many pleasant festival gatherings ; and reports (only too slenderly) the pro&not; gress of the various Colleges in London, Manchester, Ancoats, Salford, Hali&not; fax, Boston, Cambridge, Wolverhampton. It also announces the founda&not; tion of a College at Liverpool ; and the foundation of a Working Men&rsquo;s Institute in Wellclose Square, London, and a &ldquo; Working Men&rsquo;s Mutual Improvement and Recreation Society &rdquo; in Lancaster, which promise per&not; haps to be Colleges hereafter.,What we want is more. Oliver asks for more. We want more from all our friends, especially those in the country, and students. We all need to know our work better, and one another better. Teachers, students, friends, should therefore help this Magazine,&mdash;having, I say, the work at heart.,Having the work at heart ! The Christmas Bells are even now going ; and the New Year is at the door.,Vernon Lushington.,24 th December, 1860.

JAN&Uuml;AEY 1, 1860.,TO OUE SUBSCEIBEES.,Tlie Council of the London College has resolved, notwithstanding the pecuniary difficulties alluded to two months ago, to continue for the pre&not; sent the publication of the Magazine. The Editor therefore desires to lay before its readers and subscribers a statement of its financial position, that he may determine as soon as possible whether a permanent guarantee can be found against the loss still likely to be incurred. The case stands thus. The actual deficit on the past year will be from &pound;10 to &pound;12. The smallness of this sum is due to the considerable amount (about &pound;19) subscribed as a guarantee fund to commence with. This was nearly all collected from friends of the London College. It is hoped that a few contributions will be sent to make up this deficiency. In regard to our future prospects, it is found that, as originally estimated, the sale of 1000 copies will defray all expenses. The actual sale may be set down at about 500. To meet the deficiency therefore we have either to create an artificial sale of 500 additional copies, or to obtain direct money aid to a like amount. It is proposed then to distribute this mixed liability among the individual mem&not; bers of the various Councils of Teachers and their friends in the following way. Allowing for the natural, sale being diminished to the extent of, say, 100 copies, by this artificial distribution, let us reckon that we have to find buyers or donors to the extent of about 600 copies (&pound;45). To do this it is desirable to obtain from 42 to 45 subscribers of a guinea annually : each subscriber to take copies to the amount if he desires them, leaving as many as he may not require with the Editor or Secretaries to the various &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, to be distributed among &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;, the press, etc., or in such manner as may best promote the publicity of the Magazine and the object of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. Would not the following be a reasonable &ldquo;assessment&rdquo; of the liability among the members of the various Coun&not; cils, legai d being had to the position of the various Colleges as to money and patronage P&mdash;London, 15 subscribers ; Manchester, 7 ; Salford, 7 ; An- coats, 2 ; Sheffield, 1 ; Halifax, 2 ; Cambridge, 6 ; and Wolverhampton, 3 : total, 43. r,This pioposal has been submitted to the London Council, and appears to them the best way of meeting the difficulty. Friends of the vaiious Colleges may be reminded, that in no way can the principles which we seek to cany out be more effectively propagated than by the dispersion of copies of a publication which we tiy to make an actual mirror of the life and studies of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. The Magazine has certainly served its chief purpose of affording a means of communication between the va- iious CoUeges. It has done a great deal towards uniting and consolidating the efforts made for the same ends in remotely distant parts of the countrv and has been partly a substitute for that personal intercourse which can never become more than casual between dwellers in Lancashire and London.
Insomuch as it fulfils these purposes, we may regard it as a part of the common apparatus whereby all the Colleges are more or less assisted ; and considered in this light, the expense of its maintenance will not seem so very large an item in comparison with the total expenditure for College purposes.,I have to request therefore that the above plan may be taken into con&not; sideration as soon as possible by the Councils of the various Colleges. Upon the general feeling expressed in their replies to it will depend the continu&not; ance or noncontinuance of the Magazine. In a little time the deficiency in question may be much lightened or altogether disappear. The present number contains the first of a series of papers which ought to find, on grounds of the deepest personal interest, a reader in every English work&not; man. But, speaking generally of the attractiveness of our pages, I can only say that it must be measured by reference to the College work, of which this is the current record. If that work has in it heart and life, the heart and life must make themselves felt here as &lambda;&nu;&epsilon;ΐΐ as within the College walls. A man&rsquo;s College work touches in every point his most vital interests, social and political, personal and domestic. Out of all these points of contact arise questions of importance, some of them tremendous problems which mankind has always been trying to solve, and perhaps never will see its way through. On all such matters, nevertheless, whoso hath serious thoughts or honest convictions, let him say them forth for the benefit of his fellows. Here, at least, he shall have audience.,The Editor.,WILL COLLEGE NIGHT CLASSES BE OE USE TO WOMEN ?,Some of the teachers and students of the London College think it would be advisable to open the classes to women as well as men. Will they let an old member of &ldquo; the Women&rsquo;s Classes&rdquo; say a few words on this point ?,As to whether the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; would under&not; stand the respect due to their own wives and sisters within the walls of their College, is a question one does not like to hear spoken of.. If they would not, where is the knowledge of duty of which so much is said as being the great lesson taught by the College ? The doubt seems to ques&not; tion &deg;the very existence of the College, as a College teaching men their duties in all the relations of life ; therefore I pass over that point.,What classes should we working women join? The Drawing and Music classes would, I think, be the only ones we should attend with much profit. The general lectures undoubtedly we should attend with both pleasure and profit, but the general classes of the College I do not think would be of much use to us if we were to attend them. The drawing and the sing&not; ince, I am sure, would be of immense use to us, and I am sure we should have a very great pleasure in attending them : but whether we could, with much advantage, attend classes for Latin, Greek, Algebra, Geometry, Logic, or Political Economy (whatever that is), is, I think, very doubtful. W e see that these subjects take up our husbands&rsquo; and our brothers&rsquo; whole leisure time ; how therefore could we keep up with them P And they tell us, as an excuse for their attention to that (and comparative inattention
to us), that if they do not keep with the rest of the class they cannot do much good. Besides, do we want these things? Will Algebra or Greek help us with our work ?,&amp;lt;c For woman is not undeveloped man,,But diverse.&rdquo;,Do we require the same training that our husbands need ? Our husbands have two lives,&mdash;one in the world, and one in their homes. Tiiey have to keep a home ; to set up in the world a temple, of which we shall be the priestesses. Like the dwellers on the borders of civilization, we are the sowers sowing the seeds of happiness ; they, the armed guard to protect us from the rough hands of the outer world.,As our husbands have two lives, they require two trainings. We have only one life and require only one training. This training for the outer battle of life wanted by our husbands may doubtless be got by them from the study of Latin, trigonometry, and possibly political economy; but this training, however good it may be for the battle of the outer world, falls far short of the training wanted for their other life. In this inner life of home, man and wife Avant the same training. As bearing on this common end, music and drawing are immensely valuable. The connection between the beauty of a drawing and the neatness of a home, between the harmony of a part song and the harmony of kind words and loving looks, is far more real than imaginary.,I said, at the beginning, that I wished to speak to the students of the College who were advocating the opening of the classes to women ; and I will only say to them, You can do more than this for us ; you can show us the use of your College learning better than by making us formally partakers of it ; you can yourselves be our teachers. Show us, in your own lives at home with us, that you rightly understand the College teaching. Show us that it does teach you to value your homes ; that it does give you a bet&not; ter understanding of your position in your families ; that you do the better comprehend the duties of a parent. Make us your companions; do not hand us over to class teachers ; be our teachers yourselves ; we shall not ask you to teach us Algebra or Latin, but give us the fruits of your learn&not; ing ; be agreeable companions ; throw as much energy into the endeavour to interest us as though we were a fellow-student in the same class come to see you for an evening&rsquo;s talk. Do not come home only to pore over books we do not understand, nor want to understand ; do not make &ldquo;Col&not; lege work&rdquo; an excuse for neglecting us. Separate us from our servant office, and what time you can spare from your all-absorbing class, devote to us as earnestly as you devote the other portion of your leisure to ypur class work. You will take a practice class, and you will throw into the teaching all your energies to fulfil the trust reposed in you. You feel the responsibility of your office reposed in you by your teacher, and you work hard. Accept a greater responsibility reposed in you by a greater Teacher, and work as hard at the task. Devote the same energy, the same heartiness, in teach&not; ing little prattling tongues to say &ldquo; Our Father, which &aacute;rt in Heaven,&rdquo; Devote the same energy, the same heartiness, the same trouble, to saying to us, in your acts, i -,&ldquo;Lay thy sweet hands in mine, and trust in me.&rdquo;,I have said, devote the same energy, the same earnestness ; but you will
find it requires more energy, more earnestness, than your class work at the College. Try to adapt your College learning to the direction of your own homes, and you will find it a difficult task. Regard us as uneducated ; imagine a &ldquo; gulf which no courtesy can bridge over, a gulf which is ever widening,&rdquo; as you add to more of your College learning, and you will find your task impossible. We shall object to things which we cannot under&not; stand being talked about, as having anything to do with us and our homes. We shall resent it. We shall find growing in our minds a feeling of hos&not; tility to the College and its teaching. Show us, however, that your College teaching does not place a &ldquo; gulf&rdquo; between us; that it brings us nearer to each other ; that it sends you home to us with a full consciousness that home is not only a place for preparation of college lessons, but it is also a place for bringing forth the fruit of your lessons. Show us that you can banish all thoughts of books and College to have an hour&rsquo;s play with your little boy or an hour&rsquo;s chat with us. You have often told us, when coming home from some College meeting, how pleased and interested you ueie by the conversation of some new teacher, or some visitor-friend of your teacher&rsquo;s ; how they have, by a few happy words, made clear some difficult point to you ; often how they have, in a few moments, let you into the very heart of some study of which you had never thought. Let it be so between you and us. Teach us not by formal lessons, but by your company. Lou provide books for your classes ; provide books for us that, with your aid given as freely and heartily as to a practice class, will teach us what we Avant. I don&rsquo;t think the admission of women to the College would do any harm ; but it will not, I think, remove any &ldquo; gulf&rdquo; that may exist. If any such &ldquo; gulf&rdquo; exists it must be bridged over, not by us, not by our educa&not; tion at College classes, but by you ; not by your courtesy, but by your sense of duty to yourselves, to us, and, above all, to yom ehildi en.,J A Student&rsquo;s Wife.,[Our correspondent here gives a mother&rsquo;s view of the relations between the College and the home. Will some unmarried sister tell us what she thinks on the matter ?&mdash;Ed.],ON THE STUHY OE LITERATURE.,Of the advantages to be derived from the study of Literature there can be no question., There is no need for the &lsquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Maga&not; zine&rsquo; to occupy its pages in advocating them. The question to be con&not; sidered is &middot; How is this study to be prosecuted ? How are these advan&not; tages to be secured ? This is not a new question by any means. _ It has Diizzled the heads of many ages, and has not yet met with any satisfactory solution. It occurs to every man so soon as the notion of something better than desultory reading dawns upon him. But we are all of a lazy sort, more or less ; and, finding the skein tangled we have no energy to unravel it So for the most part, we relapse into the old system, or rathei, no system, and devour whatever comes to hand indiscriminately&mdash;a green&middot;&middot; backed novel, or a stray volume of history, or an antediluvian magazine.
Now it is certain this can be no healthy diet. Imagine the complete dis&not; order that must prevail in a mind fed on such promiscuous viands. Ima&not; gine the labour that is wasted through the neglect of a proper economy. We are making ropes of sand if we read in this way. There is no cohesion between the grains we bring together. Chaos was not in a confusion worse confounded than are our minds.,But it is hugely easier to find fault than to find remedies. It is some&not; thing, however, if we admit the fault&rsquo;s existence. When we find our knowledge made comparatively useless by its want of arrangement, and when our brains are in a very maelstrom of confusion, we may at last recognize tue importance of some method in our reading and the thought it awakens.,The grand virtue of the student is self-denial. He cannot afford to follow all his whims and fancies. If he devotes himself to algebra, for instance, he must not be tempted from that devotion by the fairest heroine that ever appeared in fiction. And so in studying literature, when once he has adopted his method, he must adhere to it rigidly, let no attraction seduce him from it, let him dally with no volume that lies out of his course.,But our student has not yet adopted his method ; he has only learnt to feel its necessity. He sees that he cannot open the gate except by unlock&not; ing it lawfully and honestly ; but how to do this, he sees not. Where is he to find the key ?,What an overpowering prospect is before him ! the literature of the world from its creation. Naturally he lifts up his hands in despair, and is for wallowing once more in the mire of Umnethod. And who is not simi&not; larly appalled at the sight of so vast a subject ? Does a man at any other time feel so infinitely little, so intensely ignorant, so hopeless, as when he thus surveys the whole mass of literature ? He grows dizzy at the spec&not; tacle. If not positively driven to despair by it, he confesses at last with humiliation, that of that extensive country before him he can never hope to visit but a very scanty portion, though he may have all possible facilities for travel, and the greatest imaginable energy and perseverance. It remains therefore for him to choose his portion, and be content with a mere bird&rsquo;s- eye view of the rest of the landscape.,Now he may make this choice in several ways. He may choose a par&not; ticular subject,&mdash;poetry, say and may set himself to trace its history, and read its effusions through all times ; and similarly he may choose other subjects. _ He may choose some particular nation, and confine his researches to its writings. He may choose particular periods in the histories of many individual nations those brilliant periods, say, upon which the genius of the whole people seems, as it were, to have been concentrated, and to have put forth all its powers&mdash;the &lsquo; golden ages,&rsquo; as they are called. For this last choice there is, we think, something to be said. If a student can o- e t an outline of literature in general, then some idea of his own country&rsquo;s literature, and then a comparatively full and complete knowledge of a cer&not; tain particular period in that or some other literature, we submit that he will not have toiled in vain. This may seem an insurmountable task ; but we do not deem it so. Every man may achieve this much, if he will &middot; not in a few weeks, it may be, but surely in due time. And can he lay out his years to greater advantage ? Let him remember too the innumerable helps
that are prepared for him. In these days he will have no rough and rugged and unfrequented paths to traverse, but fairly-beaten roads. He can ma&not; nage this journey, let him but so resolve.,We think that every student will see the essential importance of each item in this scheme, however fearful he may be as to his powers &oacute;f exe&not; cuting it. He cannot but long for some knowledge of the relation which the literature of his own country holds to the literatures of other countries. Some intimate relation it must hold to them, he knows ; that it can be en&not; tirely isolated, is impossible. He wishes, therefore, to ascertain its relative position, to contemplate it in its true character, not as a whole but as part of a whole, to learn something of the influences which have acted upon it and given it shape. Of course he can gratify this wish only by the aid of handbooks and manuals; and for this purpose he will find such works fairly satisfactory, many as their defects undoubtedly are. He may gather from them such a sketch as he seeks for. He must resign himself to its being but a brief and general one.,As to the necessity of some acquaintance -with the literature of his own country, he will be troubled with but few doubts. If he wishes to under&not; stand the state of things around him, to trace its origin and progress, and investigate the thoughts and tendencies that accompany it, he must turn his eyes to the pages of literature, and hope there to find an answer to his inquiries. But we need not dwell upon the utility of this branch of his studies. It is certain that no intelligent mind will consent to ignore it. The study of his own country&rsquo;s literature is pursued by such-a mind in&not; stinctively. But, as in a country like England for instance, whose history is enriched with the trophies of peace no less than of war, this literature covers a vast and untraversable space, he must once more be content to re&not; ceive other travellers&rsquo; accounts of the greater part of it ; he must submit to be informed by them of its general features and characteristics, and, with regard to many provinces of this mighty empire, must depend very mainly upon their report.,And now, having so far exercised a wise forbearance, and contented him&not; self with mere outlines, he selects a certain period in that literature, and gives all his energies to its investigation. He selects that period which, from his general reading, or from its acknowledged importance, or from some other reason, appears to him to be most worthy of his pains,&mdash;either as the period displaying the most wonderful development of the human mind, and most abounding in intellectual riches ; or as most nearly affect&not; ing his own time, as swaying over it the utmost influence, and lending the greatest assistance in the interpretation of it. We may say that the student will be guided in this choice by the character of that object, which of all the objects to be gained by the study of literature seems to him of the most pre-eminent importance. He who aims especially at his moral culture, will not be inclined to choose the &aelig;ra of the Restoration as his particular field, however remarkable in many respects he may admit that &aelig;ra to be. The lorer of highly imaginative poetry will have no disposition to embrace ar&not; dently the eighteenth century. In fact, every one will, we fancy, consult his own tastes and tendencies in the selection he makes.,With this period he will endeavour to make an intimate acquaintance. He will scrutinize it as closely as lies in his power. He will live on terms
of the compietesi familiarity with its great names. He will examine their claims to the thrones they occupy. He will give a diligent ear to all their words, and do his utmost to learn the lesson they teach. All his other knowledge will be made to contribute to his knowledge of this period ; and his knowledge of this period will cast light on all other periods with which he may be brought into contact. It is impossible to state how much profit may be derived from the energetic study of a single epoch.,With respect to the best method of prosecuting this study, it is not easy to pronounce. W e are not prepared to prescribe any particular method as absolutely the best. Of course, we suppose the student to have already formed a general notion of the period he chooses. His care is now to fill in the outline. Perhaps his wisest course will be to search at once for that author who may be said most perfectly to represent the time in question, and having found him (if he is to be found), to make him the central figure in his group of worthies. This Representative Man may be regarded as the key to his time ; all contemporary writers have but served to illustrate in detail those truths and aspirations which are all comprehended in him. They but dilate upon the difierent sides of his character. The student has made a great advance, therefore, when he has found him. When he has instituted a skilful analysis of his character, he has before him all the lead&not; ing features of the period, and can at once &quot;proceed to their separate inves&not; tigation. If these leading thoughts cannot be found embodied in an indi&not; vidual man, they must be sought for elsewhere. The student must begin with the general group, and discover from these combined the knowledge he desires. In the former case he finds the treasure in the keeping of one man ; in the latter, he finds it in the hands of many, dispersed and scattered.,Another method confines itself to the several subjects which compose the literature of the time,&mdash;Poetry, Piction, etc.,&mdash;and studies each one sepa&not; rately. The disadvantage of this method is, that there is an utter want of unity about the view so taken. We should hold him to be a sorry por&not; trait-painter who should divide his work into as many distinct and uncon&not; nected parts as his subject had limbs, and should assign a separate canvas to every individual member. Neither can we admire this method when applied to the study of an age ; our views must inevitably be distorted if this unity is disregarded.,We make these remarks in all humbleness of mind, by no means ex cathedra , but from the pupils&rsquo; bench. Perhaps they may serve to awaken some reader s attention to a most important subject, and assist his investi&not; gations of it by an occasional suggestion at least.,We have a few words to add with respect to the English Literature Class in our London College,&mdash;the only Literature class at present existing there. Its history, so far, cannot be said to be encouraging ; which is the more surprising, because, from the nature of its subject, it might have been rea&not; sonably expected to be an extremely popular class. We wish earnestly to call the attention of our students to this matter. Let them be sure that they do rate this department of the College&rsquo;s studies at its proper value,As to the management of the class next term, the method adopted will be something such as that sketched above, A particular period has already been chosen,&mdash;the eighteenth century. It is not hard to see the motives that dictated this choice. Last term ivas devoted to the opening &aelig;ra of
this period ; Addison, Swift, De Doe, Bolingbroke, and Steele, formed suc&not; cessively the subjects of the lessons. For next term, Pope will be studied as the leading writer&mdash;selected as the Representative Man, that is. The Principal will superintend this study, and will besides give some general lectures on the literature of the age under consideration. His Assistant will take in hand the surrounding writers, at least the most remarkable of them, giving some account of their lives and their works.,Whether some such method as we have indicated might not be applied with great advantage to other literatures, is worthy, we think, of considera&not; tion. Are there not many students in the College who would be glad to obtain some idea of foreign literatures ? Of course we must avoid attempt&not; ing too much. We do not think that in occasionally (say in one term a year) instituting classes for the study of some foreign literature, we should be doing so.,At all events, classes of this sort in classical literature might be esta&not; blished. Our present Latin and Greek classes do not serve the end we are contemplating. By all means let our students betake themselves to the rudiments of those languages ; they will find in them a most excellent discipline for the mental powers. The little acquaintance they so make with etymology will be of immense use. But when we see Tiro stumbling- through his declensions, or translating through brick walls, with a calm and self-satisfied aspect, or ascribing to his author the most unfounded and unmeant sentiments, we cannot close our eyes to the melancholy conviction that many cycles of years must elapse before that well-intentioned student will be able to read his Plato with either comfort or advantage. We do not in saying this disparage in the least the Latin and Greek classes. We suspect that even of well-educated men a large proportion owe their know&not; ledge of Classical literature, not to the study of the originals, but to trans&not; lations. We submit that it is only by this means that our students can hope to gain a similar knowledge.* We think, therefore, that it would be no useless measure to establish a class in classical literature on much the same principles as those on which the English literature is conducted. If this proposition satisfies a want felt by the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, we think there would be little difficulty in executing it ; but of this we shall be happy to say more in a future number of the Magazine.,J. Hales.,HISTORY OF TRADES&rsquo; UNIONS AND STRIKES.,The question of Trades&rsquo; Unions and Strikes, to which attention has frequently been directed by different members of our College, has just been taken up as a subject of popular discussion generally, and owing to the recent strike of the men employed at Messrs. Trollopes&rsquo;, is at present,[* The example of the most important hook in the world ought to suffice to disperse the usual frivolous objections to the use of translations. There are scarcely a thousand Englishmen who can read St. Paid with ease in the original, and perhaps not a dozen competent to give an opinion on the meaning of a verse in Genesis.&mdash; En.]
occupying that prominent place in public attention without which no great social question is ever understood. As is usual with all new questions, much misapprehension appears to prevail on both sides ; on the one hand, the advocates of the masters treat combination amongst workmen as a mere social evil that has recently sprung up amongst us ; whilst even the advocates of the men seem to overlook their real origin, and date their ex&not; istence only to the beginning of the present century. The subject is one so intimately connected with the work of our College, that we propose to insert in our Magazine a series of papers, giving as well as can be collected a short historical account of the rise and progress of Trades&rsquo; Unions, and of the Strikes which have been directly or indirectly caused by them. The right of the workmen to associate together for the mutual protection of those engaged in the same trade, is one almost as ancient as the constitu&not; tion itself. Soon after the Conquest, we find voluntary associations of mechanics and trades established for the mutual protection of each other. At first they had no further object than the relief of their members in times of distress, which was done by the mutual contribution of their members. Hence they were called Guilds, from a Saxon word, yıldan , to contribute, and from their name many have inferred that they had a Saxon origin. It was not, however, till the reign of Henry the First that these associations seem to have had any connection with trade, but from that time we find the various trades separated from each other and from the rest of the community by their several guilds, which formed a bond of union between the employers and employed. Each guild had its own byelaws, which regulated the terms of employment and provided for the common interests of all the members equally ; all persons not members of the guild were termed foreigners, and were absolutely excluded from following their respective occupations until they became members of the guild. At that time the restrictions imposed on foreigners (non-freemen) was one sanc&not; tioned by the law, and great was the outcry when any attempt was made to relax it. Thus, to confine ourselves to the building trades, about which the present agitation has arisen, no man could formerly be employed iu such trade unless he belonged to the corporation, or was resident in the town where he wished to follow his occupation. In 1548, the Parliament of Edward the Sixth endeavoured to remove this restriction, and passed an Act to permit all workmen concerned in building houses, etc., to follow their occupation in all cities and towns corporate, although they did not li\e therein, noi weie fiee of such corporation. The popular feeling was so much against this Act, that in the following year it was repealed, appa&not; rently by the interposition of the city of London. The repealing statute (3 &amp;amp; 4 Edward YI. c. 20) sets forth, &ldquo; that the city of London bein 0- the most ancient city of the realm, the artificers and craftsmen of the &amp;amp; arts, crafts, and mysteries are at great costs and charges in taxes, etc., and that if foieigneis (non-iieemen) should come and work among them, contrary to their ancient privileges, the same should be an impoverishment and driving away of the freemen being artificers of the crafts, arts, and mys&not; teries aforesaid, within the city of London, to the great hurt and destruc&not; tion of the said city.&rdquo; The reasons for this repeal are expressed as above only in respect to London, yet the repeal being general, took in all other corporate cities and towns, and so remained for many centuries afterwards.
Similar restrictions prevailed in other trades ; and so long as the guilds adhered to their original constitutions, rules were made for the mutual pro&not; tection of members, as stringent as any now in operation in the Trades&rsquo; Unions. In a future number we shall insert some of these rules, but for the present confine ourselves to the more immediate subject of the rise of Trades&rsquo; Unions. About the beginning of the eighteenth century the guilds, according to evidence laid before the House of Commons, had in many in&not; stances become restricted to the masters, and for the first time the interests of masters and men seem to have been treated as antagonistic to each other. The men, finding themselves excluded from the guilds, began to form societies of their own, which at first were called Congresses. In the journals of the House of Commons of the year 1777, we find several complaints made to that House of the workmen of different trades meet&not; ing in congress to regulate their wages. One instance is very remarkable, and will suffice on the present occasion to show that Trades&rsquo; Unions were at that time in active operation. The case we refer to is that of the hat- makers, whose complaints were inquired into by a Committee of the House ; and from the evidence taken before that Committee, it appears that as early as 1772 the men had met in congress and made demands of addi&not; tional prices, which the masters were forced, in order to carry on their trade, to submit to. In 1777 the men again met in congress, their wages at that time being nineteen shillings a week. The result of the meeting of the men was that they demanded that their wages should be increased from nineteen to twenty-one shillings, with a reduction of the hours of labour of two hours a day, and threatened a strike if their demands were not acceded to. The masters were obliged to compromise the matter by increasing the wages to twenty shillings, and reducing the hours of labour one hour a day. The same report also mentions that the congress made byelaws for the regulation of journeymen, and levied a subscription of twopence a week on each man ; and it is further stated that at one shop five out of fifty men having refused to pay the subscription, the master was told he must no longer employ these five men, and was compelled to discharge them, being threatened with a strike if he did not do so. We have been thus particular in our account of this case, as we think it affords conclusive proof that Trades&rsquo; Unions were in active operation nearly a century ago, and that it is owing to them in a great measure that the workmen have attained their present comparative freedom. In our next number we shall give the rules of some of these Unions, which in spite of the Combination Laws (then unrepealed) were at that time allowed in this country.,OUGHT PHIZES TO HAYE A MONEY VALUE?,Any student who has attained a certain amount of knowledge in any one subject of the London College studies, and passes an examination in that subject, receives a certificate that he has passed that examination, and has thereby shown that he has attained a knowledge o&iacute; that subject. Is that certificate a prize or not. Ask any student who may write G.S. after his name, whether his certificate is a prize to him, and doubt not that he
will tell you he prizes it as much as if it had a money value of &pound;5 or &pound;10. Ask any one who has passed the Associateship Examination, whether he prizes the name of an Associate (and an Associate receives nothing but the name, not even a piece of paper), and rely upon it he will tell you he prizes it exactly as he would a set of certificates, one for each subject of the As&not; sociate Examination. Ask either a certificated student, or an Associate, гс1гу he values his certificate or his name of Associate, and he will tell you, most probably, that he prizes it, not as a reward for his work, but as an evidence, especially to himself, that he has used the ability which has been given him, that he has worked hard at some study, and has actually leamt the subject of it to some definite and useful extent.,The certificate, therefore, is a prize, though it is a prize having no money value ; but is it the less sought after for that reason P Is it not more valued because it has no money value ? Could not any one of those who have risen to the dignity o&iacute; Eellows of the College, by giving the same amount of intense work to any one subject that he gave to five, have made almost sure of obtaining one of the &pound;10 prizes of the Society of Arts? I think he could. Why did he value the name of Associate and Fellow more than &pound;10 ? Because one was a money prize, a prize that would reward him for knowing more of a subject than some number of competitors ; a selfish prize ; a prize, the attempt to gain which would make him regard his fel&not; low-students with an involuntary feeling of hostility ; a prize that would make him regard their gain as his loss, and their loss as his gain ; while the name of Associate and Fellow would mark him as a student working with his fellow-students ; as one who took a share in their work, who wished them as much progress as he wished himself ; one who was bound by his very name to help them to gain as much, or greater, knowledge than he himself possessed.,To take another point of view. Do we find that the certificate and As&not; sociateship are insufficient to make men work ? I think we may fairly as&not; sume that if money prizes were offered, the men who have won the honorary prizes would, if they chose to try, carry off the money prizes too. What do those men think of prizes? One of them, Mr. Tansley, has expressed his opinions. Our Editor thinks he is riding a good principle too hard. Without stopping to ask whether a man can do this, I will say, &ldquo; I think not.&rdquo; I believe that he has expressed the opinion of all who* have gained certificates. I believe his simple language conveys admirably the right view of the question. Would those men have worked harder for a money prize ? I have known men work steadily for a twelvemonth, giving up almost every leisure hour, working far into the night, to gain a certificate oi an Associateship. But more, far more than this, I have known them hinder themselves to help each other. I have known one man give up houis and hours e\eiy week to do a fellow-studenCs work for one who was haid pressed foi time to enable him to attend classes, to help him in his pieparation for an examination in which both men were going in at once. Would this have been the case had there been one prize only ? Is not such an instance of true College feeling worth more than a hundred prizes that would tend to destroy or repress such feelings ? When these men have got their certificates and associateships, do they prize them less be&not; cause they have no money value ? Let this answer : I quote the words of
a student who had gained a certificate, in answer to the congratulation of a fellow-student hundreds of miles away:&mdash;&ldquo;I thank you heartily for your kind congratulations, but remember that-is the man who set the glo&not;,rious example ; and remember that to him I am indebted in a great measure,for ray success, for who helped me, hindering himself to do so, but-?,To him I am deeply grateful ; I feel that to such a friend is due my deep&not; est gratitude and best thanks.&rdquo; A man who had gained a prize he did not value, would scarce express himself so warmly.&mdash;And the prizehere was only a certificate. If a certificate is so highly valued, how much more would an associateship be ! Let a successful student speak for himself, while writing to a brother-student, immediately after passing his Associate Examination :&mdash;&ldquo; And now, old fellow, let me heartily and sincerely thank you, for you have done much for me ; you have personally helped me in all the subjects. To you only I owe my success in one of the subjects. Eor all this, and more than this, thank you, old fellow,&mdash;thank you.&rdquo;,These, I think, are sufficient evidence of the good fellowship that has hitherto existed between students trying for certificates and associateships. Would not prizes of money value, given to the best men, weaken this feel&not; ing? If they did, would any gain compensate for such a loss? Students of older Colleges are scattered over the world in after-life ; so, to a less ex&not; tent, are students of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. Think of what value to a man, forced to leave his beloved College for years, must such friendships be,&mdash;friendships cemented by nightly common study for years, cemented by a thousand offices of goodwill, and even sacrifices for each other&rsquo;s College success ; cemented by that hearty faith in each other that comes of the daily unrestrained intercourse of such a College life. Would not the contention for the one prize of fame, the head certificate, do much to weaken this co-operation of study? Would not each student, in the words of our Principal, be tempted to say to himself, &ldquo;Watch that fellow ! He has got a thought of yours. If you do not keep a sharp eye upon him, he will pocket it, and pass it off as his own.&rdquo;,I have read Mr. Greenwood&rsquo;s letter, and feel the force of it ; but I still believe that we have no need of prizes beyond our ordinary certificates and associateships. I write as one who has felt all these excitements of working with a view to &ldquo; doing &rdquo; examinations. I am fortunate enough to possess two certificates : one from the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, which has no money value, and one from the Privy Council Committee on Education, which is Avortli nearly &pound;30 a year. Eor the one I worked hard for six months ; foi one I gave up my whole heart and mind to the needful work. Of one, worth intrinsically only the value of the paper, I feel proud to this day ; the other I regard as an ordinary business document, of no special value in itself, and valuable only for the money it brings me every year. Why ? One was given me by our beloved Principal, in the presence of teachers I esteemed, and of fellow-students whom I valued as my dearest friends, and given me, not as a reward, but as a recognition of my work in College classes ; the other was sent me by men I never saw, as a reward of money value for the acquisition of so much knowledge. Let the question be asked of all successful students, whether prizes of money value would have excited them to greater exertions? I believe they would a U answer, &ldquo;No.&rdquo; I know most of them would. If so, I submit, with all deference for our
teachers, whether of London or elsewhere, that the question is at once settled.,I have touched here only on the influence of money prizes as aifecting the social intercourse of the College. On the points spoken of by Mr. Tansley, nothing I could say could put them in better light than he him&not; self has done.,Having said all I have to say on this point, I will, while writing, say a few words on a subject of far deeper importance.,Mr. Hughes, at the last meeting, spoke of the students not attending prayers, saying that he could not undertake to lead the social life of a College in which the very bond and tie of social life was wanting. I regard this step of Mr. Hughes with feelings of very great thankfulness ; it is, I believe, a most important one. I know of many a young man (I am not speaking now particularly of the College) who will give up his whole mind to any study he begins ; who will devote half his nights to algebra or Latin ; who will read the first chapter of Genesis for its geology, or the facts of the Bible History, to get up the &ldquo; subject &rdquo; for an examination ; but who never dreams of seriously, earnestly opening the volume and reverently studying it as the guide of his inmost life. I have known men (I am not speaking of the College), hardly to be excelled in &ldquo; intellect and moral knowledge,&rdquo; who devoted no time whatever to this study ; whose &ldquo; moral knowledge &rdquo; was a deep-felt conviction of their duties as citizens of the world,&mdash;a conviction that there were duties in this world, and that they had their share of these duties to perform ; and yet, strange anomaly, they never sought the origin of these duties, never sought their end and aim more than that they knew their duties were something they were bound to do ; but why they were bound to do them, who was the Great Creditor to whom these duties were due, and, aoove all, which was the best means of performing them, they never cared to seek. They never bowed their heads in prayer. The feeling,&#39; &ldquo;God be merciful to me a sinner,&rdquo; was unknown to them. Yet they were men of noble character ; men who would scorn a mean action ; men whose lips were never sullied by a falsehood ; men who, I am convinced, would have died rather than do aught they thought dishonourable ; men who won the respect and esteem of all who knew them. Yet there was one thing want&not; ing to make them loved. Seeking no mercy themselves; without the blessed feeling that they needed it ; without the utterance, &ldquo; Forgive us as we forgive others,&rdquo;&mdash;they had no love, no charity, no mercy for any they deemed less perfect than others. Those who have read Mrs. Gaskell&rsquo;s c Ruth &rsquo; will recognize such a character. It is because that, without a prayerful spirit, all intellectual studies tend to foster this spirit of Pharisa&not; ical self-righteousness, that I rejoice so heartily and so thankfully that Mr Hughes has spoken out on this point. For this, as for his help to the Ritb Corps, and many other things, I, as an old student, and one who knows well what the College is meant to be, thank Mr. Hughes most heartily.,A Student from the Beginning op the London College.,December, 1859.,P.S. Is not this done at present in a much better way, without putting a prejnium upon mere intellect ? Have no men received presents of books &iacute;rom friends of the College ? And, lastly, have not these men been selected, not with reference only to intellectual abilities, but with more reference to their personal qualities ?,CORRESPONDENCE.,SUNDAY RIFLE DRILL,,Sir, &mdash;The Committee of our Rifle Corps have announced, with no ordinary emphasis, that they will not &ldquo; authorize &rdquo; Sunday drill, I venture to ask whether they look upon the question as a settled one, or whether they are open to reconsider it so far as regards future arrangements ? The notion of Sunday drill will doubtlessly shock some for whose good opinion we care a great deal (as indeed it will startle a good many respectabilities for whose opinions we don&rsquo;t care a jot), and the course which has been temporarily adopted is therefore a prudent one. But allow me to state what appears to many of us to be unanswerable grounds for another decision.,Our Captain has written that he &ldquo; won&rsquo;t march out with our company until we can go through all the usual movements of a Light Infantry company as well as any regiment of the line.&rdquo; And General Sir John Burgoyne declares that unless we can do this mode&not; rately well, no one will have any confidence in us. How are we to attain to this pro&not; ficiency and to avoid &ldquo; dawdling into a sort of second-rate militia proficiency ?&rdquo; Very many of our members (I think I may safely say the greater number) don&rsquo;t leave busi&not; ness till seven or eight o&rsquo;clock. To accomplish the minimum weekly drill, the eight o&rsquo;clock men will have to attend at least three nights a week, and this must interfere with their attendance at classes. And then when we come to judging distances and shooting, that must be done in the open fields ; they are now so far from the centre of London that many of us, except iu July, would never get there by daylight. How are we &ldquo; to look to our butts and take good aim&rdquo; after dark ?,If all our &ldquo; governors &rdquo; would be patriotic enough, as Sir John of the Round Table puts it, to give us a Saturday half-holiday, there would be an end of all difficulties. But they say it can&rsquo;t be done, business won&rsquo;t permit it.,Great battles have been fought on Sundays. Was it not, as our great poet sings, &ldquo;on a loud Sabbath &rdquo; that we,&ldquo; Shook the spoiler down In the world&rsquo;s earthquake,&mdash;Waterloo&rdquo;?,If it be lawful to fight for one&rsquo;s country on Sundays, may it not also be lawful to train for that purpose on Sundays, at least when other days are not available ? Four hundred years ago, as I need hardly remind anybody, there were butts iu every township, and every man had to keep a bow his own height, and was bound, under a penalty, to shoot up and down every Sunday and Feastday. In those days it was written that &ldquo; The might of the Realme of Englande standeth upon archers.&rdquo; If the same is to hold good now of riflemen, ought we to stop at this old question, &ldquo; Is it lawful to do good on the Sabbath day ?&rdquo; The question is beyond all doubt a difficult one. It is, I know, puz&not; zling a good many of us. I am in hopes that after a temperate discussion of it some satisfactory solution may be found, and in this hope I leave the matter to wiser heads than mine, only asking, is the work of securing peace to the world one unbefitting a holy day ?,W. The folio win- is fiora the Daily News, December 20th &ldquo;A proposition, originating with the Council of the Ancoats forking Men s College, and the gymnastic section of the students, has been made tor the formation of companies in connection with those institutions On Saturday evening, a preliminary meeting was held at the schools, St. Andrew&rsquo;s Square at which several gentlemen who take a prominent interest in the College were present! Ihe Rev. Canon Richson presided, and answered various objections urged against the formation of Rifle Corps. He did not agree with those who urged that the association of a number of men tor such a purpose had a tendency to demoralize them. The same objectum might he urged agam&eacute;t cricket. He advocated the formation of companies as distinguished from clubs, because he regarded military drill and discipline as calculated to develope the physique of the male portion of the community, which was nowhere more
required than in Manchester. In their schools they had children with fine forms and intelligent faces, but their growth into manhood was attenuated and stunted for want of training. He would not object to train any working-man to the use of arms, but he did object to arming skulking and drunken fellows who were too lazy and unprincipled to work.&mdash;Professor Greenwood also exjmessed his approbation of the movement, and sug&not; gested that the members of the Ancoats College might form a company in conjunction with the central Manchester and the Salford Colleges.&mdash;Mr. Fletcher thought Salford too distant to admit of arrangements being made for drill to accommodate both Ancoats and Salford.&mdash;Other gentlemen having spoken, chiefly on matters of detail relative to the cost of clothing, Mr. Underwood proposed, and Mr. Duffy seconded a resolution, request&not; ing the Council of the Manchester College to consider the subject, with a view to co&not; operation with the students at Ancoats.
