THE,r&oslash;&oslash;rkrag fffieiťs &euro;ńląt,No. XIV. FEBE &uuml; AE Y 1, 1860.,HISTORY OF TRADES&rsquo; UNIONS AND STRIKES.,By T. R. Bennett, Esq., M.A., Christ Church, Oxford, Barrister-at-Law,,(&amp;lt;Continued from p. 10 .),We have already stated that Trades&rsquo; Unions were in active operation during the last century, and the numerous petitions presented to the House of Commons against combination in the early part of the reign of George the Third, show that they were not confined to one trade or one locality. At first their main object was the relief of their members in distress, and it was because of the distress suffered by their members when out of work that they first began to collect funds for the support of workmen when out on strike. Of this we have a good instance in the case of the shipwrights of London, who first formed a society in 1793, called the &lsquo; Saint Helena Benefit Society.&rsquo; This rose in consequence of the multiplicity of accidents which so generally occurred amongst shipwrights, that they found them&not; selves completely exhausted for want of support when any accident did happen ; and to prevent this, they united to form one common association for the assistance of their members generally in distress, A second society was formed in 1812, called the *Heart of &Ograve;ak,&rsquo; and to this the shipwrights are indebted for several almshouses founded for their benefit, but neither of them took any part in strikes, nor have we records of relief being afforded to any member when out on strike till the year 1824. In that year some shipwrights from the North came up to London to solicit relief for the dis&not; tressed condition of themselves and their fellow-workmen, stating that they had had a dispute with their masters and were out of employment in con&not; sequence. The tale told by these delegates was so pitiable, of their having sold their beds and being absolutely in a state of starvation, that the ship&not; wrights of London from mere sympathy determined to assist the poor fellows (as they called them), and appointed a committee to collect sub&not; scriptions. This was done from pure charity, without any intention of dic&not; tating to the masters or perpetuating the strike. Each man subscribed sixpence a week, and continued his subscription for about two months, when the masters were obliged to give way, and an amicable settlement was made. The northern shipwrights then wrote to say their disputes were arranged, and that they should not require any more assistance ; at the same time thanking their brethren in London for what had been done. On receiving this communication, the committee posted a placard calling the people together, for they appear to have had no other means of communi&not; cating with each other at that time. At the meeting which was held in consequence, the committee, after reading the correspondence they had had with the northern shipwrights, handed in their accounts of the money they had received, and the money which had been expended. Before thev sena* vol. ir. &bdquo; J 1
rated, some one casually remarked, &lsquo;As we have been subscribing sixpence a week to relieve other people, suppose now we raise a fund for ourselves, which may serve us not only in times of distress, but be applicable to many useful purposes.&rdquo; A discussion ensued on this, and various projects, all for the improvement of workmen in the trade, were talked of. Thus we find that the chief objects to which this fund was to be devoted, were, the relief of shipwrecked carpenters and others in distress, the institution of a mould- loft, to teach their sons to draft and so forth. The only object at all con&not; nected with the regulation of their wages was the suggestion of a price- book or mode of payment, the necessity of which had long been felt, and which was suggested more as a part of the education of apprentices and young workmen than from any desire to control those prices. So began the London Shipwrights&rsquo; Union, which, having been formed solely for the purposes of benevolence and education, was afterwards led to take an active part in the strikes of 1825, because the masters had interfered with their own former prices as set down in the price-book. Somewhat similar was the origin of the Shipwrights&rsquo; Union in the North which was formed in 1823. &deg; Prior to its formation the men at Shields were in the habit of forming societies to relieve each other in distress, and had subscribed funds which were applied solely to that purpose. Not being systematically or&not; ganized, these societies dwindled down in consequence of the death of ori&not; ginal members, and no provision being made for a constant succession. To prevent this the Union was formed entirely for benevolent purposes. A strike having taken place at Sunderland in 1824, the members of the Shields Union made a subscription amongst themselves for their relief, at so much a man, not out of the stock of the Society, but as a voluntary and inde&not; pendent contribution. This came to the knowledge of the Shields masters, and they threatened that if the men did not give up their Union, they would discharge every journeyman, and take nothing but apprentices. The men refused to give up their Union, and on the masters beginning to cany their threat into execution, the men struck for the first time. They were unable to stay out on that occasion, and returned to work. This was in February. In the following April, the masters having combined together and matured their plans, discharged every man who would not take an oath to leave the Union, and not belong to any association or club except those of a charitable nature. Although their &Uuml;nion was formed for charitable purposes, and its common funds were applicable exclusively to chaiitable objects, yet two only of the men took the oath ; the rest remained out of work seventeen weeks and four days, when the difference was arranged by a meeting between the masters and men, at which it was agreed that work should be resumed by the men without reference to what had passed on either side. As the men were allowed to retain their Union, this was vir&not; tually a triumph for them, and this triumph was increased by a promise given by the masters not to take any more three years&rsquo; apprentices, terms which had no connection with the origin of the dispute, but were imported into it during its continuance. It is supposed, though it cannot be stated as a certainty, that it was during this strike that application was made to the shipwrights of London for their assistance, which, as soon as the stilke in Shields ^was over, led to the formation of the Shipwrights&rsquo; Union in the Thames, to which we have above referred.
Another trade remarkable for disputes between masters and workmen in the early part of this century was that of the Coopers, who do not appear to have had any regular systematic organized society, and yet in seventeen years we have five strikes recorded, the termination of which ivas so favour&not; able to the men, that in the evidence given by one of the witnesses deputed by the master-coopers to state their case as to the necessity of combination laws to prevent strikes, he is reported to have said that &ldquo; when strikes took place and differences existed, the men invariably gained their point.&rdquo; One of these strikes was in 1808, and was caused by a demand on the part of the men for an increase of wages. This, after the strike had lasted a few days, was assented to by the masters. A second strike was in 1813, when a similar demand was made by the men, and also assented to.,In 1816 the masters sought to reduce the wages ; the men resisted, un&not; less the masters would work by a list, which was refused. The men then struck, and almost immediately tendered a list, which the masters gladly agreed to, as by the terras of this list the wages were slightly reduced. Three years afterwards we find the men asking for the restoration of their origi&not; nal wages, which not being complied with, they struck, and remained out a fortnight or three weeks, and before they would resume work made out a list at the advanced prices. This was assented to, and an agreement was made that the prices in the list should remain in force from the 1st of February, 1819, to the 1st of February, 1820, and so on from year to year without alteration by either party unless fourteen days&rsquo; notice was given. In April 1835 the men gave notice of an alteration of terras; but the masters contended that as the 1st of February in that year had passed without notice, the men were precluded from varying the terms of the list of 1819 until the following February. It was during this strike that the evidence I have above quoted as to the success of the men in their strikes was given, and made the foundation of an application for a renewal of the more stringent provisions of the combination laws. In all these strikes there was no great combination to support them ; and it is a curious fact in connection with the history of our earlier Trades&rsquo; Unions, that they con&not; templated the strike of an individual workman as well as that of large and united bodies. This is evident from some of their rules that are left on record, with a copy of which we propose to close this article. These rules bear date 1813, but are supposed to have been first framed and acted on in the last century. They are headed&mdash;,&ldquo; Rules to be observed by a few friends, called the * Good Intent,&ldquo; Art. 1. That this Society be held at the Trade Division, Queen&rsquo;s Head, Fleet Street, Bethnal Green, and that none be admitted but such as are members of the said Trade Division.,&ldquo; Art. 2. That each member pay one halfpenny a week, and not receive any benefit under six months from his admission.,&ldquo; Art. 3. That if any member is turned out of work for demanding his price (in a becoming manner), he is to be allowed shillings per week,(according to the stock) until he gets work again ; and any member that helps him into work, to have five shillings for his trouble and five shillings from the trade book, which makes ten shillings for helping a man into work, that demands his legal price and loses his employ by so doing (sic).
&ldquo; Art. 4. That the money of the Society be kept in the trade box, and not to be taken for any purpose whatever.,&ldquo; Art. 5. That no member shall have any relief that owes more than one quarter&rsquo;s money.,&ldquo; Art. 6, That any member not clearing the book two weeks after quar&not; terly night, to fine one halfpenny ; the second quarter to fine one penny ; and so on for twelve months ; if not cleared on the fifth quarter, to be ex&not; cluded.,&ldquo; Art. 7. That when thirty members are admitted, every fresh member afterwards to pay twopence on admission.,&ldquo; Art. 8. That the Secretary collect the money, and that some of the members see that the money and accounts are right every quarter ; and if anything is deficient, the Secretary to make it good.,&ldquo; Art. 9. But should there be any more than one member on this fund at one time, then the said sum or sums to be equally divided among them.,&ldquo; Art. 10. That any member owing more than two quarters to the trade book, to have no benefit from this fund until he clears the aforesaid annars.,&ldquo; Art. 11. That the Secretary, being accountable for all deficiencies, be allowed one halfpenny per quarter from each member for his trouble.&rdquo;,Then follows a scale, according to which the allowances are to be made.,&pound;.,50 members with.1 in the box,,2,From 50 to 80 members with,From 80 to 100 members with,From 100 to 150 members with,From 150 to 200 members with,s.,3,3,4 4 4,4,5,6 5,5,6 6 6 6 7 7 7,7,8,d.,0 per week.,6 0 6 0 6 0 6 0 6 0 6 0 6 0 6 0 6 0 6,(and by way^of,For every pound in the box, sixpence per week advance ; illustration it is added)&mdash;&middot;,&ldquo; From one hundred and fifty members at &pound;20 in the box, it will amount to sixteen shillings and sixpence per week, by being turned out of work for demanding legal prices.&rdquo;
THOUGHTS ON DEILL.,BY A LONDON VOLUNTEER (NOT OF THE COLLEGE RIFLE CORPS).,FIEST NIGHT&rsquo;S EXPERIENCES.,Drill certainly extends man&rsquo;s sympathies in quite new directions.,Two of the primary elements which go to make up the defender of his country are the Sign-post and the Te-to-tura.,Since yesterday, I think I can enter far more into the feelings of both. To the Sign-post belong position, stand-at-ease, attention, falling in ; to the Te-to-tum right face, left face, right-about face, half face, front, eyes right. Marching, is but the Sign-post put into metrical locomotion; dressing, the same shaking into place.,The Sign-post, the Te-to-tum,&mdash;sympathetic entities henceforth. But the File !,Eight file, left file&mdash;mystic synonyms of the odd and even&mdash;how ye do bother one !,Wooden today, shall I ever be iron enough to fraternize with you ?,Shoulders square to the front ; heels together, toes turned out at an angle of 60 degrees, arms hanging straight from the shoulders, palms flat to the thighs, body straight, inclining forward, bearing on the forepart of the feet, head erect, eyes looking straight to the front, position &ldquo;perfectly easy and natural , without any stiffness or constraint .&rdquo;,Oh ! Colonel Lysons !...,A patriot turned forty-five performing the extension motions offers a spectacle such as none but the immortal Gods can sufficiently admire.,To be told to touch your toes with your hands, and at the same time to look at your fugleman (unless through your legs, he standing behind ?) is an insult offered by patriotism to man&rsquo;s intelligence at once and to his cervical vertebrae.,It was said by a friend, himself a serjeant of volunteers : &ldquo;Drill interests, so long as you are yet learning, i. e. so long as you are tempted to face right when you should face left, and left when you should face right, and have to watch over yourself accordingly.,But wuen, as with the regular, it has become by time and repetition purely mechanical, it must be intolerable to any who think about it.&rdquo;,True so far as it goes. The object of drill is to turn a man, from a man, into a perfectly fitting and perfectly working part of a war-machine.,He may and should, as a man, have pleasure in learning to fit and work perfectly as such.,And the war-machine being perfect, there should be a pleasure in work&not; ing it, for the officer who does so.,But for the private, when perfectly drilled, the pleasure of drill in itself
must be infinitesimal, if any ; nor can one wish for him, as a man, that it should be otherwise.,If once, from being unpleasurable, it becomes unpleasant, it must soon, as my friend says, be intolerable. The recoil from its mechanism, for those who look not beyond, must be towards the wildest self-indulgence.,Hence no doubt in part the childish love of mischief which often marks the soldier ; the reckless debauchery to which he is prone ; the brutal ex&not; cesses and violence into which he sometimes falls.,What then ? Is drill bad ?,Quite the reverse. Did it only help to make us enter into the peculiar temptations which beset our brethren in the army, their habitual weak&not; nesses and sins, it would be invaluable.,Even were those temptations, for them, beyond withstanding, those weaknesses and sins, for them, beyond overcoming, it would still be invalua&not; ble, and would make volunteering a duty ; since every drilled volunteer tends to diminish the need of a regular soldier to the extent of his own efficiency ; and for himself, occupied as he is in other ways, the temporary weariness of drill should never produce the like effects, and could never palliate them.,But drill should lead beyond itself. For your war-machine is made up of men after all, and the more you humanize it, the less oppressive will be its mechanism.,A common spirit, a hearty friendship between officers and men, and be&not; tween the men amongst themselves, may make each portion of the war-ma&not; chine fit and work in its place with positive pleasure ; drill may become but the daily occasion of meeting and working with friends.,This is often realized, even in the regular army, where officers have ren&not; dered themselves really beloved by their men, and a thorough fellow-feeling has grown up amongst the latter.,But how much more easily should it be realized among men mostly scat&not; tered and busy elsewhere during the greater part of their lime, and for whom drill should be at the worst, however monotonous, a diversion from the perhaps equally monotonous labours of the workshop, the saleshop, the counting-house ; for men not gathered together at haphazard like most of the regular regiments, but united together by the ties of neighbourhood, as in the great bulk of our volunteer corps ; profession, as in the Inns of Court corps and others ; origin, as in the Scotch and Irish corps Ы London ; fellowship in work, as in the corps formed from separate establishments, the different University corps, or, above all, in the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; corps ! Nothing but mismanagement, a bad choice of officers, or a selfish spirit among the members, should be capable of making volunteer discipline weigh heavier than a child&rsquo;s burden.,Higher stimulants at once and softeners to discipline there should no doubt &amp;amp; be than mere friendship and fellow-feeling; loyalty, and love of country, and the sense of duty, and the endeavour to do all things to God&rsquo;s glory, and the habit of looking on all work as direct Christian work which can be done openly in God&rsquo;s sight, and which has to be done for the sake of His creatures.
But these motives, whilst they should be always latent, will hardly ex&not; hibit themselves without affectation otherwise than under the pressure of emergency ; will hardly stimulate habitually more than a few, though they may by them sufficiently leaven the whole ; will above all never manifest themselves more efficiently than by that friendship and fellow-feeling from which we started.,Keep these therefore always in sight, most of all in choosing officers. Yet remember, that the friendship and fellowship must have their root and centre in the work, and can only be efficient when it is well done.,A good fellow who is not a good officer is not a good fellow for officer. A really good officer must be a good fellow for his work.,Nor should volunteers be over-hasty in choosing a commander. The recruit&rsquo;s first ideal of a commanding officer is necessarily the drill-serjeant. If volunteers choose too soon, they run great risk of being commanded only by Nature&rsquo;s drill-serjeants. Now drill-serjeants may be good commanding officers,&mdash;as perhaps Serjeant Reed might have taught the London Col&not; lege, had his modesty allowed it ;&mdash;but they may not.,As a general rule, the volunteer commander, when not already a mili&not; tary man, should be one accustomed to the exercise of responsibility, in the habit of deciding for himself ; for many occasions must occur, in which lie will be forced to decide off-hand. A man, on the other hand, accus&not; tomed, as private secretary, clerk, etc., to surrender his own will to another, without opinion of his own in matters of business, must be a very re&not; markable man to be fitted at once for command. Let him rise to* it, if need be.,Education visibly tells in drill. For drill consists of two parts,&mdash;what ia taught to the head, and what is taught to the body.,The head accustomed to learn, learns quickly, and then teaches the body, which thus has two teachers for one.,The head unskilled to learn, learns slowly, and cannot help the body, which sometimes even learns the quicker of the two, so that a man may learn to make motions mechanically, without being able to describe them ; for there is memory, as it has been said, in man&rsquo;s whole body.,Yet the education needed is not body-learning, but concentration of thought, which can be acquired by any thorough study, and which is greatly aided by quickness of eye, such as is trained by the honest prac&not; tice of any art.,Should rifle corps therefore be composed only of educated men ?,Quite the contrary. Because men are educated, they should help to educate others.,The soldier, and therefore the volunteer, learns not only from himself and his officer, but from his comrades. He is helped to learn wrong, if they teach him wrong ; right, if they teach him right.,Put a man with an uneducated sense of time into a squad which beats time correctly, and he will soon learn to beat time himself. Put him among those who feel time no more than himself, and the labour of teach&not; ing him will be unspeakable.
A mixture of classes then must be the leading feature of this movement. Neither should one or two classes alone be armed, nor should they, so far as possible, be armed separately ; although the different incidence of lei&not; sure hours may render this sometimes unavoidable.,The limit to volunteer enrolment should be, the point at which it is not expedient to substitute it for the chance of a militia ballot.,For this should be left the men without family, without fixed residence ; above all, the idle and the loose ; the men who are not fit to be trusted with uniform and accoutrements.,Considering the money value of these last, it is but right that some pay&not; ment should be rigorously exacted from the volunteer,&mdash;at least a yearly subscription by instalments,&mdash;and that he should not receive his uniform till he has paid up an amount which is a sufficient earnest of his sincerity.,Pleasant is contemplation ; but far different is practice. Pleasant to fra&not; ternize in theory with the sign-post and the te-to-tum ; most unpleasant on being bidden to &ldquo; right-about face,&rdquo; to find oneself spinning round the wrong way (as I did, alas ! last night). Those facings, how shall I again face them ? And oh ! that most tantalizing &ldquo; breaking off and reassem- bling.&rdquo;,&ldquo; This should be done frequently ; it is a very important practice.&rdquo;,Colonel Lysons ! Colonel Lysons ! V&raquo; ere you ever drilled tealess from 8 to 10 p.M. ? * J. M. Ludlow.,INTRODUCTORY LECTURE,On the &ldquo;History of Industrial Progress in England from 1800 to I860,&rdquo;,Delivered at the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, Jan. 21st, by F. Harrison, Esq,, M.A., Oxford.,The last hundred years have witnessed in Europe the greatest era of change which has ever occurred in history. During this period there has become established, or rather introduced, that which may be almost con&not; sidered as a new form of human society,&mdash;one which, to us at least, must appear a final one,&mdash;-the basis of which is universal industry, and the vital principle of which is universal education.,Such very sweeping language will no longer appear exaggerated if we consider all the changes of which we are the witnesses. We see around us a sudden and incredible expansion of industry in every form,&mdash;in agri&not; culture, in manufactures, in mining, in commerce, in inventions and every art and appliance of life, in colonization, and intercourse at home and abroad, working alongside of a development of science, of free inquiry, of general instruction, and ever widening public knowledge and public opinion ; all which together have had the effect of dissolving the ideas, habits, and authorities which clung together up to the middle of the last century. In their place we see all political power now virtually depending upon the command of industry (be it agricultural, commercial, or manufac&not; turing), whilst all political or social struggles virtually depend upon the questions involved between the different kinds ot industry and the different classes, as employers or employed, whom it calls into action.
All the grave questions of the day arise out of this new and sudden change. They are wholly unknown to earlier times. They absorb all thoughts and all energies. We see a general claim on the part of the whole body ol workers for admission as a component part of the body politic ;&mdash;a claim which is now universally admitted, the mode only re&not; maining the subject of anxious struggle. We see the more prominent of the actual workers so advanced, both materially and intellectually, as to claim an entire social and political equality with those hitherto considered above them, and a determination on the part of the bulk of their body to participate largely in both these kinds of advancement.,In the meantime the wonderful results of labour and skill change almost every relation and habit of life, and stimulate an activity before which everything gives way, and to which everything is possible. If we turn to the intellectual movement which works parallel to, but somewhat in ad&not; vance of, the industrial, we see the long results of philosophic thought daily bearing visible fruits tor our increased comfort and happiness, science daily becoming more and more the servant or the guide of industry ; all the old systems of philosophy, religion, or policy brought to the bar of scientific criticism, the area of general knowledge widening with infinite rapidity, and its might and power daily increasing. But the grand cha&not; racteristic which strikes us, and which is decisive of an entire change in society, is the undeniable fact that education is now claimed for and left open to all ; that it spreads and rules with an ever-increasing ratio.,Tuin to all you see around you ;&mdash;is there a sphere of society or a de&not; partment of thought which does not wear broad traces of convulsion and change ? Philosophy, science, religion, politics, art, every institution, every class of society, every form of life and thought, is visibly heaving with some irresistible force, and struggling amidst uncertainty to a surer ground. In the midst of such a universal movement, can we ever forget that we are witnessing a real revolution in history ? And can we doubt of the true,character of this movement when we remark these two prominent facts ?_,1. that all the struggles around us arise out of or relate to industry ; 3, that all modes of solution which enjoy any credit, depend mainly upon the spread of education and of reliable and useful knowledge.,Such, in its widest sense, is Industrial Progress ; and in order to point out to you (more by way of suggesting than teaching) some of its prominent chaiactenstics, I have thought it not useless (even though it is somewhat aimcult).to state what I take to be its full meaning and importance . W do 1 tllink it will be waste of time to trace the true origin of this immense expansion of industry which commenced before the close of the last century This, like every other great change in society, must be ulti&not; mately attributed to progress in speculative knowledge, iť is undoubtedly connected with the same movement which involved the great Prendi revc- iution itself; is due to the new order of ideas which had gradually risen out of the philosophical and scientific discoveries of the eighteenth century to which all the nations of Europe contributed, and the distant results of which they ail telt.,But to confine ourselves to England especially,&mdash;what a long series of events do we not find concurring about the same time to hasten her ex&not; pansion of industrial activity P In science we cannot overrate the influence
of the ideas of Newton gradually extending through all branches of exact science, giving inquiry a surer basis and a reliable method, and placing mechanics in a position to bear practical fruit. In physics and in chemistry the discoveries of Boyle, Cavendish, and Davy, introduced the habit and skill of experiment, the true starting-point of practical inventors. In politics, the growing strength of Parliament, and the element it contained of popular election, gradually opened the political arena, and through it social position and power, to wealth, and gave an enormous stimulus to the love of accumulation. Merchants, even manufacturers and successful trades&not; men, began to be a power in the State : a company of merchants conquered a mighty empire in the East, opened an inexhaustible field for activity, and returned to become statesmen or social leaders. Then in the West, the successful resistance of the American colonists and the wonderful growth of their empire, at once opened a new field for exertion and new materials for industry. At the same time commenced the vast system of coloniza&not; tion through the Southern seas, called out by the discoveries and voyages of Cook and his successors in the task of investigating the surface of the earth. These, together with the decay of the Erench, Spanish, and Dutch marines, all of which had been ruined in the long commercial wnrs that had marked the preceding hundred years, had called out the materials and the need for a greatly-extended commerce.,Commerce in its turn gave birth to fresh manufactures. Then began to be searched more deeply the bowels of our island for its treasures in iron and coal. Then fresh modes of internal intercourse were demanded. Canals, roads, tramways, steam-engines, steamboats, and railroads, became a necessity. The mill took the place of the loom ; and the factory of the workshop. It would be a mistake to suppose the great increase of our in&not; dustry or our commerce to be solely due to the discoveries of our naviga&not; tors, or the inventions of men of science ; to the voyages of Cook, or the spinning-jenny of Arkwright, or the steam-engine of Watt; to the rail&not; road of Stephenson, or the canals of Bridgewater. It was these discoveries which were made necessary by the spread of industrial energy. But throughout the whole sphere of thought and of activity, each element of progress acted and re-acted the one upon the other. The way in which they are all connected together is most suggestive and most astonishing. How the astronomical discoveries of Newton and of La Place acted upon the whole range of mechanics ; these upon navigation, the knowledge of the globe, and the desire for fresh discoveries ; this again upon commerce with new colonies, an extended marine and new trades ; that again necessitating new productions at home to exchange for the raw products of the new countries ; these new productions requiring extended means of producing, and new means of transport. Then how the mechanical experiments of abstract thinkers started the first ideas of inventors ; how the theories of Davy introduced fresh means of mining ; how the genius of Watt filled up the gap between abstract thought and fruitful action ; how the chemical discoveries of Lavoisier extended to every branch of manufactures ; and again how, in the reaction, navigation came to the assistance of astronomy, invention to that of mechanics ; how new processes stimulated new views of thought, may lead to constant reflection. Then we trace the multiplied and varying consequences of these efforts in the social system. The in-
creasing power, rank, and opportunities opened to the possessor of capital, the immense stimulus to exertion thus called out, the increased facilities, and comfort thrown open to the poorer, the utter transformation of ideas and wants consequent upon this change, both in the class of capitalist and labourer, the political struggles which speedily ensued upon this new accession of strength and self-confidence. The change from a rural to an urban population, from agricultural to manufacturing energy, from separa&not; tion of individual workmen to association in large masses. Then ensued the groivth of an enormous body of appliances to supply the wants of this new class. Everything, in fact, became at once multiplied, popularized, and cheapened. Then follow all the further indirect consequences of this newly developed form of existence. The necessity of congregating work&not; men together, necessarily leads to association, the power inherent in which becomes gradually apparent. They begin to see their strength; they know that it lies in union and in knowledge ; they form themselves into powerful bodies ; they put themselves often intellectually on a level with their employers ; they assume to become a power in the State ; they have their destiny in their own hands. Then arise all those fearful consequences resulting from an undirected, unregulated struggle in the race of competi&not; tion. Hence all those horrors of our industrial towns which make all thinking men confess that before social questions all others are insignifi&not; cant. Then come upon the workers themselves, as well as on all&deg;who watch the struggle from without, the conviction that by education alone can their lot be permanently improved. Then, side by side, it is seen how almost the same workers, under somewhat different conditions, may enjoy either most of the opportunities which a highly complicated society can offer even to the wealthy, or may be thrust down into a state of desperate misery and abasement. Hence spring up a whole crop of difficulties, questions, struggles, and wants in society, wholly new to this generation&rsquo; which come home even to the thoughtless, and engross all activity and attention. Then, in the vague distrust and unrest which penetrates all classes, come various suspicions, complaints, and doubts, of every institu&not; tion, constitution, society, or system, which foils to see or fails to satisfy the needs which exist. But among all these comes forth loudest one common cry, that education become sound, comprehensive, and universal; and thus the whole inquiry ends as it began, with the diffusion of knowledge! And as the whole movement may be ultimately traced to new principlesof thought, so its whole direction and success depends upon their general extension, adoption, and application.,Thus, to resume in one short sketch the outline of the great change whilst similar causes produced similar results generally throughout Europe&rsquo; the special causes in England were clearly a political and national condri tion, in which everything lay open to energy and industry. There were no more enemies to conquer on the high .seas, no arbitrary powers to be resisted at home. The philosophers had suggested paths of fertile inven&not; tion. The new-found worlds in the West and the East offered boundless resources. Personal freedom and the absence of all oppressive cares or wants at home, gave every opening to individual exertion. New com&not; merce created new manufactures, these a new manufacturing population. They, again, called out a new industry. Then capital increasing by
natural accumulations with great rapidity, engrossed an enormous power. Agriculture fell more and more into the hands of great capitalists ; pro&not; duction of all kinds in like manner. Industry became associated, congre&not; gated, and began rudely to organize itself. The struggle for existence called out constantly new efforts, each attended by a change in material or social life, until the whole future, the whole power of the State, has come to depend upon the command of industry ; and all inquiry takes, in some form or other, the direction of understanding, guiding, and elevating the industrial progress.,Such an entire range of new habits and ideas forms nothing less than, what we call, a social revolution. Up to the close of the first fifty years of the eighteenth century, England was not radically different in spirit from Avhat it had been for centuries. The England of George II. was not in true character very different from the semi-feudal England of James I. It was a difference in degree but not in kind. But the England of 1860 differs from the England of 1760 in essential character. A hundred years ago, authority virtually depended upon birth&mdash;industry or wealth made no claim to power, and none would have been admitted. The Church ex&not; isted almost without a rival. Society was supposed to be permanently di&not; vided into hereditary classes. The class who enjoyed leisure assumed a natural pre-eminence ; the wealthy merchant knew his station. The arti&not; san looked neither for a higher station nor an improved lot. Population had gone on slowly increasing for centuries, and nothing promised a sudden increase. Great Britain consisted of the English Isles and some outlying- plantations. The bulk of our industry was as it had been for centuries, agricultural&mdash;wool was still the staple of export trade; the land was culti&not; vated by thousands of small yeomen proprietors. Material improvement in life was gradual and slow ; new inventions were rare, excited little interest, and established themselves with difficulty. Science, ingenuity, skill, and toil were not incessantly working towards ever fresh improvements and de&not; velopments. There were no great industrial centres which by their num&not; bers, strength, and still more by their wants and misery, occupied a large space in the minds of politicians and thinkers. The whole range of ideas, the infinite appliances, and the extension of activity called out by this new condition of things, did not exist. The luxuries and comforts of life were restricted to few,&mdash;consumption was as fixed and as moderate as produc&not; tion. Education was a matter of privilege, and its spread was regarded as impossible and dangerous.,Now I propose to direct your attention to the leading features of this change. In so doing I shall make no attempt at novelty, and shall pro&not; bably state few facts with which you are not already familiar. I shall en&not; deavour to do little else than to classify the principal departments in which this industrial activity may be arranged, to draw your notice to the leading facts connected with the gradual expansion of each branch, recall to your minds the statistical data on the main points, and lastly, point out the social consequences which they involve and the prospects to which they lead us for the future.,(To Ъе concluded in our next.)
NEWS.,Manchester&mdash; The general meeting previous to the commencement of the second terra of the third session was held in the rooms of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, on the 11th January. Mr. Oliver Heywood, chairman of the Council, presided, and gave a short introductory address.&mdash;Professor Scott, who was then called upon to speak, thanked the Council for giving him an opportunity of expressing his continued goodwill to the insti&not; tution on the occasion of his reluctant withdrawal from the office of teacher of the Logic Class, which had hitherto connected him with the practical business and interests of the College. When it was first proposed to form a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; in Manchester, the suggestion was received with much interest in the places in which such Colleges were aheady established, and especially in the Metropolis. It w&rsquo;as thought that the experiment of establishing a College in a place like Manchester was calculated to excite peculiar hopes and fears and anxieties. &ldquo; It seems to me,&rdquo; said Mr. Scott, &ldquo; that Manchester is one of the fittest seats tor such a College ; and that there ought to he a greater number of persons here who would take an earnest interest in it, than in most places. There are some very obvious reasons why this should be so. My friends in London, when discuss&not; ing the matter, speak of the intelligence, the habitual industry, and the love of work in mental directions as well as in material, that they believe to exist in the northern part of the country, and especially in Lancashire: and they believe that peculiar results may be anticipated from the establishment of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; here. But it is not to these considerations that I now desire to direct special attention. Manchester is empha&not; tically the capital of money-making in this country, and probably in Europe. I have been very much struck with the number of persons who come to Manchester, as to a kind of diggings, to see what they can get, and not with a view of making themselves in any permanent way a part of the society ; very frequently they settle elsewhere when their temporary purpose is served ; and the proportion who succeed in what is considered by many the grand object of life is remarkable. The number of men who come to Man&not; chester and succeed in amassing large fortunes, and the power of commanding all those advantages in life that money can give, is conspicuously great. But why should that make Manchester an especially appropriate place for a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;? After all, those who succeed in acquiring riches are, and must for a long time yet remain a very small minority. The large majority cannot, in the nature of things, attain to the possession of wealth and the advantages its possession affords. Then is their life worth&not; less? No; there is something attainable that is still better worth seeking; and the development of the faculties of the mind in the struggle for wealth may be valuable in another direction. In the presence of the power of wealth, and of the search after what man may have, it is natural to recur to the power of the motive not to have but to be The grand aim of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; is to contribute to success in the latter direc&not; tion All that &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; can reasonably be expected to do is to give help of the sort that is mutual. It is not the mere possession of knowledge that should be an incentive to exertion. A man who has gone through a University course and has acquired a great amount of learning, is often an exceedingly poor creature ; and the mere man of letters, the student by profession, may often be tempted to envy a practical man in the humblest walks of hie, the command over his own powers of body and mind&rsquo; which he has acquired by actual use and experience. One way towards attaining all the best objects that a man can pursue, and being all that a man ought to be is to seek the acquisition of a higher literary and scientific culture. It is well for a mak to admit and to feel the great advantages to society as a whole, as well as to individuals, derived from the incentives to the acquisition of wealth by honest industry ; but it is better that they should remember that though ail cannot be rich, as sure as God had made them all can and ought to be men (cheers) He has put upon us no responsibilities which He does not intend us to meet ; and if He intended us to act truthfully and intelligently it is within our reach to know, to practise, and to act intelligently accordingly (applause).&rdquo;- Mr. Ransome read the Report for the first term of the Session 1859-60, embodying the following facts:&mdash;Number of new students registered for the term, 46, being with one exception, a larger number than during any preceding term ; total entries, 133 &middot; entries for classes, 19o ; average attendance, about 65 per cent. In the Logic, English Language English History, and Zoology, the average was higher; the greatest deficiency in timas m previous terms, appearing in the Mathematical classes. A large proportion of the
members were very loose in their attachment to the College, and left after a brief and cursory attendance. In order to stimulate exertion, and to give tangible evidence of progress, the Council had instituted prizes and certificates for deserving students; and those students who passed satisfactorily through a certain complete course, would be elected into the Council. A Students&rsquo; Committee would also be formed during the en&not; suing term. Explanations from teachers followed. Dr. Angus Smith alluded to the difficulty of teaching science in the College, considering the very limited time which the class of Chemistry, for instance, devoted to the subject. It was impossible to do more than lay down the preliminary laws of the science, and even in this respect, in a very imperfect manner. The establishment of preparatory and practice classes, however, would be of the highest use in meeting this difficulty. He had students who were quite fitted to take charge of a junior class of this character.&mdash;Reference was made to the pro&not; posal received from Ancoats College for the formation of a rifle corps, and which was to be referred to the consideration of the Students&rsquo; Committee.,London .&mdash;The University of London has just instituted the degrees of Bachelor and Doctor of Science (B.Sc. and D.Sc.). The former is to rank with the degree of B.A., and is to be conferred after two examinations (besides that of matriculation) &ldquo; attesting the general knowledge acquired by the graduate of the fundamental principles and most im&not; portant facts of the chief divisions of Science.&rdquo; A detailed statement of the nature of the examinations may be had of the Registrar of the University. A copy can be seen on application to Mr. Shorter.,The General Meeting usually held in the Christmas holidays was converted into a New Year&rsquo;s social party on the 5th January. The evening passed very pleasantly, both teachers and students bringing with them plenty of fair friends, sisters, wives, and &ldquo; in&not; tended dittos,&rdquo; as they say in the North. Each floor of the house had its own attrac&not; tions. Above, the walls were covered with the productions of the drawing classes, testi&not; fying to the vitality of the Art department ; and Mr. Bunney&rsquo;s beautiful sketches of Swiss Alps and Italian lakes attracted a constant stream of admiring gazers. On the first floor was displayed a part of the treasures of the Museum, with due exposition of the mysteries of eocene and pleiocene fossils. There were also exhibited a few specimens of a more &ldquo; recent formation,&rdquo; the Enfield rifle, which excited their proper amount of interest. But the ruling attraction was, as usual, the music. By the help of some kind friends of the College we were able to produce a pleasant little vocal concert. It in&not; cluded some of the best English glees and madrigals, old and new ; some bits never to be too often heard, from the music of the immortals (Rossini&rsquo;s &lsquo; Israelites&rsquo; Prayer &rsquo; and Mozart&rsquo;s &lsquo;Non pi&ugrave; andrai&rsquo;) ; here and there some (equally imperishable) old English songs, such as &lsquo; Annie Laurie,&rsquo; best beloved of native ditties. For the enjoyment afforded, the party was indebted principally to two young ladies, the Misses Thompson, whose exer&not; tions were conspicuous alike in the solos and choral pieces. The College singing classes assisted in one or two of the best-known glees, and the whole assembly joined in the &lsquo; Tight little Island &rsquo; (the College &lsquo; Marseillaise &rsquo;), and greeted Mr. Hughes&rsquo;s wonderful narrative of the &lsquo;Three Bristol Sailors&rsquo; with a chorus of inextinguishable laughter. During all which time an incessant series of tea-parties was being carried on downstairs, till the evening came to an end : the 230 people present all agreeing that a few hours so spent once every six mouths made a pleasant interlude to the serious work of the,Rifle Corps .&mdash;The following are the donations not acknowledged in the last Magazine :&mdash;,&pound;,s.,d.,&pound;.,s.,d.,S. Men eminent in their profession had consented to assist in imparting instruction to the classes in the College, and much iuterest was felt in the success of the enterprise. One thing chiefly desirable was of course a library, and for the purpose of collecting one donations were generously made. It was very naturally suggested to Mr. Lincoln that it would be graceful and pleasant for the American Boston to contribute to this object. Accordingly, the matter was brought to the notice of our booksellers and private citizens, and the idea was received with cor&not; dial approbation. The result is, that a large number of volumes has been sent to the Mayor for transmission abroad, from publishers and from the most prominent of our citizens. A question arose as to the admission of American books into England. This is satisfac&not; torily settled by a correspondence between Mr. Lousada, the British Consul at this port, and the London Foreign Office. MARCH 1, 1860.,FIFTH ANNUAL REPORT OF THE LONDON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,The College has increased in numbers during the last year. The in&not; crease in the last two Terms cannot be accounted for merely by the desire for knowledge in the Class from which its members are chiefly drawn. A Rifle Corps has been established among us. The eagerness to join it has induced many to enrol themselves as students. We believe that this par&not; ticipation in the great national movement will, on the whole, promote the best interests of the College. To give our men common objects and com&not; mon interests, to connect those objects and interests with those of all other classes, and of the nation at large, has been quite as much our aim as to introduce them into lecture-rooms for specific kinds of instruction. Their zeal to increase their knowledge will, we believe, be in proportion to their zeal for the well-being of their country, and to their conviction that they, as much as the wealthiest, must stand or fall with it ; that they, as well as the wealthiest, may do something that it may stand and not fall. What&not; ever makes them more efficient citizens will make them better learners. At the same time the Council desire, for the sake of the Rifle Corps, that it should be kept in subordination to the main purposes of the College ; they would discourage any enlargement of its numbers which might tempt the students to forget that they were not originally brought together for drill, but for lessons.,The finances of the College have now been for some time under the di&not; rection of a Committee, composed for the most part of Students. They have applied themselves to their task with great earnestness and skill, and the College owes them much for their careful control of its expenditure. We can scarcely promise ourselves that it will soon be entirely self-support&not; ing ; but we trust that those friends who have sufficient confidence in us to trust us with their contributions will find that they are not wasted.,An opportunity having presented itself of acquiring, for a moderate sum, the freehold of the house adjoining the College, the Council thought it best to make the purchase, applying to this object the sum of stock (&pound;900) placed at their disposal by two friends, as stated in the last Report, and borrowing on mortgage the remainder of the amount required. The debts which appear on the statement of accounts are therefore a charge on the fieehold of the two houses. The one just purchased is for the present held on a lease, of which eleven years are unexpired, at a rental which furnishes a certain sum annually towards the liquidation of the debt. A portion of the balance of &pound;135. 6s. 3 d., mentioned in the Cash Account, has since been invested in the purchase of &pound;100 Three-per-Cent. stock, to be applied to the same purpose.,The Magazine has given much satisfaction to the Council, and they believe
to the students. As the price is necessarily kept low and the circulation is limited, and as the usefulness of it is generally recognized, they have thought it their duty to support it by individual subscriptions, since they were not justified in devoting to it any of the College funds. The mana&not; gers of other Colleges have felt that it was a valuable medium of commu&not; nication for them, and have kindly given it both pecuniary and literary aid. The accounts of the proceedings which are contained in the Magazine make it unnecessary that we should dwell upon them in this Report.,The scheme which was alluded to in the last Report, for giving greater coherency to the studies of the College, and for assisting those who are entering it to choose their courses, has worked thus far satisfactorily. The Council, by the advice of a Committee appointed to consider the subject, are adding to this scheme a more regular system of Examination than was found possible at the commencement of the enterprise. They are not sa&not; tisfied with recording that six students have received certificates of com&not; petency during the past year in different subjects and that one was elected an Associate of the College ; they trust that a majority of the members of the classes will seek to obtain these honours. The Examinations will not be of a kind to alarm any student ; but they will enable him to test his own proficiency and to ascertain his weak as well as his strong points.,The teachers of the Drawing Classes speak very favourably of their pro&not; gress. Drawings and paintings were exhibited at a Conversazione held at the College early in the year, which we believe convinced those who saw them that the artists must have been taught by a thoroughly sound method, and must have made the best use of the opportunities.,The Preparatory and Practice Classes, taught by students, have been in full operation during the year. The Council attach much importance to both these parts of the College work. Experience has shown their veiy great value in lessening the deficiencies of class-teaching.,The progress made in the Museum, partly through the kindness of friends, chiefly from the activity of the students, will appear from the ac&not; companying Report on the subject.,The usual tables of the number of Students, of the classes from which they are taken, and of the financial condition of the College, are subjoined.,REPORT ON THE MUSEUM.,The Museum is rapidly increasing ; almost too rapidly for the size of the room which it occupies. During the Vacation Terms about ninety-four specimens were added, mostly minerals, and presented by Mr. Decket, E.G.S., Wolverhampton, Mr. Ludlow, Mr. Litch&not; field, and Mr. Pattison. Two large cases have been filled by the specimens received in the course of last Term, more than 1,777 in number, and consisting of minerals, fossils, recent shells, butterflies, etc. The principal gifts have been as follows :&mdash;from Mr. R. C. Taylor, the whole of his private collection, containing principally London daj&#39; and chalk fossils collected by himself from the strata round London, with a number of minerals and recent shells; from Mr. E. Cook about 130 butterflies and moths collected by him&not; self from Norfolk (our first donation of insects) ; and from Mr. Bell, about 100 speci&not; mens of building-stones, which we hope is but the beginning of an Industrial Collection. We are now in a position to get our minerals named, thanks to Mr. Davies, Jun., of the British Museum, who has undertaken the superintendence of that department. He has
already named about half our collection of minerals, including the beautiful set of Alpine specimens, presented by Mr. Ruskin. As soon as the naming is completed and the fossils are arranged in the new cases, we intend to draw up a list of duplicates, to he inserted in some future number of the Magazine, so that we may be able to exchange with the Pro&not; vincial colleges or other museums. John Fotheringham, Hon. Mansfield, Esq. ... 500,Dr. Josh. Skey.5 0 0,&pound;45 0 0,EEPOET OF THE WOMEN&rsquo;S CLASSES, 1859.,The number of Pupils has averaged about the same as in the preceding years. The attendance in the first Term (as usual) much exceeded that in the later months. Some lectures on Household Economy, given early in the year by Mr. Tegetmeier, Avere much appreciated by the Pupils, The subjects included home-ventilation, stoves, fuel, lighting apparatus, washing and cleaning, besides something of the principles of Economical Cookery.,During the summer a Class was commenced for Book-keeping, which met with sufficient success to warrant its being resumed as soon as possible.,The only other fresh subject introduced among the Classes last year was English Grammar, for which there had been so many applications from the Pupils, that the Class was commenced last Easter Term. The attendance has been excellent and regular, and increasing in proportion to that in the other Classes. There is every encouragement to continue the subject.,Some disappointment has been experienced in connection with the sub&not; ject of Fiain Needlework, the attendance at which has shown a tendency to de&not; crease ; but the Teachers are determined to make another trial after Easter, when daylight may permit of its being carried on from five till six o&rsquo;clock!
With the exception of the Grammar, all the higher Classes have been less frequented than the more elementary. Writing and arithmetic con&not; tinue by far the most popular studies.,As to the &Lambda; ocal Music, in spite of the spur which might have been ex&not; pected from the practising for the Evening Conversaziones, and lately from some regular practices of the Men and Women&rsquo;s Singing Classes combined, the number of Pupils has continued to decrease ever since the teaching by a professional master was discontinued ; and it will be seriously considered before the beginning of next Term whether it will be better worth while to continue the Women&rsquo;s Singing Class, or to make use of the time for sub&not; jects more generally appreciated.,A new arrangement of fees has been made for the present year, by which the pupils enter for the subject, instead of (as formerly) for the afternoon, which included two subjects. The object was to ensure their only joining the Classes they valued ; but as yet the success of the measure seems very doubtful. The immediate result has been a diminution in the attendance on the higher Classes, which is the reverse of what was anticipated.,An additional interest has been added to the Classes for Eeading this year, by introducing the most advanced Pupils to the study of Shakespeare, which has already proved a great attraction to many of them. The most advanced writing Pupils are exercised in writing from memory, and on va&not; rious subjects. The English History, Geography, and Natural History are but scantily attended ; and the first effect of making the Bible Class free to all Members of the Classes has been that of seriously diminishing the number of Pupils.,Another newly-established order this year is, that one of the Teachers reads Prayers every evening at five o&rsquo;clock. The attendance is of course quite voluntary; but hitherto most of the Pupils remain for the Prayers, which are short and simple.,February, 1860.,Table of Attendance at the Women&#39;s Classes , 1860.,&#39;,Lent,Term.,Easter,,Vacation.,Michael&not;,mas.,Writing.,61,34,22,26,Vocal Music.,52,31,14,16,Lectures on Domestic Economy,51,&mdash;,&mdash;,&mdash;,Arithmetic.,47,30,16,24,Heading.,34,25,14,16,History.,31,18,&mdash;,9,Bible Class.,29,15,&mdash;,10,Geography.,26,20,&mdash;,16,Needlework.,26,8,&mdash;,4,Book-keeping.,&mdash;,&mdash;,10,&mdash;,Grammar.,&mdash;,24,&mdash;,21,Natural History.,&mdash;-,19,&mdash;,8,Aggregate number at Classes .,. s,89,56,22,39
