Twining . .,. 1 0,0,Miss Wodehouse .,. 6 10,0,33,Miss Williams Wynn,. 1 1,0,&pound;26 5,0,&pound;16,5,0,Worn, en s Classes. &mdash;,-Abstract of,Secretarys Cash Account ,,1859.,Ъг.,&pound;. s.,d.,Or.,&pound;.,s.,d.,To Balance, Dec. 31st, 1858,12 3,2İ,By Rent .,30,0,0,&bdquo; Donations and Subscrip-,&bdquo; Secretary&rsquo;s Salary . .,20 10,0,tions .,26 5,0,&bdquo; Lectures on Household,&bdquo; Pupils&rsquo; Fees ....,29 16,6,Economy . . . .,5,0,0,&bdquo; Advertisements . .,4,3,0,,, Materials used by Classes ,, Balance in hand, Decem-,2,2,5,her 31st, 1859 . . .,6,9,31,&pound;68 4,8*,&pound;68,4,81,ON THE STUDY OE CHEMISTEY.,IN TWO PARTS.,By Professor Angus Smith, Owen&rsquo;s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, and &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,,Manchester.,I .&mdash;The Romance.,You ask me what are the advantages to be gained by studying Chemis&not; try. I might say, read Ure&rsquo;s &lsquo; Dictionary of Arts,&rsquo; and see what is due to Chemistry ; and when that is done read History, and see how much our progress is owing to it, and then yon will not know it all. But you want all these sources of information condensed into a Magazine&mdash;the experience of ages put into a few lines. Well, if anything can condense learning, it is Chemistry. There was a time when men could only make alum in the East ; no, they did not make it, they gathered its crystals that effloresced or grew out of minerals containing sulphur, alumina, and potash, and found it useful. He who first learnt that it could be made from a stone
in Italy had a statue erected to him ; but here iu Manchester we make it as fast as we please, and only wish the people would use it up faster. Now it is made almost in a second; it can even be done by a kind of ex&not; plosion, so rapid is its formation, and it must be made rather slower than might be, to save the shock to our nerves. This I call condensing time and work and the knowledge of ages. Then all the accumulated workers in alum could not give the information which is really now condensed into a few words in a chemical book. Chemistry is a great condenser ; all its words mean great volumes of arts. But Chemistry does not always deal with arts, although it can throw them off in thousands at its will. Neither do we learn Chemistry merely to make money. Not so at least does it ever, come to the student. Let us look at the young chemist : he is only a child, but he is evidently bom to study Chemistry, because his constant question is, Of what is the world made ? If it is so large and so heavy, why is there so little, and such a mere thin fflm of it exposed to our observation ? How can the heat of it within be so great as you say, so that it will melt cast-iron only thirty miles down ? and how did the heat get there ? and if there, why does it not come faster out ? These are the wandering thoughts that haunt the chemist&rsquo;s opening mind ; these the thoughts that mix up with his play, and blend with the strange stories of the nursery, till the world becomes a great romance and every stone under his feet a riddle. Chemists tell us that clay is only silica and alumina ; and what are they ? Clay was used from time immemorial to make bricks ; the oldest houses we have are made of bricks ; the newest houses preparing are being made of bricks : clay seems fitted for the sole purpose of making bricks. True, there is some of it used to grow corn, but the stiffness of the clay is often injurious ; and they wish much at some farms in the coun&not; try that there was a little more sand in it. Can they really grow food out of clay and sand ? But clay is used for more purposes ; I see it used for pottery. Surely that must be fine clay, and the land which holds it must be aristocratic land ! No, it is not aristocratic ; it is waste and barren. But they have even learnt to make the finest china of clay. How beau&not; tiful it is ! Many arts have combined to make it perfect. The potter has prepared the clay, and his fellow-worker has prepared the colours,&mdash;both are chemists,&mdash;and the artist has brought his taste, and made out of the combination a very gem. The despised clay has become a noble substance, a casket worthy of receiving the most beautiful ideas of the artistic mind.,The young chemist is astonished ; nobleness and lowness are confused in his mind. That ignoble substance that he wipes from his feet becomes precious iu his eyes ; he scarcely dares to say that his shoes are dirty be&not; cause they are merely covered over with clay ; he will not call it by the undignified name of mud. If it has become so noble, may not all clay be&not; come so, and this earth, that is commonplace, become enriched in a trans&not; formation ?,The young chemist is awed ; he is almost afraid. What powers have brought that gem to light out of filth and darkness P Are dust and dark&not; ness only a mask under which things lie waiting for their resurrection P The young chemist lives in a dream, and the secrets of the universe hang heavy upon him; and this because he visited a porcelain manufactoiy, and saw many scores of people working out this problem in the simplest.
easiest, and most commonplace way. The youth is lost in thought ; but he feels that there is a strange power lying in all matter, ready to en&not; noble it at the call of him who knows the secrets of nature. What might not happen to him if he were to read and explore ? The great caverns of the earth might empty out great secrets on him ; the strange powers lyino- under his feet might endow him with some rare gift in the shape of a spe- cial knowledge granted to his devotion. It was pleasant thus to come into a world that was not, as at first appeared, a mere desert, out of which with gieat difficulty a laborious spade could dig out a few potatoes, house- holded by paternal care for a winter&rsquo;s store. No; for him there now bloomed a future of unalloyed happiness, when each special fragment of his study would reveal to him its hidden meaning ; his only evening would be the darkness of a problem, and his only morning its clear solution.,We find in the &lsquo; Arabian Nights&rsquo; Entertainments,&rsquo; that Aladdin, and in&not; deed many others, are represented as going down into holes in the earth, which turn out to be richest storehouses. There were trees which grew rubies and sapphires and corundums as large as apples or orano-es &mdash;pre&not; cious gems given to favoured mortals in scarcely tolerable profusion, al&not; though given to us so scantily that we are glad of a small piece like a head of a pin to surround with gold ; and still better pleased if we can get a piece the size of ten pin-heads, making us somewhat distinguished among our acquaintance;&mdash;of what were these rich gems composed? Really his eyes did not deceive him; they were made of alumina, and alumina lay m the clay beneath his feet. y,Cannot you imagine a time when men knew chemistry so well that they took the clay and made it into those beautiful gems you see ? The ancients were fond of this kind of chemistry, and may have attained it in their seaich for gold : or was it merely glass gems which they made ?,1 fear these Egyptians must have tried many such tricks, with their know&not; ledge of glass and their love of seals and gems. Poor Aladdin&rsquo;s gems may have been glass too. I do not doubt that he represents a man of wonder&not; ful wealth who rose rapidly to such greatness as wealth can give, and who came rapidly down when his wealth was spent. Chemistry thus brought the young ehermst into the circle of morals, and he speculated on the rise and fall of nations as of individuals. Did Athens fall when she exhausted her silvei mines m Laurion P or did she fall because her spirit had left her ? and would a noble spint have brought her more silver mines, at least in some corner of the world ? Surely there can be no difficulty in the answer It is we who bring out the treasures of nature by our minds acting on it.&rsquo; ш ľ ! a ure ^at brings out the treasures of our mind by acting on us We then have only to look anywhere with genius, and out of the dust be&not; fore us will arise smoke and fire and great powerful spirits that wifi obey our command. The genius that came out of the old pot in the fisher&rsquo;s net was an emblem of tne fisher&rsquo;s power. We do as he^id ; we take a few dirty pots and call them cells, put in dirty iron and zinc Lnd disagreed vitiiol, and make it encircle the earth at our bidding, giving out the mean wlule choking vapours. It would be most interesting to learn Tth&iexcl; ancients knew e ectncity or galvanism, and if the memory of these o-^at things dwindled away into tales of spirits. If so, this Will greatly&#39;alter,e gieat old alchemists, who instead of being men that kept truth alive
and slowly increased it, will become rather like wretched savages, through whom civilization gradually dwindled away till almost extinguished. Here then is a new theory of ghosts found by the young chemists ; and if, like many other theories, not true, still bearing on it the stamps of a great truth, that the great forces of nature are made by lower minds into undignified spectres. The dark and terrible tempests of a mountainous and dreary land speak to the deep feelings of the inhabitants, and are, as sym&not; pathetic companions, looked on as &ldquo; riders of the storms,&rdquo; and when the lightning comes from them, &ldquo; spirits ride on beams of fire.&rdquo; (Ossian.),But these storms themselves, why are they, and in what is their terrible power ? The earth is very calm, seldom does it move ; slowly in the course of ages continents arise, and bring from the deep ocean lands fit for great nations. Slowly continents disappear, having fostered great nations they retire to the obscurity of the ocean. The beautiful islands of Greece that once rose so enchantingly out of the sea, are in some cases sadly di&not; minished by the return of the movements which gave them birth. Are they in time to go, and is there to be nothing permanent because the centre of the earth is a monstrous agent, ever sending out chemical force without thought for man and his necessities ? Or is it that man must learn that the earth is not immortal, but that it is in constant change ; every season alters the land and every year diminishes the soil, which is for ever flowing to the ocean ? Our wealth is flying before us, wrenched away before our eyes, and our very land is ceasing to be. Is it not better that it should sink into the ocean to be renewed, than that it should stand blasted and unhappy,&mdash;a bare rock, incapable of nourishing the living or of giving the shelter of a grave to the dead? Landscapes themselves must change ; and there will be a season among the ages as there is among the years. If then the earth is so changeable, why not the air ? The light air may be ex&not; pected to change more readily. And it does change ; not a moment is the same as the preceding, because of the chemical forces. The air becomes heated and expands ; it changes by imbibing water ; the water acts differ&not; ently on the rays of light ; they again on the scene and on our spirits, our feelings, and on the flowers and plants. The heat goes, the water falls or is deposited, the plants are refreshed, the spirits are made elastic. The water has been conveyed in vapour to a place of cooling, and terrible storms have been caused by the change in the bulk of the air, on account of the expanding heat and the contracting cold, so that the light and elas&not; tic spirits of the earth have become excited, and awful then are the ele&not; ments when their wrath is aroused.,In conversation the elements mean chiefly, if not entirely, the atmo&not; sphere and all that floats in the air. It is in the atmosphere that nature chiefly expresses itself. It is there that we look for its smile and its frown, ahd it is there that its chief utterance is found. It is in it that we look for the calmness of the heavens, and in it we see into depths that are infi&not; nite,_a real eternity, No wonder then that we see change in the air ; it,has much to express, much to do, and much to undo ; without its activity the business of the world would stand still. True, we could sometimes, as we imagine, spare its violence when our friends aie on the sea and the winds begin their busy work, clearing out the impurities of the world, and sweeping away such vessels as unthinkingly have existed near some cob-
webs to be destroyed. But the work of the world must be done, and here we come in contact with the morals of creation. Stern and terrible are the laws which govern matter. We may steer between the insterstices of ice ; but if we come between two great floating masses that are decided on touching at the spot where we stand, there is no help for us. It is not in nature that we can find mercy for the ignorant ; for the wise man that knows its laws there is the most beneficent power. Yet there is mercy, alter all, in the constitution of man himself, who readily bends to all those movements which look like the whims of the weather.,It was an age of poetry when the young chemist thought of these things, and, ignorant of exact scientific laws, blended his general reading in physics with his reading in metaphysics. He had found that men had attained great wisdom and great fame by metaphysics; others had led in their studies a pleasant life, almost idle as it appeared. One for years had sat in his chair, and out of the depths of an active soul had produced ideas that had awed generations. The great preachers and great poets too, when they did their work, retired to calmness and comfort, and, shut up in themselves, obtained revelations of truth or of beautv, which they brought forth to a delighted people. Why not study all the truths of the universe in the same way ? How pleasant it would be to enter one&rsquo;s study, and before the night was over produce a new and beautiful theory of the earth and of all animated things in it. The theory should include chemistry and physics as well as natural history, and it should also tell how the world began and how it would end ; and show also how it was that bodies like ours were inhabited with souls so strange and so diverse in character. Several people have done so, but they have not done it well. It is better first to see what they have done and how they have done it.,Let us look. All persons ivho have attempted this have more or less given some attention to the real state of the world,&mdash;to the actual facts of the case. Even Avicenna, with his Eastern imagination, has not neglected that; and old Bather Kircher, who could see, as it would appear from the ■fine old plates in his book, deep into the recesses of the earth, did not neu&not; leet facts entirely, but speculated upon his long experience of a quakin&raquo;&rsquo; land, full o&iacute; stiange exhalations. What is curious too, nearly all persons &#39;vho have written have shown that former writers were wrong; and when they wish to show it clearly, they all turn to the facts of nature, that they may produce out of them material to convince their hearers, and these facts of nature are always deemed more conclusive than the most wonderful thoughts of the most original thinkers. Yet you say facts are not thoughts nature must have many ways of being explained, and a fertile intellect tan bend the mere material things o&iacute; creation to its superior will. Is its own will not divine, or a part of Him who created it P There are various modes of speech ; the dumb man is not behind in showing his will on many points; the child learns thoughts and facts greater in number before it can speak than in any equal space of time afterwards. Easy impressi&not; bility and absence of active interfering thoughts conduce more to the re&not; ception of knowledge than the brilliancy of intellect.,The world speaks by its facts. It may be that it has voices still more c ear to a higher reason than ours, but to us the facts are gradually made clear by close observation and by careful reasoning. We turn them into
words. Now when in words, they are not merely facts expressed, but they become in us thoughts and opinions, history, and even prophecy. The oldest things are brought up historically before us, and the newest things are filled with the most vigorous life, and the future, to a certain extent, becomes revealed. The works of nature show the thoughts of nature as it thus worked in silence, and, we had almost said, in secret, from the begin&not; ning of time. Our science is really not our thoughts ; and if so, it is not true science : true science is the thought of nature, the plan of Providence, and the mind of God,,Surely then it tvas absurd for our young chemist so to settle creation. He soon learnt its folly. He had committed two faults ; in the first he had seen science but as a romance, and had sought it for a strange amusement ; in the second, he had seen it as an intellectual feat. Can I call the first a fault ? It is not well to do so ; the youth must rejoice in his dreams, for they are often glorious, and we cannot tell how far they are true. But the second is a fault, and one too common, to make science an intellectual feat. The great thoughts of the Creator when he made the world must be extremely interesting, and there must be enough in them to satisfy every mind ; but they are not to nourish vanity in men who make use of them. We must look and learn, and when we have observed we must not too hastily conclude. There is time enough in the world to study God&rsquo;s laws, although we may not find it in our lives. If we conclude hastily we feed on husks carefully peeled off the fruit for our own use, and we refuse the abun&not; dant measure that lies beneath of full and generous nourishment.,We shall follow the chemist to the laboratory, and see in what manner he overcomes some of the first difficulties.,HISTORY OF TRADES&rsquo; UNIONS AND STRIKES.,By T. R. Bennett, Esq., M.A., Christ Church, Oxford, Barrister-at-Law.,(Continued from p. 20 .),Our last article was devoted to showing the causes and effects of strikes, as developed in some of the trades of which we have the most perfect re&not; cord in the early part of the present century. The list might be easily extended, for in spite of the numerous Combination Laws which then dis&not; graced our Statute Book, we find combinations amongst all classes of workmen where uniformity of wages for time prevails, except in those where the regulation of wages was entrusted to the magistrates or some independent tribunal. The existence of these Combination Laws is in itself strong evidence of the inability of the masters to contend against their workmen when both were left to themselves, and if we refer to the Parlia&not; mentary journals of that day we find still more conclusive evidence of this in the petitions presented by the masters to the Houses of Parliament, pray&not; ing for redress against the Unions of their workmen.,It would be but a repetition of what has been already said, if we were to take the history of every trade, for all alike (with one exception to which we shall shortly refer) present a series of disputes, combinations, and strikes,
which, from the complaints made by the masters, seem generally to have terminated in favour of the men. Thus, to confine ourselves to the hatters, to which we referred in a previous number. The petitions of the master manufacturers of the hat trade sets forth, &ldquo; That the hatters in and near the city of London had been for several years past, and then were, in difficulties, and unable to carry on their manufactures, by reason of the great scarcity of journeymen in the trade, and of their repeated demands to have their wages advanced to unreasonable prices, and their hours of work reduced;&rdquo; and the petition concludes by stating &ldquo;that the petitioners were without remedy unless they could obtain the aid of Parliament.&rdquo; Part of the redress asked for was &ldquo;that the magistrates might be empowered to fix the rate of wages.&rdquo; The result of this appeal to Parliament was the appointment of a Committee, before whom the evidence was given as to the congresses of the men to which we have re&not; ferred before, and the report of this Committee led to the passing of an Act in 1777 for the better regulating the hat manufacture. It would have been well for both masters and men if this Act had contained some pro&not; vision for regulating wages in accordance with the prayer of the masters&rsquo; own petition, but for some reason or other this was omitted, though the provisions of the statute were very stringent as regarded the men. In one of the clauses it was provided, &ldquo; that if any person should attend or solicit any journeyman hatter to attend any club, or should subscribe or pay any money to the same, he should be committed for three months.&rdquo; In spite of this stringency the Hatters&rsquo; Union continued to flourish, and we have nu&not; merous strikes recorded, in most of which the men gained a decided ad&not; vantage over the masters. The only unsuccessful one was in 1794, when the men struck for an advance of wages, but afterwards went in without any alteration. The other strikes were in 1803, 1810, 1817, and 1820. The most remarkable was the one in 1810. The price of provisions had increased, and the men asked for a corresponding increase of wages. This the masters refused for some time, but after the strike they made propo&not; sals for a compromise, and a meeting was held, attended by both masters and men, at which a uniform scale of prices was fixed.,The rules of the Hatters&rsquo; Club by which, in spite of the Act of 1777, the men were more than a match for their masters, may not be unin&not; teresting to our readers. If a man was out of work for any proper cause he obtained a ticket, generally called a blank, to travel with and take with him. This entitled him to be relieved at the different towns where there were men in the trade. In London, if a stranger came in and proved that he was out of work, he received five shillings, and if he brought a blank from another town he had in addition to this a bed for three nights, and two pots of beer each night to drink. The blank was in effect a cer&not; tificate of membership from the club to which he belonged, .and was a voucher for his being out of work (whether on strike or not was immaterial) ; and that of itself entitled him to assistance from all members of clubs in the same trade, until he could find employment. Indeed, it is remarkable that combinations amongst the hatters, though necessarily secret in conse&not; quence of the Act of 1777, were more systematic than in perhaps any other Trades&rsquo; Union ; thus showing the absolute inefficacy of all Combi&not; nation Laws where an equivalent for being subject to restriction was not
given to the workmen. The Hatters&rsquo; Combination Act continued in force &rsquo; till 1824, when it was repealed with thirty-four other Combination Laws that at that time disgraced our Statute-Book.,A ery diderent was the effect of the London Weavers&rsquo; Combination Act, commonly called the Spitalfields Act, which was passed in 1773, chiefly through the exertions of an alderman of London. This was the only Act of the kind that secured to the workmen any advantage for the great dis&not; advantages that all Combination Acts necessarily impose upon them ; for whilst on the one hand it prohibited combination, on the other it gave power to the Lord Mayor, Recorder, and Aldermen of London to regulate the wages of the journeymen weavers in case of disputes between them and the masters, if either party made application to them for that purpose. To understand the full advantages of this Act we must refer to the his&not; tory of the circumstances under which it was passed.,For some time previously there had existed in Spitalfields a regular book of prices, by which articles all the manufacturers were bound in honour to pay their men. This having been departed from by one or two masters, several others, in self-protection it was alleged, followed the ex&not; ample set them. The journeymen weavers then broke out into open riot. The chroniclers of the times tell us that the district of Spitalfields became a scene of the greatest tumult, and had all the appearance of a besieged city. The church and many of the large houses were converted into tem&not; porary barracks for the soldiers, who were unable to suppress the outrages of a starving and infuriate mob.,At this crisis Alderman Oliver procured the passing of the Spitalfields Act, which immediately, as though by some secret magic, restored order. The provisions of this statute were simply that after the 1st day of July, 1773, a uniform price was to be fixed for the wages of journeymen weavers in London and its district ; and the fixing of this was referred, in case of dispute, to the magistrates of London above mentioned. After the Act had come fairly into operation, we find that the magistrates did not in the first instance interfere in the regulation of wages, but left the masters and men to make their own bargains collectively, and these bar&not; gains they enforced under the penalties stated in the Act upon all indivi&not; dually.,A joint committee of masters and men were appointed when occasion required, consisting generally of from three to five on each side, who dis&not; cussed the subject of wages until they came to an agreement of what they thought would be a fair price for labour. The agreement was then taken before some magistrate, who ratified it ; and it then became by law a fixed price, from which neither could deviate, until it was altered by a subse&not; quent agreement. Either party was at liberty to propose such alteration, and we find the list was occasionally changed, particularly where there had been a change in the price of provisions since its last settlement. If at any of these meetings the masters and men could not come to an agree&not; ment, either might appeal to the magistrates in Sessions ; and on the hear&not; ing of this appeal, they might give any evidence they thought proper in support of the wages they were seeking to establish as fair wages for the future. We have an instance recorded as having occurred in 1803, Avhich shows the practical arguments in use at that time, when any dispute arose
as to the alteration or resettlement of the price list. Provisions had been at a very high rate during the war, and after the peace of Amiens had been made, it ivas thought they would immediately fall. The men waited for some time, but finding the high prices still keep up, they determined to ask for a proportionate increase in their wages. This the masters refused, and the men appealed by petition to the magistrates. The petition was opposed by the masters, and the chief grounds of opposition alleged were, that sufficient time had not been given to see whether the peace would bring with it the expected reduction in prices. The magistrates, after a full discussion of the question on both sides, agreed in the view taken by the masters and dismissed the appeal; and with this decision, though against them, the men appear to have been satisfied. In another instance recorded, the magistrates are reported to have decided in favour of the men. These are the only two instances mentioned in the evidence before the Lords&rsquo; committee in 1833, where the masters and men appealed to the magistrates to fix the rate of wages ; in all the other instances it seems to have been settled by an amicable arrangement, and the only applications made to the magistrates were to confirm the rates after their amounts had been previously agreed upon. Indeed, during the half century that elapsed from the passing of the Act to its repeal in 1824, though the weavers of London, in common with all other classes, were often called to experience seasons of distiess, no tumultuary proceeding ever took place amongst them.,It is said that at one time deputies were sent from the North to sound their dispositions, and to excite them to attend popular meetings for Ea- dical Eeform ; but their answer was, &ldquo; that they were well employed, and fairly paid for their labour, and were therefore satisfied.&rdquo;,It is to be regretted that we have no spirited legislator of the present day able to settle the relations of employers and employed on a similar basis. Such a system, if fairly introduced now, would, it is reasonable to suppose, be a mutual advantage to both masters and men. It would se&not; cure the masters from the bad feeling and injury that invariably result from stiikes, and would exempt them from fear of mere workmen&rsquo;s law, which combinations as they now exist often serve to maintain.,On the other hand, the men would be protected from many of their pre&not; sent struggles and anxieties, besides which both parties would have a better understanding with each other. Our French neighbours have gone so fai&not; as to establish courts to improve this understanding, called Conseils des Frudhommes. These have jurisdiction to settle all disputes in and relating to trade ; and if these could be adapted to our English manners, we can&not; not but think it would tend greatly to improve the condition of our work&not; ing classes.,In some future number of our Magazine we will insert at length an ab&not; stract ot the purposes and proceedings of these French courts ; but for the present, we content ourselves with calling attention to them as the only antidote for strikes, for under the present relations of masters and men whilst there is so little identity of interests, it is useless to condemn the spmt o&iacute; combination, whereby either party strives to ensure their respec&not; tive interests in total disregard of the other. 1
HISTORY OF INDUSTRIAL PROGRESS IN ENGLAND from 1800 to 1860.,By F, Harrison, Esq., M.A. ( Introductory Lecture, continued. r ).,I shall reduce the subjects for consideration to as few as possible, select&not; ing in each case the most general and comprehensive, putting what I have to say under the four following heads ;&mdash;,1. The Progress of Population, with the leading facts as to the health, occupations, and character of the new population.,2. The Progress of Production&mdash;agricultural and manufacturing, with the new population and habits created ; the increase of accumulated capital, and the new position and power it has organized.,3. The Improvements in Transport, including the principal facts relating to foreign trade and internal means of locomotion ; and some account of the principal inventions for this and kindred purposes.,4. The Progress of Consumption, including changes in wages, prices, habits of life, and the principal social consequences involved in this change.,1. With regard to the increase of our population at home (although this in itself is neither a cause nor a necessary effect of the social revolution whicli has been going on, but seems principally to impress upon us the great changes which have occurred), we shall see that the population of this country, which for centuries had but slowly advanced, and during the whole eighteenth century had not succeeded in doubling itself, suddenly during the first forty years of our century increased twofold. That this increase is far from applying to Ireland, where a slow but steady increase was abruptly arrested by the consequences of the great famine, which threw back the population to the position of twenty years preceding. That the actual rate of increase in England has been nearly evenly about 14 per&not; cent. in every ten years, or about 1^ per cent, in each year. Thus,&mdash;that the increase during any year in the nineteenth century is equivalent to at least that of five years during the greater part of the eighteenth. That this increase has however been accompanied by an increasing longevity ; so that the statistical tables show a steadily diminishing proportion of deaths, whilst in the varying proportion of marriages may be accurately read the his&not; tory of domestic prosperity or pressure. That of this great increase the most remarkable feature is the creation of new towns and groups of towns, and the increase in particular towns, some of which, such as Manchester, have doubled themselves four times within about forty years, Birmingham three times, Sheffield, Leeds, etc., in somewhat similar proportions. That in fact nearly the whole of this increase of population is one of the manufac&not; turing, not of the rural districts. That whereas towards the close of the last century the proportion of the agricultural to the rest of the population was nearly one-half ;&mdash;it has now long fallen beneath that of one-quarter ; and that notwithstanding the immense amount of new land taken into cul&not; tivation, and the increased impetus which has been at the same time given to agricultural activity.,From this it follows that the great change in the social body is found in the manufacturing rather than the agricultural population. I know it ap-
pears that a smaller number of hands now produce even on agriculture a far larger return than formerly. And although the increase of laud under cultivation is very great, the actual number of hands employed is actually less and is constantly diminishing. As indeed might be naturally expected, nearly the whole o&iacute; this immense increase has taken place in a few districts and counties, so that it has occurred always in the way in which it was least prepared or protected. Indeed the more we study the increase of po&not; pulation and its statistics, the more we see it to be convulsive, irregular, and full of alarming consequences in many ways ; the more it indicates the consequences which legislation or material changes make in the happiness and the growth of the people. As a parallel to this must be considered the spread of emigration, through which two millions and a half had, even by official accounts, left this shore within a period of twenty years,,2. Now when we come to the second branch of our inquiry, the progress of production&mdash;of producing power and capital accumulated&mdash;we shall find an entire change in the whole system of industry. For instance, within a few years England has changed from a country which feeds itself to the condition o&iacute; one which is fed in great measure from abroad ; and from an agricultural to a manufacturing population within little more than half a century. Now with regard to the importation of corn, during the early years of the century the actual average did not exceed 500,000 qrs., and up to the repeal of the corn laws it did not reach 1,000,000 qrs. Since that time, as you are aware, it has been six or seven times as much. Nor is the change less in the mode in which this food has been raised.,The number ot families who have been employed in agriculture has risen but little during the century, and of late years has distinctly dimi&not; nished. And the capacity of raising more food by the same number of hands has been increased by one-fourth within ten years. Indeed the ex&not; tent of the change in the agricultural system is nothing less than the change from that of a state of ordinary husbandry to a system of scientific manu&not; facture of food.,The mode also in which this has been effected has not been without its social and political consequences ; for it has been calculated that from about the 700,000 proprietors of the soil at the beginning of this century, the ever- decreasing number has now fallen below 20,000. Let us take note of the true import of this amazing result, when we remember that the proprietors of France are probably about 5,000,000. Agriculture, in fact, under the presence of our vast expansion of industry, has been forced into a per&not; fectly new activity. It has long called in to its aid the assistance of scien&not; tific chemists and the inventions of mechanics, but seems on the point of undei going a still further revolution by the introduction of steam. This fact alone is sufficient to account for the collection of land into the hands of capitalists and its cultivation becoming a matter of commercial specula&not; tion. Heie we see the parallel or the contrast to that system which in France ias substituted a democratic subdivision of the soil for the feudal tenure &middot; and \v e can no longer doubt of the true nature of the movement around us when we see before our eyes the last stronghold of the feudal ideas trans- iormed into a field for purely industrial activity.,As a proof of the immense stimulus thus given to produce, we find up to the peace of 1815 a rapidly increasing amount of waste land, which since,VOL, II.,E
the reign of George III. has been estimated in the aggregate as 7,350,000 acres, or at least one-tenth of all the land cultivated in the United King&not; dom. Thus, whilst the actual amount of land in cultivation has increased by one-tenth, the number of actual cultivators have distinctly diminished, whilst the number of proprietors of the soil has diminished to one-tenth. Set against these facts the enormous increase in value and in rents, calcu&not; lated at 150 per cent, upon the whole, the wholly changed position of both proprietors and cultivators, and we have social and political consequences almost endless, the bearing of which can neither be mistaken nor over&not; looked.,When we come to consider the progress of manufacturing industry, the first point which will occupy us will be the introduction and use of machinery. This, though not the cause, has been undoubtedly the means, and is equally a test of the great development of production. We shall find how, during all the latter half of the fifteenth century, mechanicians throughout the world were at work, each in connection with the labours of abstract thinkers, to produce the implements by which the work was to be carried on. How slowly and gradually the use of the new invention was brought to bear, until actual necessity, rather than accident or skill, seemed to bring them to perfection ! Then the steam-engine, which had existed for one hundred years, and even had been perfected by Watt long before the close of the eighteenth century, and the spinning-jenny which was per&not; fected not much later, did not produce any visible effects upon industry until the first years of the nineteenth century. It will perhaps be worth our while to spend some time upon the story of the lives and labours of one or two of the principal inventors. Indeed, nothing is more com&not; pletely a type and illustration of this era of industrial progress than the history of such men as Crompton, Arkwright, Watt, and Stephenson. There we see the mode in which the most abstract of speculations are brought to bear by practical thinkers upon useful inventions in the service of industry. We shall see how largely the whole sphere of thought, espe&not; cially in England, is turned towards the work of increasing the command of man over matter. How largely success in industrial improvement en&not; grosses public attention. How wealth, social rank, and even political favour, are not wholly beyond the grasp of the successful workman. Lastly, how even a high education can, for the resolute mind, penetrate down even into the darkness of the mine or the workshop. And how, amidst all the turmoil of public life, some few quiet workers can bring about an immense and incalculable revolution. It was in 1785 that the first steam-engine w r as used in cotton-mills, and about the same time that Arkwright and Crompton&rsquo;s inventions of spinning and the mule were brought into general use. But in 1800 the whole steam-power in use in Manchester had not exceeded 430-horse power; it was in 1801 that Cartwright&rsquo;s power-loom was brought into practical use. Since the date of the first introduction of steam-power, the expansion of the cotton manufacture has amounted to something like an increase of a hundied- fold. During the present century the amount of cotton imported for manufacture has doubled itself more than fifty times, dhe intioduction of powder-looms did not however extinguish the hand-looms for a long time ; they continued the unequal struggle without much diminishing in
numbers &middot;, but it is obvious what must be the effects of the sudden intro&not; duction of the new system of production, and I shall bring together some of the main facts relating to the consequences of the use of new machinery upon the population employed. This will lead us to some of the statistics relating to the population employed in our cotton manufac&not; ture, and in that of the old staple of our commerce&mdash;-wool ; and to some notice of the consequences which the introduction of new machinery has had upon the population formerly employed in these trades, and the deep- seated and wide-spread misery they have endured in prolonging the un&not; equal contest.,With the increase of mining industry and the production of iron and coal, the facts, although of startling character, do not present nearly the same value. They have simply created a new industry&rsquo; and called into existence a new population. They have not to the same extent sup&not; planted an industry already in existence, and overcome an already existing body of workers in the contest. Consequently for them the statistics of social change are wanting in some of those qualities which give such im&not; portance to the details of the cotton and wool manufactures. But not the less in them we have the story of a new class of population, suddenly called into existence without any thought of the means of adequate supply, protection, and guidance. These are then too abundant examples of all the consequences which follow upon such sudden, unlooked-for, and un&not; directed social revolutions&mdash;consequences which it is still the almost uni&not; versal piactice to assert; beyond, it is impossible to foresee and impolitic to interfere with.,3. Again, with regard to the third branch of the inquiry, that relating to transport of men and goods, including therein the construction of roads&rdquo; canals, and navigation, and railroads ; though it would be easy to spend our time over a series of astonishing and curious details, it will be more profit&not; able to state simply those leading facts which point out in a striking man&not; ner the vast expansion of industry and intercourse, the proof of constantly increasing wealth, mechanical skill, and material power; and still more par&not; ticularly those facts which testify to the immense work of skifi and labour and the imperfect results which have followed from an entire absence of control, or more often from an unwise control, over the development of this industiy, which, more than any other, has a national character.,4. It is, however, from the fourth branch of the whole &#39;inquiry that most may be learned,&mdash;the facts relating to consumption. This, in point of fact, wifi lead to the entire question, how far the industrial progress has succeeded in reaching into the bulk of society. I hope, by some&rdquo; not too special inquiries, to get a general idea of the actual change of life brought about by the work of the last fifty years, and in what respect the conditions of material existence have been altered, for upon that depend, in the first instance, all other prospects of improvement. It wifi be easy, from some comparison of the rate of prices and that of wages at different times to see how much in reality the comforts of daily fife have been extended to all &middot; how far, again, that increase in comfort has taken place in legitimate pro&not; portion to the increase in the comfort and luxuries of the wealthy If as I feel little doubt, it appears to us that the accession of prosperity&rsquo;has not been adequate even in the most favoured class, and that there has been a
positive decline in that of the least fortunate, it will be impossible to re-* fuse some notice of those efforts (call them strikes, agitations, or what else, that grow more and more systematic, to obtain a larger participation in the produce of labour, or to pass over the principles which are put forward in support of them, or on which these claims are really based.,Whilst upon this subject, it will be impossible not to give some slight sketch of the history of Pauperism,&mdash;that endless field for complaint and perplexity ; how, from a moderate sum in the last century, this tax at first gradually, then suddenly and convulsively, rose, almost doubling itself within a year, until it readied one-half of the whole interest upon the na&not; tional debt, and more than one-third of the whole national expenditure, until it had virtually revolutionized the whole system of wages, and intro&not; duced a new system of agricultural labour. If, indeed, we reflect that the number of paupers has exceeded 1,500,000, which is greater than that of the entire number of persons engaged in agriculture, we may have a notion of the extent to which the system had been carried. But alongside of this creation of a permanent pauper class, equal to about five per cent, of the entire population, the history of the variation of wages and prices show how at every step the lot of the skilled workman has increased, and the daily increasing facilities which are placed in his grasp. All the appliances and advantages of life, in some sense or other, are thrown open. It re&not; quires nothing but nerve and patience to increase these largely, and render them safe from fluctuation or diminution. It is from this starting-point that he has at his command the opportunities for increasing his moral and intellectual position, and thereby his political and social importance, to a degree that is of boundless extent. To such means must he look for his future, and not merely for his own future, but that of his fellows whose future is virtually entrusted to him, as to one who alone has the keen sense of common interest with his less fortunate brethren, and the strength to obtain for them any just demands.,NEWS.,Salford .&mdash;At the General Meeting on February 7th (Mr. Chadwick in the chair), the Report of the Council of the College for the past year was read. Having stated, by way of preface, the circumstances which had called this and kindred institutions into existence, it went on to say that the anticipations formed of their usefulness had been verified by experience. &ldquo; Earnest workers have readily answered the call for help made by this in&not; stitution. Men, who are fitted at once by complete sympathy with the canse and ability to give effect to their good feeling, have given time and labour wfith no niggard hand as teachers of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; have shown that they can appreciate the efforts made in their behalf. Entrusted to a large extent with the ma&not; nagement of their own College, the general and well-sustained interest in its w&#39;ell- being existing among them, is sufficient proof that they are not wanting to themselves and their own cause. It becomes clear that if they have not been widely reached hitherto, it is because the means of approach have not been understood. They have been approached on the side of the intellect only and treated to all the ologies, when in truth they had hearts as well as heads, the former needing not less than the latter their proper satisfac&not; tions and delights. This College has fortunately seen the need for the combination of the social and intellectual elements, and the result is that in this seventh term upon which the College is now entering it has not less than two hundred students ; and these
two hundred are not isolated units having only a distant class-room acquaintance,&mdash;they are animated by and made one by mutual good feeling, and in every class in the College friendliness and sympathy on the part of the teacher have been followed by the confidence and respect of the student. And the present outward circumstances of the College not less than its internal condition give the best guarantee of its permanence and continued growth.&rdquo; The report then narrated the manner in which the present building came into the possession of the College and its incorporation with the Salford Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, and continued as follows :&mdash;&ldquo;During the past year there has been some fluctuation in the number of students. In the first term the number was 178 ; in the second term, 165 ; in the third term, 126 ; and in the fourth term, 186. This statement brings out the fact that during the six months of more genial weather and longer days, extending fromMarch to September, the numbers were less than in those months when out-door occupations and amusements are less agreeable. It seems to point also to the need for some better provision for out-door instruction and recrea&not; tion to attach the students to the College during the summer months. The present con&not; dition of the College as to numbers is especially pleasing. As already observed, though not properly belonging to this year&rsquo;s report, the number for the term of 1860, viz. two hundred students, is larger than in any previous term. The classes for writing, bookkeeping, gram&not; mar, composition, French, algebra and geometry, practical arithmetic, mechanical drawing, chemistry, logic, Latin, elocution, physical geography and rudimentary instruction, have all been in active and efficient work during the past year. To make provision for the in&not; struction of the newly-entered students, and to prevent the progress of the more advanced being retarded, the classes have in most cases been divided into two sections. The ma&not; thematical and grammar classes have found among their own advanced students efficient teachers of the second sections formed in each of those classes. Further subdivision would, doubtless, be advantageous, since with two sections only to each, many must be classed together who are in very different stages of progress. But more complete classi&not; fication is at present difficult from the double want of teachers and of space. It is hoped, however, that another year will not elapse without a considerable addition being made to the staff of voluntary teachers, and to the class-rooms of the College. It must be said, also, that many subjects which are indispensable in a liberal course of instruction still find no place among the College studies. History, political geography, astronomy, phy&not; siology, and political economy, are all subjects of high importance, and their introduction would increase the attractions and the usefulness of the College. The Council recom&not; mends its successors to obtain teachers and form classes for the study of the subjects above named. The Council announces with pleasure that a class for drawing and paint&not; ing, under Mr. Charles Mercier, artist, is about to be formed, adding the elevating and refining influences of art to the severer studies of the College; and it is hoped that Mr. Mercier&rsquo;s class will soon be filled with earnest and diligent students ready to work their way through the alphabet of art to an intelligent appreciation of the works of its masters. The Council, in order to stimulate the students to increased exertion, as well as to obtain the decision of an independent body on their actual progress, has resolved to place the College in connection with the Society of Arts, and a local board of that society is in course ot formation, whose duty it will be to conduct the preliminary examination of can&not; didates for the final examination of the society.&rdquo; The beneficial effects of the social element introduced into the College are thus alluded to :&mdash;&ldquo; It has been found that to attach the students warmly to the College it is as necessary to call forth the warm and genial feelings of the heart as to educate the intellect. The more completely a home feeling pervades institutions such as this, the more certain will be their success : and with a social cup of tea, with song and recitation and cheerful talk,&mdash;not unmingled now and then with a burst of hearty laughter, nor yet quite free from curling wreaths of smoke &iacute;rom a certain well-known weed&mdash;the common hall of the College was opened with all due absence of form ; and in that hall, with its cheerful fire, and chess and drafts and pleasant company, refreshed, if they wish it, at a small cost with a cup of tea or coffee, the students ot the College, when not more seriously engaged, may spend a pleasant evening. These conclusions however have been arrived at: first, that it is fruitless to expect that persons competent to join the higher classes will be found external to ourselves : they must be drawn from the Elementary Section; they must have gained here such a power of application as will enable them to appreciate truly those higher studies. Attention must therefore be bent specially upon the Elementary Section. The second conclusion that experience has taught us, is this : it is impossible, in a state of society so changing as that of Man&not; chester to rely on voluntary teaching for the Elementary Section, and therefore the assistance of those teachers who are able to give voluntary teaching regularly, must be supplemented by paid teachers as far as the funds of the College will allow. The College will always be deeply indebted to those who have taught so long and so patiently ; but some permanent supply of teachers for the Elementary Section must be provided. During the first Term of this Session&mdash;ending with Christmas&mdash;the Elementary Section has had a regular attendance of from forty to fifty members, all above the age of fifteen, and the intermediate of about twenty-five. The present Term has commenced with numbers nearly the same, but with every prospect of increase. The physical studies of the College have been carried on with great vigour. Ou the whole, the position ot the College is encouraging. Many of those who have given their aid in the higher classes, Mr. M&lsquo;Cann, Mr. Fletcher, Rev. G. S. Allen, Mr. Story, Mr. Spier, Mr. N. Underwood still are firm friends as ever, aud the Council cannot but congratulate their President and themselves on the acquisition of Mr. Walker, certified by the Government as their Free-hand Drawing Master. But the new arrangement of the Elementary Section must entail additional expense. The College has never hitherto asked for a single subscription from those employers of labour in the neighbourhood whose workmen attend it; perhaps now therefore, without a sacrifice of that independent spirit which they desire always to maintain, and with a fair claim for sympathy, they may look for support and co-opera&not; tion from those gentlemen who are able to give them.,London&mdash; Mr. Roderick G. Mayor, M.A., Fellow of St. John&rsquo;s College.,CORRESPONDENCE.,A WORKING WOMAN ON WOMEN&rsquo;S CLASSES.,Sir, &mdash;Permit a working woman&mdash;one who has had to depend on her daily labour for daily bread&mdash;to thank you and the other gentlemen of the College for the interest you evince in the education of women. I believe much good has resulted from the after&not; noon classes ; still more would accrue from the establishment of evening classes for those who cannot spare time in the afternoon. Your correspondent, &ldquo;A Student&rsquo;s Wife,&rdquo; appears to me to mistake the position of working women altogether : it is not they who are the &ldquo; Priestesses of the Temple that is only the position of the favoured few. I cannot help envying the dear lady who &ldquo; has an armed guard to protect her from the rough hands of the outer world.&rdquo; Unfortunately the majority of working women have to en&not; counter for themselves the battle of life, not unfrequently to compete with men for the means of earning their daily bread. God help us if the contest unfits us for the ameni&not; ties of home ! &ldquo; Music and Drawing,&rdquo; why crowns and coronets seem scarcely more be&not; yond our reach than these ladylike accomplishments. All honour to the generous men who are willing to teach us even Algebra and Trigonometry, not that we may become thereby more fit Priestesses of the Temple, but in order that we may be better qualified to do our duty in that state of life in which it may please God to place us, whether it be His will that we fight the battle of life singly, or, like many a widowed mother, we have to perform the duty of father and mother towards our orphan children. I will not in&not; trude longer on your valuable time.
