APEIL 1, 1860.,EXAMINATIONS AND PRIZES.,The Council of the London College have lately been considering how they could extend their present scheme of Examinations, so as to make it tell with greater effect upon the class-teaching. They have at length adopted the plan embodied in the subjoined rules.*&mdash;It has been felt that our existing &ldquo; Certificate &rdquo; Examinations have in practice little or no bearing on the work of the greater number of the students. Certificates have hitherto been given to those whom the Examiners have declared to possess &ldquo; competent &rdquo; knowledge of any subject comprised in a course of lessons. The standard of attainment so defined is necessarily too high to be reached by more than a very few. This would not be so great an evil if the use of Examinations were merely to select the fitting recipients of an honorary distinction. But an Examination can be made, as long expe&not; rience has shown, a most valuable aid to study, in a manner quite apart from its tendency to call into play the competitive stimulus to exertion. To show this, it will be sufficient to assume, without dwelling on the ques&not; tion what is the main object of our teaching, that whatever is learnt should be learnt thoroughly and effectually. However small be the time de&not; voted to the work by teacher or student, thus much should be, and can be secured. And towards securing it Examinations contribute in several ways. Eirst, by making the student distinctly conscious of the extent and the degree of his attainment, by telling him how much he is really master of ; how much of his knowledge is complete and accurate ; how much is partial and inaccurate. Secondly, by practically inculcating on him the necessity of putting his ideas into a shape sufficiently distinct to admit of their being reproduced in answer to an Examiner&rsquo;s questions. Here it is that an Examination becomes of most use, not being so much a sequel to, as an actual part of, the teachiug. Let a student merely follow a train of ideas set before him by his teacher. He may see the line of argument, assent to its various steps, and acquiesce in the conclusion. Thus in a certain sense he may be said to know the result ; but put him down before a blank sheet of paper, and ask him to reproduce what he has so acquired, and to exhibit the grounds on which it rests, and the chances are great that his presumed knowledge will turn out to be a delusion. Ideas, which seemed clear enough as the words flowed from the teacher&rsquo;s lips, now flit before his view in shapeless confusion. He has a &ldquo; general notion &rdquo; of the principles stated, but can give only a misty account of them. This dis&not; covery of his ignorance forces him to go over his ground again : recollect&not; ing where it was that he was at fault, he can now fix his attention on the,* [la making a few remarks on the advantages of such a plan, it must be understood that 1 am merely expressing opinions of my own, for which the Council is in no wav responsible.&mdash; R. &szlig;. L.} ^ YOL. II. p
critical difficulties of the question ; and if he has anything of the real stu&not; dent in him, he will not rest till he can place distinctly before his mind every step of the process. Then, if told to reproduce it in an examination, he can satisfy himself and other people that he knows what before, as old Socrates would say, he only &ldquo;seemed to know.&rdquo; In some subjects, such as Language and Mathematics, the exact reproduction of the substance of the teaching can be carried out almost to the very letter. In others, such as History or Politics, the complexity and magnitude of the ideas with which the student has to deal make the clear expression of facts and reason&not; ings an infinitely more difficult task. But this very difficulty, we think, makes the task more wholesome and needful. Confusion of terms and indistinct&not; ness of conception, in dealing with the huge aggregations of facts which constitute the history of a people, are the sources of pernicious and obsti&not; nate fallacies : in the handling of triangles and numbers such confusion or indistinctness finds itself out, and so enforces its own remedy. Scarcely any subject of study then need be excepted from a scheme of Examination.,It will not be possible, nor would it be desirable, to make the attendance on these Examinations compulsory. All that is desired is, that the stu&not; dents should regard them as a regular part of the class-work, and as esta&not; blished more with the view of making that work sound and effective, than of calling out the boyish motive of personal emulation. Each student will indeed be trying his strength, but not directly against that of his fellow- learners. He will be measuring his attainments against a given standard of excellence. Those who reach the point hitherto defined as that of com&not; petency in the subject will receive the &ldquo; certificate &rdquo; as before : the special feature of this extended arrangement is, that it provides for the recognition of a degree of attainment which, though not equal to that so defined, is still, as far as it goes, satisfactory and meritorious.,Out of a class of say twelve students, it may reasonably be expected that though only one or two may prove themselves so completely to have mastered the substance of the course of lessons as to be described as &ldquo; competent &rdquo; therein, there may still be say four or five more who show that they have done as much real work as may fairly be expected of the average of earnest students. These it is intended to mark by the designation &ldquo; passed with,credit.&rdquo; r . .,The present scheme, it will be observed, includes no provision for the o-iving of Prizes. The Council, or at least a majority of them, think that the reasons advanced against the introduction of prizes more than out&not; weigh any pleas that have been urged in their favour : and till the great body of the students manifest a strong wish in the opposite direction, our Colleo-e will probably, in this particular, continue to differ from the prac&not; tice of the Manchester and other Colleges.* We must not forget that this question, like so many others, in connection with our College work, should really be argued without reference to precedent, so different are our circumstances from those of any other colleges or schools, old or new. The experience of old universities can be appealed to only as showing indirectly what might be the effect among us of establishing a similar system. But even if we consented to follow old precedents, it is by no means clear that they would furnish any ground for establishing the ordinary kind of prize-,* See vol. i. p. 190.
system. At the old universities what are called prizes are really honorary distinctions, their intrinsic value being to most of the students quite insig&not; nificant. On the other hand, the real prizes are the solid material advan&not; tages flowing from the so-called honorary distinctions. Prizes of this lat&not; ter kind, scholarships and exhibitions, let us have by all means as soon as they can be got. They would be a real help to students, and not merely signs of superiority to others. At present, certainly, there seems no likeli&not; hood of our colleges becoming so endowed ; and endowments, after all, have not proved such invariable blessings to the world as that we should sigh very earnestly for this boon. But by offering small premiums, such as books and the like, we should probably be calling into play some of the incidental evils of an endowment system without enjoying its solid advan&not; tages. A book-prize would be enough to excite a selfish sort of emulation, especially among students of small means, and would certainly not benefit the student more than by saving him the trouble of visiting a library. Within the narrow limits of class competition the effect of a prize would almost necessarily be to make the superiority of one man over another, and not the attainment of a given standard of excellence, the measure of success. Once introduce this feeling, and you weaken, perhaps destroy, that pleasant sense of sympathy in the prosecution of a common object, which is the surest and healthiest stimulant to exertion.,On this point, again, the experience of our old universities is apt to be misunderstood. There the greatest rivals are indeed often the closest friends ; but this is because the field of competition is so large that every candidate, generally speaking, feels that his powers are being measured, not against those of his friend, but against a standard of attainment practically fixed by reference to the steady average of large numbers. Let the com&not; petition be narrowed, as in special cases it sometimes is, to a degree which makes the personal rivalry plainly felt, and the vicious effects I have alluded to do really occur. The struggle for fellowships in a small college will sometimes keep alive jealousies for years. In framing the scheme now submitted to the students, the Council have tried to keep in view what they conceive to be the chief dangers incident to the attempt, and they hope that the plan adopted will secure most o&iacute; the advantages supposed to result from the establishment of prizes without occasioning the evils to which they almost inevitably give rise. Е. B. Litchfield.,SCHEME OE EXAMINATIONS ADOPTED IN THE LONDON WOEKINO MEN&rsquo;S COLLEGE.,^ The Piincipal and Council have agreed to the adoption of the following,1. Every teacher shall examine his class at the end of each Term, in the woik of the derm, either in or out of the regular class hours, and either orally or by writing.,. 2 &middot; At the end of every complete course an Examination shall be held in the subject of the course.,F 2
3. A part, at least, of every such Examination shall be oral, and the Council shall seek to procure the services of such Examiners as will under&not; take to attend personally ; the duty of orally examining to be discharged by the last teacher of the class if no other Examiner can be obtained.,4. At each Examination at the end of a course, certificates of com&not; petence shall be awarded to those who shall merit them ; and the names of those who, though not receiving such certificates, shall have passed the ex&not; amination with credit, shall be posted in the hall of the College,,5. Every student attending any course is expected to attend the Exa&not; mination at the close of that course or the next similar Examination ; and any student having passed any such Examination with credit may offer himself at the next following Examination for a certificate of competence.,6. Special Examinations for certificates of competence shall be discon&not; tinued, unless the Council shall at any time order such an Examination after particular consideration of the circumstances under which it shall be applied for.,ON THE STUDY OF CHEMISTRY.,IN TWO PARTS.*,II ,&mdash;The Study.,A chemical student once went to a distinguished professor on the Con&not; tinent, and told him he wished to learn Chemistry, but especially chemical analysis. The professor said that analysis was easy for those who knew the substances separately, but that first of all he must learn the properties of the bodies with which he had to deal by making numerous preparations. And taking, of course, the advice, the student obtained a long list of sub&not; stances, all of which were to be made in such quantities that they might be used by others in the laboratory, and in such a state of purity as would not be a disgrace to the establishment. It was something novel to deal with such large amounts. The salts of barium were to be made : these were made from the sulphide of barium, which again was made from sul&not; phate of baryta. A few pounds were used ; a large crucible of course was necessary, and large quantities of charcoal were required to keep up the heat for several hours. Instead of an amusement in a corner, Chemistry had become a business and a labour. By continuing such processes for some time, a great variety of experience was obtained. Every experiment or preparation required peculiar adaptations of apparatus according to the peculiar properties of the substances. The professor&rsquo;s experience, carried out by his student, was eifective. He had said that all his knowledge of ordinary bodies was learnt in a similar way. Once he had been an appren&not; tice with an apothecary, and had learnt much exactly in the same way as children learn the use of their hands and feet or the ordinary machinery of a household, so to speak. In after-life the salts of the metals were as fa&not; miliar to him as his daily bread, and he distinguished iron from lead as easily as he could distinguish turnips and potatoes. Long familiarity can,* Continued from page 44.
alone lead to this readiness of perception ; although long familiarity in persons of little observation will never produce it.,Here, then, is a mode of learning Chemistry ; but, after all, it is by no means complete. There are persons without number who can make nu&not; merous chemical preparations, and have, by long familiarity, the nicest ap&not; preciation of qualities in the bodies they work with, and in the methods adopted ; but they never become chemists. The reason of this lies simply in the fact that they are not made familiar with the theory of every action as it goes on. Lectures, then, are absolutely needful ; or, what is the same thing to some persons, careful reading in the theoretical part of the sub&not; ject. Many persons attend chemical lectures, but few who do so under&not; stand their full meaning. Probably there is no science wdiich is so gene&not; rally misunderstood as Chemistry. Chemical facts are expressed very vaguely by most persons ; almost always falsely. It is possible to have some very clear ideas of mechanics and of astronomy, and even of light and electricity, without a very long devotion to the study ; but chemical ideas elude the grasp of most persons, and are reproduced in a most awkward form, which one scarcely likes to contradict, but which one cannot agree with. Mere lectures, without illustration, would be valueless ; and even with ex&not; periments they are insufficient for the majority of mankind. The fingers must perform the experiment until the mind becomes familiarized with the idea. This can only be done by repetition.,We may say, then, &ldquo; Is each student to begin as the professor spoken of advised, and make preparations of every kind in large quantities, attending lectures once a day besides ? This may be excellent, but impossible forus.&rdquo; There is another method. Dr. Wollaston was not fond of showing his la&not; boratory ; but once, when he was asked to show it, he brought a tray with a lamp, a blow-pipe, and some glass tubes, saying, &ldquo; This is my laboratory and, indeed, it was true that with very little and very small apparatus he could make many valuable experiments. Berzelius, with a similarly small amount of apparatus, has extracted from less than a grain of a substance twelve different bodies. We see, then, that there are various methods of working with chemical substances. Por those who have a large laboratory at their command the abundant preparation of large and beautiful speci&not; mens is an education of the m&oacute;st valuable kind. This is especially valu&not; able if conducted in a public laboratory where many are doing the same ; each receives the experience of the other, and the tune necessarily spent in waiting on operations is often made profitable by seeing the preparations of our neighbours.,So numerous are the experiences to be gained in Chemistry, so abundant the facts, and so variously explained, that of all studies it is best conducted in company, where the eyes, the hands, and the minds of many contribute to lead us to true impressions. The best chemists generally come out of an active laboratory.,What, however, is the very first step to be taken to learn. There can be little doubt that the first step is attendance at lectures. There we obtain a general idea of the modes of thought adopted by chemists. It is remark&not; able how differently chemists think of external substances from the ordinary observer. The ordinary views are often somewhat chimerical; they are mixed up with fable and romance, and an old philosophy and worn-out supersti-
tion. These fragments of decayed antiquity are constantly in the mouths of men when speaking of ordinary phenomena. It would lead us too far from the subject to prove this. Chemical facts carefully observed by many men are brought to us in the forms in which they present themselves in Nature, as much freed from imagination as hitherto possible. It is mar&not; vellous how much we tinge the facts of Nature with our imagination. We see only a small part of that which we fancy we see, and are blind to much that is before our eyes. Careful attention to chemical lectures gives the first initiation into the mode of thought and observation adopted by the most successful investigators of Nature in that department. When the lecturer makes the experiment, he shows how the operations are performed, and a certain progress in manipulation is effected. It is not well for the lecturer, when speaking to students who intend to enter a laboratory, to have his experiments so prepared beforehand that he can at once perform them. It is much better to show the students as much as possible of the preparation, due regard being had to the saving of time.,Tuen what should the lecturer begin with ? It would be much to the advantage of chemical classes if the students spent a session studying physics. At present the chemical lecturer must teach a portion of physics; that time might be saved him if he obtained students who had first learnt portions of physical science. At the same time this remark applies less to our Universities than to such places as a Working Man&rsquo;s College, where the lectures given are few. No man can make any progress in Chemistry until he has some idea of heat, of light, of electricity, of galvanism, of hy&not; drostatics, and of pneumatics. At the same time, be not discouraged by such an array of names. It is true that the chemist is continually intrud&not; ing more and more into other sciences ; but there are certain portions carefully picked out of each as essential for the chemist, whilst the rest are left for his leisure. These portions arc generally found at the beginning of treatises on Chemistry.,In reading chemical works, the physical portion is the most attractive, and it is generally best known to all who do not study in the laboratory. We there find laws spoken of with more definiteness than in Chemistry ; and the phenomena are of a kind more included in ordinary observation. This feeling is so general, that it would be well simply to take advantage of the large amount of human experience on the subject, and read the phy&not; sical portions of chemical works until they are familiar, reserving the che&not; mical for the laboratory at first. The chemical facts are often stated with great dryness, and no book at first seems so uninteresting as one on Che&not; mistry. The facts are all connected with figures, and great and little re&not; sults are stated as of equal importance. But it is in the workshop or la&not; boratorium where Chemistry shines in interest. The most common facts generally excite astonishment ; and as they may be infinitely varied, every one has the power of seeing various sides of a natural phenomenon in rapid succession, instead of waiting for days or years until it should chance to occur again, as in astronomy and meteorology. We seem in the laboratory to have the power in our own hands ; we feel a certain dominion over Na&not; ture ; and we can question it at our pleasure. It is from this ready con&not; tact with Nature that chemists begin to use their knowledge earlier than the students of other sciences. The power passes more rapidly into their
own hands, and they ftnd the ramifications to be so great that frequently a young man finds at the outset that he has obtained a task which will give him agreeable and instructive or profitable occupation for the remainder of his life. From this it follows, also, that the seductions of Chemistry are very numerous, and the numbers that are led aside from it to the mere obtain&not; ing of gain is very great. But even they help to subjugate Nature.,After having learnt such physical laws as generally begin works onChemis- try, it is needful to learn a few chemical laws, such, for example, as go under the name of the Atomic Theory, or Theory of Combinations. The essential part of this is easy and simple, but it is remarkable how few see the whole matter clearly. Let it be mastered, and then begin to work. It would be a hopeless task to read on and learn chemical phenomena from a book&mdash; probably all the ideas obtained would be quite false. Begin to make the ordinary gases, and obtain correct ideas concerning them. Many lessons may be spent on them. You cannot enter too much into detail ; you cannot vary your experiments too much ; everything you do after having done your utmost will be too little. And let it be remembered that it is of the greatest importance that you should begin with calculations at this point, if not sooner. I do not say that you should learn just now to measure the gases experimentally, although it would be much better if it could be so arranged ; but you must know the calculations, and be able to make them easily. By mastering the subject the gain will be found marvellous. It is almost needless to say that a very ready knowledge of arithmetic is essen&not; tial to a chemist. Attempts have been made to introduce mathematics, or, I ought to say rather, algebra, but they have not yet succeeded, simply be&not; cause the calculations are perhaps, with few exceptions, better performed with ordinary numbers. Many of the chemical formulas, too, are put in an algebraic form by some chemists ; but these things must not frighten the student, as they are mere phantoms, which disappear at the touch, and resolve themselves into old clothes and rotten furniture. But there can be little doubt that in time Chemistry will disclose a phase in which mathe&not; matical knowledge will be essential, and that phase would seem to be begin&not; ning, although some chemists greatly exaggerate the present appearances.,Having come this length, Chemistry is begun : and how are we to pro&not; ceed ? Hitherto the knowledge has been partly experimental and partly obtained by reading and listening, but now must begin the actual learn&not; ing from Nature, the questioning for oneself. Ina large laboratory the plan of the professor spoken of might do ; for the students of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, it is well to remember the tray of Dr. Wollaston. This tray certainly will not apply to the physics of which we have spoken and the management of gases, and these &lsquo;may best be studied bv the experi&not; ments being made by several in concert under the superintendence of a professor, apparatus being rather expensive. When this is done, the student may obtain for himself about a dozen test-tubes, a lamp, gas or spirit-lamp, and a filler-stand with funnels and filters, with a blowpipe and a little platinum wire. To these must be added the re-agents as they are re&not; quired, which must be kept in solution in bottles. We now begin the study of inorganic substances soluble in acids or alkalies or in water. For persons who must trust a good deal to self-education, no plan seems to succeed so well as that of commencing with analysis. It does not seem at all in order : it seems to be unscientific ; but it will bear examination care-
fully. Chemistry is only in part a science ; very little of it is reduced to actual law. It is a branch of natural history, more properly speaking. We must leam in it an enormous amount of facts, many of them as yet quite isolated, like those of botany, mineralogy, and other studies of Nature. To learn them is fatiguing, but the nature of the mental operations in chemical analysis is such as peculiarly to fix the facts on the mind. If all the phe&not; nomena were isolated, we should never remember them ; but at once our books of analysis classify the characters of bodies. This would be too little for most minds, and so in analyzing them practically we classify them practically. A student passes sulphuretted hydrogen through an acid so&not; lution and obtains a black precipitate ; his mind is at once referred to lead, silver, mercury, etc. ; he learns by the eye a classification which ex&not; perience shows the memory to retain lightly if only read. In another slightly acid solution he obtains no precipitate. He at once excludes all the so-called noble metals, with arsenic, antimony, lead, bismuth, and others. Had he learnt these salts separately, he would have been obliged to gene&not; ralize for himself; by learning them during the search for other bodies, the classification becomes a necessary step, one that the mind performs easily and retains readily. In the same way in a few lessons we learn that some oxides of metals are soluble in alkalies, some not ; some soluble in potash but not in ammonia ; and thus we attach to each distinct properties and begin our actual knowledge of the alphabet of Nature, or the elements.,The value of this process partly arises from the fact that whilst we analyze we actually prepare numerous salts ; they are not crystallized, but we observe many of their characters nevertheless. We become familiar with the general relations of bodies to water, acids, and alkalies, and to each other ; and the elements, instead of being a confused idea, are arranged in the most per&not; fect order. The book from which this arrangement is to be best learnt is, as far as the writer is aware, Fresenius. No work attempts the minuteness, and no work really gives analyses fully, unless it be some one very lately published. Nevertheless analysis has been learnt, and learnt remarkably well, without Fresenius, and perhaps with active minds it is better to give less aid than that writer gives. The best part of Fresenius might be given in a shil&not; ling volume. Another reason why analysis is so valuable is that every experiment we make in it must have a meaning affixed to it. We are obliged to draw the conclusion at once. If we manufacture substances, we may allow many properties to pass unheeded ; not so in the course of analysis. It is well known that we learn those things best which fix them&not; selves by determining some of our own actions. The properties become as part of our own characters.,When this part of Chemistry is reached, it will be easy for the student to see what ought to come next ; but I would decidedly recommend a course of preparations to come here. Most persons go on to quantitative analysis, and then begin a scientific inquiry, being ignorant of many of the ele&not; ments, An entrance on the field of all the elements, with a preparation of many usual as well as unusual salts, would be useful as well as interesting. So seldom are some of the salts made, that we may question whether any living men have seen some of them, although described in books. Too ready is the young chemist to begin to draw conclusions on new bodies, and to enter the region of inquiry. Inquiry will be easier for him after much knowledge.
After this time it will be well to use a balance and to estimate quan&not; tities. This will be easy if the preparations alluded to have been made with care and quite pure.,After all, there is nothing very different in this from the course often pursued iu laboratories. It may however be a consolation to find that almost all preparations and analyses may be made with test-tubes, glasses called beakers, basins, and tubes, all of these costing little.,I would insist most on these two points, that for intellectual culture the study of chemical calculations is highly valuable, from the minuteness with which it compels you to study the phenomena ; next, that analysis trains the faculties to reason on chemical phenomena as observed by one&not; self more rapidly and independently than any other known physical study.,I would therefore recommend particularly that every experiment should be made on paper, as well as in the test-tube ; the formulas given, but the quantities especially minutely calculated. Every number relating to a body should be remembered. Its weight in combination, its specific gravity, its specific heat, its boiling and melting temperature, or its freezing-point. He who does not attend to quantity and its numerical expression very care&not; fully will never live to be a chemist.,There is, after all, little to be said as to the study, after a student is fairly in motion. The mind can then direct itself or it is valueless.,Books for reading in Chemistry are few ; but of all, the Familiar Letters of Liebig are the best. They may be read at an early or even at the first stage, in order to begin with a bird&rsquo;s-eye view of the ground to be conquered.,To give instances of the mode in which these studies may be pursued, would require another paper or more.,The space allotted me is now occupied. Let the student remember that the love of Nature smooths down many difficulties.,E. A. S.,DO WOEKING WOMEN WANT SINGING AND DEAWING ?,{To the Editor.),Sir,&mdash; My fellow &ldquo; working woman &rdquo; tells me I do not understand the position of &ldquo; working women.&rdquo; I hope I do. I ought to do so. I do not use the term as meaning only those who work to earn money. I consider every woman who cares for her home and children as a working woman. But even using the term in its more limited sense of meaning only workers for money, I think I know something of them. I have lived for some years in a village where the greater number of women have, occasionally, to &ldquo; fight for themselves the daily battle of life,&rdquo; and even &ldquo; to compete with men for the means of earning their daily bread.&rdquo;,I do not find, however, that they consider themselves less priestesses of the temple of home. I find that it is because they care so much for their homes that they work so hard for them. I find them careful for the education of their children, tender nurses to them in sickness, careful of neatness and comfort in their homes, as far as it is possible to be, while waging a daily battle with starvation or the Union.
My fellow worker will, I am sure, admit that these women are worthy the name of working women, both in her sense of the word and in mine ; and so far from not understanding them, I claim for myself the ennobling name of a working woman, both as working in my family, and without it, so that I am not without hope that any &ldquo; working woman &rdquo; would accept me as being a fellow worker.,My friend thinks that only a &ldquo; favoured few &rdquo; have any Temple of Home of which they are the priestesses. I hope and believe, though the solitary battlers of life and the widowed mothers are numbered by thousands, still that they are very few compared to those who have a stronger helpmate to guard and cherish them.,I cannot help thinking however, that even for those who have, doubly, the title of working women, singing and drawing will be far more beneficial than the ordinary subjects of the college teaching.,Beyond reading and writing, I believe singing to be more useful than any other study. The more stern the daily work, the more needful for the recreation to be agreeable and easy.,I see in our own little village no woman learning mathematics, history, or natural history, but I see them meeting, voluntarily, twice a week, to form a choir for the village church.,I have as much occasion to thank the &ldquo; generous men &rdquo; who teach at the College as most of the students of the poorer classes, but I believe that the main if not only object of their teaching either men or women is to make them better priestesses and better guardians of the Temple of Home, believing that thereby they best fulfil their duty &ldquo; in that state of life unto which it may please God to call them.&rdquo;,A Student&rsquo;s Wife.,NEWS.,London .&mdash;Some members of the College gave a treat to two hundred of the poor of the parish, on the 15th inst., at the schoolroom of St. George the Martyr. As this is the first occasion on which the College has done anything of the kind for its poorer neighbours, the success of the attempt deserves some record. The entertainment was paid for out of the surplus proceeds of some private subscription dances, the promoters of which thought they could not hetter employ this little fund than in providing some amusement for those to whom an evening of pleasant recreation is but too great a rarity. The pro&not; gramme, as summed up on the invitation-tickets, ran thus :&mdash;&ldquo; Tea at 6 ; Music at 7 and 8.30; &lsquo;Box and Cox,&rsquo; a farce, at 8; Sandwich, Beer, and a Pipe, at 9.15. Bring your own mug and knife.&rdquo; Under the direction of the Rev. J. Back, the Rector, two hundred of these tickets had been distributed, and their bearers were waited on at tea by some sixty or seventy members of the College and visitor-friends. This part of the entertainment over, then came the vocal concert. The choir was small, but the most important section of it&mdash;the ladies&rsquo; voices&mdash;were extremely good ; and though there had been no previous rehearsal, the music went with spirit and &eacute;clat. The important duties of accompanist had been undertaken by au able pianoforte-player, Miss Boddy, who also charmed the audience in the course of the evening by a dashing performance of one of Thalberg&rsquo;s fantasias on a popular melody. The people seemed to like the music, applauding most vigorously the English glees and madrigals &ldquo; Who will o&rsquo;er the Downs,&rsquo; the &ldquo; Chough and Crow,&rdquo; etc., but listened also with evident pleasure to well-known bits of Weber, Mendelssohn, and Rossini, of the sort which delight equally the most un&not; tutored and the most scientific ears. At the appointed hour the platform was impro-
vised into a stage, and we had half an hour&rsquo;s laugh at the crosses aud conflicts of Box and Cox (two of the College teachers, Messrs. Hales and Russell), and the despair of Mrs. Bouncer (better known as W. H. Ricketts, student and corporal). Then came more music, glees and solos, and the ť Tight Little Island&rsquo; (Mr, Hurst), with chorus tutti fortissimo. This patriotic tumult presently subsided into depth of utter stillness, save for an occasional baby&rsquo;s squeal, as the clear tones of a lady singer were heard in the old ballad &lsquo;Annie Laurie&rsquo; (encored unanimously), by which time the sandwiches and beer had made their appearance, in quantity without stint, giving all a plentiful supper. Then the concert wound up with the usual loyal demonstration, and vote of thanks by acclamation to the givers of the entertainment. The result of the whole was that every&not; body was very happy, and the people for whom the f&ecirc;te had been arranged showed most abundantly that they appreciated the good intentions of the entertainers.,The usual General Meeting took place on Thursday, March 8th. The Principal, in his opening address, alluded to the establishment of the Rifle Corps as an event materially affecting the present condition of the College, observing that the Council had made up their minds to the possibility of the drill somewhat interfering for a time with the ordi&not; nary work of the classes, in the full belief that the existence of the corps was of solid advantage to the College ; and that so long as the corps w r as kept within the College, and formed a part of it, it would be a source of strength, and not of weakness. After an&not; nouncing the names of new teachers, and entering into some explanations as to the new examination scheme, Mr. Maurice awarded Certificates of Competency to Mr. Ord (Algebra), Mr. Townsend (Arithmetic), and Mr. Dalziel (Logic). Brock.,Mr. Buskin gave an account of the condition of the Drawing Class, touching on some of the points connected with its management, which distinguished it from the rest of the College classes. Referring to the examinations which are to be held on other subjects, he said that nothing of the kind could be attempted in his class ; that auy sort of com&not; petition in art-work was invariably pernicious, leading men to strive for effect instead of truth. It was impossible, moreover, for a teacher to be sure that in his own instruc&not; tions he did not give an advantage to one student over another; he found it, he said, impossible to conquer the temptation to bestow most help on those whom be saw making the most use of what he taught them. This kind of premium was the only thing in the shape of a prize which could find its way into the Drawing Class. Mr. Ruskin then said that as he could only come seldom to the College meetings, he should take the opportunity of telling the students a few of the things which had struck him most during his summer tour on the Continent. He then gave a very lively account of some of the places in which he had been looking at the great masterpieces of German art. &ldquo;The pictures which I saw in Belgium,&rdquo; he said, &ldquo;made me feel the immense difference that exists between the Venetian art and that of the so-called Religious schools. Once I thought, as most of the world does, that the greatest religious art is that which presents the religious element free from all connection with earthly things. A painter of the southern Italian schools, when he wanted to make a religious picture, painted only saints and angels, and these generally he put in the skies, far away from anything earthly. This is one kind of religious art ; another, and, as I now believe, a greater kind of reli&not; gion, is that which is mixed up with the everyday life of men, and this was the religion of the great Venetians. Veronese and Titian, when they were paiuting a sacred picture, did not confine themselves, like Angelico, to the representation of saints in glory, or a martyr in his ecstasy, but treated the scenes of this human life in a true human manner, pouring into them their religious faith. As an instance of this, take Veronese&rsquo;s great picture of his own family. He represents the Madonna coming to pay a visit to the household, and he treats the thing as if it was a piece of simple fact. Every figure in the picture, his wife, himself, his children, great and small, down to the schoolboy hiding behind the pillar, and the dog snarling suspiciously at the visitor, is represented as doing exactly what might be expected under the circumstances. In these various figures&rdquo; (Mr. Ruskin described them all) &ldquo;every phase of religious feeling, as differing in different persons and ages, is simply and truthfully brought out. Contrast with this the manner in which Rembrandt and Rubens drew a family picture. Rembrandt has painted himself and his ■wife, and made it in many senses a wonderful picture ; but what scenes does he choose as an illustration of the beauties of his family life ? He is sitting at supper with his wife, holding her on his knee, with a glass of champagne in his hand, and a roast peacock,
with a magnificent tail, on the dish before them! As to Rubens, when he paints his wife and child, instead of showing her in the presence of the Madonna, he makes her act the Madonna, with himself as St. Joseph, and his child as the Christ, and the picture becomes one broad field of blasphemy. It is by looking at works such as these that one learns to understand the difference between the religion of the Venetian painters and the religion of the Germans.&rdquo; Passing from Belgium to Prussia, Mr. Ruskin said that what struck him in Berlin was decidedly not the high art which his German friends showed him, but the singular success with which the people seemed to have pursued the art of making themselves comfortable. The villa life of the citizens, with their pleasant gardens and open air tea-drinking, was an advance by many degrees on the so-called comfortableness of English people. Going to Dresden, he saw the trea&not; sures of the &ldquo; Green Vault&rdquo; at K&ouml;nigstein, the curious impregnable fortress of Saxony. The principal use of this place was to serve as a convenient retreat, where a mis&not; governing monarch might retire whenever his subjects became unruly, and shut himself up like a rat in his hole, till they had tired of expressing their discontent. But the great sight of the place was the Green Vault, the repository of the royal jewels, an amazing mass of treasure, remarkable for showing how stupid and contemptible the richest art may become, when even the noblest materials are worked, without a sense of what is either beautiful or reasonable. Here were collected the produce of the Saxon mines, gems of every hue, without number, and beyond price. But with all these splen&not; dours the craftsmen seemed to have known how to make but two things only&mdash;sword- hilts and drinking-cups ! Save only that for the royal diversion they were sometimes fashioned into wonderful forms of puppets, lilliputian jewelled dolls, with beads of emerald and stomachs of pearl. But it was in the article of drinking-cups that the artistic and royal souls gave free vent to their imagination. There were cups of every conceivable shape, and with every imaginable device for making the drinker spill his wine. Cups like birds and beasts, cups like fishes and shells, all equally gorgeous and equally ugly. It seemed in fact as if when an old Saxon monarch looked out into creation, and saw any one of the beautiful things on this earth, his first and only thought was, &ldquo; Can&rsquo;t I make a mug of it?&rdquo; Out of this world of wonder, then, there is not much to be learnt, except that to be a king is sometimes very sad, dreary work, and that it is a good thing to have something given us to do in the world better than playing with pearl- stomached mannikins, like the lords of K&ouml;nigstein.,After Mr. Ruskin&rsquo;s address, Mr. Hughes, Captain of the Rifle Corps, addressed a few words to the meeting, chiefly, as he said, to explain why he and the other officers had not been to Court on the day of the Volunteer Lev&eacute;e. &ldquo; My reason for not wishing to go to Court,&rdquo; said Mr. Hughes, &ldquo; and the majority of the corps thought with me, was that I think the business of the Rifle movement a very serious and solemn affair, and that we ought to keep as far aloof as we can from the frivolity and dandyism which is being imported into it. &lsquo; Let not him that putteth on his armour boast himself as one that taketh it off.&rsquo; At present we have done nothing for the Uneen. We have not fired a blank cartridge or even snapped a cap; we have just learnt a few steps, and ordered our uniforms. Are these such great achievements that we must needs glorify ourselves thereon by dancing and dining and kissing of hands ? This would be to forget the real grounds on which we have set about the work. My reason for being a Rifleman is, that I believe that no man, and no nation, can do the thing that he ought if he has to go in fear of any mortal creature. England at this moment is in fear, because she is weak. Till she is strong, she dare not say or do what she would choose. Meanwhile great things are happening to which we ought to have something to say. There is going on before our eyes a most noble and wonderful revolution : a whole nation is passing visibly from death to life. Years ago, when I was at Rugby, I recollect how our boyish minds were touched by Dr. Arnold&rsquo;s telling us what he had seen of the then sad state of Italy ;,_Low that her whole condition was a lie ; that if you saw an official, you knew he was,robbing, not increasing the revenue ;4if you saw a gendarme, you knew his business was not to catch thieves, but to thieve ; and if you saw a soldier, you knew he would not fight. Such it was then; and now this same people is awakening to life, declaring that it will be a nation, and proving every day that it means to become one. And to all this what does England say ? Read the temper of the nation as reflected in the columns of the &lsquo; Times.&rsquo; This very day it publishes an article simply pooh-poohing the whole affair. That is because England is too weak to declare her mind. Well, then, I re-
solved that so far as the efforts of a single individual Englishman could reach, I would do what in me lay that she should be vulnerable no longer. In treating of the first point she mentions, viz. whether (to use her own words) &ldquo;the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; would understand the respect due to their own wives and sisters within the walls of their College,&rdquo; she seems to forget the nature and composition of the mass of men, who, during any given term constitute &ldquo; the College.&rdquo; For she asks, &ldquo; if they would not, where is the knowledge of duty of which so much has been said as being the great lesson taught by the College ? The doubt seems to question the very existence of the College,&rdquo; etc. I do not quite&deg;see it m this light, and perhaps &ldquo; A Student&rsquo;s Wife &rdquo; will not, when I tell her that in any term, the new members of the College form one-fourth of the whole number of students. Fhe great lesson ot the College cannot have had much effect upon, these men whatever it may have had upon the older members. And, let me tell &ldquo; A Student&rsquo;s Wife &rdquo; that so far as I understand it, the College professes not to be a worker of miracles or an instantaneous caster out of devils ; but it is satisfied if it can help a man, with&rsquo;much manful striving on his own part, to rid himself of his sins and weaknesses : it pro&not; fesses not to be a second Cross, on reaching which, as with Bunyan&rsquo;s &ldquo; Pilgrim,&rdquo; a burden of sin shall in an instant fall from the bearer&rsquo;s shoulder ; but it is a place where a man may, if he will, be helped gradually to loosen the bonds which have held that burden there ; or, to speak less metaphorically, it is a place where he may, with much active sympathy, be taught that there are objects worth living for, other than the gratification
of his weaknesses or passions. Whether men will respect their own wives and sisters is not the question. Will they respect the wives, sisters, and daughters of other men ? is the widely differing question we have to answer. I believe they will, as apparently does &ldquo; A Student&rsquo;s Wife,&rdquo; though our beliefs rest upon different grounds.,Now comes the question &ldquo; What classes would working women join ?&rdquo; and your cor&not; respondent answers it by saying she thinks that only the Drawing and Music classes would be attended with much profit. Shall I be excused if I think differently&mdash;and on these grounds ? In the first place, Drawing has never formed part of the teaching in the present &ldquo; Women&rsquo;s Classes,&rdquo; of which your correspondent professes to have been a member. May I ask, has &ldquo; A Student&rsquo;s Wife &rdquo; and the other members of these classes only found profit in the Music Class ? For answer we find that on the average from sixty to seventy persons have attended these classes, many of them attending more than one class during the week, and the report just published says, &ldquo; With the exception of the Grammar, all the higher classes have been less frequented than the more elementary.&rdquo; So much for the exclusive study of music and drawing. &ldquo; A Student&rsquo;s Wife &rdquo; then mentions several subjects taught in the College classes, aud asks if working women would care to learn them. Let it be granted, that possibly they might not ; here is a list of subjects omitted by your correspondent (though they are to be found in the prospectus), which I believe women would study, and with &ldquo; profit &mdash;English Grammar, English History, Arithmetic, Book-keeping, and French, in addition to &ldquo;Drawing;&rdquo; and I do not hesitate to say that the Preparatory Classes would be well attended by women, were they permitted to enter them.,Not to trespass further upon your space, I will only say that I have reason to think this proposition is looked on with more favour than I had hoped for it, and I have no doubt when the proper time comes, women will study side by side with men in Great Ormond Street.
