MAY 1, I860.,SHALL WE LEAEN LATIN?,BY GODFREY LUSHINGTON, M.A. FELLOW OF ALL SOULS COLLEGE,,OXFORD.,Whether a classical system of education be right or wrong, it is certain that many of the reasons which once did justify, or are still used to justify it, are now untenable. There was a time when Latin and Greek were sup&not; posed to be the only honourable languages, classical the ordy literature ; when Latin was the common European language, when positive science, thanks to theological oppression and ignorance of the Baconian method, was comparatively unknown. It was natural therefore that language, whether grammar, logic, rhetoric, or the like, should then be held the only know&not; ledge worth acquiring, and that classics should be adopted as the universal medium of instruction. But nowadays we have philosophers, historians, poets, from every European nation, writing in their native tongue ; and be&not; tween Bacon and Faraday lie a long series of physical discoverers, inventors of whole sciences, which have indefinitely enlarged the borders of know&not; ledge, and have changed the face of modern life. Classics therefore have no longer a pretence, whatever they once had, to constitute universal know&not; ledge. Sometimes we hear that c classics make the gentleman :&rsquo; a curious idea (contemporary, by the bye, with the corresponding idea that it was dan&not; gerous for a working man to learn anything), and springing partly from the fact that at places of education where classics are taught, the scholars come exclusively from the upper classes, partly that gentlemen usually learn classics and nothing else. However, that the modicum of Latin and Greek which is the possession of an ordinary student who has passed through Eton and Oxford, constitutes his title to all that we value in the name of c gentleman,&rsquo; I for one should be sorry to think. Again, we are told that one can get * style&rsquo; only from a study of the classic models. Why not, I should like to know, from a study of the non-classic models, such as Shak- speare, Dante, Goethe ? But indeed &pound; style,&rsquo; so far as it is a thing of high positive value, is no borrowed form, but must be always original&mdash;let the student&rsquo;s thought be right and clear, the expression of it, or the c style,&rsquo; may well be left to shape itself, or at any rate put aside as a matter for after-consideration. Or it is solemnly argued that the classical system must be a good one, because so many great men have been good scholars ; ol&not; that it must be a high education, because it professes to be so, and, com&not; mercially speaking, does not pay. These are some of the reasons often given, better ones than these exist and will be considered presently,&mdash;but the truth is that there is recognized in England no theory of education constructed either upon a classification of knowlege or with reference to the intellectual wants of those who are to be educated : the classical system,VOL. II. p *
(with slight but daily increasing modification) is universally accepted at our schools and colleges, just because it exists as an institution. Naturally enough ; for not only is the classical prestige powerful, but any change in this respect implies a revolution. Strong indeed would be the faith or great the folly which would sacrifice the existing complete apparatus of grammars, manuals, text-books, and teachers, and the harmonious co-opera&not; tion of schools and universities, to enter upon a system yet untried, and for which the whole organization has to be created. Witli our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; however it is otherwise. We need not shrink from the question, Shall our Students learn Latin ? We have no reason to follow the example of other Colleges, unless that example be a profitable one. I propose therefore to discuss the question on its own merits, and with this view to consider separately : 1. The general benefits of a study of language. 2. The special benefits of a proper study of Latin. 3. How far these benefits are peculiarly suited or accessible to the working man.,I. Language .&mdash;What a number of interesting questions does the very word raise ! Consider speech as the power to express thoughts by sounds, or as the art of governing the motions of the tongue. Or consider speech only as the art of naming, how wonderful it is ! Adam, we are told, gave names to the animals ; that is, to individual living objects which he with his own eyes saw there before him ; but Adam&rsquo;s sons have learnt to give names to classes of things, to series of things, to things abstract and unseen : to give,&ldquo; to airy nothings A local habitation and a nameľ,Once more, consider speech as grammar. What a step is there from a list of unconnected names to the formation of simplest sentence ! And an or&not; dinary sentence, with its full complement of noun, pronoun, verb, adverb, adjective, etc., all harmoniously moving together, what a work of art it is ! We forget this, for it requires an effort to remember it, just because we cannot help speaking in sentences. Even so (to compare great things with small) the cloth coat on our back we forget was once a fleece of wool on a sheep&rsquo;s back, and that it is human invention which has made it what it is. Nor is the interest in grammar merely an artistic interest due to it as a beautiful machinery of expression. Grammar is a science, the first, the most primitive of sciences. All the divisions of grammar, such as singular and plural, active and passive, adjective and noun, verb and adverb, etc., are found upon investigation not to be arbitrary at all, but to be necessary ; that is, they could not be otherwise than they are : they have a counterpart in nature ; the laws of grammar were not invented by grammarians, but are the elementary laws of human thought, which existed, though undeclared, coevally with the creation of man. It is only lately however that the sci&not; ence of grammar has been acknowledged, and there is yet ample room for further discovery in it.,This brings me to mention the other new science connected with Lan&not; guage, the science of ph&uuml;ology. Philology is doing for language what geology is doing for the earth. Geologists, by examining existing strata, seek to reproduce the ancient world as it was, to exhibit the various phases through which it has passed, and to call up again the forms and life of de&not; funct animal creation. So philologists, by the comparison of different lan-
guages, investigate the birth and growth of language and thought ; work out the pedigree of the family of tongues, tracing it through various gene&not; rations up to one common head ; reconstruct the primitive language, not a word of which remains ; and penetrating into pre-historic times, show the existence and even the habits and thought of a nation whose very name has perished.,Descending to a directly practical point of view, the study of language may be made useful as a mere intellectual training. Practice in grammar obviously disciplines the mind ; so too must translation. Translation con&not; sists of two processes. Pirst, the understanding of the original. Secondly, the production of a counterpart in another language. To perform the first you must not only understand the particular words, the particular phrases, the grammatical construction of each sentence, and the sequence of the successive sentences (or argument), but what requires a far higher effort, you must comprehend the style of the writer and the spirit and feel&not; ing of the whole. To accomplish the second you have to find and choose words, to turn phrases, to construct sentences, to make the whole harmo&not; nious with itself and faithful to the original. Now to go through these processes with any degree of precision or artistic excellence is always diffi&not; cult, just as it is for a painter to make a really accurate painting of the simplest object. It would be difficult, if the two languages were alike, or were of two contemporary nations, or even (were that possible), belonged to one and the same nation speaking both alike indifferently ; it becomes much more difficult where the languages belong to different races, different periods of the world&rsquo;s civilization. Prom the practice of translation then we may hope to gain this much : we shall learn our own language better after weighing and measuring it with another, just as we form a more correct estimate of ourselves if we live in society with our fellows. We shall gain control over our minds by applying them to subjects chosen not by us, but for us and by ascertaining exactly the thoughts of others upon these, and by expressing our own, we shall acquire a power of ready expression, and a habit of accuracy that is of very great practical value.,II. The special benefits of a proper study of Latin .&mdash;This too raises various questions. The Latin language, is it a fine language ? is its gram&not; matical mechanism brought to perfection ? How is it related to other lan&not; guages P How to our own P Latin literature,&mdash;who were the Roman poets, philosophers, historians ? what was their special excellence ? how is their literature related to the literatures of other countries, of our own country ? Latin history,&mdash;is it the history of a great people ? of a people that has left its mark upon the fortunes of the world ? of a people related to our own nation ? Let us briefly answer these questions. Latin is a thoroughly mature language ; the cases of substantives and persons and moods of the verb are represented not by prepositions or auxiliary verbs with pronouns, but by &ldquo; inflexions.&rdquo; Contrast&mdash;,Pat-er, the Father. Patr-is, of the Father. Patr-i, to the Father.,Am-or, I am loved. Am-aris, thou art loved. Am-atur, he is loved.,These inflexions are a proof of a language&rsquo;s maturity, being simply a con&not; solidation produced in the lapse of time. A language with inflexions is,G 2
like a block of Purbeck marble that once consisted of loose shells. The superiority of inflexions are evident : they dispense with the necessity of the numerous small words, as &lsquo;I,&rsquo; &lsquo;thou,&rsquo; &lsquo;of,&rsquo; &lsquo;to,&rsquo; &lsquo;shall,&rsquo; &lsquo;have,&rsquo; etc., which encumber a modern sentence. This is one reason why Latin is terse, and answers so well for epitaphs or inscription on coins. Again, Latin holds a most important position in the family of languages. It is the key to modern European languages : six of them, including Italian, Spanish, Portuguese, and French, are but so many dialects of Latin. English is, if not wholly Latin, half Latin, for which we have to thank our Roman and our Norman conquerors. Latin pure was once the recognized language of Europe, all others being held mere provincialisms. It was the language of Court and Church, of diplomatists, historians, lawyers, physicians. In Latin were written the works of the Fathers and the early Christian liturgy; in Latin the Magna Charta ; in Latin the State letters written by John Milton for Oliver Cromwell; in Latin the &lsquo;Novum Organon&rsquo; of Bacon. Even to this day Latin is employed by physicians in their prescriptions, and is the only language of European coins and monuments.,Latin literature has received exaggerated praise. Yirgil was once glori&not; fied as the second greatest poet in the world, and all aspiring orators were required under penalties to shape their periods after Cicero. But after the most generous judgment of Horace, Virgil, Lucretius, Cicero, Catullus, Livy, Terence, and, the greatest of all, Tacitus, it must be confessed that Latin literature is mostly imitated from the Greeks, and as a whole decidedly in&not; ferior to Greek, English, German, and Italian. This was a natural conse&not; quence of the national character. For the things out of which a literature grows the Roman cared little or nothing. He lived in the town, not in the country, and was indifferent to the beauties of landscape. The pro&not; ducts of his art were not indeed useless monstrosities like the Egyptian pyramids, but neither were they mere d&eacute;coratives, as Greek statues : they were vast public works like the Flaminian way, the Cloaca Maxima, grand&not; est of sewers, or the indestructible causeways which still seam the surface of our island. His philosophy, represented by the two schools, the Stoic and the Epicurean, was not speculative, but practical. His amusement was not, as with the Greeks, dramas or works of fiction, but triumphal proces&not; sions over actual enemies ; or if games, the gladiatorial games in all their awful reality. Romance was contrary to his sense of self-control, and his faith was not in God, but in the destiny of his country. In a word, the Roman genius lay not in literature, but in practical life, in government, and so what remains as their greatest literary monument is not poetry, or oratory, or history, but their law&mdash;a fact, strange to say, almost wholly ignored in our classical education. Still Latin literature has in it the na- tional virtue, the spirit of order. Here as elsewhere where the Roman was, law reigns supreme ; the thoughts are just, the style careful and dig&not; nified, and the sentences move along with martial precision. As one reads one is perhaps not forced to think, nor kindled into enthusiasm, but one learns a lesson of discipline. To all who are &lsquo; spasmodically &rsquo; inclined, a course of Latin reading might be prescribed with the happiest effect. At any rate, Latin has the advantage which always belongs to a dead lan&not; guage. It is new to us, just because it is old. Ancient writers seize on the broadest and simplest characteristics, and talk to us freshly about our
common universal human feelings : whereas modern writers, to escape cant or dulness, have too often to seek out and dwell upon trifling pecu&not; liarities and personal individualities.,Roman history is a magnificent study. We see her, from first to last, as a single city upon a single hill : then upon her seven hills : then con&not; quering neighbour after neighbour till all Italy is won : then out beyond, struggling to death with Carthage, subduing cultivated Greeks and rude barbarians, spreading north, south, east, west, mistress of Europe, Asia, Africa&mdash;of the world&mdash;until dissolved by her own luxury and shattered by barbarians her empire falls asunder, her language and her law remain. We see Rome pass through all forms of government,&mdash;first a monarchy, then an aristocracy, then a republic, finally a despotism : we see her pass from a most exclusive nationality to a supremely catholic empire ; from the extreme of austerity to the extreme of voluptuousness ; from paganism to Christianity. The history of Rome is the history of the civilization of Europe.,All this seems to imply that we should say &ldquo; yes &rdquo; to the query with which I started ; but how far this would be the right answer from a work&not; ing man in search of an education is a point to be further considered.,{To be concluded in our next.),A LETTER TO THE TEACHER OF A BOXING CLASS ON PRIZE FIGHTING.,My dear Hughes,,I was asked the other day what I thought of the fight about which everybody was talking. I gave a stammering answer. For the questioner knew or suspected that feats of bodily strength would have little interest for me, let their moral effects be what they might. He would therefore have attributed my judgment to my taste ; he would probably have thought that I was &ldquo; saving sins I was inclined to, by damning those I had no mind to.&rdquo;,But I do feel strongly on this subject : to you I am not afraid of saying how strongly. I have been told that you must necessarily be an admirer of Heenan, or Sayers, or both ; that for you to denounce prize-fighting would be a great inconsistency. For did you not, it is asked, superintend a Boxing Class in our College ? Did you not describe a fight at Rugby, and make your hero a principal in it ? That reviewers should draw these conclusions from these premises would not cause me much concern. That working men and schoolboys should imagine a connection between them is more serious. For their sakes I wish to explain why the principles upon which you have been acting in the College, and which you have illustrated in &lsquo; Tom Brown,&rsquo; according to my understanding of them, are not identical with the principles of those who denounce all opposition to prize-fighting as canting and humbug, but are the very reverse of them. If you indorse generally my interpretation of what you have done and said (of course I may have mistaken you in various particulars), you can ask the Editor of the &lsquo; Working Men&rsquo;s Magazine &rsquo; whether he would be disposed to publish this letter. If you differ from me.as widely as some
of our friends suppose that you do, I know that you will express your conviction frankly,,I have taken your reasons for opening a Pugilistic Class in our College to be mainly these :&mdash;,1. You believe that a cultivation of the bodily powers is desirable for the health of the body and of the mind likewise.,2. You believe that this cultivation is especially needed for the London working man, whose ordinary pursuits are often sedentary, and who misses many o&iacute; the out-of-door advantages which belong to the rural labourer.,3. You believe that such cultivation belongs peculiarly to a College ; because it bears, or ought to bear witness, that the animal and intellectual powers are meant to grow together and to work together, and that the lower are to serve the higher.,4. You believe that there are great objects to which both the animal and intellectual powers should be devoted, e. g. to the succour and protec&not; tion of the weak ; and that these, which are likely to be forgotten if they are left to the ordinary influences of society, ought especially to be indi&not; cated in the education of a College.,5. You believe that on this ground the art of Pugilism is more impor&not; tant for our pupils than any gymnastic art ; because it may be turned to the lowest, most savage, most mercenary ends ; or may be redeemed to be the instrument of a chivalry of Avhich the Mechanic is as capable as the Peer.,To these maxims I cannot give too hearty and thorough an assent. I give it with the more confidence, because I know from experience that a man is morally and intellectually the worse for having neglected the physical energies which God has endowed him with ; that he is more the victim of his body, for not having confessed that the body is as sacred a trust as the soul.,I may have read your novel all wrong; but if I made out its meaning, it was in strict harmony with the lessons which you have applied in the discipline of grown men. I fancied that you had especially laboured to show schoolboys : 1. That the bully is the most detestable of all crea&not; tures, one who carries in him the heart of the coward and the sneak.,2. That a boy who is no model boy, but liable to all ordinary boy temp&not; tations, may nevertheless be brought to confess a principle and to pursue noble and worthy ends. 3. That such a boy may find the best gratifica&not; tion of his fighting propensities not in avenging his own wrongs, but in defending a friend who is physically weaker than himself. 4. That there may be a moral courage in these physically weak boys which the physi&not; cally strong have not, and which they may admire and imitate. To this last doctrine you have done more justice than any of us who are conscious of that weakness could have done. We should have been inclined to exag&not; gerate the timidity and hesitation which so often accompany the monkish student. Though I believe we ought to be aware&mdash;those most who have suffered very little from sickness&mdash;how much boys and men with wasted frames have been able to defy and to endure.,Well, if this be so, I am sure that those noble lords and honourable gentlemen who broke the peace, as the Home Secretary told them they did, when they watched Heenan and Sayers breaking each other&rsquo;s heads, must count you a far greater enemy than that same Home Secretary, For he, while he laid down the law so clearly and decidedly, comforted the
minds of his brother legislators by giving the best possible reasons&mdash; reasons respectable by their antiquity as well as by the comely modem dress in which he clothed them&mdash;for thinking that they had done a most creditable and patriotic act : that they had, consciously or unconsciously, been obeying a more sublime and transcendental law, while they set at nought the statutes of the realm. They had gone forth to endure for many hours the biting of a cruel east wind with all the possible terrors of the police, that they might hinder their countrymen from using or feel&not; ing the bowie-knife, the stiletto, the shillelagh ; that they might teach the men and women of St. Giles&rsquo;s, to aim at a certain scientific method when they were tearing each other&rsquo;s faces or caps. Far be it from me to assert that any lower motives than these could have influenced those distinguished men to incur such hazards. But you, whose work lies among less refined natures, among people of a more average and level mo&not; rality, will be inclined to doubt whether our statesmen did not miscalculate a little the etfect of their noble and disinterested sacrifices, whether the Al&not; dershott meeting could accomplish the objects which Sir George Cornewall Lewis, with his official experience, seems to have hoped from it. Why should the shillelagh, or the bowie-knife, or the stiletto, be so offensive to the mem&not; bers of that crowd, provided they fell into circumstances where any of these weapons was the customary and approved one ? What did they learn there which should lead them to think that the hand might not avail itself of con&not; venient instruments, if they offered themselves, to assist its weakness and increase its chances of inflicting pain ? They saw a stand-up fight you say. They saw what the English or Irish or American fist could do. That is to say, they saw what two exceptional fists&mdash;specially trained fists&mdash;could do in the work of drawing blood and stopping eyes. They associated the work of those fists with certain sums which honourable gentlemen would have to draw from their bankers or the Jews, and which omnibus cads will proba&not; bly have to save out of their wives&rsquo; suppers or gowns or firing. Will Sir George Cornewall Lewis, will Sir Richard Mayne, affirm that those beat&not; ings of wives which are not generally thought to illustrate the manliness and heroism of the English race, were less likely to occur after the stand- up fight than before it ? Was there ever a time when they were more likely to occur ? What has it done to connect the development of the muscles with the development of the mind and character as you wish them to be connected ? What has it done to make Pugilism subordinate to chivalry as you wish it to be subordinate? The more skill you attribute to Heenan or Sayers&mdash;the more you dignify that skill with the name of sci&not; ence&mdash;the more you leave the feeling on the mind of the beholder that the ends of art and science are animal&mdash;I avoid the more vituperative adjective brutal&mdash;not human. And the more you mix national rivalship with the exhibition, the more you associate the national feeling with that which is animal, not with that which is human. America defies England to show that it can produce, what ?&mdash;as stout a man ?&mdash;no, but as formidable a filli- buster as she can. England is jealous of the reputation of her child in that line, and so fillibustering grows more and more to be the profession and glory of both countries. Now you have maintained that fillibustering and courage are not the same thing, but the most opposite things in the world ; that the latter is to be brought in our men and boys on purpose that they may not envy the other, but despise it.
Nothing is so dangerous as to assume that the amusements which attest the character of a nation must be effectual to confirm that character. Certainly no nation less vigorous, less conquering than the Romans, would have invented gladiatorial exhibitions, or have drawn continual delight from them. But I apprehend these exhibitions contributed just as little to the preservation of Roman valour as of Roman humanity. We sepa&not; rate the two ; are we sure that there is not some eternal law which unites them ? The Roman exulted in seeing others suffer ; did not he become less able to endure himself? Was not the vicarious courage of the spectator in the amphitheatre a preparation for the courage which delegated the de&not; fence of the land to the mercenary soldier? May not the prize-fights be working, not in the same direction with the Rifle Corps, but exactly in the opposite direction ? The ultimate purpose of the fight is to secure a belt for a certain man ; the spectator&rsquo;s only concern is that he sees wounds, and loses or wins money, upon the quantity of wounds which those whom he is looking at have to bear. This is to prepare men for feeling that each one&rsquo;s strength is given him for the benefit of his land !,Of course such language as this will be pooh-poohed as cant by a num&not; ber of clever people. But I am writing not for them, but for you. I believe you cannot pooh-pooh it, if you are consistent with the doctrines you have put forth. I feel as strongly as you or any one else that there is a lazy, self-indulgent dislike of pain, to which we in this age are prone.,I feel the temptation bitterly. And I see how easily it puts on the ap&not; pearance of benevolence, though it is not at all benevolent ; how easily it associates itself with the detestable and accursed doctrines about the little consequence to a nation who are its rulers, provided the individuals of it are able to make money and live at ease, which have been set forth in the debates upon Savoy. What I want is that you should not help the advocates of such heresies by allowing them to appear as the cham&not; pions of humanity when they ought to be regarded as its enemies. This was the mistake which was committed by an eminent man of the last age to whom allusion was made in the House of Commons on Triday. Mr. Windham, the refined and accomplished gentleman, denounced, as he might well do, the growth of effeminacy in the land. He would resist it by en&not; couraging bull-baiting and cock-fights. He enlisted the moral sense of the country against him. It was felt that he was cultivatiftg not manli&not; ness but brutality. His passion for paradox, his determination to believe his countrymen always wrong and himself always right, helped to fostei the very evils which he felt to be so serious. He obliged those who had no wish to be Quakers to turn Quakers, since his Quixotism for barbarity appeared to be the only alternative.,I think you have pointed out another alternative. I hope you will persist in holding it forth. You want to resist those who set up money as the god which is to swallow up all national feeling and all individual greatness. You cannot resist them by sympathizing with prize-fighters. You must teach working people and school-boys to keep aloof from them, or they will begin with being gamblers, and end with being slaves.,Yours affectionately,,&Gamma;. H. Maurice.
ANNUAL REPORT OF THE WORKING MAN&rsquo;S COLLEGE,,HALIFAX.,In presenting the Annual Report of the progress of this Institution, which has now reached the commencement of the sixth year of its exist&not; ence, we have cause to congratulate each other on its continued success in attaining those special objects with a view to which the College was originally formed. At the present time the number of students is con&not; siderably larger than at any former period. The subjects studied in the classes are more practical and more adapted to the men and women for whose especial benefit the College is intended. While the issue of useful and instructive, and not merely amusing books, from the library has in&not; creased, the young men who attend the classes have shown, by the subjects which they have selected for study, that they have got clearer views of what the education of working men ought to be, namely, that it is not, by giving them a distaste for the loom or the shop, to make gentlemen of them, but that it is designed on the one hand, by teaching them the appli&not; cation of science to arts and industry, to make them intelligent and skilled workmen, and on the other hand, by showing them how to dig in that mine of lore which lies hid in the shelves of the library, to which they have free access, to give them a refinement of mind, which will be its own reward, and which will &ldquo; lead the artisan to such a knowledge of the prin&not; ciples on which our social relations are based, and of our political history, as may give increased stability to society.&rdquo;*,Such studies as algebra and higher mathematics (which used to be the favourite subjects in the College), we have endeavoured to discourage, not because they are not of great use in disciplining the mind, but because they are not of such practical use to the working man as geometry, men&not; suration, English language, political economy, history, etc. If, for instance, working men are to make themselves worthy of being entrusted with the responsibility of aiding in the selection of their country&rsquo;s legis&not; lators, surely the study of political economy and history is essentially necessary to render them so. In the no less important study of the laws of health, we are sorry to say that we have not yet been able to secure a lec&not; turer, notwithstanding that a member of our committee has for some time held out the hope that he would undertake the task.,The study of chemistry under the able direction of Dr. Paley has made great progress. We regret exceedingly that in consequence of his intended removal from the town, we shall shortly lose the services which he has so long given gratuitously. We are glad however to state that Mr. Noble (who obtained last December a first class government certificate in inorganic chemistry) will take the direction of these classes. He will have the advantage of a new and enlarged laboratory, which has just been fitted up with additional apparatus. The list of certificates granted by the Society of Arts last year makes it unnecessary for us to say one word in praise of the manner in which Dr. Paley has conducted the classes. We can only thank him most heartily for having established and carried them on so successfully.,* Report of East Lancashire Union of Evening Schools, 1859.
^ In the same list (which is given below) will be seen the result of Mr. J. C. W ainhouse s gratuitous labours in the book-keeping class. The press&not; ing demands of business having compelled him to discontinue for the present his attendance at the College, Mr. Middlebrook, we are glad to say, has taken his place.,A\ e have at last arranged to set on foot the class for adults in elementary instruction, which we have long desired to do. The class is conducted by Mr. James Spencer, one of the oldest and most successful students of the College.,The study of theology is still very much neglected by the students. This may appear a very unhealthy sign,&mdash;but it may be partly accounted for by the tact that many of the students are engaged on the Sunday, on which day this class meets, in giving instruction in various Sunday-schools of the town.,M e continue to have full confidence in the system of Examinations carried out by the Society of Arts. The Eev. J. H. Gooch, M.A., the head-master o&iacute; Heath Grammar School, continues chairman of our local board. We have passed sixteen candidates for the Society of Arts Exami&not; nation which is to be held in May.,No better system of examination could possibly be devised, and we pro&not; test against the insinuation contained in the last year&rsquo;s report of the York&not; shire Union of Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes (page 11), that those examinations are not &ldquo; a practical fact.&rdquo;,We may here mention that at the public distribution of certificates in October, granted by the Society of Arts to students of the College, we were honoured by the presence of the Chairman of the Council of the Society of Arts, Sir Thomas Phillips, and other members of the Council ; Sir J. Kaye Shuttleworth, Bart., Sir W. Page Wood, Mr. Adderley, M.P., and other gentlemen whose names are well known in the cause of popular education. It was gratifying to hear strong approval of our system ex&not; pressed by men who are competent to give an opinion on the subject. So gratified was Mr. Bohn, the eminent publisher, with what he saw of the College, that he made a present to the library of one hundred of his best books, for which we beg here publicly to thank him.,Last Easter the Annual Conversazione was held, and proved a great success.,Though the numbers on the books have increased, we draw attention to the fact, that the number of clerks and persons not coming actually under the denomination of working men has decreased during the year.,In the Young Women&rsquo;s Institute, though the numbers are slightly below those reported last Easter, the classes are again as efficient as they were. In consequence of the resignation of Mrs. Noble (who had for a long time conducted the classes), which took place last Midsummer, the numbers during the autumn fell off very considerably. Since Christmas however (thanks to the exertions of Mrs. Akroyd, who is president of this branch of the Institution) the classes are as flourishing as ever, under Miss Ash (Government Certificate), along with Miss Hudson (Government Cer&not; tificate), and Miss Penny (Government Certificate), the teachers in the girls&rsquo; and infants&rsquo; day schools.,The class for Domestic Cookery answers the expectations that were,.
formed of it. There is one practical difficulty connected with it, namely, that most of the young women being employed in the factories during the day, the only time available for instruction in this (as indeed in all other things) is the evening , which is not the dinner-time of the working classes. Hence it is not easy to dispose of the viands.,In Vocal Music the classes have advanced so much that they intend to give a public concert during Easter week.,Sewing and Cutting-out are still made the most important parts of the education of the young women.,A very flourishing branch of the West Eiding of Yorkshire Penny Savings-Bank has been established in connection with the Young Women&rsquo;s Institute.,The detailed reports of the annual College Examination will be found below, from which it will be seen that a larger number of College Certifi&not; cates have been gained than at any previous examination.,The following Tables are appended to the Eeport ;&mdash;,Young Men. &mdash;Number on the books, 200 ; admitted during past year, 123 ; average nightly attendance, 78; average weekly attendance, 110. Spencer, 2nd ditto.,NEWS.,Boston.&mdash;The Working Meris College and the American Present. &mdash;A late number of the Magazine contained a notice of a gift sent to the Boston College by the Mayor and some citizens of the &ldquo; intellectual metropolis &rdquo; of the United States. To give a fit&not; ting acknowledgment of this graceful and kindly act as well as to inaugurate the public existence of the College, its promoters held a festival Meeting on the 12th inst., at the Exchange Hall. About six hundred people assembled for tea at half-past five. The
Rev. G. B. Blenkin, M.A., Vicar, Principal of the College, presided ; and amongst the company present were J. C. Little, Esq., Mayor ; M. Staniland, Esq., M.P. ; Captain White, (Artillery) ; the Revs. J. H. Oldrid, C. E. Lamo, T. W. Sale, J. Topham (Gos- berton) ; Dr. Adams ; Professor Connington ; T. Garfit, Esq. ; W. Garfit, Esq. ; Rev. A. Garlit ; etc. etc. The tea tables were presided over by about fifty of the principal ladies of the town. The Artillery band, under the direction of Mr. Keller, and by permission of Captain White, attended in uniform, and added much to the pleasure of the evening&rsquo;s proceedings. After tea the assembly was increased to about a thousand, the majority being working men and their wives.,Grace having been sung, and the tables cleared,,The President rose and stated that the object of the meeting was twofold&mdash;first, to celebrate the inauguration of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, and second, to acknowledge the munificent present from across the Atlantic. He then detailed the history and pro&not; gress of the College : it had risen from the working men themselves, and since its com&not; mencement, a few months ago, four hundred and seventy members had joined. It com&not; prised a news-room, welt supplied with newspapers and periodicals, and class-rooms, where almost every branch of education was taught to those desirous of receiving it by gentle&not; men of the town, who, without fee or reward, had give&rsquo;u their valuable services. Its financial prospects were good ; the Council had decided to make it self-supporting, and he was happy to state at the present time it was not one penny in debt. Donations to the amount of &pound;53 and subscriptions from honorary members (&pound;15) had been received, and these sums had been expended in fitting up the College and purchasing books for the library. It was intended that the actual payments of the working men who were mem&not; bers should cover the expenses. At present their library was but a feeble institution, but they had on the previous day received a most handsome present of books from Shad- worth Hodgson, Esq. He hoped that the cordial feeling which had hitherto existed be&not; tween the two Bostons would continue not only to their children, but to their children&rsquo;s children for many generations. Amongst the American books sent was one (the History of East Boston, by Sumner), for his Worship the Mayor, and he had now the pleasure, in the presence of that vast assemblage, and in the name of the inhabitants of Boston in America, to present the book to his Worship.,The Mayor said he received the present with the greatest possible pleasure, and hoped to hand it down as a heir-loom to his family, as a token of the kindly affection which had been exhibited by the Daughter Boston to the Mother Town.,Captain White was next called upon and made a very eloquent address.,Mr. Burrell , a working man, next addressed the meeting. He proposed &ldquo; Our Ame&not; rican Brothers, and thanks for their munificent present.&rdquo; He detailed the advantages the Institution conferred upon the working classes, showing how the mind was developed and expanded by study and education. He conceived that the establishment of the College would make its members more intellectual, better husbands, and better members of society. Temperance lecturers had lectured to them, and the thunders of the law had been hurled at them to induce them to turn from their evil ways, but nothing had been prepared for their leisure hours ; now, however, that want was supplied by the 6 Col&not; lege. It had, he said, been argued that if the working classes were better educated they would be above their station, but he felt assured that nothing was more likely than educa, tion to put the right man in the right place.,Mr. Markham, , another working man, followed in a very able speech in which he pointed out the advantages of the College, and instanced one case where a member had ш a short time learnt to read, who before scarcely knew a word in a book.,The President regretted the unavoidable absence of one of the members for the borough (H. Ingram, Esq.), and requested their other member to address them.,M. Staniland , Esq., M.P., then spoke at some length. He commented on the effect which the spread of education had had on the institutions of the country contributing to the reform of abuses, the improvement of our administration and criminal iurispru dence, and the raising of the tone of political feeling. He instanced the present rational treatment of our colonies, as compared with the mismanagement which had lost to them the country whence they had now received such a handsome present.,The Rev. J. Topham (Gosberton), in an entertaining speech, eulogized the efforts of the working men to improve their position, and gave instances in which it had heen done ш other l )laces With great success. There was nothing, he remarked, but what working
men might attain to by perseverance and industry. He announced that he had succeeded in obtaining a promise from Dr. Yeats, a gentleman of great learning, to give them a lecture in the course of the ensuing winter.,Dr. Adams said he had been requested to speak on the subject of the &lsquo;&lsquo;Two Bostons but the evening was now so far advanced that he felt he could not trespass further on their time by giving this &ldquo; tale of two cities,&rdquo; and entering into many tempting details. He might otherwise have carried them back in imagination, to that early epoch, in the be&not; ginning of the fourteenth century, when &ldquo;many miners digged deep&rdquo; the foundations of that noble church steeple&mdash;unsurpassed in England&mdash;which towered above our town ; and might have shown them that brave old lady. Dame Margery Tilney, and Sir John Truesdale, then vicar of Boston, depositing their &ldquo;great gifts&rdquo; of &pound;5 sterling each on the foundation stone. He might have conjured up the ghosts of the old Augustine and Carmelite Friars, who tenanted the many religious houses, which formerly stood around. He might have pictured their streets lined once more with quaint old houses, with angu&not; lar gables, latticed windows, and overhanging buttresses, such as we still see in that brave old structure in South-end ; and he might have peopled them with the portly burghers, the Flemish, French, and German traders, who resided here,&mdash;men with peaked beards and slashed doublets,&mdash;whose rich argosies floated over every sea. But he would have preferred to speak of later incidents, for he held that Boston conld boast greater glories than these&mdash;for from her loins had sprung that noble race who had been the pioneers of civilization in the New World, the &ldquo; Pilgrim Fathers,&rdquo; who had founded one of the mightiest nations of the earth. He would have liked to tell of how, in 1620, the &lsquo;Mayflower&rsquo; sailed with these early Puritans from Plymouth Roads to the shores of Massachusetts. The very ground whereon they had knelt on landing was hallowed by their descendants, just as &ldquo;Telľs Rock,&rdquo; where the patriot jumped ashore to achieve the freedom of his country, was still held sacred by the Swiss peasant. And he would fain have talked of how, a little later, the &lsquo; Griffin&rsquo; set sail from the Downs, with her three hundred Boston Puritans, and with them that great and good man, the Rev. John Cotton, &mdash;one of their Chairman&rsquo;s predecessors,&mdash;and of how these emigrants founded the city which they w r ere now thanking for their kindly gift. He would fain have spoken also of the many glories of this New Boston, but on these he had no time to enlarge. Coming back to the work before them, he said the poet&rsquo;s aphorism, &ldquo; A little knowledge is a dan&not; gerous thing,&rdquo; had often been shown to be untrue. A little knowledge was better than none ; and the more a man had, the happier and the better he was sure to be. He spoke of the rise of the College ; of how a small band of intelligent artisans&mdash;thirsting after more knowledge, and banded together for mutual improvement&mdash;had been its nucleus.,It had been got up by the working men, and it was managed solely by them. For the success of the evening&rsquo;s entertainment they were especially under deep obligation to the ladies, who were always foremost in every good work in Boston, and were the main up&not; holders of its many charities. He was also glad to see that so many of the workmen&rsquo;s wives and children had come to the meeting. He concluded by proposing the resolution which (considering the lateness of the hour) he had been asked to substitute for his ori&not; ginal subject&mdash;&ldquo; The Ladies, with thanks for their great kindness in presiding on this occasion.&rdquo; This was seconded by T. Garfit, Esq., who took the opportunity of an&not; nouncing his gift of a drinking-fountain for the use of the working men.,A vote of thanks to the President, and an exhibition of dissolving views by Mr. T. Small, concluded the proceedings. _,London. &mdash; Rifle Corps. &mdash;The names of members entered for the corps are now above 280, but only about 200 men have been sworn in, and some of the rest have withdrawn. The Seijeant-Major has, however, formed the corps into four Companies&mdash; making one battalion,&mdash;and, in order to mix the old and new men well together, has &lsquo; quartered &rsquo; the corps for each Company, putting Nos. 1, 5, 9, etc., in the First Company ; 2, 6, 10, etc., in the Second ; 3, 7, 11 , etc., in the Third; 4, 8, 12, etc., in the Fourth. The officers of the Third Company have been elected&mdash;Captain Roebuck, Lieutenant (&quot;Wil&not; liam) Sutton, Ensign Tansley. It will be seen that these are names of three students who, from the time of their joining the College years ago, have devoted themselves without ceasing to forwarding the College work, in class, in social meeting, by coaching others, in the library and at home, and in every other possible way. Two of them, Mr. Roebuck and Mr. Tansley, have won for themselves places as Fellows and Members of Council of
the College ; and while all have been forward to the work of the corps, Serjeant Sutton&rsquo;s late extra drill (after 10.15 p.m. on Tuesdays), and Mr. Roebuck&rsquo;s careful supervision of belts, pouches, uniforms, etc.&mdash;besides collecting the money for them&mdash;have shown that the corps will get the benefit of the same qualities that the College has so largely pro&not; fited by. The recognition of the services of these officers by the new members of the College and Corps is especially grateful to the old members of the College with whom their names have long heeu household words of affection and esteem. The Fourth Company has (till its officers are elected) been put under the command of (2nd) Lieutenant Fothe- ringham, whose handling of his Company on our Good Friday field-day, on Wimbledon Common, called forth such encomiums from our Serjeant-Major, and showed him to be better up to his work than the First Lieutenant, who writes these lines. For a full report of the meeting which we have not space to reprint, see &lsquo; Halifax Guardian,&rsquo; April 14th.,CORRESPONDENCE.,TO THE EDITOR OF THE &lsquo;&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt; MAGAZINE.&rsquo;,, т с , Lancaster, April 17.,My dear Sir,&mdash; We have here a pretty good Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, but attended by comparatively few of the working men. Some of us are concerting plans for adult classes next autumn and winter, but all my efforts to get unpaid lecturers this last winter tailed, and if we have these classes we must have paid teachers. I had a history class last winter, but with only five or six scholars ; this winter, a mutual improvement class (virtually a discussion class), with an average attendance of thirteen or fourteen.,Under these circumstances do you think it would be well to try and get the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution converted into a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ? Is not Lancaster too small a place oi such an attempt? *. e. not a sufficient number of the educated classes to make it woi k well The population is 16,000. Having paid teachers seems rather inconsistent with the idea and spirit of the original movement.,Do you think it possible to connect these efforts for educating the working classes with measures (most urgently needed), for giving useful and interesting occupation to the men whom we draw away from the public-houses by the Temperance agitation ? These
latter are ecarcely up to the notion of classes, and want amusement more than instruc&not; tion, at first especially.,We are to have a meeting for conference soon on both these subjects, and I shall be glad of an answer through the columns of the Magazine, if that is least trouble.,I am very truly yours, Henry Solly.,[Mr. Solly&rsquo;s own estimate of local circumstances will be a much better guide than any advice from a distance. One caution however is : Don&rsquo;t start a &lsquo; College unless you have a fair prospect of success. A present failure may check future effort for years to come. Also, if anything like an adult school is wanted, keep it quite distinct from the College. This is for many reasons very important. As to the payment ot teachers, that would not be a very great departure from our first idea, which was, and still is, to get teachers from among our students, and let them be paid as soon as we can afford it. THE,Staking Pen&rsquo;s College Paghine.,No. XVIII. JUNE 1, 1860.,ON THE USE OE TRANSLATIONS.,BY J. W. BALES, B.A. CAMBRIDGE.,Is it worth a working man&rsquo;s while to spend the hours he can spare for study in the acquisition of dead languages ? Is it well for him to give up his precious hours to the study of the Latin and Greek Grammars ? I am inclined to think that this is not well. Not that the study of Grammar is of trifling importance, for indeed it is of the greatest importance, both di&not; rectly and indirectly ; bnt the study of it should begin at home, and in the case of most of our students should end at home. At all events, their native language should be made the basis of all their grammatical studies. Surely it is but the order of common sense, that we should thoroughly master our mother-tongue, and acquaint ourselves with its history and phi&not; losophy, and its usages and powers of expression, before we attempt to stammer in any foreign dialect. Is English Grammar in fact such a mean, confused, unphilosophical thing, as our treatment of it intimates that we deem it to be P Of course, to understand his own language, the student must have some little knowledge of the languages that have affected it. I would comprehend this much knowledge of these other languages in his study of English Grammar. There can be no doubt that at present our handbooks are wofully imperfect things ; and indeed it is perhaps owing mainly to this deficiency that men so eagerly desert the study of their own language for languages upon which much greater pains and labour have been bestowed. But the truth remains the same. Is not his own tongue the working man&rsquo;s most advantageous study ?,Is he then to be excluded from all perusal of the Classics ? Certainly not ; for how can any one be called educated in any sense of the word, who is ignorant of the writers that have exercised the most powerful influence upon every age, and greatly controlled the human mind ? Clearly these great masters must be studied, and carefully. But this study must be achieved by the translator&#39;s help. The characters on the wall are strange and undecipherable. We must call in some interpreter to read us their meaning. We have no time to study ourselves the original scroll. There is an enemy waiting outside our gates too, is there not ? And our feast will soon be over. There is nothing for us, therefore, but to call in other aid.,There is a deep prejudice against Translations prevailing in very many persons&rsquo; minds. How far is it defensible ?,I speak now of the student who aspires to an acquaintance with Greek and Latin literature &mdash;not of him who aims at the extension of his know&not; ledge of language or of Grammar. We may call him the general student. I find him content with nothing but the original text. He wishes to read the very words Homer employed, to see the jewel in its original setting.,VOL. II. &#39;B&rsquo;
lie objects to reading his author with another mau&rsquo;s eyes, to depending upon an intermediate version, or in any way having to rely upon a third person. The presence of this third person annoys him sorely. The charm of the conversation is dissolved. Often he can discern that the great man is saying something worthy of his great name, but that his Boswell is per&not; fectly incompetent to report it. The interpreter has views of his own, it may be, and garbles the great man&rsquo;s words to illustrate and support them. Or he has no congeniality of feeling with the great man ; he cannot appre&not; ciate him, cannot make out the point of his observations, or recognize the divine truths that are enwrapped in his sayings. Or, if in every way he is a fit exponent of the hero at whose feet he siis worshiping, and whose high wisdom he would publish to the whole world, he discovers that it is all but impossible to translate this wisdom into other words, that to do so perfectly is entirely impossible. r .,Undoubtedly there are serious objections, so serious that no one who has time and opportunity for investigating the original writing will not at once set himself to do so. And I think they are very legitimate objections. A perfect translation is, beyond all controversy, impossible, I mean by a perfect translation, a translation that represents to an English mind pre&not; cisely the same ideas that the original represents to a Greek mind, to take the case of a Greek author. Is it too much to say that there are infinitely few words in any language, that have a complete equivalent in any other language? So many different associations belong to every indi&not; vidual word in all languages, that their identity in any two languages is indescribably improbable. We translate * regnum &rsquo; a c kingdom / but I sus&not; pect that &lsquo; regnum 5 called up in a Roman&rsquo;s mind thoughts and memories very different from those suggested to an Englishman by the word &lsquo; kingdom.&#39; In fact this is only saying that words have a history and antecedents,; and as there are no two men whose fortunes have been totally similar, so there are no two words surrounded by exactly similar connections, with their shades of meaning of the same intensity and variety. If we proceed from words to sentences, we have the difficulty of idiom to encounter. In some few cases an idiom may be found common to two languages, often from the one language having adopted it from the other, and the careful trans&not; lator will not neglect the advantage. But for the most part there will be found a wide difference between the idiomatic usages of any two languages. And the translator will strive in vain to preserve the force and conciseness of his author. He will find weak and ineffective his rendering of what in the original is full of vigour and home-thrusts. And then there is the question of siyle. Do we not all know what a subtlety style is_? It is. not everybody, I fancy, who can distinguish Addison&rsquo;s papers in the Spectator from Steele&rsquo;s. And yet there is doubtless a great difference between the styles of them. Our perceptions are too dull Tor these tine distinctions. İt is only when,an author&rsquo;s style is marked by some obvious, some staring peculiarities&mdash;Dr. Johnson&rsquo;s sesquipedal words, for instance &mdash;that most of us can in any way be said to apprehend it. Let our trans&not; lator, however, be well trained in this delicate criticism; ,will he succeed in transferring his author&rsquo;s style into his translation? Will he convey to his reader any sort of idea of the turns and elegancies and gracefulness of the original work? &ldquo;Let every man know,&rdquo; says Dante, &ldquo;that nothing
which has been harmonized by the music of linked measures can change from one language to another without breaking all its harmony and sweet&not; ness.&rdquo; And so ot prose works. Do we enjoy in the translation the native rhythm, the ease and flow and animation which marked the first expression of the thought? I am afraid not. There is a constraint, an awkwardness, an uneasiness about most translations. The translator &lsquo; dances in fetters/ and we are for ever hearing the clinking of them, and noticing the unna&not; tural postures they force him into. He cannot be simple and unaffected, somehow. His tongue is tied, and he cannot speak freely, but falls into a strange jargon and never seems to know what idiom he is talking. Let us look into Mr. Bohn&rsquo;s Classical Library ; though there is nobody who will not allow that the specimens of the art of translation which that series (so invaluable to private schoolmasters) contains, are not the finest in the world, ought not the translation to be translated ? Do we not need an English version of those inestimable volumes, or at least of the majority of them ? To say that a translator must have perfect command over both the languages which he has to handle is to say what every one will assent to, but what involves an infinity of labour. Of this labour I shall have occa&not; sion to speak more fully presently. n gioilnignmi bar&oslash;q,Is not this a formidable catalogue of objections ? And the catalogue is not yet complete ; and yet we venture to recommend the use of translations. Let us now examine the objections to the other course that lies open to the student, and see whether, if o&iacute; two evils it is good to cimose tlie less, he must not choose the translation in preference to studying the original Ian- gt&igrave;$ge. l J - f d &beta; &#39; ujmauB&#39;iJ о // .эшвшлдшх vfa&aring;onoeabni,Let us remember that the end which our student proposes to himself is a knowledge of classical literature,&mdash;an end most excellent and most worthy o&iacute; attainment. How much toil and trouble and time is he pre&not; pared to bestow upon the means ? Let us suppose that he daringly re&not; solves upon studying the original languages, as many of our students do resolve. At the expiration of a period, which, had he taken the other route open to him, would have sufficed to make him respectably acquainted with the classics, he will be found but little advanced on his journey. He will be found still in his swaddling-clothes, utterly unable to walk a siimle step without much assistance. He begins to be aware of the stupendous difficulties that impede the mastery of a dead language, and at last to despair of reaching the summit of his hopes. Indeed the difficulties of the Latin and Greek languages can scarcely be exaggerated. I do not mean to say that the comprehension of c tu mones &rsquo; and the other Spirited sentences that adom the early pages of the Latin Delectus, is an insuper, able achievement. The student may soon be able to translate these con&not; cise paragraphs fluently, and enjoy them in all their fulness. But when will he be capable of reading H&ograve;race and Virgil and the classics, in short with pleasure and facility? When will he be in a&#39;condition to dispense with his Liddell and Scbtt ? That is a great and glorious compilation but its presence terribly taints the savour of a pleasant author. It makes a very objectionable accompaniment to a classical air, does it not? It is a great weariness to the flesh, that everlasting &lsquo; turning up &rsquo; of words in the Lexicon. What a vast amount of time is lost in the mere thumbing of the Dictionary&rsquo;s leaves !,H 3
And lot our student be careful not to exaggerate the position he will hold, if, after infinite pains and labour, he succeeds in making himself a scholar. He will not even then find it an easy matter to pluck the golden apples. He will find that Time has beset the way to them with many thorns. H&eacute; will be tortured by various readings, and corrupt texts, and impenetrable obscurities. Many words that were once instinct with a wonderful lit&eacute;, that could marvellously move the passions, that were inter&not; woven in the mind with the most powerful associations, to us are feeble and languid. This verse, that is now become a mere &ldquo; commentators&rsquo; cross,&rdquo; once, perhaps, made the Athenian theatre ring with laughter, or hushed it into a deep silence. How obscure to us is Aristophanes, for in&not; stance. How different from that Comedian whose wit kept the population of Attica in a roar during the whole Dionysia. The mists of many cen&not; turies have arisen between us and those great days, and them no toil of ours can scatter. .saaoone ban &middot; iliiw эшоэтэто,The student&rsquo;s labours, therefore, are by no means at end when by dint of untiring perseverance and incalculable exertion he has to some extent acquired the language. And the question arises whether the result justi&not; fies th&eacute; course he has taken, involving, as that course does, the sacrifice of so many other studies of infinite moment. Has he laid out his life to the best advantage P The time he can devote to his mental improvement is limited, and he has a vast field of literature to traverse. Is it wise in himl to have tarried so long on one spot ; above all, to have spent so much time ; ytij/pitfl&szlig; Ί&omicron; вээвирэквш odJ if In? &middot; аотП/псорэл Surely this is not the most prudent course for the working man. It may r be the true course for &ldquo; wealthy men who care not how they give,&rdquo; whose hands have abundant leisure comparatively. But for the man upon whom the most rigid economy of his time is imperatively incumbent, who has less tim&eacute; add fewer opportunities at his disposal, is this the true courses however great his energy may be, however inextinguishable his ardour, however commanding his- talents ? This question the members of our College, Who attend the Latin and Greek classes with a view to perusing tHe Ldtiii&#39;and 1 Greek authors in their own tongues, will do well to ponder.,Obviously there is only one other path which the student can follow. And I think that oil reflection he will certainly admit it tobe the true one, in spite of the numerous objections to which it is undeniably liable. next academical year there will he formed, under the superintendence of the Principal, a Greek Literature Class, which will be conducted on pre&not; cisely the same principles as the present English Literature Class , only that we shall then use translations, not the original works.,■onJlJfi 9 fl&iacute; 8&iacute; 9I&iacute;g 9Ί60 l&szlig;9T&sect; 08 8Л rmc&iacute; OVf&icirc;fl oi ,8&Igrave;fI9V9 lo vibib&#39;t,&iacute;leng&iacute;iil Sili,&#39; 19 fii 0 ОП İM,inni,&mdash;,&#39;890 9&iacute;l,&mdash;- ;,Jgnia f&icirc; &rsquo;{(J ,00&Igrave; ,801998 łl,oiifilenm^ HALL WE LEARN LATIN ? (Part TI.),&uuml; o) trn n m &omicron;&upsilon; difid ł&amp;gt; ,йшй 8 увй * ДшиЪо&#39;ш ih rrr, г \,BY GODFREY LUSHINGTON, M.A. FELLOW OF ALL SOULS COLLEGE, yrtfi fifidJ diow i&szlig;di то! miift donor &ouml;&Eacute;fteH&Eacute;wimhi nfi to emleai i&szlig;di yibi&iacute;qmie odi oi aedwo g&igrave; doidw* э /fi ; гшШгпт inses,In the previous number I stated that there was still to be discovered a general course of study, scientifically constructed upon the relation of all the different kinds of knowledge to one another and to the human mind, which should be universally adopted at all places of education. I also stated that this void is, at our great public Schools and Universities, filled, or supposed to be filled, by the usurpation of the classical system, but at &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; is confessed to be still unsatisfied. In default then of a general course, with us each man has to choose a subject of study for himself. This, if he has no special bent of his own, which is the common case, is a hard matter. Considering this perplexity, a great service would be rendered by any one who would write a book, which should point out not only the subject-matter of each branch of study, i. e. what abstract knowledge might be transferred from the book to the head of the learner, but also the practical value of such knowledge when at&not; tained. By practical value I do not mean the sum of money into which it can be turned ; quite otherwise. The man who studies with a view to cash payment only, is as guilty of desecration, and will as surely be disap&not; pointed of all reward that is truly valuable, as he who marries for a for&not; tune. Least of all should the working man do so ; he should be the first to recognize that, a man&rsquo;s riches consist not in the multitude of things which he possesseth ; otherwise his class are cut off from happiness. By practical value, I mean the effect of the knowledge acquired upon the life and chaiacter of the studcut&amp;gt;&iexcl; ; .&middot;. This however will vary in different cases; for though all knowledge gives an inward stability and elevation, and also,know the student, who he is, what are his opportunities to learn, what his most pressing wants ; we must know too the study, how far and in what manner it is from its nature capable of application in everyday life. Now our student, be it remembered;&middot; is- aPricing fn&aacute;ri.bl As such probably he comes to the College not well educated; he has learnt to work with his hands, but not with his head ; he has no conception of what exact know&not; ledge is. He has very little: time for f&sect;tudy ,^&omicron;&eta;&rho; hpur or perhaps two a day, and that only alter the dayrfe wolrkeis done, hi His instruction is con&not; fined to two hours a week. He has no companions in study ; those around him are perhaps coarse, or care nothing for books; he himself has no great ambition. On the other hand, he is comparatively safe from preju- dide, for he&middot;&#39;is ready dio Confess ignorance* and* as . ho is content to be

troubling himself with the varieties of declensions mid conjugations. But for the higher walks of Grammar, where it passes into Philology, Latin Gram&not; mar or even the whole Latin language is quite inadequate ; for nothing here can be accomplished without a comparison of many languages, and, above nil, a knowledge of Sanskrit. Nor could I recommend the student to learn Latin as the first step in Philology ; for Philology, though it holds out the promise of the most intensely interesting discoveries, presupposes such a minute and abstruse investigation of archaic verbal forms, as for this reason alone to be beyond the reach of any ordinary student.,If practice in translation be the one thing needful, no doubt Latin is a better medium than French or German. For Latin being a dead lan&not; guage, greater skill is of course required to find exact equivalents in mo&not; dern words for its ancient thoughts, or in its ancient words for modern thoughts. But it may be questioned whether translation, being- simply a practical training, is calculated to satisfy the student&rsquo;s desire of knowledge. At all events I think it should be reserved only for the advanced student, already well read both in Latin and English ; for how can a person appre&not; ciate the fine shades of meaning of the particular passage in the original (as it is necessary to do), who has no general familiarity with the language? Or how can he accurately express himself in the translation, if he is not already master of a vocabulary ? Or how can he do either, if as yet he has no literary feeling or judgment ? To set a boy of fourteen down with a dictionary to spell out an ode of Horace into indifferent English, or a pas&not; sage from Burke into still more indifferent Latin, has long seemed to me, except as an elementary grammar exercise, mere waste of time.,But it will lie said&mdash;should not Latin be learnt for the sake of English, as one-half of English words are Anglicized Latin words P If so, it may be answered, why not also learn German for the sake of the other half? But in good truth, for all ordinary purposes we can arrive 1 -sufficiently &amp;gt;at the meaning of words by observing how they are Used in conversation and in books : greater precision no doubt is to be had only by ascertaining the history of the word. But rigorous precision of this kind can hardly be an object to our student, especially if it has to be purchased at the cost of so much time, for words, like men, are apt to have long pedigrees. Rarely will the history of the English word end with the Latin, but the student will haply be referred from the Latin to the Greek, and from the Greek to s Banyait,- Ш dt otli&copy;&ouml;(^( &amp;gt; to Ьйш &lsquo;Feigh&iacute;tb of Philol ogicaL&iacute;ggiofese 1 Anek here nothing short &ouml;f the most exact knowledge will avail. It is as useless to aba able to say that &#39;i so and so^is a Latin word, or comes from the&raquo; old Sanskrit, unless you know exactly What it means and how it is employed in Sanskrit and Latin, as it would be ibi- yon 1 if you desired to make us understand John Smithson&rsquo;s character better through his antecedents, to tell us that he descended from one Smith in the eleventh century, but who ori What; SmM ivahpldr h&oacute;Wdiam^hfeq&agrave;p&eacute;ltMhis nadie, or what was the in- tervetoing dink between him and &#39;Smithson, you really did not know, odi S&#39;M! wdihen&#39;ht&uuml;dy&#39;L&auml;tin - literature as^theobasis of English literature ? I so- why nőt als&oacute;&eacute;i ancient Greek, of&#39;which Latin literature is more than anything else the expression? why not modern German, &iexcl;which lies at the root of so much modern European thought ? why not English history too, for literature is in a senso the reflex of the times in which it was written ?

You think knowledge is tobe got ł-hetip - you&rsquo;ll c&ograve;llie and &lsquo;pay Battle Massey sixpence a week, and he&rsquo;ll make you clever at figures without your taking any trouble. But knowledge isn&rsquo;t to be got with paying sixpence, let me tell ypii^if ytpj/re tp know, figures, you must turn em over in your own heads, and keep your thoughts fixed on &rsquo;em. There&rsquo;s nothing you can&rsquo;t turn into a sum, for there&rsquo;s nothing but what s got number in it -even a fool. You may say to yourselves, &ldquo;Pm one fool, and Jack&rsquo;s another* if my fool&rsquo;s head weighed four pound, and Jack&rsquo;s three pound three ounces and three-quarters, how many pennyweights heavier \у0ПИ ; п1У ! Шй Ьё thatf Jack&rsquo;s ? ?| &rsquo; A man that&middot;had&middot;got his heart in learning figures would nIake&#39;&Iuml;&uuml;iwLft&oacute; fettsfei^^nď&#39;^&oacute;ť^&rsquo;eŕn&#39;in his h&#39;&eacute;a&aring;t : %heii he s&aacute;ťat his shoemaking, he&rsquo;d count his stitches by fives, and then put a price on his stitches, say halfa lai thing, and tM&Auml;&ouml;&eacute;4&eacute;W &iuml;&ouml;ttfcii taeyke &iquest;Wfldb^fft&oslash; t&oacute;-honr^and th&egrave;m&#39; ! &acirc;sk himself how much
money he&#39;d get in a day at that rate ; and then how much ten workmen would get work&not; ing three, or twenty, or a hundred years at that rate&mdash;and all the while his needle would be going just as fast as if he left his head empty for the devil to dance in. But the long and the short of it is&mdash;I&rsquo;ll have nobody in my night-school that doesn&rsquo;t strive to learn what he comes to learn, as hard as if he was striving to get out of a dark hole into broad daylight. 111 send no man away because he&rsquo;s stupid ; if Billy Taft, the idiot, wantod to learn anything, I d not reiuse to teach him. But I&rsquo;ll not throw away good knowledge on people who think they can get it by the sixpenn&rsquo;orth, and carry it away with &rsquo;em as they would an ounce ot snuff. So never come to me again, if you can&rsquo;t show that you&rsquo;ve been working with your own heads, instead of thinking you can pay for mine to work for you. That&rsquo;s the last word I&rsquo;ve got to say to you.&rsquo;,With this final sentence, Barile Massey gave a sharper rap than ever with his knobbed stick, and the discomfited lads got up to go with a sulky look.&rdquo;&mdash; Adam Bede.,.f&iacute;ns&iacute;giiČL Ъ оиот i&iacute; J o til g ni^qi&iacute;ooo еайвкяуЬ adl &#39;k Lib ко ni owl t İ80iiT &iacute;k ,чо эоо ii&szlig;/ll о&#39;юггг riofl&iacute;iom o&iacute; ion are ito Y Я.И),Ьпк^пЭ ί&omicron; orli m gbomq łfcrfw &iacute;n &omicron;ί&kappa;ί&delta; .G,&Aacute;&amp;gt;nd oil!,London. The Council have resolved to add a new Class to the five into which the members of the College have been hitherto divided, namely, that of Scholars. It has been found that many men prefer to confine their studies to one branch of the College work,&mdash;as English Language and History, or Language and Literature, or Art, or Mathe&not; matics,&mdash;rather than spiead their attention oVer the range of subjects required for an Associateship-iThese men then are to be, when competent, our Scholars in Art, English, etc., and, will rank, between the holders of a ; single Certificate of Competency and the As&not; sociates. *,&upsilon;&iota;&iota;&upsilon; Sdluc year,&mdash; a tent ana tea m tne garden, singing out of doors,and m, and the drawings of the Drawing Clasśes. Some too were present at a rowing-party of sixty, from Richmond to Hampton Court, to see the chestnuts in blossom, the first of the country trips resumed from last year : see last September&rsquo;s Magazine.,F. P.,London. &mdash;For the third term of the sixth year the total entries (Preparatory Classes included) were:&mdash;New Members, 115 ; Old Members, 284 ; together, 399, and of these, 9 У did not enter any class. The numbers in the different classes were :&mdash;Drawing, 50 ; Vocal Musie (&sect; II. 15, &sect; I. 5), 20; Latin (&sect; III. 15, &sect; II. 11, &sect; I. 8), 34; Greek, 4; German, 8; French (&sect; III. 34, &sect; II. 18, &sect; I. 18), 70; English Grammar (&sect; IT. 16, &sect; I. 14), 30; Composition, 7; Literature, 6 ; Bookkeeping, 12 ; Arithmetic, 17 ; Geo&not; metry (&sect; III. 14, &sect; II. 3, &sect; I 4), 21 ; Algebra (&sect; III. 8, &sect; II. 9, &sect; I. 3), 20 ; Logic, 5 ; The Microscope, 4; History of England, 7 ; Preparatory Classes, 55.,The only Course-tickets taken out were :&mdash;In French, 8 ; in Latin, 1.,Cambridge. &mdash;The College had its Annual Excursion on Monday the 28th. About thirty of its members, including the three Secretaries, Mr. Gray, Mr. Hadley, and Mr. Macmillan, and accompanied by one or two friends (Mr. Litchfield, from the London College, being one of the number), spent the day very pleasantly at Audley End. The house, park, and grounds were opened to them by the kind permission of Lord Braybrooke and his brother the Vice-Chancellor. The brightness of the day, the beauty of the place, and the highly satisfactory completeness of the commissariat arrangements, all helped to make up a most refreshing holiday. After the dinner-tea, which took place in the aviarv,&rdquo; Mr. Hadley (in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Gray and Mr. Chaytor for their*care and trouble in ordering the proceedings of the day) took occasion to allude to the Rifle movement, and strongly urged the students to take their places in the ranks of the College Company now in course of formation. The thanks were duly voted and ac&not; knowledged, but the party were altogether too happy to indulge in speech-making, and proceeded to enjoy the beauty of the summer evening. A vigorous game of у rounders &rdquo; in the park, finished only by the coming twilight, and a visit to Saffron W alden made the hours ίΐ&gamma; merrily along till ten o&rsquo;clock. After a taste of the Walden ale, the word was given, &ldquo;fours, quick march,&rdquo; for a return to the station, and a lively thiee-mile tramp, lightened by music and the moon, closed a very happy day.
