JULY 1, 1860.,COUNTRY COLLEGES, HOW THEY MAY BE ESTABLISHED.,For some time past efforts have been making in Lancaster to establish a &ldquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,&rdquo; or an institution of like aims and purposes. Various meetings and conferences have been held, chiefly with the desire of coming to an arrangement with the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute now existing in the town. The speeches and discussions at these meetings, which are fully reported in the local papers, have been of great interest, the main topics being the desirability or otherwise of mixing up work and recre&not; ation, the best way of introducing the social element into such an insti&not; tution, and the comparative advantages or disadvantages of Free Libraries supported by rates and by voluntary subscriptions, etc.,The establishment in provincial towns of Colleges similar to those now in existence is a matter of vital interest. If this cannot be carried out we shall have to acquiesce in the theory that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; can only live and thrive in some great centre of population and industry, where a number of peculiar circumstances conspire to make the experi&not; ment successful. The questions mooted in the course of the agitation at Lancaster are just of the kind which will probably occur on the attempt being made in any manufacturing town of moderate size. The following extracts from the reports of the proceedings of our Northern friends will therefore be interesting to our readers, and valuable to any who may feel inclined to try the experiment of founding a College in other places.,A correspondence has passed between Mr. Solly and Mr* Maurice (Principal of the London College). Mr. Solly writes as follows :&mdash;,&ldquo; There are several gentlemen here interested in the education of the working classes, and who are desirous of doing something next.winter to promote it more efficiently by , having adult classes taught the subjects taken up by your &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. On the other hand, there are a number interested in the Temperance movement, and who are asking the question that comes now from all parts of the country, * How are we to provide rational amusement and improving occupation of an evening for the persons whom we persuade to give up the public-house ? &rsquo; ... I am almost sure that the true organization for the purpose is the one you and У оцг fellow-workers have so admirably inaugurated in London. But it is only within a very recent period that it has occurred to me that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; was the society by which reclaimed drunkards might be suitably entertained and educated (and by which, even, drunkards might be reclaimed). I had always thought of the College merely as an institution for promoting the education of the more intelligent, skilled, and steady artisans. But you have invari&not; ably based it upon the idea of Brotherhood, and maintained that we must revert to the original fundamental conception of the College as a Society, a fellowship for mutual help in the great purpose of life, viz. education&mdash;education of the whole man, physical, intellectual, moral, and spiritual. And if this be the true idea, as I am sure it is, cer&not; tainly it cannot be beneath, or wide of, the function of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; to* seek to bring within its influence the more unfortunate members of the Working Class, and to use such agencies as shall be suited for their less advanced or perverted condition, thus bringing them on by degrees to appreciate the Opportunities of ffiighęr culture* Will you tell me if I am right in thinking, therefore that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,VOL. II. t b
might legitimately,&mdash; i.e., according to its true conception, and the views of those earnest men who in various parts of the country are promoting these Colleges,&mdash;have time and rooms devoted to rational amusements, chess, draughts, etc., readings of poetry and fic&not; tion, cheap concerts, recitations, etc. (even a newspaper room ! though the reading news&not; papers is too often a miserable waste of time), by way of attracting and occupying those who have hitherto spent much of their leisure time in the public-house, always of course with the view of leading them in due times and seasons to higher and more improving occupations ?&rdquo;,Mr. Maurice, in his reply, said :&mdash;,&ldquo; I cannot see the least reason why you should not establish a College of which it shall be a main object to secure amusement and occupation for those who will otherwise fre&not; quent public-houses. The more various the methods and organization of Colleges are, provided the principle of fellowship for a moral end is kept in sight, the more is the principle likely to be recognized. , . . Experience appears to show that there is a dan&not; ger of Colleges or Institutes for amusement sinking into mere concert clubs or danciug clubs. But against that, you will of course be on your guard.&rdquo; Mr. Maurice added that &ldquo; they iu Loudon should strongly deprecate, however, the idea of prescribing formu&not; las for other Colleges.&rdquo;,At a Conference held in the Council Chamber, at which were present the Committees of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute and Temperance Society, and others interested in the scheme under consideration, Mr. Solly referred to a resolution passed at a late meeting of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, to the effect that &ldquo; amongst the best means to secure &lsquo; the profitable employ&not; ment of the leisure hours of apprentices and artisans,&rsquo; was &lsquo; especially the formation of adult classes in history, literature, political economy, and sciences generally, which persons of education are earnestly invited to as&not; sist in conducting.&rsquo;&rdquo;,He observed that &ldquo; although the Lancaster Mechanics&rsquo; Institution had done much good in various ways, there could be no doubt that both here and elsewhere these Institutions had been far less successful in attracting and benefiting working men than the class just above them. Coupling with this, the want experienced by the teetotallers, of some pro&not; vision for the entertainment and improvement of those whom they have reclaimed, the question had occurred to him whether the friends of Temperance and Education could not co-operate more closely than they had done. Mr. Solly spoke of the interest which had been excited in the minds of many friends of the working &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;, by the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, from the first meeting at Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s house, at which he had been present ; and then referred to the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; which had since been established in Halifax, Manchester, Wolverhampton, Oxford, Cambridge, Boston, etc. . But these admirable iustitutions had not succeeded in bringing working men together to the extent that was desired, and one reason he believed to be that they had not suffi&not; ciently aimed at attracting the large class who now frequent the public-house. One of H.M.&rsquo;s Inspectors of Schools, who had taken a lively interest iu the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; at Manchester, had recently expressed to him the same view, and authorized him to give it as his (Mr. Morell&rsquo;s) conviction that these Colleges &lsquo; have not gone low enough.&rsquo; Having some doubt whether the originators of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; would consider such a variation from their own programme as was now contemplated in the light of a development or a perversion of their views, he had written to Mr. Maurice, who had re&not; plied in the terms above cited. Mr. Solly therefore thought the Temperance Society on the one hand, and the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute on the other, might co-operate and form a College in which they could combine some modes of amusement with advanced classes, not by amalgamating the two societies, but by friendly co-operation. He also referred to the importance of the corporate principle, out of which all true civilization had sprung, and upon which it was based, and he ventured to say that working men some&not; times betrayed a truer instinct in going to a public-house for the sake of fellowship than in simply joining classes for self-culture.&rdquo;,At a second meeting, which was public, and attended by about 700
working men, Mr. Solly, at the request of the Committee, gave a further explanation of the views of the promoters. He said that&mdash;,&ldquo; After complimenting the Chairman on the spirit in which he had introduced the subject to the meeting, he went on to say that there did appear to be some reason for that meeting, because&mdash;since the working classes had in the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute such a spacious building, such good class rooms, and such an excellent library&mdash;why did they not avail themselves of the advantages thus afforded them ? What the promoters of that meeting wanted to know was why the operative classes did not avail themselves of the facilities for instruction afforded by the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute to a greater extent than at present. He would, as succinctly as he could, set forth some of what he thought the reasons why their Institution, and other institutions of a moral and educational character, did not succeed in attracting to them that number of the working classes which they ought to do. No one could deny that those institutions did a great deal of good, but they might do inore. (Applause.) Now, what working men wanted was not merely opportunities for acquiring a good edueation, but also for social intercourse and brotherly fellowship as well as for that mental improvement which sprung out of one mind coming in contact with another. Many gentlemen who had studied at the universities of Cambridge and Oxford, and who knew the advantages offered by them, had maintained again and again that the principal benefits derived from those educational institutes, were the facilities they afforded for friendly and intellectual intercourse between the stu&not; dents who studied at such places. It was equally important for members of the work&not; ing classes that they should have the same means of mental cultivation. Many persons attended public-houses who were influenced in visiting them not chiefly by the love of drink, but for that social intercourse and brotherly friendship of which he had just spoken. No institution, he felt convinced, could succeed unless they presented such facilities for social intercourse. They should not provide institutions merely for work, but for amuse&not; ment as well. All work and no play was proverbial for making Jack a dull boy. (Ap&not; plause.) People worked all the better for intervals of relaxation. Now, it was for those assembled on that occasion to consider whether these combined advantages could not be obtained by judicious organization. A great deal had been done by the temperance movement towards effecting moral and social reform&mdash;by men who had conquered habits of sensual and debasing enjoyment. Yet the advocates of temperance constantly complained of the absence of all recreative facilities as a great hindrance in the way of persons giving up the habits of which he spoke. What the working classes wanted was a place of intercourse and fellowship. But why, it might be asked, did a working man not spend his time at home ? Should he not do so ? Undoubtedly ; no one was more urgent on that point than he (the speaker.) But the Secretary of the Temperance Society had told them that the wives of working men had asked that some place of amusement miMit be provided for their husbands and sons. If a working man heard a good lecture or concert &middot; played a game at cricket or chess&mdash;thus passing a pleasant hour&mdash;the greater was th&eacute; probability that he would make his home happy. He (the speaker) could not see why they should not afford means of recreation in connection with the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute lie could not see why, as a member of the committee had suggested very sensibly, there should not be a room in which the members would be at liberty to enter into conversa&not; tion. The rules of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, as at present in existence, required silence but would not it be possible to have a conversation room for those who wished for an interchange of ideas ? He thought it would not be unadvisable to have coffee and re&not; freshments in a mild way&mdash;embraced in the new scheme. In the evenings it would be very pleasant, after the day&rsquo;s labour, to have a game at cricket, quoits, skittles, or any other healthy sports. (Applause.) It had been suggested that a field should be taken and converted into a gymnastic arena, where the members could assemble of an evening Then there was music-and they were all aware of its elevating and refining influence&#39; He believed also that readings from Shakspeare, Dickens, and other celebrated authors would be very attractive and useful to the artisan classes ; for often when a man was to&eacute; tired to read himself, he was not too tired to hear any one else read to him (Applause ) Any society, however-and this was the main point&mdash;having for its object either educa&not; tion or amusement was inevitably doomed to failure if the members of such institution became connected with it solely with the view of getting all the benefit they could from it. There must be a more generous spirit displayed than that&mdash;members should join,i 3
with the intention of giving as well as taking. The chance of a society being useful and successful was in proportion to the prevalence of a brotherly spirit among the members. It was not right to join any institution or society founded for mental and moral im&not; provement and recreation merely with the seltish view of taking all and giving nothing. This has been a main cause of the want of success in movements both for education and amusement intended to benefit the working classes. Now he apprehended what the present meeting wanted to find was the common ground which would induce that bro&not; therly spirit. He believed they would find it in efforts for mutual improvement and mu&not; tual recreation. (Applause). But for that purpose it did not do to form an institution out of one particular class. Class distinctions should be thrown down in an enterprise of this kind, and they must all be bound together by their common sympathies with each other in a great cause, by their common interest in the improvement and happiness of all the members. (Applause.) Those who could boast of education ought to be willing to impart their knowledge to others, and they would find themselves gainers as well as those whom they instructed. Those also who take part in a discussion class, who give a recitation, sing a song, or play a game at draughts, would be giving valuable help ; and those who have only their company and good humour to give, should give that, and they too would thus be doing their part in mutual helpfulness. All would find that they could give more and more by degrees. (Applause.) The rev. speaker, after dwelling on the desirability of the different classes thus co-operating together in the cause of educa&not; tion and recreation, proceeded to say that one of the speakers, at the meeting held in the Town Hall a fortnight previously, had very properly alluded to the importance of get&not; ting youths under good and useful influences before they were led away by others less worthy. Now, could anything like successin this direction be attained without bringing into action the principle of brotherly association ? These youths needed some kindly fellowship of an elevatiug nature into which they could be received as they grew up not mere classes and lectures. Now if the working men were willing to form a society which should be established for the purpose of affording mutual help and assistance in the important objects above referred to, he thought it might be attended with very great success. (Applause.) He fully believed there was every disposition on the part of those who had it in their power to assist the working classes to do so, and the question for consideration was, whether it would not be best done by developing old institutions, and reviving the brotherly spirit which ought to animate them. He did not want to set up a rival institution to the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, but to help to form a society for mutual improvement and recreation, which should carry out more thoroughly the objects for which that house in Market-street had been bought, and in a spirit of hearty fellowship, for the benefit of all classes. He thought that could best be done under the name which had been adopted in London, Manchester, Halifax, and many other places where such societies have recently been formed, viz., a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. . That name &bull;implied brotherhood for noble purposes&mdash;it also implied brotherhood in which the work&not; ing man had to take a prominent part.&rdquo;,The result of the various meetings and conferences is, that on the 22nd June a body of about fifty or sixty working men formed themselves into the proposed society, accepted an oiler of co-operation made by the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, chose officers, and approved of some rules. At the adjourned meeting, fixed for the 29th, it was expected that many new members would enrol themselves. The name &ldquo; College&rdquo; has not been assumed as yet, nor is the point pressed by those who desire that the society may ultimately take that title.
REASONS FOR A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,BY HENRY SOLLY, MINISTER OF THE ENGLISH PRESBYTERIAN CHAPEL,,LANCASTER.,What is a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ?,A society or corporation for systematic education, mutual improvement, and recreation, in which the benefit of working men is especially aimed at.,Why should such a Society be called a College ?,Because that is a name many hundred years old in this country, and expresses better than any other a brotherhood or fellowship of grown-up persons united together for the mental and moral improvement of each and all.,Why should such Colleges be established ?,Because the means hitherto adopted for carrying on the educational and rational recreation of the Working Classes have been insufficient for that purpose.,What is the constitution and government of these Colleges ?,That depends on the views of those who form the College. But the simplest plan is to have a council, consisting of the voluntary teachers and of certain persons in addition, elected annually by the rest of the Society.,This Council should arrange for the classes, lectures, meetings, rational amusements, and all other plans necessary for carrying into effect the ob&not; jects of the College.,There should be some small annual subscription (perhaps 2s. 6c?.), pay&not; ment of which, and signature of the Rules, would constitute any person above a fixed age a member of the Society, with a small payment in addition for attendance on each class.,All persons forming such a College should have an equal voice in arranging its constitution, and in appointing the members of the council by which it is to be governed.,The value of cordial union in a permanent society is shown by its effi&not; cacy in promoting other subjects than education and amusement. The co-operative societies at Rochdale and Leeds for instance, and the various Benefit clubs over the country. Then again how little strength or power for permanent usefulness would be found in Christian churches, if the members of them merely came together once a week to join in worship and listen to a sermon. But when they form a Christian brotherhood, a true fellowship for the promotion of each other&rsquo;s welfare and of the great objects for which their church exists, they gain that wonderful power which the genius of Wesley foresaw when he formed his organized societies.,Mutual Improvement Societies are excellent things in their way, but they do not provide that systematic teaching and those regular classes which are needful for the real education of working men. Moreover, it is not merely mutual teaching which is required by them. Some working men in every town, many in some towns, are prepared and anxious for more advanced and systematic teaching than they could afford one another ; and which it is hoped persons of education would be found willing to impart, if students could be found thankful to receive it. There is the more reason to hope for this, because nothing so improves and deepens a man&rsquo;s own
knowledge of any subject as the benevolent effort to impart that knowledge to others. Only by teaching others can we complete our own education.,Female classes should of course be formed in connection with these Colleges, where practicable, as they have been in cases where efficient female superintendence and tuition could be obtained.,Th&eacute; advantages then to be gained by a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; over Me&not; chanics&rsquo; Institutions are,&mdash; the permanent organization of learners and teachers in a brotherly fellowship for education and recreation , attracting the humblest working man by rational amusement and instruction given in a form that he can enjoy, and inviting the more educated artisan by affording the higher culture which he requires ; while at the same time all are expected to give as well as to receive. The advantages of such an organization over Mutual Improvement Societies and Recreation Societies are, the systematic and continuous instruction (and the certificates which may be given that such education has been received) as well as the provision for rational recreation, all which can be afforded in their most efficient forms only when persons of different classes and attainments unite together in friendly co-operation for these important purposes.,Societies established merely for promoting what in itself is a most im&not; portant object, viz. rational recreation, fail from not being connected with higher objects, and from not being able, therefore, to develope the brotherly and corporate principle.,In an organization intended for the benefit of persons of all sects and no sect at all, it is obvious that there must be no specific form of belief put for&not; ward as the basis of the Society ; but inasmuch as there can be no true brotherhood without the recognition of a common Fatherhood, and no per&not; manent efficient organization for a moral purpose without the virtual confes&not; sion of a divine order on which all human associations should be grounded, it is believed that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, without presuming to intrude upon the belief or non-belief of any person, must be essentially religious, alike in its foundation, constitution, and practical working.,Lastly, if such organizations as are now spoken of should be established throughout the country, under the common name of Working Men&rsquo;s Col&not; leges (which seems probable), they will derive considerable support from the common sympathies which will unite them as Colleges in the same corporate and brotherly spirit which should inspire the individual members of each College. Certificates of proficiency might be granted after annual examin&not; ations, which would have a distinctive value in various ways. Recom&not; mendations and introductions, afforded by one College, would be a ready passport to a welcome and position in another, as well as in society at large. Ultimately, perhaps all the Colleges might be embraced in the higher organization of a University.,What do working men want ? is a question which the promoters of a Col&not; lege must often be wishing to get answered. An interesting attempt has been made, by the promoters of the movement in Lancaster, to ascertain as nearly as possible the actual wishes and wants of the working men of that town. Four hundred papers of queries were distributed ; of these 149 were returned filled up, and from them has been made the following Abstract of Returns from Working Men, showing that&mdash;

The following are interesting specimens :&mdash;,&ldquo; I do not like the idea of singing songs and smoking in a reading-room. I would have a separate room on purpose for to smoke.&rdquo;,&ldquo;Did belong to the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, hut did not think there was justice shown to the working classes ; if there had been, there would have been a good body of working men enjoying that Institution.&rdquo;,&ldquo; Providing there was a room for reading, songs to be sung, chess, drafts, etc. ; I object to tea, coffee, ginger-beer, and lemonade, because it would make it same as a public-house.&rdquo;
&ldquo; If we obtain and attend these places of recreation, what have the fair sei to do ? Have they to be left at home by themselves ? They appear to have been entirely over&not; looked; can&rsquo;t there be no means provided for their instruction and amusement as well ?&rdquo;,&ldquo; Let us have a Free Library if we can get it, but let it be free from taxes.&rdquo;,&ldquo; Classes for History, French, Chemistry, and Geometry, have been tried and failed ; working men do not seem to appreciate them.&rdquo;,&ldquo; I would not resort to such a room as you mention. I think it would sink to some&not; thing little better than a beer-shop or gambling-room.&rdquo;,&ldquo; I have no desire for a Free Library. I think the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution fairly used offers the working classes all they need require. I know there are gentlemen connected with that place who will very gladly give every facility to the useful studies. I think an effort should be made to secure the youth of the town from a Street Education , and you will then stop the feed-pipe from whence flows the evils complained of.&rdquo;,&ldquo; Thackeray is an author of whom the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute never heard.&rdquo;,&ldquo; I do not see that the men of Lancaster have become children again, that they want to play at toys.&rdquo;,&ldquo; We (four in number) have, three of us, belonged to the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute at different periods of time, and think it contains all we desire, if we felt inclined to go ; the subscription is merely nominal ; if it was quite free, we do not think it would add one more reader. We, as a sample of working men, not one night in twenty feel inclined for anything except a little light reading or taking a short walk, which the abundant supply around our native town ought to satisfy any reasonable man. We do not approve of your amusement-room. We think we know what all these things create a taste for, having experienced the same, but there are parties who think you have made a capital hit. A better thing could not have been thought of&mdash;a combination of a public-house, temperance coffee-house, gambling-house, and singing and dancing-rooms. We do not see the use of a recreation field. After a hard day&rsquo;s work we feel very much inclined to sit down quietly and rest ourselves, but there are men who would like a little innocent wrestling, or the manly art would be very desirable.&rdquo;,&ldquo; I would suggest that the funds for the maintenance of the library, etc., be raised by voluntary subscription, as I am of opinion that it would be supported better by the working classes than it would be by compulsory taxation.&rdquo;,&ldquo; I think all the amusements you have suggested would be better attended if the early closing movement could be brought about.&rdquo;,&ldquo; I object to Works of Fiction as a nuisance to a complete library.&rdquo;,&ldquo; Would not attend any institution where smoking was allowed.&rdquo;,&ldquo; All my time is devoted to my dear wife. I have plenty to do at home, without reading fresh books.&rdquo;,&ldquo; My opinion that freer the place the more frequenters.&rdquo;,&ldquo; 1 think the whole of your suggestions are uncalled for. Suppose I was to indulge a little in four of them, they would cost me 4j. per quarter, or 16j. yearly, which neither me nor any other working man can afford to pay if he will do justice to his family. What 1 and a major part of the working men want is a free access to the Mechanics Library and reading-rooms. This, I think, can be accomplished by the proposal of a penny in the pound, which I am ever willing to pay.&rdquo;,The popular objections to educational movements could scarcely be put in a more trenchant form than in these &ldquo; ipsissima verba &rdquo; ot the persons chiefly interested. They show most forcibly that any attempts to make working men students, without treating them as men, i.e., with a full understanding of all their social and domestic circumstances, must utterly fail. Ought they not also to suggest some serious questions to those who would have the nation give new and large political responsibilities to a vast class who confess that their education as citizens is only just be&not; ginning. Nothing certainly can be more rational, than that a working man should find a short country walk or a pipe a better conclusion to a long day&rsquo;s work than a couple of hours&rsquo; study of history or grammar ; but
till this state of things is somehow or other altered, to invest such a man with the power of saying his &ldquo; yea&rdquo; or &ldquo; nay&rdquo; to great political questions, would be as absurd as to ask an Englishman of ordinary capacity to elect a Professor of Sanscrit or an Equity Judge. Our College can have no higher ambition than that of giving working men a right and reasonable claim to a share in the responsibility of guiding the destinies of the country ; and any means that contribute to this end&mdash;cricket and the &ldquo; manly art,&rdquo; or geology and the &ldquo; Principia&rdquo; &mdash;they may legitimately employ.,HINTS FOK POOE STUDENTS.,BY A SELF-TAUGHT PLOUGHBOY.,At a meeting of a &ldquo; Mutual Improvement Discussion Class&rdquo;&mdash;Subject : Which is the best way for a Working Man to obtain a Knowledge of English Literature P&mdash;the follow&not; ing remarks were made by one of the Class.,. . . Unfortunately there are many amongst those who have their bread to earn by the sweat of their brow that are not circumstanced as those I have been describing. Probably an inferior education, a want of money, or a want of sufficient leisure, or perhaps all three, may stand as barriers against the attainment of knowledge, and yet there may be the inclination, strong and powerful, and yearning for knowledge.,It is this part of the question which I particularly intend to endeavour to grapple with, viz. to advise as to the best way to make the best use of a limited leisure, with an inferior education, and a limited amount of spare money, in the study of English Literature.,1 will suppose a young man who has two or three hours to spare each day after his toils are over, and who can read a little, but has no spare money at present.,I would say to such a young man, although there must be some uphill work for you, yet do not despair, for experience assures me you may yet succeed if you have the inclination and will to persevere, and after havın&raquo; got through the first part it will not be a task but a never-failing fund of amusement, and will lead you from things low and grovelling to things noble and elevating.,You must begin as some very eminent men have begun ; you must im&not; prove yomself in reading by going to a night-school and persevering at home, and at the same time endeavour to scrape up a small sum of spare money, for you will require a few particular books to be always ready at your right hand, which you cannot have and keep always there, out of any circulating library. J,While you are learning to read let your books be literature of the first class, so far as outlines go, say outlines of History, Philosophy, Political Economy, Geography, etc.,While this is going on you must at the same time endeavour to obtain a knowledge of English grammar, for without knowing the grammatical form
of expression and the arrangement of words, you can never definitely know what an author means by what he says. This is very important.,The learning of English grammar you will find rather an irksome, un&not; interesting task at first, but commence with one of the first grammars, such as the one entitled * Mary&rsquo;s Grammar,&rsquo; and at the same time you are learning you will be drawn on by the amusement it will give you. Never mind, though you may think it seems adapted for a child ; the simpler the better for self-instruction.,We will now suppose that you can read pretty tolerably, that you have a tolerable knowledge of English grammar, and that you have accumulated a small sum of spare money.,You must then lay out this money in a class of books which will be of such general use to you that they may almost be called the keys of English literature. Buy yourself a good explanatory Dictionary, so that you may always come at the true meaning of any word which is new to you. The one which I have used is Johnson&rsquo;s quarto stereotyped edition ; but I am told there are better authors at the present day. Next buy a good Bio&not; graphical Dictionary. The one I use is Maunder&rsquo;s, price 10s. Also pur&not; chase a good Gazetteer, so that whenever you meet with the name of a place which you are not acquainted with, if it be one of any note, you can find out all about it.,And lastly, if your means will allow you, purchase Maunder&rsquo;s &lsquo; Scientific and Literary Treasury,&rsquo; which embraces an extensive range of subjects in Literature, Science, and Art; the price is 10s.,These are all books of reference, and let them be always at hand ; and now choose your branch of literature to commence with&mdash;Jet us suppose it to be English history. Get Ince&rsquo;s ť Outlines of English History,&rsquo; price Is., read it carefully through twice or three times, or until you perfectly understand it and can retain the principal events in your memory, as well as the memorable men recorded in its pages, as to whom you will be able to learn particulars by referring to your Biographical Dictionary.,You are now acquainted with an outline of the wars of your country, an outline of its mercantile rise and progress, an outline of the rise and progress of its Christianity and its literature, a list of the literary and other eminent men who have flourished and died, and you are now in a proper position for going into more details.,You must now connect yourself with the best circulating library your means will afford, and that which before has been laborious will now be&not; come an exhaustless recreation. You will have at your command the de&not; tails of the life of almost every writer whom your history points out to you, and the greater part of his very writings themselves, and you can read, mark, compare, and leisurely digest them.,At this stage I would advise you to read Shakspeare&rsquo;s historical dramas and Bulwer Lytton&rsquo;s * Harold, the last of the Saxon kings.&rsquo; They will set before you the manners and customs of the people, and the character&not; istics of the principal men of their respective times more intelligibly than the best written histories ; but be careful not to read fiction before you have read the facts, and when fiction has no facts for its foundation never read it at all.,I would strongly recommend well conducted mutual improving discus-
sion classes as great aids to acquiring knowledge, and also visits to the sites of great events* such as those of battle-fields, antiquarian remains, etc., where perhaps more of the reality can be obtained by half an hour&rsquo;s survey than by a week&rsquo;s reading.,Hitherto I have only alluded to English history, but 1 think the same plan may be successfully followed in all, other branches of English litera&not; ture generally. Be careful to have a good knowledge of the outlines ba&not; fore you proceed to voluminous reading. &lsquo;Outlines&rsquo; are now published very cheap, and some of them particularly good, in almost every depart&not; ment of English literature. Inoe&rsquo;s &lsquo; Outlines,&rsquo;* so far as he has published, are the best I know of for self-instruction.,Except so far as I have already advised*&rsquo; I would not recommend any particular or systematic plan, only always to werk in that particular vein of literature which most captivates your mind for the time being. This will make your researches no task, but a pastime, and your mind will be led to soar amongst things far more noble than can possibly fall to the lot of the street-saunterer or beer-tippler.,What I have now advanced is no imaginary plan. It is the real expe&not; rience of my own Kfe. Do. not for a moment, think that I am under the presumptuous impression that there is no better plan. &ldquo; In th&egrave; multitude of councillors there is wisdom,&rdquo; and I hope that this plan, in going its round amongst the succeeding speakers* maybe altered and remodelled until it becomes the; very b&ccedil;şt.,It would be a great step, in advance to; know which is the greatest agent towards promoting a taste for reading. &laquo; Wąs I asked my opinion, J would say a well-conducted mutual improvement discussion class. For instance, if since our class Commenced we could calculate how many useful volumes have been searched through and pages read in political economy&raquo; philosophy, and. English history, and which otherwise would, not have been read, 1 believe t&igrave;bie result would be an astounding one, and would add to the deserved reputation for usefulness which the class now enjoys.,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt; VOLUNTEERS.,The London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; has seen fit to take up the Volun&not; teer Movement. It has, by forming a Rifle Corps, announced its&#39;belief that the business of such a Corps has some relation to the business Of a College. It is on this groirnd, and not because a Volunteer Review has furnished the material for the week&rsquo;s gossip, that I venture to devote a page or two of this Magazine to the expression of a few thoughts on this subject. If these thoughts were merely speculative, I should not think it,* [Sec No. 4 of the Mag. for a list of the blonders in the * Outlines of English His&not; tory a few of them have been, corrected in a later edition. Miss B&ccedil;ale&rsquo;s little v Text/ Book * (BeD, &amp;amp; Dalby, 2s.) is a very intelligent, &agrave;nclj so far as I can judge, a trustworthy summary of facts and dat&eacute;s. Using Ince&rsquo;s &lsquo; Outlines of French History* the Other day, 1 found an average of three oť four stupid mistakes&#39;in every page. Why cannot some reasonable person with a little leisure devote himself to the remedying of this disgraceful deficiency in the simplest instruments of education?* - &Epsilon;&eta;.] :
worth while to put them in print. They have arisen from a consideration of the facts of the case, and they are meant to have a practical issue.,It will be quite superfluous to discuss here the question whether the Rifle Movement is a good thing or not. The points I wish to submit to the serious consideration of the College and of its friends are these:&mdash;1st, How far the formation of a Rifle Corps has helped the College ; and 2nd, How far the College has helped the Rifle Movement. As to the first, we shall be compelled to allow that the corps, setting aside for a moment its own merits, has actually damaged the College. In one or two ways it may have been of a slight advantage. The notoriety of its first establish&not; ment recalled a few old students who had quitted us ; and it is possible that among those of its members who have been attracted from outside, there may be some who will prove valuable additions to the ranks of the College. But against these small items of gain have to be set some very serious losses. The experience of the last few months has proved that the everyday practical work of the College and of the corps do not get on well together. The central idea of the College work is study, that of the Corps is, for the present, drill. The spare hours of our students, as of all busy men, are limited, and in the conflict of Study v. Drill, the study has had to go to the wall. The classes have been languidly attended &mdash;some have dropped for want of students, and some for want of teachers. Irrespectively too of the mere sacrifice of time, the distraction caused by the introduction of я new and noisy occupation, absorbing for the time the attention of all engaged in it, has upset the equilibrium of the College, It has borne the appearance for the last few months of two institutions rolled into one, a Drilling School and a College ; and, as might have been expected, the noisier element has overpowered the quiet one. Further, the Rifle Corps has seriously altered the constitution of the College by introducing into it a large number of nominal members, who have in re&not; ality nothing to do with the work of the place. That all this has been doing harm to the College is 1 think sufficiently evident. To come to this conclusion it is not necessary to settle whether student&rsquo;s work or volun&not; teer&rsquo;s work is the worthier or more important, or whether volunteers are likely to be better or worse men than students. It is sufficient to see that soldiering and study are not taken up from the same set of motives or by the same set of men, and that this diversity must tend to disintegrate the College. Nor do I want to suggest a doubt whether the establishment of a corps was not an object for which it was worth while to incur these dis&not; advantages. All I desire is that we shall look our sacrifices in the face, and see if the object cannot be carried out without further serious damage to the College. The corps was originally a part of the College. As its members then stood up in the ranks, they had the consciousness of being bound together by a strong previously existing tie. Perhaps in no corps that has been formed could the feeling of mutual confidence have been more spontaneous or hearty. This feeling ought to have made it the best * corps in existence, and no doubt has helped to make it as good as it is ; for how should not a man work better and fight better among men with whom he has already been doing other work and sharing other struggles. Our first mistake, therefore, was in not taking measures to secure that the corps should be composed of such as had been, or would become, real mem-
bers of the College. The admission of others, however, was allowed with another view, in expectation of a result the probability of which we must now give up for reasons explained below. These two evils&mdash;the extension of the corps beyond the ranks of the College, and the intrusion of the business of drilling on the business of the College&mdash;might be at once re&not; moved or mitigated by determining henceforward that the present strength of the corps shall not be increased except by the addition of bond fide students, and by obtaining the use of another parade-ground. The first measure might be easily carried out by the officers of the corps. The second would not be difficult if the suggestion I have next to make can be carried into eftect. Some such arrangements as these I believe to be posi&not; tively necessary. If the present relations between the two bodies continue, before another year is over, the College will have become simply a rifle corps, with certain educational classes attached to it. If any one* thinks this a hasty judgment, I can only say it is one formed after some years&rsquo; in&not; timate knowledge of the place, and confirmed more and more by daily observation. Coming now to my second question&mdash;How has the College helped the Volunteer Movement ? I am afraid we shall again have to give but a sorry answer. A great, a very great opportunity has been missed. The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; is, so far as I know, the only body in London which had any claim to take the lead in organizing the action of the class which it represents. The Trades&rsquo; Unions are founded for purposes too special and restricted to allow of their assuming this prominence. Let us consider what we might have done or tried to do by making the most of this position. When the volunteering began, it was called a national movement. It is still so in name, not in substance. Eegarded as the visible expression of but a strong and noble feeling among certain classes, the Ее view of the 33 rd of June was a great spectacle. The sight of those steady battalions tramping along in seemingly interminable lines, might fairly excuse the p&aelig;ans in which we have ever since been singing their glories. But to any one who could think of what he did not see, as well as what he did, that spectacle proved clearly enough that the movement, though within certain limits a proud success, has been na&not; tionally a failure. The vast class, or aggregate of classes, which we have been accustomed to call &ldquo;the people&rdquo; was not there. Some two or three companies, originated by the public spirit of a few well-known employers,* besides our own and the Scottish Artisans, were the only re&not; presentatives of the working class, their total number perhaps a fortieth of the entire force. Had this class sent its fair quota of volunteers, the sight would have conveyed something like the impression of a nation in arms. And what short of this should content us ? That mighty host, loosely numbered at 30,000 men, has melted down in the official return to 18,500, of whom thirteen thousand came from the Metropolis. Does any man in his senses call this a force commensurate with the enormous popu&not; lation of this province-city? Why, it does not give a man to each street We can only say, as did the Emperor Nicholas on a like occasion, &ldquo;This is very beautiful, but where are the people ?&rdquo; The resources of the whole country cannot be reckoned more than seven or eight times that of the London district. This would give a present available volunteer force of about 100,000 men. A fine army this, no doubt, but something very dif-,* Messrs. Spottiswoode ; Truman, Hanbury, and Co. ; and Silver.
ferent from the &ldquo; household guard &rdquo; one million strong, that was to have been, and that ought to be. The hindrances hitherto have been many. It is partly true, no doubt, that the operative class would naturally be longer than the ranks above them in realizing the feeling of personal responsi&not; bility which impels a man to assume a share in the defence of the country. This would be to them a newer and a stranger lesson than to those whose social rank makes them more sensibly conscious that they have some, however little, personal influence. Much too must be set down to the de&not; liberate prejudice on the part of men of other classes against the admission of working classes into the force. Foolish people talk as if their dignity would suffer by serving alongside of their poorer fellow-citizens. And many look with a nervous dread on the idea of arming those whom tra&not; dition has taught them to consider the dangerous classes. Dangerous truly they were, in days when physical force was almost their only appeal against cruel and oppressive legislation. Now that they have no famine&not; breeding corn-laws to complain of, who shall say that the men whose bread depends most immediately upon the security of capital, have not a &ldquo; stake in the country &rdquo;? But the great obstacle to their joining the Eifle Move&not; ment is without doubt the mere material one of pecuniary cost. If a working man cared about the thing, he would give up as readily as any one else his two or three hours a week : but is it fair to ask him to pay &pound;3 or upwards,&mdash;say two weeks&rsquo; income,&mdash;for the privilege of serving his country. Would the Inns of Court have numbered 600 men if each had to bear at starting an equal proportionate expense, say from &pound;15 to &pound;50 ? On this point, no doubt, we should have to fight a great battle against t he dandiaca l vanity inherent in human nature. The Volunteers have been tlTesTaves of tradition and the tailors : none more so, I am grieved to say, than the Committee of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; corps. In spite of the clearest evidence that a serviceable dress could be got for one half or one third of the price, they determined that their uniform should cost &pound;3. Now, before the real working men can be expected to take part in the movement, they must be relieved from the apprehension of this absurd tax. That it is absurd and irrational any one may satisfy himself by inquiring into the matter. You may get a well-made uniform of light brown &ldquo; drill&rdquo; (a mate&not; rial which will outwear two suits of cloth), with any amount of red trim&not; mings, for &pound;1. This would be exactly of the colour and style most admired at the 5 Ее view. Or why not take to* &ldquo; corduroys&rdquo; at once ? that would be a rational and noble uniform for the working men of England. It would be sufficiently evident, one would think, without quoting facts or argu&not; ments, that a reasonable uniform cannot put a man to more than a few shil&not; lings&rsquo; real expense. For is not drilling and fighting a kind of work, and a work that can be done in any dress that is fit for any other kind of work ? Would not the ordinary dress of a bricklayer carry him through a cam&not; paign ? It only remains then that a thousand men shall agree that the next coat and trousers they get shall be of the same colour, and in six months they will be in uniform. But no ; men have been accustomed to see a certain sort of cloth tunics about the streets, on soldiers&rsquo; backs ; and without that cut, and that material, they think nothing can be done. The greatest soldier now living is fighting in a red jersey : 800 red jerseys have cleared an island of 18,000 &ldquo; regulation&rdquo; jackets. Could not Englishmen fight in red jerseys too ? Another feeling which has to be conquered is the
detestable flunkeyism which shrinks from the confession of inferiority sup&not; posed to be implied in a cheap dress. But this (learnt from the upper classes) is surely not invincible. Its prevalence only points out how much work there is yet to be done by &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. Let then this material obstacle be once overcome, and working men will be found eager enough to assert their citizenship. None have a stronger sympa&not; thy than they with popular freedom. Have they not glorified Kossuth and Garibaldi ? and would they shrink from sharing in a work which would be really the greatest aid to national freedom throughout Europe ? Tell them that England, when unassailable at home, will no longer be obliged to re&not; main a tame and cautious spectator of the greatest work done in Europe since 1789 ; tell them that the strength of this country is, as matters now stand, the best guarantee for the progress of liberty abroad. Let themunder- stand this, and there is no reason why, in a short time, at least one-fourth of the 4,662,000 men in this kingdom between the ages of 18 and 45 should not be efficient volunteers. England would thus be really unassailable : every hedgerow would be alive with rifles, and invasion would cease to be even dreamt of. Let this be tried, steadily and seriously. Let our little corps form, say three companies, retaining their old name and dress in the Working Men&rsquo;s Regiment. Let them be called, if they like, the &ldquo; Col&not; lege Companies,&rdquo; and be composed of actual students only; and let fresh Companies be formed from the general mass of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; of Lon&not; don. The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; will thus be taking its right share of a great national duty, without sacrificing the objects for which it exists. The 19th Middle&not; sex may then be the first of a long array of like regiments, famous for good drill and steady shooting, rather than gay uniforms and musical prome- na( ^ es &middot; R. B. Litchfield.,CORRESPONDENCE.,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGES&lt;/span&gt; AND TEMPERANCE SOCIETIES, ETC.,Lancaster, June 14, I860.,Dear Sir,&mdash; My last letter was not meant for publication, but I am thankful you printed it, since it has called forth the very interesting letter given in your number for this month. For we are in the midst of an agitation here which, I think, must lead to good results, and among them to the formation of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; in the autumn ; hut at present our aim is simply to bring men together for rational recreation : this is the thin end of the wedge Now the main point I have been urging is just that on which your correspondent dwells with deserved emphasis at the close of his letter, viz. the spirit of brotherly kindness and helpfulness, which is an essential element in the idea of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; but which you will never be able to evoke in a Mechanics&rsquo; Institute. But this great principle does not need a number of educated men to establish and work it out in practice. It is on this ground that I desire to see the great Temperance movement linking itself with these Societies, so far as encouraging their formation and persuading their converts to join them. The glory of that agitation has been the brotherly spirit which from the first has characterized it, but its weakness has been the want of educational and recreative agencies for the men and women it rescued from the slavery of drinking habits. These are just what &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; can supply. Was not one established there ?,NEWS.,London &mdash;The annual Summer Party took place on Wednesday, the 27th June. Our readers will recollect the account of a similar gathering given in the Magazine this time twelvemonth. Of the general aspect of the festival it will be enough to say, in the words of an ancient historian, that it was &ldquo;.like the last, only more so.&rdquo; The only additional feature was the mixture of the Volunteer uniform with the gay colours of the young ladies The weather graciously permitted a resort to the parade-ground (late garden), where the first portion of the music was sung. The choir was composed of members of College singing classes, assisted by various friends, most of whom had given similar y kind help on a pr&eacute;viens occasion. For the success of the music the party were chie y indebted to Mrs. Dickenson, who had kindly entertained the choir at a rehearsal, and to Mr W Evans (the well-known inventor of the English Harmonium), who was good enough to undertake the troublesome duties of accompanist The singing continued till nearly eleven o&rsquo;clock, but al fresco tea-drinking and miscellaneous merriment deferred Lill nearly midnight the dispersal of the party.

AUGUST 1, 1860.,THE SECOND EEPOET OE THE MANCHESTEE WOEKING,MAN&rsquo;S COLLEGE.,(Read at the General Meeting of the Council and Students , held Friday, July 6,1860.),The General Eeport of the Manchester Working Man&rsquo;s College has hitherto been presented at the Anniversary of the opening of the College in January, but this year it has been postponed to the present time, in order that, for the future, we might not be required to review the events of detached portions of two sessions. This arrangement greatly increases our present task, since we shall have to look back beyond the limits of the present Session as far as the spring of 1859, and record the varied fortunes of the College during the greater part of two academic years.,During this period, the number of students actually attending our classes, although fluctuating slightly, has, on the whole, greatly dimi&not; nished,&mdash;the total number of students in each Term, as shown below (Table I.), being 137, 133, 89, and 94.,Table I.,Total Number of Students.,Members of Mechanics&rsquo; Institution .,Other Students.,Occupations of Students :&mdash;,Operatives.,Clerks, Warehousemen, etc. . . .,Shopkeepers and Assistants. . . .,Teachers and Librarians.,Miscellaneous.,Session 1858-59.,Session 1859-60.,Second,Third,First,Second,Third,Term.,Term.,Term.,Term.,Term.,137,128,133,89,94,43,33,27,15,15,94,95,106,74,79,40,40,36,23,26,78,60,81,59,54,15,17,12,6,8,4,5,4,3,4,&ldquo;,&mdash;,2,The falling-off in the second Term of Session 1859-60 was greater than at any previous time. It is satisfactory, however, to feel that this large defection from our ranks was not so much due to want of appreciation of the studies of the College, as to the widely-spreading impulse of the rifle volunteer movement. It was found impossible to form College companies like those so energetically raised in the London College ; and the small VOL. II. K
amount of leisure enjoyed by our hard-working students would soon be absorbed by the steady and regular drill required in the Manchester regi&not; ments. When the first hard rudiments of military discipline needed in the service of the country have been acquired, we hope to welcome back our old members, strengthened by their bodily exercises for the mental training of our classes. Another cause of the falling-off in this (the second) Terra was the temporary discontinuance of the Logic Class. The Mathematical Classes generally have been poorly attended ; and for the last two Terms the Geometry Classes have not been formed, and the Mechanics and Natural Philosophy Class has also been discontinued. It is a matter for great regret that these subjects, so well-approved as instruments of mental culture, should be so little estimated by the working men of Manchester. We are glad to report, that in the last Term the Logic Class was again formed, under the
direction of Mr. Walker, Head-Master of the Manchester Free Grammar School. An English Grammar Class, under Mr. Newth, was commenced this Term, and numbered twelve members, although it had not been an&not; nounced in the prospectus. It promises well, and may be expected in future Terms to assist a much larger number of students. At the begin&not; ning of this Term, in consequence of the strong wish expressed by many of the students, an unsuccessful attempt ivas made to form a class in Geo&not; logy. Mr. E. W. Binney, however, kindly conducted about thirty of our members to the Geological Museum in Peter Street, and afterwards took them a very enjoyable excursion to the neighbourhood of Collyhurst.,The attendance in the several classes has varied very much, but during the past Term has averaged seventy-five per cent., showing an improvement upon that of the two previous Sessions, when it was sixty-five per cent. Con&not; sidering the engrossing nature of the occupations of many of the students, this proportion must be considered as very remarkable. The classes which show the highest average are Greek, Advanced Latin, Chemistry, and English Grammar. It con&not; sists of representatives elected from each of the classes ; and thus while it supplies a ready means of communication between the Council and the students, it also forms a strong bond of union between the several parts of the College.,Since its election it has greatly assisted the Council in deciding against the formation of a Volunteer Rifle Corps in connection with the College, in ascertaining causes of diminution in our numbers, and in obtaining the feeling of the students upon several other subjects.,At the suggestion of this Committee two very important additions to the social element of our College have been made. An Essay and Discussion Society has been established, and a Cricket Club is in process of forma&not; tion. Both these measures have the consent and cordial support of the Council, and as the chief impediment to the completion of the latter scheme is the want of a suitable ground, we would appeal to those friends who live upon the outskirts of the town to help us in obtaining for our doubly hard-working students, that recreation and manly exercise which is so essential to the healthy prosecution of their studies.,The question of the removal of the College to a separate building has lately occupied the attention of the Council. It is a step of much im&not; portance to our future career, but from the hearty support which the measure receives from both the Council and the Students&rsquo; Committee, we
trust that it may be carried out before the commencement of another Session. Among other important advantages which would arise from its accomplishment, we may mention that it would render possible those more intimate relations with the Ancoats Working Man&rsquo;s College which are so desirable for both.,The course of study required for the Associateship of the College has at length been decided upon, and will be appended to the Report. Although the amount ol knowledge which will be required in order to pass the exami&not; nation is not small, we trust that several of the students will be able to try tor this honour next Christmas, and those who succeed in obtaining it will give proof that they possess the elements of a sound and liberal education. It is from among these well-tried men that we may look for much future help, both on the Council and in carrying on our other work. Lookin g back over the two and a half years that the College has been m existence, we cannot but remark with gratitude, that although much less m numbers than when we started, our College seems, at length, to be Se down into a strong and healthy condition, and is answerino&middot; the expectations with which it was founded. The novelty which at first lent a fic-
titious strength to our undertaking has worn away, and those who now seek us, we know to be animated by a sincere desire to submit to real instruc&not; tion, and to improve as much as they can the talents which have been en&not; trusted to them.,We fear that some misapprehension as to the objects of the College still exists in the minds of many.,The College was not intended to inform working men upon the special subjects of their trades, nor to assist them to be better workmen except so far as this followed, as it assuredly would, from their being better men. These objects are provided for, and would be better carried out, in the Trades&rsquo; Schools and Mechanics&rsquo; Institutions.,It does not profess to give the earliest rudiments of education, since these may also be obtained elsewhere, and would not require the organiza&not; tion and high degree of instruction offered at a College.,We do not propose to take working men from their own proper sphere of duty, nor to give them a distaste for their employments by mere orna&not; mental acquirements, but, on the contrary, we expect that as they advance along the pleasant but difficult paths which lead to knowledge, they will learn the true worth and dignity of their calling, and will fear to debase it by any act unworthy of their guild.,We hope that the institution, now fairly started upon it course, may in process of time grow up into a Working Man&rsquo;s College, worthy of the name; that it may be a Christian brotherhood&mdash;a body , with many mem&not; bers ; men brought together by the common objects of systematic study, united by the firm bonds of good fellowship, and animated by a hearty zeal in the cause of their Alma Mater.,Peculations,Respecting the Associateship of the Manchester &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt;
