WHAT TO LEAEN: A PLEA FOE LATIN.,BY THE REV. J. B. MAYOR, FELLOW AND TUTOR OF ST. JOHN&rsquo;S COLLEGE,,CAMBRIDGE.,To the Editor.,Sir, &mdash;I am glad to see the question of a general course of study for working men discussed in your Magazine. I quite agree with Mr. Lush- ington that this question involves a larger one, the consideration of which can no longer be deferred, viz. What is the best course of study, not for this class only or for that, but for all ? That the Universities themselves are not satisfied with the course required for their Pass is, I think, evident from the great alterations which have been, and are still being, made both at Oxford and Cambridge ; and attention has been drawn to the same question in the country at large from the necessity of framing schemes for the so-called Middle Class or Local Examination as well as for lectures in &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;.,At present, however, I wish to confine my remarks to that part of Mr. L.&rsquo;s paper in which he discusses the advantages of the study of Latin to a working man. I was for sometime teacher of a Latin Class in the Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College at Cambridge, and must confess that I estimate the educational value of this study somewhat more highly than he appears to do. Perhaps I shall state my own view of the matter most clearly if I suppose the case of a man of fair abilities, who has had no regular training since he learnt his &ldquo; three E&rsquo;s &rdquo; at school. In these he has the necessary instruments of knowledge, and since that time he has probably acquired some notions about Politics and Divinity of a more or less practical nature. Now, if he comes to a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, he finds classes in history, grammar, natural sciences, languages (ancient and modern), mathe&not; matics, philosophy, music, and drawing, etc. : which of all these is he to join ? It seems to me that what such a man especially needs is, first, to have his sympathies widened, in order that if his own sphere of life should be narrow or comparatively uninteresting, he may add to it an interest drawn from larger spheres of action or of thought, and thus learn to form a fairer estimate of classes or individuals removed from himself. For this purpose, history affords far the best training; and I think that if the lecturer takes care to connect his subject with geography, i. e. with the physical influences which act upon nations ; with religion, politics, and law, showing the moral and spiritual influences which act upon nations ; and finally with political economy, exhibiting the prudential laws of national development ;&mdash;if thus treated, no subject is of such primary im&not; portance to the student, whether in the higher or lower ranks of society. Next to this we require some study which shall give accuracy of thought. No doubt, mathematics will have this effect, but I think experience shows that the mathematician is apt to draw too broad a line of demarcation between the deductions of science and the reasoning which is required in common matters : in the one case the conclusion follows from the premisses as a matter of absolute certainty, in the other it seems to him a toss-up whether things are true or not. He has not been trained in that reasoning
on probabilities, which is by far the most important instrument for the pur&not; poses of life and of study also, excepting in the case of the exact sciences. Another objection to mathematics higher than arithmetic, for one who can only devote a small portion of his time to study, is that they are not immediately useful : they do not assist him in his everyday employments. There is, however, a study which, equally with mathematics, teaches clear&not; ness and accuracy of thought, and which, moreover, deals with probable evidence, and is immediately useful in speaking, reading, and writing. This is the study of grammar, including the analysis of sentences and the art of composition.,If it be granted then that these two subjects, viz. history and grammar, have the first claim upon the student, to what is he next to turn his attention ? I think that this is a point which should be determined by the tastes and circumstances of the individual. Thus a man may join a music or a drawing class either because he has a natural taste for tliem, or because his situation in life makes it desirable that he should be able to sing or draw : the same with chemistry or geology, or with trigonometry or astronomy, or finally with French and Latin. Phil&ocirc;sophy (by which I understand the study of some philosophical book, say Butler&rsquo;s Sermons, or Herschel&rsquo;s &lsquo;Discourse on the Study of Natural Philosophy,&rsquo; under the teacher&rsquo;s direction) appears to me to be in itself more important than any of the subjects just mentioned; but practical interest may often fairly turn the scale against it. In some quarters there seems to be a reluctance to grant this. It is thought a slight to Learning to own that it must bend to Working. On the contrary, I believe it to be a great misfortune in any station of life that the intellectual interests should stand aloof from the practical pursuits. It is an unnatural disunion which kills both one and the other. We want thought and knowledge to give interest to practice, and practice to give life and reality to thought. Each man&rsquo;s work, till it is seen in connection with some ennobling idea, must appear mere useless drudgery, and each man&rsquo;s speculation will end in idle dilettanteism unless it can find an issue for itself in work.,With this preface, then, I proceed to the special case of Latin. As&not; suming that the working man is acquainted with history and grammar, under what circumstances will he be justified in giving his precious hours of leisure to the study of Latin P The first thing to be asked is, Will ii do him good in his business ? Will he become a more skilful workman and receive higher wages in consequence P I put this frankly forward because I believe it is in the first place hopeless, and, secondly, wrong, to urge a man to disregard money consequences. That many make money the only consideration is too true, but that is no reason why we should affect to deny that it is and ought to be an important consideration with all. Now in some employments, e. g. that of the bookseller or printer, there is no doubt that a man is a better workman for knowing Latin. This then is, in my opinion, a sufficient reason for his learning it. But will he, beyond this directly practical advantage, gain any higher indirect advantage in mental training, such as may set his conscience free from any scruple of having bartered away the better for the worse, the birthright lor the mess of pottage ? I belief he will in many ways. Three have been noticed by Mr. Lushington : &ldquo; Latin,&rdquo; he says, &ldquo; may be studied, 1st, as a gramma-
tical exercise ; 2nd, as explaining the meaning of English words ; 3rd, as illustrative of English literature.&rdquo;,As regards the first point, I suppose few would deny that it is a great help to a clear conception of the science of grammar to have learnt to dis&not; tinguish between accidental idioms and fundamental laws of speech by a comparison of one language with another. This is a help, however, which is granted rather to the advanced scholar than to the beginner. That specified by Mr. L. is chiefly available for beginners ; it is the advantage ottered by an inflexional language as affording a sort of &lsquo; memoria technica * of the modes and relations under which facts may be viewed. A third advantage which I should mention under this head is the more lively curiosity which we experience when we are at work upon an unfamiliar subject ; the satisfaction of feeling our progress from day to day where the memory is exerted pari passu with the reasoning powers ; the absence of confusion when we are introduced to a system without any conflicting pre&not; possessions connected with the systematized subject-matter.,These advantages attach in some degree to the study of any foreign language, but more particularly to Latin, from its greater fullness of in&not; flexions. A further advantage appears to me to have been somewhat understated by Mr. L, He seems to think the knowledge of derivations of little interest unless we can trace each word back to its ultimate root, and he considers that in this respect German affords at least as important a key to the treasures of our language as Latin. On the contrary, it seems to me that it is at present impossible even for the most advanced philologist to be certain that he has reached the ultimate root of a word ; and even if it were possible, I maintain that that part of the history of an English word which ascends higher than the Latin is to an Englishman far less interesting and important than that which begins with the Latin. Everybody knows Coleridge&rsquo;s remarks about the use of a hunt after an etymology ; and if we take the Dean of Westminster&rsquo;s books as supplying instances of such a hunt, I think we shall find that in the majority of cases a knowledge of Latin only is required. But even without starting upon a hunt of this sort, I think a Latin scholar of very small pretensions has a great advantage over a mere English scholar, from his almost unconscious perception of the meaning of derivative forms as well as of the root-mean&not; ing which underlies large families of words. Take, for instance, the words derived from traho , jacio, rogo, struo. If we compare English with German, we do, it is true, discover interesting analogies, but we do not commonly arrive at the parent word : we only meet with relations equally removed from the parent with ourselves. And even if we had reached the parent word, the relationship discovered would still be less interesting than in the case of an Anglicized Latinism, because the Teutonic portion of the English language has undergone far less change of meaning than the por&not; tion which is derived from the Latin. Almost all our Latinisms in their transit to English have passed through the crucible of the imagination, and come forth with a metaphorical colouring upon them, and this is another reason why our words are so much more picturesque and definite to one who knows Latin than to one who is ignorant of it. The word &ldquo; meretri&not; cious &rdquo; may serve, for want of a better, to illustrate my meaning.,With reference to the use of Latin as illustrative of English literature,
Mr. L&rsquo;s objection is much the same as that which I have just endeavoured to meet. He thinks it is not worth while tracing your descent halfway. You must be content to be terree filius , if you cannot prove the ancestry of a god. And he thinks the connection between the English mind and the German more important than that between the English and the Roman. Now, without going fully into this subject, I may refer to the fact which will be conceded by all, that the style of many of our best known English authors, such as Milton and Johnson, has been very much influenced by their knowledge of Latin : not only has this determined the framework of the sentence and the selection of the words, but the minor ornaments are often made up of allusions to the writings of Latin authors. Nor can it be said that this is merely the taste of a by-gone age. Take up a number of the &lsquo; Saturday Review,&rsquo; or a novel of Thackeray&rsquo;s, and you are sure to meet with passages which are entirely without meaning to the English reader. To quote one which I happen to remember ; what could a reader think of &ldquo; the atrocious mind of Newdegate,&rdquo; in the &lsquo; Saturday,&rsquo; a few weeks back, if he could not recall the line of Horace of which it is a parody ? In such cases as these, in almost all cases where the form rather than the matter of literature is concerned, a knowledge of Latin is all that is required to appreciate the author&rsquo;s meaning. As to the question whether German or Latin has had the most influence upon our literature, it seems to me almost ludicrous to make any comparison between them. The one literature has become partially known to us during the last half-century, through the writings of Coleridge and Carlyle, and far the larger part of the Germanism of style or thought which can be discovered in English books may still be traced back to these two as to their source. Latin for fifteen centuries supplied Western Europe with a common language, literature, and law, and still forms the staple of education to the literary class in England, so that I believe a quotation from Horace or Juvenal would be more familiar to most men who have been brought up in an English public school than a quotation from Milton.,It only remains for me to say a few words with reference to the intrinsic worth of Latin literature. No doubt it is far below the Greek in originality and depth ; but is it so clear that Trance at any-rate could produce an orator to match with Cicero, a historian equal to Tacitus, or poets superior to Virgil, Horace, Lucretius, and Catullus ? Even if it were true that the moderns had the advantage in loftiness of tone and philosophical spirit, still might it not be of service to many who looked with a certain suspicion upon the commonplaces and conventionalities of this nineteenth century, to compare them with the opinions which prevailed among a people as highly educated as ourselves, yet existing under such widely different con&not; ditions ? Mr. Hales suggests that translations should be used for this purpose, and I think the suggestion a very good one ; yet, as he owns, the translation rarely preserves the style of the original, and amidst the pre&not; tentiousness, self-consciousness, and sentimentalism which disfigures so many modern works, I do not know whether it may not be the manly, unaffected style, full as much as the excellence of the matter, which com&not; mends to us the study of the ancient authors.,I have been chiefly occupied in controverting Mr. Lushington&rsquo;s opinions, but in much that he has said I cordially agree, particularly in his con-
eluding advice to the student, not to take up Latin merely because it has been the fashion in our old schools, but to set clearly before himself what advantage he may hope to derive from its study, and at what cost of time and labour. No doubt some would find that the Latin Class was not the one best suited to themselves ; others, I hope, would only be confirmed in their belief that in their case the study of Latin would be productive of equal pleasure and profit.,ART INSTRUCTION TOR THE PEOPLE.,Mr. Buskin&rsquo;s Evidence before a Barliamentary Committee.,The recent Blue Book on Public Institutions contains much valuable evidence. Among the persons examined were Mr. Maurice and Mr, Ruskin. The examination of the latter gave rise to a lively discussion on the effects of competition between foreign and English workmen, and the relative positions of the working class in this and other countries. The following extracts from Mr. Ruskin&rsquo;s evidence will interest our readers :&mdash;,1592. Have you any other suggestions to offer, calculated to enlighten the Committee on the subject entrusted to them for consideration ?&mdash;I can only say what my own feel&not; ings have been as to my &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; [at the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;]. I have found particularly that natural history was delightful to them ; I think that that has an especial tendency to take their minds off their work, which is what I always try to do, not ambitiously, hut reposingly. I should like to add to what I said about the danger of injury to chefs- d&#39;&oelig;uvre ,&mdash;that such dangers ought not to he allowed to affect the great and unreplaceable works of the best masters ; and those works, I believe, are wholly valueless to the work&not; ing classes. Their merits are wholly imperceptible except to persons who have given many years of study to endeavour to qualify themselves to discover them. But what is wanting for working men is historical painting of events noble, and bearing upon his own country: the history of his own country well represented to him; and domestic pathos brought before him. Nothing assists him so much as having the moral disposition de&not; veloped rather than the intellectual, after his work ; anything that touches his feelings is good, and puts new life into him ; therefore I want modern pictures, if possible, of that class which would ennoble and refine by their subjects. I should like prints of all times, engravings of all times ; those would interest him with their variety of means and sub&not; ject ; and natural history of three kinds, namely, shells, birds, and plants ; not minerals because a workman cannot study mineralogy at home ; but whatever town he may be Ш, he may take some interest in the birds, and in the plants, or in the sea shells of his own country and coast. I should like the commonest of all our plants first, and most tully illustrated ; the commonest of all our birds and of our shells ; and men would be led to take an interest in those things wholly for their beauty and for their separate charm, n-respective of any use that might he made of them in the arts. There also ought to be, for the more intelligent workman, who really wants to advance himself in his business, specimens of the manufactures of all countries, as far as the compass of such institutions would allow. 1,1596. Are you of opinion that it would be conducive to the general elevation of the people m this country, if our works of art and objects of interest were circulated more expeditiously and more conveniently than at present throughout the various manufacturing districts ?&mdash;I think that all precious works of art ought to he treated with a quite different view, and that they ought to be kept together where men whose work is chiefly concerned with art and where the artistically higher classes can take full advantage of them. They ought, therefore, to be all together, as in the Louvre at Paris, and as in the Uffizii at г lorence, hut kept separate from the collections intended for the working classes which may he as valuable as you choose, but they should he useable, and above all things so
situated that the working classes could get at them easily, without keepers to watch what they are about, and have their wives and children with them, and be able to get at them freely, so that they might look at a thing as their own, not merely as the nation&rsquo;s, but as a gift from the nation to them as the working class.,1674. In your experience, in the last few years, have you not seen a very marked im&not; provement in the working classes in this country in every respect to which you have alluded ; take the last twenty years, or since you have turned your attention that w r ay ? &mdash;I have no evidence before me in England of that improvement, because I think that the struggle for existence becomes every day more severe, and that, while greater efforts arc made to help the workmen, the principles on which our commerce is conducted are every day oppressing him and sinking him deeper.,1695. It is your conviction that we may look upon this vast extension of trade and com&not; merce and competition, altogether as an evil?&mdash;Not on the vast extension of trade, but on the vast extension of the struggle of man with man, instead of the principle of help of man by man.,1697. You did not intend to cast a slur upon the idea of competition?&mdash;Yes, very distinctly. I intended not only to cast a slur, but to express my excessive horror of the principle of competition, in every way; for instance, we ought not to try to grow claret here, nor to produce silk ; that we ought to produce coal and iron, and that the French should give us wine and silk.,1699. Each country producing that which it is best fitted to produce?&mdash;Yes, as well as it can ; not striving to imitate or compete with the productions of other countries. Finally, I believe that the way of ascertaining what ought to be done for the workman in any position, is for any one of us to suppose that be was our own son, and that he was left without any parents, and without any help ; that there w r as no chance of his ever emerging out of the state in which he was, and then, that what we should each of us like to be done for our son, so left, we should strive to do for the workman.,NEAVS.,Lancaster .&mdash;The Working Men&rsquo;s Society now (22nd July) numbers 570 members. From the Rules, which are here subjoined, it will be seen that the Society is at present a different thing from a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. Its objects, however, are thoroughly consistent with the purposes for which we desire to establish Colleges. It aims at meeting wants equally pressing and equally legitimate, though somewhat different in ex&not; tent and in kind. Among the large numbers who have shown, by joining the Society, their desire to have these wants supplied, a certain proportion will, we hope, be found whose chief object will be systematic study. How best to meet this demand the pro&not; moters of the present Society will then be able to determine. In the meantime all members of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; must wish prosperity to this spirited and energetic movement.,&ldquo; KULES OF THE SOCIETY.,&ldquo; Object .&mdash;The mental and moral improvement of the members, and the promotion of rational amusement and recreation.,&ldquo;Principle &mdash; Every member shall consider himself engaged to promote these objects for his fellow-members and himself, as far as lies in his power.,&ldquo; Means .&mdash;A field for out-door sports in the summer-time should be taken for the use of the members, and a room for conversation, discussions, recitations, vocal and instru&not; mental music, chess and draughts, and other rational entertainments throughout the year. Classes both for mutual improvement, and also, when practicable, under the instruction of skilled teachers, should be formed during the winter half-year, and the delivery of lectures of an improving and interesting character. Concerts should also be given to the members if the funds of the Society permit, and excursions into the country for all the members be arranged once or twice in the year. The collection of geological and botanical specimens and objects of natural history should also be promoted, and members invited to take rambles together in search of them. Games of chance, matches for money or money&rsquo;s worth, betting, gambling of any description, and all intoxicating drinks are strictly prohibited, both in the field and in any rooms used by this Society.
Discussions on controversial theology and party politics are also excluded from the meet&not; ings and classes of the Society.,&ldquo; Constitution. &mdash;The affairs of the Society shall be conducted by a president, vice-pre&not; sident, treasurer, secretary, and committee. The latter to consist of thirteen members, six of whom, with the above-named officers, shall be chosen half-yearly,&mdash;the officers to be members of the committee ex officio. Five to form a quorum. All are to be elected by ballot, and to be eligible for re-election. They shall represent, as far as possible, the different trades, factories, workshops, etc., in the town, and shall undertake the whole management of the Society and arrangements for the classes, lectures, recreations, etc., above mentioned. An appeal may be made from any decision of the committee to a spe&not; cial general meeting of the members, but otherwise such decision shall be final.,&ldquo;A special general meeting must be summoned by the committee, at a generally con&not; venient time and place, on the requisition of at least ten members. A week&rsquo;s notice of such meeting must be given, and the object stated in the notice. The half-yearly meet&not; ings shall be held early in January and July each year, when the treasurer and committee shall present a report of what has been done in their respective departments during the year.,&ldquo; Subscriptions. &mdash;That the subscriptions to this Society for working men above the age of 18 shall be Is. per quarter; from 14 to 18 years, 9 cl. per quarter; for boys from 8 to 14 years, 6 d. per quarter. But these last can only be admitted on the recommendation of two adult members. Subscriptions shall be considered due on the 1st January, 1st April, 1st July, and 1st October, and must be paid in advance.,&ldquo; Alteration in Constitution .&mdash;The constitution of this Society can only be altered, either in omission or addition, by a vote of two-thirds of lhe members assembled, either in an annual or special general meeting.&rdquo;,London. Munro, and Serjeant Reed. &rsquo;,CORRESPONDENCE.,OUR VOLUNTEER RIFLE CORPS UNIFORM.,[The article in our last has evoked more than one reply. In obedience to the rule &ldquo;Audi alteram partem,&rdquo; as much of Mr. Preston&rsquo;s note is here printed as refers to the question of cost of uniform. The rest is omitted for want of space.]
Dear Sir, &mdash;As to your saying that a uniform of light-brown &ldquo;drill&rdquo; (query &ldquo;twill&rdquo;), costing &pound;1, would be exactly of the colour and style most admired at the review, I can&not; not think that even you seriously mean this. Why, the cheapest belt and pouches would cost six shillings ; and then there is to be a coat &ldquo; with any amount of red trimming,&rdquo; a pair of trousers ditto, and a cap ditto (by the way, I never heard of such a thing as a corduroy cap), for fourteen shillings ! If this could be done, I think Petticoat Lane would no longer be the place to get the cheapest clothes ; and what the poor tailors would get for making this unique uniform I dare not venture even to conjecture. As to jerseys, Garibaldi&rsquo;s may suit him very well when in action iu Italy, but when he is out of action he wears a uniform as tight fitting and as well braided as any other General. We shall never have to fight out of England ; and a jersey in such wet weather as we have had this last summer, would have held water enough to wash the wearer in when he got home. I say again, you have not experienced the advantages of our uniform, and can but have listened to those who were of the same opinion as yourself regarding it, or you would not have written against it in such depreciative language. Go into our corps, and ask them all round if they would rather change uniforms with any other corps in exis&not; tence. You will be able to count the ayes on your fingers. I am proud of our corps. Have not the men iu high places already set their eyes on us? We were the tail of a battalion at the review, but we carried our tail so gracefully, that at the sham-fight we were at the head of a battalion, and had the honour of beginning the battle. So we in&not; tend to progress ; and as far as drdl and shooting go, we will be second to none.,I am glad to see that the Artisan Company of St. Luke&rsquo;s, Soho, now part of our corps, have had enough of your dandy in them to adopt our uniform, with the exception of the stuff being a tritie coarser. St. Anu&rsquo;s, Soho, comes to us in our uniform com&not; plete, and so does the anything but dandiacal Price&rsquo;s Company.,I will close this skirmishing sort of letter with a word in favour of a band. I am afraid, by your allusion to &ldquo; musical promenades,&rdquo; that you would object to a band if we had one ; but I will ask any one who marched past the Queen on the 23rd of June, if we should have had it said of us that our &ldquo; step was as regular and front as accurate as if it had been ruled by a line,&rdquo; had it not been for the glorious strains of &ldquo; Bonnie Laddie&rdquo; which the sjdendid bands played the while ? We march tolerably without a baud ; with one w r e shall inarch as a man We can march now for eight hours and not complain of being tired ; with a band, if only of drums and fifes, we shall never be weary.,I am. Sir, your obedient Servant,,July 22 &amp;gt; rd . We called it a &ldquo;College,&rdquo; because the subjects learned resembled what were taught at colleges ; we attached no deeper meaning to the word. No Additional Fee.,SUBJECT.,French {Elementary) French {Advanced) Arithmetic,TUTOB.,. Mr. Lawrence.,. Mr. Lawrence.,. Mr. South, C.S.,The Council has adopted the following Resolutions :&mdash;,1. The College will be ultimately divided into Six Classes.,2. The First will consist of the general body of merely Matriculated Students.,3. The Second Class will consist of Certificated Students, or those to whom at the end of any course shall be awarded a Certificate of Competency iu the subject of the course.,4. The Third Class will consist of Scholars (in Art, History, Language, Mathematics, Physics, etc.), or those who prove themselves competent in any one branch of the College Studies.,5. The Fourth Class will consist of those who are thought worthy to be Associates, or permanent Members of the College. From these we require competency not only in some specific study, but in the most important studies of each department of the College. Each student will have an opportunity of answering questions in those subjects in which he takes most interest; but he will be expected to prove that he is acquainted with (1) the Bible History, (2) the History of England, (3) the Principles of English Grammar, and the Elements of (4) some pure Mathematical, and (5) some Physical study, or some branch of applied Mathematics, as Mechanics. The object will be to ascertain that he has the knowledge of those subjects which every person educated in a College ought to have ; not to elicit his opinions either in Theology or Politics. The Associate must have attended the College for Eight Terms.,6. The Fifth Class will consist of Fellows : these will be chosen out of the Associates. The Fellows will take part in the education of this College, and may assist in establishing and carrying on other Colleges. They will be chosen by the Council, after due con&not; sideration of what they have done in the Associate Examination, and of what they have been since they entered the College. Moral qualities, a capacity for teaching, and a will&not; ingness to teach, will be at least as indispensable as proficiency in any branch of study.,&amp;amp; 7. The Sixth Class will consist of the Council. It is intended that the Council be hereafter recruited from the Fellows, to the extent of at least a third of its members. The Council will be prepared to assist in the formation of new Colleges, in London or other parts of England.

SEPTEMBER 1, 1860.,WHAT SHALL I BEAD?,&ldquo; &quot;VMiat shall I learn?&rdquo; is a question several aspects of which have been discussed in these pages, &ldquo; What shall I read ?&rdquo; is a still wider and more important query ; wider, because it involves a greater variety of choice, and more important, because reading is the chief instrument of self-teaching ; and self-teaching constitutes the greater part of every education worthy of the name. At first sight the aggregate of literature bygone and con&not; temporary seems so vast as to defy any attempt at a rational selection. Let an ordinary man be turned loose into a great library ; let him burrow for a day or two in one corner of it and realize the amount of solid con&not; tents of the few yards of books before him, then let him wander for a week more along the miles upon miles of loaded shelves, piled high with various lore ; let his eye scan the endless lines of titles, names of unknown authors, writing on unknown subjects, in for a great part unknown tongues. Let him see all this, and if he is a person of common sensibility, he will feel oppressed and awed by the spectacle ; perhaps, too, a little saddened. He will at least begin to think, that for a man to set up as a person of &ldquo; general information &rdquo; is the most pitiable of impostures : and so it is. The amount of fact, let alone speculation, amassed by the continuous labours of thinkers and writers on the various special subjects of human investigation is so enormous, that to obtain even a general view of its growth and distribution is the work of a lifetime ; and the further this attempt is carried, the more will the mind be made to feel that the known is something infinitely small in comparison with what might be known, and that the knowable must always remain a mere nothing in comparison with the unknowable. In the presence of these infinitudes of impossibility, what is a man to do ? Well, he is brought back to the question, What&rsquo;s the use of it all? Let him face the cui bono problem honestly; out of its solution will come an answer to his query, &ldquo; What shall I read ?&rdquo; The use of all learning and knowledge is to help men to lead healthier, wiser, and happier lives. The only good general purpose for which books are written is to add to the sum ot human interests by recording the thoughts, experiences, and discoveries of passing or past generations. Of the things so recorded a large proportion will be ot merely special and temporary interest ; things useful to be known by men of particular countries and particular callings, or at particular dates, but otherwise of little account, A large propor&not; tion again will represent the progress made by successive generations in acquiring a scientific knowledge on certain subjects, any one of which may, lor many good reasons, be worthily made the employment of a life&not; time, but can have only an indirect interest for the general mass of man&not; kind. Anbther large portion of literature might be placed in a miscella&not; neous category, comprising all that of which the chief value consists in its,VOL. II. T
agrecableness, the aggregate of the productions of ordinary travellers, or&not; dinary poets, and reviewers, essayists, and litt&eacute;rateurs generaliv. Making these immense deductions, we come to a residuum consisting of all that is of solid permanent interest to men as men. In using these words I know that I am adopting what, to some ears, may sound like a cant phrase ; we seem to have been hearing it so often of late ; but \ve must stick to it nevertheless, as embodying the radical principle of all right social action and social progress.,Every age has produced one or more men whose lives and thoughts have coloured and moulded the lives and thoughts of the rest of their ge&not; neration, or of the generations following ; men of whom one can say, that if they (or some small knots of men of whom each was a type and centre) had not lived, the after-state of the world would have been in some mate&not; rial points different from what it has been ; men who have been the originators or main directors of great movements, who have created or given shape to great ideas, who have made their own wills and convictions tell upon the wills and convictions of their fellow-men; men who have given the clearest or fullest utterance to the feelings and passions of their time, or still more to the great ruling thoughts and aspirations of the race in all times. These are the men whose works stand few and far between on the shelves of our libraries, but have really been the seeds of that huge mass of intellectual product, the vastness of which seemed so distracting. They constitute that residuum, small in bulk but priceless in value, every bit of which is, or ought to be, interesting to everybody. It is here surely that we must look for that which will give the most interest to our lives, which will deepen our best sympathies, stimulate our best energies, and make our relations with the world about us altogether better and happier. The more we are straitened for time, the more important is it to seize at once on that which is solidly valuable, and in the highest sense &#39;practical ? Most of all should working men, if they want to make their education of any use, take this course. The men whose works I point to as those which ought to be read, whatever else we read, and, for the most part, before we read much else, are few, very few, in number. We may see this by a glance at the literature of our own age. Of the huge mass of current literature, how little has any chance of lasting ! how few men whose individual vis viva has affected or is affecting the condition of things about them ! Taking our own country, we might perhaps name half-a-dozen of such authors ; say, for instance, Carlyle, J. S. Mill, Tennyson, Euskin, Maurice. Of any of these it may be said, that if he had not lived, the thoughts and tenden&not; cies of our generation would have been in some material particular and degree different from what they are. In the works of these men and a very few more, taken in connection with the advance of physical science (an ad&not; vance so quiet and spontaneous that the results of individual effort seem to merge in one broad sweep of irresistible progress), the future historian will be able to trace the chief features of the physiognomy of our time.,A succession of similar groups represents all that is of any value in the literature of the past. The full catalogue of such names would not be a long one ; and not even all of these need make a great demand on our re&not; verence. Pope and Dryden were &lsquo;representative men/ but a rational being might go through life without opening their works, and not be much
the worse for it. Not to have read &lsquo; Kobinson Crusoe&rsquo; or the &lsquo; Pilgrim&rsquo;s Pro&not; gress,&rsquo; on the other hand, would be a decided loss. All that is in this sense greatly valuable in English literature might, I believe, be put into a hun&not; dred volumes, and of these some twenty would be equal in value to all the rest. And if great works in special departments of knowledge were in&not; cluded, such as great histories and the like, I scarcely think the amount would exceed two hundred volumes. This would probably turn out to be a very liberal estimate, for I have now behind me a bookcase containing certainly a large proportion of the books which have been of any impor&not; tance to the world from the Iliad downwards, alongside of much of less value (such as the London Directory, and several theological works), and these occupy only five shelves of about nine feet long.,To students in our colleges, therefore, and all others in like circum&not; stances, I would say most earnestly, &ldquo; If you want to make your reading pay (for we can&rsquo;t be too sternly utilitarian in this matter), read the greatest men of the greatest ages .&middot;&rdquo; who they are, it is not hard to find out. They are the men whom the common voice and the common heart of mankind,,&ldquo; The proof and echo of all human fame,&rdquo;,have confessed to be greatest, the men whose thoughts become pro&not; verbs, whose names are household words among the nations. By the very nature of the case, the lasting preciousness of what is not new is apt to be forgotten amidst the buzz of contemporary notorieties. But for this it would be superfluous to urge the point. That it is not superfluous, that men do not read the books whose names they talk of so freely, I know, as I think most of my readers must know, from personal observation. At a recent examination of a grammar class in one of our colleges a piece of Hamlet was read to each candidate as a subject for some questions : not one knew where the passage came from* And I am sure this could easily be paralleled in many a circle of so-called educated people, lor pi oof, watch the audience (pit or boxes) at the acting of a play of Shakespeare, and see if it is not obviously new to all but a very few. And how often does one see a railway traveller, unable to bear the fatigue of an hour of his own thoughts, buy in despair, and go to sleep over, a shilling&rsquo;s- worth of bad fiction, when Macbeth or &lsquo; As You Like It &rsquo; would very pro&not; bably keep him awake, and be perhaps quite as novel ! Bead then, by all means, the immortals of any age. Let us be quite sure that whatever has deeply moved the sympathies, stirred the hearts, and won the affections of thousands of men and women before us, must have in it that immortal something to which our nature too must equally respond.,But a man may ask, &ldquo; How am I to find the similarly most precious portion of the liteiature of the day ? Here are twenty thousand publica&not; tions, mostly new, added every year to the British Museum Library : how can I pick out the grains of gold from this tremendous deposit of the lite&not; rary torrent?&rdquo; The thing looks difficult, but is practically easy. Begin wheie you will, you must soon come across traces of the men on whose thoughts the world is really living ; once upon a right track, one great man will introduce you to another. Every one can feel for himself the,L 2,* Or at most only one : my informant was not quite certain.
temper of the age he lives in ; he can discern with more or less clearness what are the great questions agitating the society about him ; from what quarters come the impulses which are giving a character to the social and moral life of the time : let him keep his attention alive to all this, and de&not; termine to place himself en rapport (as the table-turners say) with the greatest minds.,&quot;We needs must love the highest when we see it.&rdquo;*,Some may object to this, that the thoughts of the greatest minds are ad&not; dressed to persons of comparatively high mental cultivation, and that to begin at this point seems like taking a premature jump over a necessary stage of mental culture. Scarcely so, for the greatness of the greatest men generally rests in their dealing with men and things on the common ground of our human nature : the faculty for apprehending a deep thought will depend much more on a natural capacity and breadth of understanding than on a familiarity with literary allusions. Besides, the chief thing to be sought for is the awakening of high interests. When once a man cares about what is intrinsically great, his intelligence will probably find means of clearing away the smaller difficulties in his path : if he feels the presence of a great mind in the book he is reading, he will at the same time feel his own intellectual deficiency ; and there could not be a better starting-point than this for such a course of actual training as our Colleges profess to supply. A very slight acquaintance with the works of great thinkers will soon teach an untrained mind, that the more weighty the thought is, the more necessary is it to have some perception of the laws governing the use of the mechanical vehicle of expression. The strict study of Language is thus the most natural accompaniment to such reading.,I dare not venture to enlarge here upon the reasons for taking up particular authors. Such an attempt would involve a series of literary essays. Shake&not; speare, Burns, Scott&mdash;these are names too great to be handled in a few sen&not; tences. There are other teachers moreover, who, so far as this College is con&not; cerned, have a better right to speak about them. But I will try and give in a few words the reasons for reading our two great contemporary poets&mdash; Wordsworth and Tennyson. To any one who knows what these names mean the following remarks will be of no interest : I am only trying to show those who have yet to make the acquaintance of these great Teachers what they may be missing. Readers of the present generation will very often be unable to understand the especial value of Wordsworth&rsquo;s poetry. That is because he was in his vocation a revolutionist. The work he did can only be fully comprehended by those who have made themselves somewhat acquainted with the state of our poetical literature at the end of the last century. Open a volume of the &lsquo; British Poets &rsquo; of that period ; see the elegant dri&not; velling which won a place for so many illustrious unknowns in Johnson&rsquo;s &lsquo; Lives ;&rsquo; dreary epics manufactured on the strictest principles of French workmanship ; ponderously facetious satires, feeble echoes of Pope and,* Tennyson&rsquo;s &lsquo; Guinevere.&rsquo; &ldquo; Might I give counsel to any young hearer, I would say to him, &lsquo; Try to frequent the company of your betters. In books and life that is the most wholesome society ; learn to admire rightly: the great pleasure of life is that.&rsquo;&rdquo;&mdash; Thackeray , Lectures on the Humourists.
Juvenal; love-lyrics setting forth the woes of Corydon and Phyllis; ele&not; gant vanities at their best, and for the most part destitute of the smallest gleam of rational feeling. Taste a few drops of these &ldquo; mild bottlings from Gastaban springs,&rdquo; and you will understand the mingled burst of scorn and delight, sneers and blessings, which hailed the appearance of the &lsquo; Lyrical Ballads.&rsquo; We are startled now by the almost ultra-simplicity of these little poems, and we forget that they came at a time when to be natural was not only a novelty, but an utter blasphemy against all the gods in Parnassus. Just in the same way we are apt to wonder at the simpbcity of the Baconian Philosophy, till we remember that it took about three hundred years (from Roger the monk to Francis the chancellor) to convince the world that observation and experiment are the true guides in discover&not; ing the reasons of things. A great truth becomes sometimes so self-evi&not; dent that we forget the preciousness of its discovery.,After making all allowances for the manifold sins of our century, we can scarcely help seeing that from the middle of the last century till the pre&not; sent time our country has been making a continuous advance in the most important elements of national life. At about the date of George III. &rsquo;s accession, our art, poetry, literature, religion, politics, and morals, seemed to have reached the lowest point of degradation. There had been one con&not; tinuous decline since the days of Elizabeth. The last three generations have witnessed an equally unmistakable revival. This may be traced doubtless to the mixed agency of many and various causes, such as, for instance, the French Revolution, the preaching of Wesley, and the necessary laws of self-development in physical science ; but among those whose individual thoughts have contributed most to the change, at least during its later stages, Wordsworth must certainly be reckoned. It was chiefly his poetry which put a new life and a new temper into our imaginative bterature ; and by shaping the thoughts of other great men, it has been the means of re&not; generating the spirit of our national art. This poetry all men should read; may we not say, working men especially, when we think of that deep sympathy with the then so little heeded &ldquo;masses &rdquo; of mankind, which flamed up in the poet&rsquo;s soul at the destruction of the Bastille, and was fed in after-years by the &ldquo;human kindnesses and simple joys&rdquo; gathered from his life among the peasants of Cumberland ? If it be asked, &ldquo; What shall we find in his poetry ? &rsquo; I should say shortly, this :&mdash;You will find a great soul saying what it feels about the world it lives in, laying itself open to be taught all that earth and sky can teach, and uttering all it learns for the benefit of its fellows. You have poetry which, in the language of his own noble definition, gives you the &ldquo; breath and finer spirit of all knowledge, the impassioned expression which is in the countenance of all science ľ From such poetry as his you may leam to read the full beauty of Nature, learn to catch, in the thick of your common workaday life, &ldquo; &rsquo;mid the din of crowded cities,&rdquo;,&ldquo;That blessed mood In which the burden of the mystery,,In which the heavy and the weary weight Of all this unintelligible world Is lightened ; that serene and blessed mood In which the affections gently lead us on,
Till with nn eye made quiet by the power Of harmony and the deep power of joy,,We see iuto the life of things.&rdquo;,The essence of Wordsworth&rsquo;s poetry may be found in one pregnant line : &ldquo; W ? live by Admiration, Hope, and Love.&rdquo; He will teach you to feel, to enjoy, and to love more deeply the beauty of the world about you. He will do this, not by dazzling the fancy with gorgeous pictures of seldom seen splendours, but by leading the mind to dtvell with sympathetic delight on the proluse loveliness of common things : to make itself,&ldquo; A mansion for all lovely forms ;,A dwelling-place for all sweet sounds And harmonies.&rdquo;,To none is this teaching more precious than to us dwellers in great cities. W ordsworth lived among the lakes and mountains ; he drew his inspiration from the storm on Helvellyn or the cloud-shadows of Gras&not; mere. One ot us might say therefore, &ldquo; What has all this to do with me ? I can get no nearer heaven than Primrose-hill, and for water see none other than the Thames.&rdquo; True, О dweller in Great Ormond-street, but for that very reason is it the more needful that you and I should have our sensi&not; bilities awakened to a keener love of what is beautiful ; that the more we have to look on ugly bricks and smoky skies, the more we may enjoy the quiet green of the sycamore in our garden, or the crimson of a Strand sun&not; set. If we but catch that catholic love of Nature which it is the office of a great poet to teach us, the Sunday walk by Finchley churchyard will give us more hearty pleasure than many a bustling tourist gathers from his scamper up the Khiue. Every wreath of cloud descried from a workshop window, every flicker of sunlight on the ripple of the Thames, every breath of flowers in a costermonger&rsquo;s basket, will be a gleam of brightness on the dull surface of our daily life. Thousands, of course, need no books to teach them all this : it comes to them out of the simple instincts of their na&not; ture ; but thousands more have felt that whatever capacity they may have had for such enjoyment, has been immensely deepened by the teaching of great poets, and chiefly, in this age at least, by those of William Words&not; worth. Such reading as this therefore I urge upon the students in our Colleges. I am merely speaking the personal experience of hundreds of our generation when I say that they will find it answer ; and if this be so, is not such a study a practical one ? More practical surely than (for instance) French or book-keeping ? and more important than logic or mathema&not; tics. For if a clear brain is a good thing, surely an open heart is a better ? Education is not the getting of knowledge, but of wisdom ; the first is,&ldquo; earthly, of the mind ;,But Wisdom heavenly, of the soul.&rdquo;,Of Tennyson, we ought not to need to say much ; yet I am afraid he is not known as he shordd be. One of the party on a Sunday afternoon walk, a few weeks back, recited a part of the &lsquo; Wellington Ode.&rsquo; The rest were, I believe, scarcely aware of the existence of such a poem. Now this is not quite reasonable. That Ode is the noblest thing in its kind in
our own or probably in any tongue. It lias in it tlie deepest pathos, the most glowing beauty, and, more than that, the highest wisdom. It puts before you the purest and noblest ideal of human life. Every Englishman should know it by heart. No one who takes a volume of Tennyson and reads a few pages will need to be given reasons for reading more. He will at once feel that he is listening to the utterance of a very great mind and Idylls,* the last poem in which, * Guinevere,&rsquo; makes the mind almost bow in a very great soul. But of all the poet has written yet, it seems to me that the most valuable is the &lsquo; In Memoriam.&rsquo; And this, not even excepting the awe at its pathetic grandeur. Of this book I will not attempt here to de&not; scribe the contents. Let me only say that I think it, speaking in sober seriousness, the greatest and most precious production of human thought given to this world for many generations. One cannot weigh it against other poems. Its form and substance place it apart from all other books. One would certainly have to go back to &lsquo; Hamlet,&rsquo; or the epic of Dante, to find anything akin to it. But it matters little what rank we give it in the hierarchy of literature. All who have quietly pondered over its pages must have felt, that as a condensation of the deepest thought on the deepest subjects, dealing with the solemnest mysteries of our nature, that little book is of quite infinite value. I say once more, let our students try this sort of reading, and they will find it answer.,В. B. I feel it better than I could describe it. Eor I felt bound to the promoters of the College before it had an existence. The services they had rendered to the cause of association had won my admi&not; ration and esteem. To them and to Mr. Kingsley I felt myself indebted, and my exertions in the present Association appeared the most fitting ac&not; knowledgment of the obligation.,When the College was established, I became a member in the first term, and endeavoured to avail myself of its benefits, as much as my avocations and circumstances would permit.,As far as systematic study is concerned, my connection with the College has been a failure, arising from many causes. But there are things to be learnt beside those for which certificates are granted, which are as real, if not as palpable. And of this description of learning I hope I have realized something more.,* J us t presented by the Author to the Library of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; London. &quot; 5 &rsquo;
Certainly among the benefits received from the College, is the Gilders&rsquo; Association to be enumerated. For although I was wedded to association, as one of the chief instruments for the social regeneration of the workman, and had made ineffectual attempts at the formation of an association before the College was in existence, still the final and successful endeavour arose from a conversation in the College Coffee Room, at a social meeting.,The conversation alluded to was chiefly with Mr. Hughes, and was con&not; cerning working men, their lives, work, and association.,Yery likely the gentleman named thought no more of the talk. But there was at least one, who could not help thinking of it ; it allowed but little sleep that night, and was ready for him in the morning. Thoughts exchanged that night rendered henceforth association the one absorbing idea. And no more peace until an experiment had been made. If made and defeated, there would remain the satisfaction of having tried, and all the talk would not have been mere wind.,Then came talks with friends, canvassing for associates ; and a weary, weary job it proved. Many from whom counsel and advice w*as sought, and some among them who knew what association was from experience, gave but little comfort and less hope. &ldquo; The thing had been tried and proved a failure.&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo; Men were not prepared for it.&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo; It might do for a time, but would never last.&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo; Are you prepared to give the best years of your life, and risk being turned adrift when too old to start afresh in life?&rdquo; Such were some of the responses met with. They were unsatis&not; factory, because they were unsound. I need not stop to say why.,After a time three men Avere found who were willing to talk the matter over, and it was talked over, until talk seemed all that would come of it.,But it must be remembered that many may agree that a thing is desi&not; rable to be done, while but few may see the way to do it, especially among uneducated men. However, in time a plan was adopted, and another mate admitted into our confidence, making five in all. We then signed our names to a simple agreement, and subscribed two shillings a week each, to create a capital.,Our plan was to rent a workshop, and try to get work to do for the trade. To commence business in any other way would have been impos&not; sible, for we could not hope to raise*money enough in any other manner. We trusted to those who were interested in working men&rsquo;s endeavours at self-help to give what assistance they could, and in time we hoped to be able to help ourselves.,With this notion, when we had subscribed about &pound;8 we determined to try our fortunes.,* This of course will be considered sheer madness, especially by those who know least of working men, and how much more trying mere waiting is than actual work. Besides, the one appointed to begin was in the blissful condition nicknamed a &lsquo; gentleman ,&rsquo; that is, out of work.,Accordingly circulars and cards were obtained and issued, wood bought, benches made, and the workshop got ready. Then came the trial.,London was open to be canvassed ; there was of course plenty of work to be got, if we could get it.,Then came the weary tramps, calling here and there, receiving bland promises from some, supercilious contempt from ethers, anything in short but the thing wanted, namely work.
But a few private jobs were obtained, and with the proceeds from them, and what assistance the associates could render, one man was kept going.,At the end of the first quarter the business transactions amounted to &pound;18.,So things wore on. One associate laid by with a severe illness, and another, in whom great hopes had been placed, sowing the seeds of jealousy and suspicion among all, work scarce, trade-work not to be got, getting into debt, faintheartedness seizing some, and the aspect of things as gloomy as well could be.,But an end comes to all things, and we were getting to the end of that form of the trial. Four months and a half, and a trade-job obtained.,We had learnt something by experience, and altered our tactics ; we sought another market ; and, in justice to my sick friend, I must say that he had advocated the plan from the first.,We very shortly obtained work from two more wholesale houses.,Now caftie less walking and more working ; and sheer downright hard work it was, the associates lending a hand at night after their day&rsquo;s work. And so through the second quarter, having done about &pound;36.,Now commenced an improved state of affairs. We shifted our quarters from an unsuitable shop to our present premises, 19, Bed Lion Square, Holborn.,Work increasing, my suspicious friend came in to work, when another side of his character was exhibited. He had to work harder, and get less for it than he had been used to, which was tolerable as long as talked about and endured by others, but too much for him. Our trade still in&not; creasing, another one was added to the number (my sick Mend), one that meant work, and from that day to this has stuck to it, as Englishmen are apt to do, when they are &ldquo; expected to do their duty !&rdquo;,Our difficulties now began to yield. A call was made at the right time, at a place where application had been made again and again. But this time they wanted something done that their ordinary people could not do by the time. Now was the opportunity, and it was made the most of. Shortly after, another order was wanted to be executed in four days: we accepted it, and completed it to time, this time winning compliments and promises of work, and a good deal of it&mdash;promises which have been kept to the letter. Another quarter got through, and &pound;87 worth of work done, and a crisis. Our associate&rsquo;s conduct became unbearable. Certainly the work we did was slavery to get a living at, and the debt increasing and a doubt seizing most as to the worth of keeping on. But we paid our associate out that is, gave him all he had contributed, deducting; nothing, and gave him what help we could to assist him to New Zealand,So four of us were left to continue the struggle. But things were mending. We made the offer to join us to one who had been spoken to and he, being out of work at the time, came and saw what we were doino&middot;&rsquo; the state of the accounts, and what our prospects were.,The offer was accepted, and he threw himself into the move heart and soul And as he began so he continues&mdash;always ready with head and hand, and always at his post.,We were fairly launched at last. We could puff together with a will- put on a spurt, and keep it on, our trade steadily increasing, a slight im-
provemcnt in the prices also, and increasing confidence placed in ns, large quantities of work given out at a time, and a desire always manifested to keep us going.,So we have gone on steadily improving, adding to our numbers as our business called for it, until all the associates were employed, and three extra on probation. And the chances are that there is not a happier, more contented, or more hopeful shop of men in London.,We work as hard, keep as sharp a look-out for whatever comes within our reach, as at first. We dare not relax our watchfulness, for we can only hold our own, and pay ourselves by so doing.,Our connection with the College has been nearly lost sight of in this statement. But Mr. Hughes and some members assisted us with orders and recommendations. And the College Social Meetings gave the forlorn manager fresh stimulus for work, during the time of his greatest need. In those gloomy times, there were two lines of Kingsley&rsquo;s that always brought fresh strength for endurance ;&mdash;,&ldquo; Up, up, up, and up,,Face your game, and play it.&rdquo;,Yours faithfully,,R. Newton.,HISTORY OR THE LONDON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,We have no such interesting story to tell, no such liberal constitution to speak of, as Mr. Rowbotham, when he told us so well the tale of the self-reliant energy of his northern friends, who, by the light of their &ldquo; half&not; penny candle in an earthenware inkstand,&rdquo; laid anew the foundation of the People&rsquo;s College at Sheffield, and carried their plan to a successful issue, declining subscriptions and gifts. But a certain interest our history has ; and this number of the Magazine must be filled, so here is part at least of what I know or believe about it.,The Paris February Revolution of 1848 (see Magazine, Yol. I. p. 73) said to a barrister of Lincoln&rsquo;s-Inn, Mr. John Malcolm Ludlow, &ldquo;Are you lawyers doing your duty to the poor in the place where you earn your fees ?&rdquo; The answer was a proposal to the then newly-appointed Chaplain of Lincoln&rsquo;s-Inn, Mr. Maurice, that some district near should be taken in hand by the lawyers whom Mr. Ludlow could get together. After refusals of nearer places, Little Ormond Yard, close to our present College, was handed over to us, and our house-to-house visiting began. We soon had to engage a mistress and open an infants&rsquo; day-school, and at night taught boys and men in its rooms ourselves. Ragged, merry, and mischievous, the young urchins * were ; and how they did enjoy their van-rides to Epping Forest ! Well, we visitors and teachers used to meet at Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s one evening in the week, to have tea and chat, and read the Bible together; and at these meetings the letters on &lsquo; Labour and the Poor,&rsquo;,* One, now a big bricklayer, with a wife and two children, came to claim acquaint&not; ance, and shake hands with me, some six weeks ago.
then coining out in the &lsquo; Morning Chronicle,&rsquo; and written by Mr. Henry Mayhew and his coadjutors, were the subjects of frequent and earnest talk, Few of us had any idea of the wide-spread misery in the workmen&rsquo;s homes around us, and fewer still knew how the slop-system had been at work lowering wages, destroying the honourable trade, and forcing wo&not; men and children into their fathers&rsquo; work. Week after week did Mr. Ludlow press these subjects on our consideration, and say &lsquo; we must no longer be accomplices in this state of things, we must get an honest middleman be&not; tween us, and some working-men at least ; we must have here an Asso&not; ciation like the French Working Men&rsquo;s Associations that I have known succeed so well, in which the manager shall be appointed by the men and by us, and be a bond between us instead of a division.&rsquo; Under Tuesday, Jan. 8, 1850, in my diary, I find the entry, &ldquo;Little Ormond Yard School, from 7.30 till 9.15 ; meeting at Maurice&rsquo;s about starting an Association of Tailors&mdash;about twenty there&mdash;talked till 12; appointed a Committee, etc. ; &pound;300 wanted.&rdquo; Mr, Ludlow had not only started the notion of an Association but had in his visitings in Fetter-lane found a manager for it in the person of Mr. Walter Cooper, a well-known Chartist speaker. Mr. Cooper got together some of his working-men friends to meet us at &lsquo; Con&not; ferences,&rsquo; at Cranboume Tavern, etc. Mr. Kingsley wrote his pamphlet,,&lsquo; Cheap Clothes and Nasty.&rsquo; Money was raised by subscription among our friends; the Working Tailors&rsquo; Association opened shop at 34, Castle Street East, Oxford Street, and on Tuesday, February 11, 1850, ap&not; peared &lsquo; Tracts on Christian Socialism,&rsquo; No. &Iuml; (written by Mr. Maurice), to explain why Working Men&rsquo;s Associations were wanted, what end we hoped they would answer, and that we had taken the name of Christian Socialists, because we adopted the trade-organization proposed by the Socialists, but we were Christians, and our object was &lsquo;to apply the princi&not; ple of Christianity to trade and industry.&rsquo; Mr. Ludlow started and edited &lsquo;The Christian Socialist,&rsquo; a penny weekly paper; other tracts by Mr. Hughes, Mr. Ludlow, Mr. Maurice, etc., appeared; Mr. Yansittart Neale came forward to help us. Several Working Men&rsquo;s Associations began business in London and the country ; Mr. Slaney got us &lsquo; The Industrial and Provident Societies Act, 1852 &rsquo; to facilitate their formation, and a great spur was given to the co-operative movement which has since so widely spread through the north-west of England. The Little Ormond Yard party, with some additions, formed &lsquo; The Society for Promotin&laquo;&middot; Working Men&rsquo;s Associations,&rsquo; and built the &lsquo;Hall of Association,&rsquo; undei&#39; the workshops of the &lsquo;Tailors&rsquo; Association,&rsquo; in Castle Street, &#39;in this Hall were begun, at Mr. Ludlow&rsquo;s suggestion, classes and lectures, to both of which women were admitted ; and the old conferences were started again. The only successful classes were Mr. Ludlow&rsquo;s French one, and the Draw&not; ing-class.,By the end of 1853, Mr. Yansittart Neale and the Co-operative Leao-ue had occupied, to some extent, the ground that &lsquo;the promoters,&rsquo; as we were called, had taken up, and we had lent or spent all the money subscribed for co-operative purposes. A great want had been all along felt for better education among со -operators, and at our meeting on January 11, 1854 the minute-book says, &ldquo;A conversation took place concerning the establish&not; ment o&iacute; a People&rsquo;s College, in London, in connection with the Associations,
and Mr. Yansittart Neale read a letter received from Mr. Wilson, the Secretary of the People&rsquo;s College, Sheffield, as to its origin and history, and also the five Annual Reports of that Institution &rdquo; and further on, &ldquo; The following Resolution, proposed by Mr. Hughes, seconded by Mr. Lloyd Jones, was carried, &lsquo;That it be referred to the Committee of Teaching and Publication to frame, and so far as they think fit, to carry out, a plan for the establishment of a People&rsquo;s College in connection with the Metropo&not; litan Associations.&rsquo;&rdquo; Our Committee of Teaching and Publication con&not; sisted of,Maurice, Rev, F. D., President. Goderich, Yisconnt, M.P.&lsquo;(uow Earl De Grey and Ri pon.),Hose, Rev. H. J. (now in Australia.) Johnson, Wm., Esq., Eton College. Kingsley, Rev. C.,Louis, A. H., Esq., Law-Student. Ludlow, J, M., Esq., Barrister.,Lumley, Edward, Esq., Publisher, Macmillan, A., Esq., Publisher. Mansfield, С. В , Esq. (dead.),Neale, E. Yansittart, Esq., Barrister. Paul, Rev. C. K., (then Curate of Banbury.) Walsh, C. R., Esq. (dead.),Westlake, John, Esq., Law-Student.,The Secretary (Thomas Shorter).,The Committee, or some of them, had several meetings, agreed on cer&not; tain maxims, and asked Mr. Maurice to outline a plan for them. On February 7, 1854, he put into our hands a printed statement of twelve pages, and from it I make the following long extracts :&mdash;,&ldquo;&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt;.,&ldquo; I understand the Committee, at its meeting of last Thursday, to have agreed on certain maxims, which they wished me to assume in any plan of education I might lay before them.,&ldquo; 1. They agreed that our position as members of a society which affirms the operations of trade and industry to be under a moral law&mdash;a law con&not; cerning the relations of men to each other, obliges us to regard social, political, or to use a more general phrase, human studies as the primary part of our education.,&ldquo;3. It was agreed that we were not bound to confine our education to our own associates, but that we should promote their interest better if we produced a scheme which should be available for the working classes generally. At the same time it was considered that we ought to do our utmost, that the members of each association should, as individuals, enter into the general scheme, and, so far as it was possible, that each associa&not; tion should be an integral part of it.,&ldquo;3. It was agreed that adult males (that is to say, males, at all events, not younger than 16) should be contemplated first and chiefly in our edu&not; cation ; though it was thought very desirable that provision should in due time be made for the teaching of boys and of females.,&ldquo;4. It was agreed that the education should be regular and organic, not taking the form of mere miscellaneous lectures, or even of classes not related to each other.,&ldquo;5. It was also agreed that the teachers, and, by degrees, the pupils, should form an organic body, so that the name of College should be at least as applicable to our institution as to University College or to King&rsquo;s College.,&ldquo;6, It was determined that the College should, in some sense or other, immediately or ultimately, be self-go verned and self-supported. . . .
&ldquo; 1&middot; I propose then, that there should be a class in the Bible every Sun&not; day evening. A class on Church History may be added hereafter.,&ldquo; If this point is settled, we may proceed to what I shall call, in the old language of the schools (which I do not think is in the least degree obsolete or unsuitable to our purpose), the Humanity course. It should consist, I think, of lessons on Politics, on Ethics, and on Language. These lectures might be given respectively on the evenings of Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday. On the same evenings I would suggest other lectures, closely connected with these, on History and Literature : the first series to be on our own. The third, or Natural Division follows. On Thursday evenings I should propose Lectures on the Human Erame. On Friday, Lectures upon Machinery, or the instruments which man wields. On Saturday, on the Relations between the Physical world and Man. Classes on Drawing, on Arithmetic, and on Music, might be fixed for these same evenings. . . .,&ldquo; 2. I justify the course which I have proposed as furthering the ends of those who are promoting Working Men&rsquo;s Associations, because I think that what working men most want is the feeling of an order in God&rsquo;s government, in their relations to each other, in the world around them, a righteous order and one into which they must enter, which they cannot make for themselves. So far as we awaken them to the feeling of this order, and cultivate it in them, so far are we resisting the selfish and dis&not; organizing tendencies of their own minds and of the world around them ; so far are we preparing them to feel that there is a right which they may pursue, and a wrong which they may reject, in the common business of life ; so far are we making the workshop itself a means of practical &#39;education, a living commentary upon what they learn. The true treatment of history, especially of English history, of ethics, of theology, will, I believe, be better preaching in support of association than any other which we could set on foot. . . .,&ldquo; 3. My reason for starting with such difficult subjects as politics and ethics is, because all our pupils are politicians and ethical theorists before they come to us. What we want is, to put order into their crude thoughts, oi rathei to lead them gradually to perceive (we ourselves learning as we teach) the order which is at the bottom of their thoughts. We must not begin from the past, but make them feel that they need the past for the explanation of the present. So, too, they will find an order in their own speech, laws governing it and correcting the incoherencies of it, which they would never get at if they began&mdash;as Dominie Samson proposed to do with his pupil, whom he. had lost at five, when he returned at five-and-twenty &mdash;from the first rudiments of grammar. We must rather get back to the first rudiments of grammar; in the course of which process we and our scholars may discover what grammar means, and what the grammar of one people has to do with that of another.,If this lemaik is true, it may be a help to us in ascertaining how tar the education of the workwoman may be joined with that of the workman, and where they should separate. The language classes the drawing classes, the music classes, might, I should think, be the same for both the starting-points of knowledge being the same. In politics they would not be the same, nor perhaps in ethics. I think it is quite clear
that women ought to have lessons in social life and order as well as in ethics, taking domestic life for the ground. They should also have lectures upon health, though I suppose the physician would feel the necessity of giving these an entirely different character from those which he addressed to men. F. J. Furnivall. -■,{To be continued .),NEWS.,Cambridge. &mdash;On Monday, July 30, our College Cricket Club played against the Eastern Counties Railway Servants&rsquo; Club. The E. C. R. went in first, and made a score of 100 runs. The College then followed, beating their opponents by 18 runs. Then after an hour for lunch, the E. C. R. again went in, but this time did not make such a good stand as before, scoring ouly 63. Thus the College had but 46 runs to get to win the game, which they did, with two wickets to spare. This is the best match we have yet been engaged in, our opponents being good cricketers, and generally our seniors in age.,A match against the Cambridge University Servants&rsquo; Club was played on Monday, August 20, terminating in favour of the College, in one innings, with 44 runs to sjmre. This is the second important match we have played this season. The batting of Mr. H. Boning, our Captain, who scored 31 runs, and of Mr. A. Brett, who scored 24, was es&not; pecially w&#39;orthy of notice. The Scores were as follows :&mdash;The College, 132 ; University Servants, 1st innings, 41, 2nd innings, 47.,Lancaster. &mdash;The numbers of the Working Men&rsquo;s Society have increased to above 600, and the Committee are busy taking measures for indoor occupation&mdash;classes, lectures, and recreation of various kinds. The leading supporters of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution are anxious that their body and the new Society should amalgamate, and have offered terms to the Working Men&rsquo;s Committee, which are still under considera&not; tion. The question is, can Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes be made to answer all the purposes, or to cherish the spirit, sought for by the founders of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, and the best friends of the working-classes ? If Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes were really composed of mechanics, the answer would be easy, but in most towns they have been converted into middle-class or tradesmen&rsquo;s clubs, and have given up class-work&mdash;at least genuine hard work&mdash;for amusement, or for lounging classes. Here, then, are two things to repel working-men&mdash;better-dressed and richer people, who, he suspects, will not welcome him and treat him with respect, and classes that do not meet his wants. On the other hand, working-men cannot ordinarily provide the building and the accommodation that the Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes offer, and they do need amusement as well as head-work. The difficulty lies in the temper of the men on each side, and no one at a distance can say how each particular case is to be treated ; but if half-a-dozen of the leading men of each body were to come together, and talk the matter over, no doubt a means of unity would be found. At Salford, the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, having an energetic Mr. Chadwick, and a most liberal Mr. Langworthy, among its officers, and being in a wealthy district, raised money, and bought the Institute right out. The Manchester College has rooms, and holds its classes in the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute there. Let the Lancaster Committee write to Mr. Nodal or Professor Greenwood, of the Manchester College, or Mr. D. Chadwick, of the Salford College.
