M.A.,Jrllofos of ф CToIiege &mdash;Mr. wm rossiter, Mr. j. roebuck, Mr. g. tansley&middot;,Associate&mdash;Mr. D. LEGGATT.,The Council has adopted the following Eesolutions :&mdash;,1. The College will be ultimately divided into Six Classes.,2. The First will consist of the general body of merely Matriculated Students.,3. The Second Class will consist of Certificated Students, or those to whom at the end of any course shall be awarded a Certificate of Competency in the subject of the course.,4. The Third Class will consist of Scholars (in Art, History, Language, Mathematics, Physics, etc.), or those who prove themselves competent in any one branch of the College Studies.,5. The Fourth Class will consist of those who are thought worthy to be Associates, or permanent Members of the College. From these we require competency not only in some specific study, but in the most important studies of each department of the College. Each student will have an opportunity of answering questions in those subjects in which he takes most interest; but he will be expected to prove that he is acquainted with (1) the Bible History, (2) the History of England, (3) the Principles of English Grammar, and the Elements of (4) some pure Mathematical, and (5) some Physical study, or some branch of applied Mathematics, as Mechanics. The object will be to ascertain that he has the knowledge of those subjects which every person educated in a College ought to have ; not to elicit his opinions either in Theology or Politics. The Associate must have attended the College for Eight Terms.,6. The Fifth Class will consist of Fellows : these will be chosen out of the Associates. The Fellows will take part in the education of this College, and may assist in establishing and carrying on other Colleges. They will be chosen by the Council, after due con&not; sideration of what they have done in the Associate Examination, and of what they have been since they entered the College. Moral qualities, a capacity for teaching, and a will&not; ingness to teach, will be at least as. indispensable as proficiency in any branch of study.,7. The Sixth Class will consist of the Council. It is intended that the Council be hereafter recruited from the Fellows, to the extent of at least a third of its members. The Council will be prepared to assist in the formation of new Colleges, in London or other parts of England.
No. XXII. OCTOBER 1, 1860.,THE PRESENT CONDITION OE MECHANICS&rsquo; INSTITUTES.,Report of the Yorkshire Union of Mechanics&#39; Institutes , read at the 23 rd Annual Meeting. Leeds, 1860. 8vo, pp. 124.,This is a very interesting pamphlet. It contains an ably-drawn Report, on the general condition of 127 Institutes, a series of detailed statements showing the actual operations of each during the past year, and various statistical tables, giving the numerical results with great clearness and precision. Erom such materials as these, we may fairly attempt an esti&not; mate of the actual amount of work now being done by Mechanics&rsquo; Insti&not; tutes throughout the country. The experience here recorded touches many points of the greatest interest to the members and promoters of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;.,These 127 Institutes include 25,519 members, of whom 2,575 are females. These 25,519 people collect or contribute some &pound;16,000 an&not; nually ; they have got 150,000 books to read, and each individual bor&not; rows 16 volumes a year; they have listened to some 800 lectures, and about 9,000 attend educational classes. No one can examine the Report, of which this is a bare numerical abstract, without being impressed with the large amount of earnest effort and valuable result here recorded. It is clear that Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes, whether they are what they ought to be or not, are at least an essential feature in the life of the manufacturing towns of the North, They embrace in many places a considerable part of the entire population. The smaller the town, the greater apparently is the influence of the Institute. That &raquo;f Leeds has 1 in 84 of a popu&not; lation of 173,000, that of Huddersfield 1 in 21, out of a population of 31,000. Marske contains 675 inhabitants, of which one-sixth belong to the Mutual Improvement Society ; while of 100 souls in the village of Acomb, 46 are members of the Library and Lecture Society, and as only two of these are females, it follows that nearly the entire male population must be devoting itself to the improvement of its mind.,Let us, with the aid of the Report, look a little closer at the work here t going on,The chief complaint which has been made from every quarter of the county, is that class instruction is neglected. Ihe cause is attributed by many to the great amount of employment which has been obtained by the working classes, and, consequently, dimi&not; nution of the leisure which they have previously devoted to attendance at the evening classes. It would appear that commercial prosperity and educational advancement do not march together, but that the former is unfavourable to the latter. It is no doubt true that in many instances men have worked excessive overtime, but then they have had more abundant means to purchase educational advantages. It may be remarked that the attendance at children&rsquo;s day schools, in our manufacturing towns, has latterly been very good. J,* The non-attendance of adults at evening classes must be attributed more to apathy
than to any other cause, and this can only be destroyed by personal appeals : general addresses seldom reach those for whom they are composed, the man must be individually engaged before he can be induced to leave the state of lethargic indifference to which he has been bred. ... It is well known that throughout the country the operative class was never better remunerated, more abundantly employed, or in so good a sanitary con&not; dition, though in this latter there is much room for improvement. Facilities for self- education are offered in every quarter of our towns,&mdash;in almost every hamlet of the agri&not; cultural districts,&mdash;therefore there can be no excuse for the working man&rsquo;s neglect of these opportunities.&rdquo;,Surely this is strong language. Have we, who call ourselves the edu&not; cated classes, done so much for those below us, that we have a right to say &ldquo; there can be no excuse&rdquo; for their not flocking into our Colleges and Institutes ? Have we given them good and honest reasons for becoming instructed ? Have we not for the most part been putting before them the paltriest motives, for ever preaching on the text, &ldquo; Knowledge is power,&rdquo; and giving it no better meaning than &ldquo; Improve your minds, that you may rise in the world&rdquo; ? Have not most of our appeals and exhortations been amplified from this maxim ? And more than that, has our practice been much better than our preaching? Is the &ldquo;superior education&rdquo; of the upper classes acquired from the pure love of knowledge for its own sake, in the philosophic belief that wisdom is her own reward, or as part of the equipment whereby a man is to push his way through life ? The idea of a non-professional or extra-professional education is treated as a paradox by the greater part of mankind. When, for instance, an M.P, gets up in a debate on the Civil Service Commission, and asks indignantly, &ldquo; Why should an Exciseman be expected to know anything about Magna Charta,&rdquo; he assumes that Providence never could have intended an Exciseman&rsquo;s brain to do anything better than calculate percentages. And most of his audience believe the same thing.,The men who value learning for its higher advantages have always been, and still are, a comparatively small class. Of this feeling, as it existed in other days, our schools and universities are noble monuments, but who dare say what would become of those ancient foundations if they were stripped of all their worldly emoluments ? The ambitious Cantab knows that if he can solve a certain number of problems in the Senate House, his bread and butter will be cut for life, and that the thickness of the buttering will vary as the specific gravity of the problems, in a tolerably constant ratio. Without this faith, how many would be worshipping at that shrine of Science? Let us not, then, be surprised at our having some trouble in persuading working men that French and Algebra, Gram&not; mar and History, have to do with something other than bread and butter. Hitherto they have been but adopting the practical logic of our own class.,The neglect complained of by the Report is accompanied by a corre&not; sponding fall in the character of the teaching, A fall, that is, from the standard originally adopted in these institutions. Their founders intended them to give to artisans who had learnt enough in schools or otherwise of the mere elements of knowledge, the means of obtaining such a degree of mental cultivation as every intelligent Englishman ought to possess who intends to fulfil his duties as a citizen. Not more than one or two of the 127 Institutes could for a moment pretend to meet this demand. A large majority give class instruction in &ldquo; elementary&rdquo; subjects only, that is, in
reading, writing, and the first four rules of arithmetic. Ten only out of the whole number have a class for the study of any other language than English, and not more than &amp;lt;|&raquo;out fourteen teach any more mathematics than Arithmetic. Physical Science is represented by ten classes in Che&not; mistry or Natural Philosophy, and one in Physiology. For the direct study of outward nature, the best intellectual pursuit, perhaps, which a working man could possibly adopt, there is no provision whatever. Of the 25,000 members, none are invited to take up Geology or Botany.,Singing and Drawing, on the contrary, appear to take a fair position. There are some dozen classes for the first, and about twenty-two for the second.,The attendants on classes are about one-third of the whole number of members ; but of these a great many are children. Middlesborough, for instance, has 190 class pupils, 120 of whom are children. The strictly edu&not; cational work of Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes may therefore be best described by saying that about one-half are elementary schools, adult or juvenile, and nothing more ; that of the remainder a great part of the work done is of the same sort, with the occasional addition of a somewhat higher kind of instruction ; and that here and there may be found one or two Institutes in which a larger scheme of education is adopted. Miscellaneous Lectures form a prominent feature in the Annual Deports ; but as they number on an average seven in the year to each Institute, they can scarcely add much to the results of the class teaching.,This state of things can, surely, not be regarded as satisfactory. Not that the work here described is not very useful of its kind, and, probably, carried on with much honest striving and noble energy. Many of these Reports contain passages quite inspiriting from the glimpses they give of manful struggle with difficulties, and resolute determination to carry out worthy aims ;* but we must not forget that most of this effort is bein&laquo;&middot; directed to the remedying of deficiencies which should never have existed, and which never could exist if our social legislation were what it ought to be. A time will, we hope, come when for a grown Englishman to be in&not; capable of using the mere implements of education, reading and writing will be thought as monstrous as that he should be lame or blind. Some generations hence, we may possibly have come to the conclusion that the spelling-book and multiplication table are not theological works. We may then sink differences of creed, and make primary instruction (don&rsquo;t let us call the three &ldquo;r&rsquo;s&rdquo; Education) an absolute social right. Till then we must have Adult Schools ; but would it not be better to call them at once by their right name, and let the Institute or College wear at least the form of what it should some day become in substance ? In a country town everybody knows what everybody else is about, and a few energetic men might almost change the face of things in a few years.,The lleport before us seems to recommend that paid teachers be em&not; ployed whenever this is practicable. It does so on the ground that their services are more to be depended on in the long-run. Several good reasons may be urged in support of this opinion, but the voluntary teaching carries with it many and signal advantages which must not be overlooked. First and foremost, it is the most emphatic assertion of the truth which lies at the root of all social life, the principle of mutual obligation. It is a spurious and sordid &ldquo; independence &rdquo; which denies this, which says : &ldquo; I will not be beholden to any man for anything; what I want I will pay for.&rdquo; Faithful service is just the one thing which you cannot get merely by paying for; least of all, faithful and earnest teaching. A man may obtain his liveli&not; hood by making that his business ; but if he does it honestly and well, those whom he teaches owe him much more than their money can pay. There is obligation still. And the more men are sundered by differences of fortune and vocation, the more eagerly should we seize on those common services which can be rendered by one man to another, or by one class to another, with interest and advantage to both. The rendering of such ser&not; vices will go far to give such stability to our social and political life, that in trying to improve it we shall not need to be haunted by a fear o&iacute; dis&not; turbing its foundations.,Having spoken of the educational deficiencies of Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes,,I ought to guard myself against appearing to overlook the undoubted ad&not; vantages which in other ways they have given to the community. Their departure from the original scheme of the founders has been forced upon them by the pressure of a practical necessity. Where an Institute becomes a tradesmen&rsquo;s or middle-class club, or a society for general entertainment, it only shows that the demand which it has thus been made to supply was more real than that which the founders believed to exist among another class. In answering these necessities the institutes have been of signal use ; and now that they have established for themselves a recognized cha&not; racter, in which the educational features have become comparatively un&not; important, and the literal meaning of their name has been merged in a new &ldquo; connotation,&rdquo; it would be very unwise to run the risk of destroying that which is of such undoubted value in an attempt to force their action into a new channel.,The promoters of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; felt the difficulty of the pro&not; blem, and thought to meet it but by an altogether new organization. Litchfield.,TRADES UNIONS AND STRIKES.,REPORT BY A COMMITTEE OF THE SOCIAL SCIENCE ASSOCIATION.,The Committee appointed last year by the Council to consider this ques&not; tion has presented its Report at the recent meeting in Glasgow. It is a paper which will give the public a juster view of the actual working of the principle of combination than they are likely to gather from the virulent denunciation with which the &lsquo; Times&rsquo; and other papers have assailed the trade societies. The Committee being composed, it is presumed, of honest and capable inquirers, its judgment must be treated as authoritative on the main questions of fact ; and this judgment is, on the whole, favourable to the societies. Omitting the paragraphs of minor importance, the Report is as follows : *&mdash;,&ldquo; The Committee have arrived at the following conclusions ;,&ldquo; 3. That the principles upon which trade societies regulate their pro&not; ceedings are more moderate, and that discussions between the workmen belonging to them and their masters have been managed in a fairer spirit than in the times before the repeal of the laws against combination.,&ldquo; 4. That the workmen belonging to these societies form a better esti&not; mate now than heretofore of the condition of their respective trades, and they are less unreasonable in their expectations of obtaining increased wages; that they understand better the necessity of submitting to reductions; that they have generally overcome the prejudices which they once entertained against machinery, and that their leaders are men of higher character and intelligence.,&ldquo; 7 ■ That the establishment of joint-stock companies, the capital of which has been chiefly subscribed by working men, and the management of which is controlled by them in different parts of England, will be of great use to working men, by contributing both when they succeed and when they fall, to increase the operatives&rsquo; experience of the relative value of manual and of intellectual labour and of capital, and by showing them that there are fluctuations in trade over which the masters have no control.,8. That there are still in many trades&rsquo; societies rides&mdash;some acknow&not; ledged, some concealed&mdash;which interfere with the freedom of the masters, and of the men within as well as without the bodies that impose them&rsquo; and which peisons ot all schools in political and social economy must ut&not; terly condemn.,11. Ihat the legal difficulties which rich societies experience in imdin&laquo;&middot; a profitable investment for their funds often increase greatly the temptation to employ them in strikes. 1,It has been published in one only of the daily papers, the &lsquo; Telegraph.&rsquo;
12. ihat, leaving out of account the accidental benefits and the acci&not; dental mischiefs of trade societies, it must be admitted that they have at times assisted the workmen in a trade more speedily to realize higher wages when the profits and wages in it have been rising, and that they have in some instances been of advantage to the master, by producing a greater uniformity of wages throughout a trade.,&ldquo; 13. Ihat disastrous as have been the immediate results of most strikes to masters as well as to men, they have not been without their use to both, by inducing wiser and more gracious concessions on one side, and less un&not; reasonable demands on the other.,&ldquo; 14. That minor questions connected with trades, which often produce serious irritation, might be advantageously referred to a mixed tribunal of masters and men, but that in the opinion of a majority of the Committee it would be over sanguine to hope for a removal of the more direct and seri&not; ous causes of strife by such arrangement.,&ldquo;15. That the rate of wages must be settled between the masters and men, and that the intervention of third parties, unless specially invited by both, and possessing in a very high degree the confidence of both, can be of little avail.,&ldquo; 16. That the Legislature may do much good service to workmen by pro&not; viding an easy and cheap remedy, both in law and equity, to meet the case of disputes between trades&rsquo; societies and the members, especially in respect to the application of benefit funds.,&ldquo;17. That the slightest return to the old policy of prohibiting combina&not; tions would be most mischievous, and that no legislative measures for pre&not; venting strikes and lock-outs would be effectual.,&ldquo;18. That combinations of masters and of men have always tended to be&not; come tyrannical when their rules, discussions, and modes of action have been kept secret ; and that willingness to expose them to the public is the best pledge they can give that they are not engaged in plots which are dangerous to the public.,&ldquo;19. That the improved education of masters and of men, there is good reason to hope, is doing more to avert collisions between them than any mere arrangements could accomplish, whether voluntary or enforced ; that the experience of the past has convinced many of the employers that not to care for their hands, not to promote their intellectual and moral welfare, not to show sympathy with them and forbearance towards them, is to ruin themselves ; and that the employed are learning that without temperance and self-government they must be slavish, that their interests are the same with those of the whole land, that the more they respect their own order the less they will be at war with every other.&rdquo;,THE UNIVERSITY OE LONDON: ITS SEVERANCE EROM,THE COLLEGES.,An important change has been made in the conditions on which de&not; grees are conferred at the University of London, as will appear from the following communication :&mdash; *
&ldquo; University op London, Burlington House, W.,August \ bth , 1860.,tc Sir,&mdash;I am desired by the Senate of the University of London to for&not; ward to you Copies of the Revised Regulations of the University in regard to its Examinations for Matriculation and for Degrees in Arts and Science, and to direct your attention to the conditions on which those Degrees are now conferred. Attendance at any particular Institution connected with the University is no longer required , but the system of Examinations has been so framed as to test with greatly increased efficiency and complete&not; ness the acquirements of the Candidates.,The attention of the Senate has lately been strongly drawn to the defi&not; ciency, on the part of a large proportion of the Candidates who present themselves at the Matriculation Examination, of sound elementary know&not; ledge, more especially in regard to the Grammatical Structure of the Languages&mdash;both classical and modern&mdash;which such Candidates are re- quiied to have studied. Reeling it requisite to insist upon the possession of this knowledge as an essential qualification, the Senate have modified the Regulations relating to Matriculation, in such a manner that no Can&not; didate will henceforth be allowed to pass this Examination without satis&not; fying the Examiners by his answers to grammatical questions, as well as by his translations from Latin, Greek, and French, or German into Eng&not; lish. 5,&ldquo; Feeling sure that I may reckon on your desire to promote the views of the Senate, by endeavouring to raise the standard of attainment on the part of Candidates for its Primary Examination, I have the honour to be, Sir,,&ldquo; Your obedient Servant,,&ldquo;William B. Carpenter, M.D., Registrar .&rdquo;,The change here announced has been made the subject of much dis&not; cussion in and out of the University, and was strongly opposed by a large minority of the Senate. By the old rule, degrees were only given to those who passed the required examinations, having been students for a certam period at one of the affiliated Colleges. The governing body o&iacute; the University has now given up this condition. They now in effect say to all comers, we will no longer ask you where you have been study&not; ing&mdash;pass our examinations, and we will give you our Decrees. To those who conceive of a University as a corporate body, of which a cer&not; tain number of Colleges are the constituent members, or in which a number of students are in some way bound to each other, this act seems like suicide pure and simple. By virtually dissolving all connection be- tween the Colleges and itself, the University ceases to be anything more than an Examining Board. The Civil Service Commission might as well assume the ancient title. 0,It is curious to observe the universal concurrence of the various ex&not; amining bodies in reporting the general want of &ldquo;sound elementary knowledge Inspectors of Schools, Examiners for Government Offices the competitions for A.A. Degrees and Society of Arts Certificates all agree m noting this d&eacute;ficience.&rdquo; Is not the mischief likely to increase teachers and taught drop into a habit of regarding Examinations as
the final end of study, instead of being merely tests of its genuineness ? And is anything more likely to encourage such a habit than the disavowal by a University of its chief and most natural function&mdash;that of providing instruction? And, again, what can be a greater encouragement to su&not; perficiality than to demand of a candidate, at the outset of his career, an acquaintance with a large variety of subjects, in some of which thorough knowledge is only to be got by long pondering over fundamental ideas ? And yet we find the Matriculation subjects include Mechanics, Hydrostatics, Pneumatics, Acoustics, Optics, and Chemistry, with an intimation that the knowledge here required is &ldquo; such as may be attained by attending a course of Experimental Lectures,&rdquo; which is as much as to say, &ldquo; we shall only ask you for a smattering of these things, as it would be unreasonable to expect you really to know anything about them.&rdquo;,COLLEGE EXCURSIONS: A WEEK IN NORTH DEVON.,For several years past some members of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; have been in the habit of spending such short holidays as their trade or employment allows them in an excursion into the country. This time three years a party was formed for a visit to Normandy. Previous to their starting Mr. Ruskin gave an evening&rsquo;s talk about French manners and customs, telling us where to see and how to look at the grand architectural monuments of the old French towns. The trip was a great success, and the travellers came back describing with enthusiasm the rich scenery of the Seine, and the venerable glories of Rouen, Coutances, and Mont St. Michel. A photograph in the coffee-room by Mr. Jefferys, records the aspect of the group as they appeared in marching order, for the admiration of posterity. Another year a party spent a breezy week in and about the &ldquo; Vale of the White Horse,&rdquo; tasted the pleasures of campaigning by a bivouac under a hay-rick, and made experience of the hospitality with which the Berkshire farmers can greet the friends of their distinguished fellow-countryman, &ldquo; Tom Brown.&rdquo; Several parties of our students have explored in turn the sides and summit of Snowdon, enriching our museum on their return with mineral records of their route, the geologists verifying for themselves the scratching of ancient glaciers along the cliffs of the Welsh valleys, &rsquo;these are new pleasures opened to the million of our generation by the power of iron and steam. If the amount of physical comforts enjoyed by working people as compared with that of the richer classes is less than it was three hundred years ago, there is at least something to compensate for this in the immense extension of the scale of enjoyment. It seems but too clear that in the matter of beef and ale the farm labourer of to-day is worse off than the man who tilled the same fields in the days of Queen Bess. It is quite certain that he does not lead by any means so jolly a life as his ancestors ; any one who inquires into the question may be convinced that the &ldquo; Merry England of the olden time w&rsquo;as some&not; thing much more than a poetic fiction. Our ancestors sang and danced in a style which would have astonished us as much as it did their foreign visitors, who went away marvel&not; ling at the surpassing sweetness of their part-music and the general hilarity of the popu&not; lation, as shown in the Merry-go-round on the village green after Sunday evening service. But if our every-day life has become somewhat grimmer in colour than of old, the very powers that have darkened it have at the same time given us new means of enjoyment. When a poor man has a shilling to spare he has a choice of luxuries which the kings of the earth could not have compassed a few generations back. He may sit at home and read &lsquo; Waverley,&rsquo; or be carried to a palace to see the cartoons of Raphael, or go and listen to the &lsquo; Messiah.&rsquo; Half-a-crown will take a London artisan to his bath under the Sussex cliffs twice as quick as George IV. was whirled thither by his galloping greys : and for a few pounds a lawyer&rsquo;s clerk may breakfast in the Temple one day and dine at Lucerne the next. A man who neglects&#39;to take advantage of these things is not making the most of the opportunities of his age. Such of our students as have tried it have found that a run in the country is well worth the little money it costs, and for the benefit of those who
can afford to follow their example it has been suggested that a brief report of one of this year&rsquo;s excursions would not be amiss in a vacation number of the Magazine. It may serve at least asan illustration of a now constant (though episodic) feature of our College life, whereof these pages aspire to be the mirror. Three College friends, then, deter&not; mined to spend a week in exploring the beauties of a certain corner of North Pevon, of which Clovelly may be called the centre. At 9.15 p.m. on Monday, September 3rd, we alighted at Bideford station. Ten minutes afterwards we were contemplating the little white town from the bridge over the Taw, and watching the rush of the ebbing tide by the light of a quiet moon. Had been recommended to an inn bearing the sign of the &lsquo; Lissome Lamb,&rsquo; thus at least sounded the name in the Lingua Dev&oacute;nica of a travelling companion : it proved to be the &lsquo; Newfoundland.&rsquo; Seeking this hostelry, we went into one next-door to it, the &lsquo; Steam Packet,&rsquo; and got comfortably housed for the night. Concerning inns, and the economics of our journey, it is well to be explicit. By our experience 5i. per diem, judiciously expended, will provide for all needs. Beds at little country inns (which are, as a rule, quite as clean as the grandest hotels) cost from (Sd . to l&iacute;., a good meat breakfast never more than l&iacute;. 6d. or l&iacute;. 9 d. (without meat l&iacute;.), and supper for the like price. These two meals, with sixpenny worth of bread and cheese and beer in the day, make up the total. One or two little extravagances increased our outlay; on the whole it is safest to have a few shillings in reserve. The same scale may be reckoned on for North Wales, Surrey, the Home Counties, Herefordshire, and the Isle of Might {credeexpertis). In Dorset, Wilts, and Hampshire you may walk cheaper. A week in those counties last year cost three of our friends 15i. each for conveyance, and 15i. for living ! but this was a miracle of economy.&mdash;Tuesday, 4th, up at 6 ; by 8 were on the road towards Hartland, through pleasant hedgerows, with glimpses of the mouth of the Taw upon the right ; gradually neariug the sea, we struck into a farm road on the right near the fifth milestone, and were presently in the fields sloping down to the waterside, 400 feet below. Our first fair view of the Channel, very lovely; had a &ldquo;pitch&rdquo; on the downy turf, enjoying a good look at the blue water ; then down to the beach for a bathe. We found the pebbles heavy walking, and determined to take to the cliffs or the road. Here we made our first experience of a real climb. Picked a place which looked easy enough, as it was for 100 feet, bordering on the perpendicular, but with such thick growth of grass that this did not matter, till presently what seemed the top from below turned out to be the first shelf, leaving about 500 feet more to be climbed, through an im&not; mense growth. of underwood and brambles. P&mdash;, who had taken to a gully full of falling shale, found progress was impossible and resigned himself to a &ldquo;glissade,&rdquo; which landed him on the beach with a speed but too fatal to one portion of his wardrobe. R&mdash; and L meanwhile gradually found themselves more and more entangled in hopeless masses of wood and bramble, every separate tree taking some five minutes to scale, but consoled by lovely patches of golden moss and purple heather, till at last the long labour had an end with no worse result than rents and scratches. That hour&rsquo;s scramble impressed us with a considerable respect for Silurian cliffs. The landlord of the * Loggm&rsquo;s Arnjs * where we lunched on &ldquo; grass pie &rdquo; (a capital compound like salad served up hot with egg Sciuce) regarded our ascent as heroic if not mythical. Rabbits were apparently the only natives known to have scaled &ldquo; Peppercombe cliff.&rdquo; An hour&rsquo;s walk brought us to the d y k es &rdquo; or cam P s . above Clovelly ; here we met P&mdash;, and descended upon the place which had been the object of our journey. The loveliness of that scene as we dropped down on the little village, by deep masses of oak wood, stretching down to the edge of the blue water seen by peeps through the branches, and then by the oddest of rude stairs, facetiously called &ldquo; a street,&rdquo; the parlour window of every cottage looking down the chim&not; ney of its neighbour, and so with many breakneck stumblings to the beach, whence the vil&not; lage looks just a cluster of white cottages hung between wood and water.&mdash;These rude notes can give no vision of all that beauty ; I can only say, Go and see it : or second best, see the picture of it as painted by Hook&rsquo;s brush or Kingsley&rsquo;s pen. Lyiu&iquest; in the little harbour &rdquo; was a cutter of twenty-two tons, &ldquo; The Ranger.&rdquo; This we found was the only medium of communication between the civilized world and Lundy a rocky island nineteen miles out in the Channel Wednesday the 5th was the day of the weekly tnp, and after negotiating with Captain Robert Lee, we agreed to make the voyage, beven A.M found us accordingly making our way by the usual style of locomotion &rsquo;half- sliding, half-tumbling down the &ldquo;street &rdquo;&mdash;to the harbour. Light breezes gave us along voyage to and fro ; we spent the day in perfect happiness, fraternized with the captain.
interchanged songs with him and the crew (two A. B.s), and after four hours&rsquo; prowl about the island, exploring its granite cliffs and bathing on the beach, set out on our return. &ldquo; Tripped the anchor,&rdquo; I should say, for we were intensely nautical for the day, and laid in a great stock of seamanship ; held tremendous discussions on the differentia of a &ldquo; marling-spike&rdquo; and a &ldquo; belaying-pin coaxed &ldquo; Rory,&rdquo; the mate (an old salt so christened from the one song which formed his r&eacute;pertoire ), into dancing a hornpipe ; then, as twilight deepened into night, we improvised a bivouac on the &ldquo;larboard side&rdquo; of the quarter-deck, a &ldquo;jib-sheet&rdquo; and &ldquo; kedge-anchor&rdquo; supplying the place of bed and bolster, till after much merriment we dozed off to the music of the ripple under the &ldquo; gunnel.&rdquo; After half an hour of this lotus-eating serenity, the pier was announced to,&ldquo; bear two points on the weather-bow&#39;.&rdquo; R-, with his usual vivacity, instantly,&ldquo; stood by&rdquo; to &ldquo; let go the topsl&rsquo;alyrds,&rdquo; while the others displayed equal seamanship in getting out the jolly-boat, and after a few more evolutions the &ldquo;Ranger&rdquo; and ourselves were in our respective moorings at the top and bottom of Clovelly street. Thursday 6th. Started westward for Hartland, taking the path through the grounds of Clovelly Court. The house is a blank mass of stuccoed deformity, a sad successor to the old seat of the Cary&rsquo;s. But this cliff walk to Mouth Mill and Gallanty Bower leads through a perfect paradise of loveliness ; every three steps give you a new burst of beauty as you look down into deep woody gorges, or across at weather-stained heights, or into the blue depths of some little bay at your feet. Half the day we lingered to enjoy these lovely sights ; then striking up a &ldquo;combe,&rdquo; followed a stream that wound through the wood, and so to the coast on the other side of Hartland Point. We had a thought to return to Bideford, but the attraction of Clovelly was too strong, and we returned to the old haunt after a walk of about eighteen miles. The evening was spent in the tap-room of the &ldquo; New Inn,&rdquo; the only house of entertainment. Music helped us to fraternize with the natives, and in return for the &ldquo; Tight little Island &rdquo; and one or two more familiar ditties we got an old Cornish song of the true West country ring, about a local tragedy, in which one &ldquo;James Bazeley the unfor&not; tunate&rdquo; was reduced to the necessity of killing a &ldquo; swine that ofttimes did go astray,^,&ldquo; a thing which,&rdquo; said the poet, &ldquo; should warning be unto both young and old !&rdquo; This sad story was sung to a slow chant of correspondingly dismal character, the singer giving out the words of each line after the manner of a hymn. Iriday, the 7th, w r as our last morning at Clovelly. A touring photographer, whose acquaintance we had made took a picture of us sitting in a boat on the beach. After this, we tore ourselves away&rsquo;from the spot, and walked by &ldquo;the Hobby,&rdquo; a lovely drive cut through the woods and following the windings of the coast, to Bideford, thence that night by the banks of Torridge (sacred to the memory of Rose Salterne), to Torrington and Beaford. Slept here, and on the next day to Exeter, riding the last five miles by train. At Exeter,,p_left by a morning train to return to the workaday world of Hoiborn Hill. His,companions spent a day and a half more in a detour to the south coast, going twelve miles westward to Dunsford Bridge, a most romantic spot, thence by the valley of the Teign to Chudleigh, and the next day to Teignmouth. Here ended our pleasant walk. We had taken it quite easily, doing a little under eighty miles in four walking days. The accommodation was so good throughout that it would be superfluous to add infor- mation about inns. Any who follow us may, however, find the following Mews, useful. The Lundy boat sails on Wednesday : if you go there, take your dinner with you ; we found only salt pork on the island, and not enough of that, and a liquor which is beer only in name. If you want to get on with Clovelly folk, talk to them about Charles Kino-sley&mdash;his name will wake up the whole village. Mrs. Squire, the schoolmistress, was good enough to give us tea in her cottage on Mount Pleasant, a charming little perch at the top of the village, embosomed in fuchsia and myrtle. Make her acquaint&not; ance by all means. Another person well known to the people is Hook, the painter. The fishermen will recollect all his most notable pictures of late years. When you go into a villane inn, insist on being served in the public room ; they have a disagreea ne habit else of carrying you off into cold parlours, where you see nothing of the natives. Fried potatoes can generally be had for breakfast,&mdash;the natives eat them. lor the rest, you must make up your mind to live chiefly on bacon. We had no fresh meat between Sunday and Thursday. Whatever route you take, don&rsquo;t leave Clovelly till the last; you will find enough to detain you thereabouts some days, and you will come away like your predecessors in the profound conviction that there can&rsquo;t be a sweeter spot on the surface of this planet.
CORRESPONDENCE.,To the Editor.,... &ldquo;I most cordially agree with, your principles in the article on reading in the last number, amazed as I am at its application. I should not fear oifeuding you if I had time to tell you my thoughts about your advice to working men. I must protest against the ignoring by a disciple of Christ of the &ldquo;Word of Life,&rdquo; to which he continually re&not; ferred, not for doctrines only, but for rules of life. I know that you took this for granted, hut is that reasonable? Does one man in a hundred know anything about his Bible? Is there not there all that a man really wants, if he can only understand its teaching ? Is not all teaching valuable as a commentary upon that,&mdash;a development from it ? And in the name of working men I must claim it as the centre, the guide, the foundation of all their reading.&rdquo;&mdash;A. K.,[In case other readers may have been similarly struck by the omission here pointed out by a very old friend, I may as well say that it was intentional. The fact that &ldquo; not one man in a hundred knows anything about his Bible,&rdquo; otherwise, that is, than in a loose traditional fashion, seemed too grave and complex to he touched upon in such a paper as the one referred to, the more so as the subject is constantly kept before the minds of the students of our own College by the existence of a Bible Class. It occurred to me, more&not; over, that any who followed the recommendation to read Tennyson or Wordsworth, would find much in their pages to remind them of that other Book, which for so many ages has been the life, strength, and nourishment of the greatest of the world&rsquo;s teachers.&mdash;R. B. L.],NEWS.,Cambridge .&mdash;The annual Examination was held in June, 1860. The subjects were&mdash; Arithmetic, Algebra, Trigonometry, English, Latin, French, Biblical Knowledge, and English History. Two Certificates were awarded&mdash;English, H. J. Ansell: French, H. J. Ansell. &rsquo;,About the 20th of October the Anniversary Tea-Party will take place, and the business of the sixth year be commenced thereat. Shall we be favoured with the company of any gentlemen from other Colleges ?,Lancaster .&mdash;The attempt to amalgamate the Working Men&rsquo;s Society and the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute has been unsuccessful. The managing bodies of both were in favour of the union. A.t a general meeting of members of &quot;the Institute, the proposal was carried by a bare majority of three votes, but at a subsequent special meeting, was definitively re&not; jected, the general body of members thus reversing the judgment formed by their own Committee. This unpleasant difference appears to have been the result of misunder&not; standings and jealousies which have arisen in the course of the negotiations.,A crowded and enthusiastic meeting of the members of the Working Men&rsquo;s Society was held on the 26th inst., at which it was all but unanimously determined&mdash;only three dissentients&mdash;to carry on their operations as a distinct Society. An appeal to the public, and a statement explaining the course they have been compelled to adopt, will appear in a day or two. . It is to be hoped that the movement will result in a permanent and efficient organization of the working men for mutual improvement and recreation, and that some men of education will come forward to do their share of the work. &rsquo; The ground is now open for the establishment of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, if the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; will accept the name. Many wish for it.,Wolverhampton .&mdash;On Monday, Sept. 10, the Council of Teachers invited the stu&not; dents, with their wives and friends, and other friends of the College, to tea in St Peter&rsquo;s School-Room, kindly lent by the Rector, the Rev. J. H. lies, a warm and steady heiner in the College work. J r,About 180 Mends met together, and amongst them we were right glad to see Lord Lyttelton, who, having to preside at a meeting in the town, came earlier on purpose to be present, and the Rev. F. D. Maurice, whose name has long been so familiar among us, but who had never before been able to come to us. &deg;
After tea, the Rector called upon our newly-formed Glee Class to give us a proof of their efficiency, which they did very satisfactorily, and the Secretary, Mr. Langley, in&not; troduced to the meeting our loved and honoured friend Mr. Maurice.,The students showed their hearty appreciation of what he has done by giving him a most cordial welcome, and Mr. Maurice, in an address of about half an hour&rsquo;s length, gave us all some weighty words of counsel, which we hope will not soon be forgotten. At half-past seven, the meeting adjourned to the Exchange, when Lord Lyttelton pre&not; sented the Prizes and Certificates to the successful candidates from South Staffordshire at the last local examination conducted by the Society of Arts. Among these were seven of our own students, who have gained certificates, though all of the second or third class. At this meeting, Mr. Maurice and other friends of our College spoke. These Society of Arts&rsquo; Examinations are admirably adapted to our requirements, and we hope they will lead to the formation of Classes in many of the towns and villages of this populous district.,Our College commences its fourth year&rsquo;s existence and work on Monday, October 8th, under hopeful and encouraging auspices. We hope to send up several candidates for the next Examinations of the Society of Arts, and to carry off our fair share of first-class certificates. I. N. NOVEMBER 1, 1860.,HISTORY OF THE LONDON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,(Continued from page 148 &middot;),&ldquo; With respect to boys, I think we shall find the great advantage of forming our College first and the school afterwards. For then the school will be always looking up to the College and be a preparation for it. We may not introduce any novelties into the teaching of reading, writing, and arithmetic, far less reckon that insignificant teaching. But if we recollect that we are framing our boys to live together as members of a society, as fellow-workers, there will be, I conceive, a spirit and a method in our edu&not; cation of them, which are not exactly what we find, or expect to find, even in the best of our national and Lancastrian schools.,&ldquo;6. I now come to the last subject, that of self-support and self-govern&not; ment. In my sense of the words, I feel the importance of both most deeply. But I am not sure whether my sense is that of the Committee ; it is certainly not that of our precursors at Sheffield. I should most strongly object to any interference of patrons, ecclesiastical or civil, and of the out&not; lying public, secular or religious, with the arrangement of our studies or with the subsequent management of the College. And if we do not mean to submit to their dictation, we must not ask for their help. We must say plainly, &pound; This is what we are willing to offer. If you like to have it, well; if not, go elsewhere.&rsquo; I perfectly agree therefore in the Sheffield principle, that the education should be paid for, and that we should not incur any expenses which the payments of the pupils will not nearly meet. I wish also that the pupils should feel themselves a part of the College, adopted into it from the first, any of them capable of holding offices in it hereafter. But I would not let them have the least voice in determining what we shall teach or not teach, or how we shall teach. We may have social meetings with them ; we may have conversations with them indi&not; vidually ; but no education will go on if we have general tumultuous as&not; semblies to discuss what has been done or what is to be done. We who begin the institution must claim authority over it, and not hastily resign our authority, however we may admit others by degrees to share it, and however willing we may be to creep out of it when the institution can stand without us. That we may preserve this position, I believe it is necessary that our teaching should be gratuitous ; all the fees going at first to the procuring of the necessary machinery for the institution.&rdquo;,On this last point of self-government, strong opposition was made by Mr. Ludlow, myself, and others, who wished that the governing body of the College should from the first consist of equal numbers of teachers and working men ; but it was settled that only teachers should be on it at first, and that gradually working men should be admitted to the extent of one- third of the whole number. At the end of our sixth year we have now two
of them, one-fourteenth of our whole number ; but the finance of the Col&not; lege is practically controlled by students only.,But I am anticipating. After several discussions of Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s plan, a circular, of which the following is a later and enlarged copy, was sent out :&mdash;,&ldquo; The Working Men of England are trying, from various motives and in various ways, to educate themselves. Some of them hope that their class may obtain greater influence in the Legislature. They desire that it should qualify itself for that position by the study of Laws and of History. Some of them think that there are many maxims of morality current among us which tend to divide and to degrade them. They wish to find out the true principle which binds them together aud shows them what objects they are to live for. Some are impressed strongly with the mischiefs that come to them from their ignorance of the causes which produce disease, and of the best means of securing health. Some wish to understand better the machinery with which they are working. Some feel what a blessing it would be to them if they could use their voices in Singing, and their hands iu Drawing. Some are puzzled with a number of doubts about the world within them and without them, which they dare not stifle, and through which they long to see their way.,&ldquo;Workingmen have, therefore, established Debating Societies and Societies for Mutual Instruetion among themselves ; or they have profited by the Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes and Evening Classes, which others have established for them. Of late in Sheffield, (and we believe also in Nottingham,) some of them have set up a People&rsquo;s College, whejre they hope for more thorough instruction than they can get by either merely talking together, or by merely attending lectures on miscellaneous subjects.,&ldquo; This thought, it seems to us, is very valuable. The name College is an old and venera&not; ble one. It implies a Society for fellow work, a Society of which teachers and learners are equally members, a Society in which men are not held together by the bond of buy&not; ing and selling, a Society in which they meet not as belonging to a class or a caste, but as having a common life which God has given them and which He will cultivate in them.,&ldquo; The hint which our friends in the North have given us must be good for us in London, though we should use it in our own way, and not slavishly copy their plans. Some of us who belong to Professions, as Lawyers, Physicians, Ministers of the Gospel, Artists, Schoolmasters, have thought that we might fulfil our own duties far better, and under&not; stand better how they bear upon each other, if we could come into closer intercourse with the Working Men,&mdash;if we could bring them into a College with us, if we could spend some of our eveuings in helping them to set their thoughts in order on the sub&not; jects in which they are most interested, and to gain information on points about which they feel ignorant.,&ldquo; Such Colleges we wish to see established in every great Town of England,&mdash;at least half-a-dozen of them in London. Ours is only a beginning. It is meant especially for the North of London. We have taken a house,&mdash;31, Red Lion Square,&mdash;which we think will be convenient for those who work in that neighbourhood.,&ldquo; Our College consists of a Principal, a Council of Teachers, and Students.,&ldquo; The government and management of the affairs of the College is vested in the Principal and Council of Teachers, with a casting vote in the Principal. In case of a vacancy the Principal is elected by the Council of Teachers, and Teachers are elected by the Council of Teachers and the Principal.,&ldquo; Any Working Man above sixteen years of age who can read and write, and knows the first four rules of Arithmetic, is eligible as a student.,&ldquo;The College is established especially for Manual Workers ; but we shall refuse no one who is not ashamed to be called a Working Man, to whatever class he may belong. Possibly very plain gowns may be provided to be worn in the Classes, both by Teachers and Scholars, as a sign that they all belong to the same Society, and for the convenience of men coming in from their work.,&ldquo; We desire to be in communication with Adult Schools which may prepare any who have not at present the necessary qualifications to be students of the College.,&ldquo;We desire, also, to be in communication with the existing Universities, which, we hope, will receive persons coming with Certificates from our College as readily as from any
other, and will grant our Students Degrees, provided they go through the necessary Examinations. How the Universities of Oxford, Cambridge, or Loudon, may be dis&not; posed to accomplish this object, we cannot, of course, foresee ; but we are confident that no fees will hinder Working Men from having all the advantages which their fellow- countrymen possess.,&ldquo; Though the education which we propose is especially intended for men who have had some experience of the world and are taking part in its business, we hope ultimately to provide teaching both for their wives and children.,&ldquo;We hope to see the time when every Factory and Shop will make provision for the instruction of its workers in the course of the day ; but at present our teaching must necessarily be confined to the evenings.,&ldquo; The subjects which we propose to treat of are these :&mdash;,&ldquo; (1.) On one evening of the week we shall speak of Politics, including under that name questions that refer to Law, Social, or, as it is called, Political Economy, Home and Foreign Policy. Starting from the topics that are most interesting to men in this day, we shall show how they are connected with the Ristory of our own country and of other countries. This History would necessarily lead on to Geography.,&ldquo; (2.) On another evening we shall have lessons on Language, beginning always with our own. We shall speak of the sources from which its words come : and of its Gram&not; mar. These lessons can only be profitable if they are illustrated from good books. Hence they will lead to lessons on English Literature, and those to lessons on Reading and Elocution. Hereafter, if any persons should wish for lessons in Latin, or French, or German, the opportunity will be given them.,&ldquo; (3.) On some evening of the week we shall have lessons in Music, and in another room on Hr awing. Though we do not design oui- College for a place of amusement, the Music Lessons will probably lead to occasional Concerts as part of the instruction.,&ldquo; (4.) One evening in the week will be given to lessons concerning the Human Frame and Laws of Health.,&ldquo; (5.) Another will be devoted to the subjects which most concern the employments m which Working Men are engaged,&mdash;to Machinery, to Geometry, to Arithmetic, and Hook-keeping.,&ldquo; (6 ) One evening will be given to some branch of Natural Science, &mdash; Chemistry, Geology, Mineralogy, for instance.,&ldquo; (7.) On the Sunday evenings there will always be a Class for the study of the Bible. Out of this may arise classes on Christian Morals, as applied to common life, and on the relation between Divinity and Natural Science.,&ldquo;Out of these evenings the Working Man will be able to take his choice of the subject or subjects which most interest him. We regard them as closely connected, and shall talk them over together, that our different lessons may illustrate each other.,&ldquo; There will be Four Periods of Instruction, or, as they are called in other Colleges, Terms, in each year. The first will be from November to Christmas ; the second, from the beginning of January to Easter ; the third, from Easter to Whitsuntide ; the fourth, from Whitsuntide to the latter end of July. The remainder of the year will be Vacation!,&ldquo; Before the beginning of every Term a Programme will be published of the Subjects to be treated of during the Term, and of the persons who will give the Lessons.,&ldquo;We call them Lessons rather than Lectures, because the Teachers will not aim chiefly at giving discussions to the Pupils, but will explain books to them,&mdash;will ask them questions, and will answer their questions.,&ldquo; The first Teachers of the College will give their Lessons gratuitously ; but there will, of course, be many expenses attending it from the beginning: and we hope ultimately, to raise up Teachers from among the Working Men themselves, who must receive regular salaries.,&ldquo;The Entrance Fee to the College for each Student wall be 2s. 6d. The exact Fee for each Course will be stated in the programme published before each Terra, and will vary from 2s. 6 d. to 5s. a Term. The Sunday lessons will be free. A Library is in course of formation.&rdquo; J,The house in Eed Lion Square was on Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s hands, having been vacated about a year before by the Needlewomen&rsquo;s Association, which had failed after some &pound;500 had been spent on it. As new fittings were,n 2 ■
necessary, a discussion as to Avays and means was held, and the old friends who had helped us before&mdash;Lord Goderich, the Hon. W. Cowper, C. Bux&not; ton, Esq., W. Powell, Esq., etc. etc.,&mdash;were appealed to, and gave us fresh subscriptions, and Mr. Maurice volunteered a course of five Lectures on Learning and Working, both to get money for and give publicity to our scheme. The Lectures were given in Willis&rsquo;s Rooms fortnightly, beginning on Thursday, June 8, 1854, and were afterwards published. They brought in &pound;87. 14s. to our funds, and no doubt interested many people in the Col&not; lege proposed. Then we had to look for students, and so we went round in parties of two or three to Trade Societies&rsquo; Committees, and tried to per&not; suade them to bring the matter before their trades,&mdash;but without much success, I fear. Mr. Walter Cooper though found some strong-shoemakers, I remember, who wanted to learn English Grammar, and them I asked for as pupils.,With the view of getting opinions from men interested in education, as to what we ought to teach, and howto teach it, we had a Conversazione in the College house, 31, Red Ljon Square, on the 25th of July. The only recollection I have of it is, that Mr, Wm. Spottiswoode told us that we must look on a College in London as quite an experiment, and that if we failed in it, still we ought to be satisfied, as it was the right thing to try, and failure would not be our fault. Soon after this we separated for the long vacation, and on coming up to town in October, I found that Mr. Shorter had received no instructions about our opening, and that he thought it was to be deterred till the new year. Not satisfied with this, Mr. Llewelyn Davies and I&mdash;one evening, in his dining-room at the Rectory of St. Mark&rsquo;s, Whitechapel &mdash; drew up a Trial-programme of Classes, and arranged a Teachers&rsquo;meeting.* It was at first proposed that the opening Address should be given at the College, or the Hall of Association, but we afterwards con&not; sidered that the publicity to be gained for our scheme by taking St. Mar&not; tin&rsquo;s Hall for a public meeting, and billing the North of London well, would justify an outlay of &pound;15 or &pound;20 better than advertisements in the papers, and we therefore took this Hall for Monday, the 30th of October. We had a full meeting. As our visitors came up the stairs, each received a copy of Mr. Ruskin&rsquo;s eloquent and noble chapter on the Nature of Gothic Architecture, and lierein of the Functions of the Workman in Art , which he and his publishers kindly allowed me to reprint, for the purpose of showing what sort of fellow one of our Teachers was. What another was like, they judged of by the written Address which Mr, Maurice, as our Principal, delivered, almost all of which was printed in the Morning Herald next day by our friend, Professor Brewer. At the end of the Address, ques&not; tions were asked us, and these were answered by Mr. Yansittart Neale and others of our Teachers. The next evening was fixed for our opening. How many pupils were we to have P We had often talked over our chance of get&not; ting men, and had always agreed that if thirty would start with us, we should consider it a success. But as we sat for six matriculation evenings, set&not; ting sums (in words) to candidates for admission, to test their acquaintance with Reading, Writing, and Arithmetic, we soon found our desired number of thirty overpassed, and by the night we really began work, we had 120.,* To our great delight came a letter from Mr. Ruskin &mdash;to whom I had sent a Circular in the hope of getting a subscription&mdash;offering to join us and take a Drawing Class.

[This Class was not opened,,applicants being referred to Mr. Hullah.],Some time ago I asked one of the oldest and most valued of our stu&not; dents, often called the Father of the College, what he thought of the Col&not; lege when he joined it. &ldquo; He said, Well, I&rsquo;d read the circular, and thought I&rsquo;d go and see what the place looked like ; so I went to the house, and saw just the plain benches, and boards or trestles for tables, and a few good books on the shelves. No show, and looks like business, I thought ; I shall join. And I entered your class. You came in and said you didn&rsquo;t pretend to be much of a teacher , as you&rsquo;d never taught before, and had all your business to learn, but you&rsquo;d worked a good deal at English, and were game to help any one who&rsquo;d worked less, and at any rate you hoped we should be good friends, and get on together. This suited me, tho&rsquo; you had a lot of most curious notions about words and grammar. I took to Mr. Mau&not; rice, Eossiter, and other fellows, and have stuck to them and the place ever,The word &lsquo; together did, and does, I do believe, express the spirit in which we desired, and desire to work, notwithstanding all our differences in opinion and the skirmishes proceeding therefrom, however sharp the lat&not; ter may sometimes have been. None of us, I am sure, who have been in the College life, can look into the eyes of the generous and hearty friends around him at any College gathering, or think of those away, without deep joy and thankfulness for having been allowed a share in our work.,But to proceed. Our second Term brought us an increase of Teachers &middot; among others Mr. Watson, now one of the Masters of Harrow, for Mechanics &middot; and M. Talandiei, a French republican and barrister, who, though dependino&middot; only on what he earned by teaching, would teach our French classes gra&not; tuitously. (Some of us were sorry to part with him afterwards, whenhe as people thought, spoke insultingly of our Queen for kissing Louis Napo &rsquo; le&oacute;n.) Then Mr. Irving, the only son of the prophet Edward Irving,,This class was split into two, of which one was taken by Mr, Litchfield.
now a professor of Latin in one of the Universities in Australia,&mdash;the quickest and most brilliant man, I think, that has ever been on our list, and one much liked by his pupils. Mr. Dante Rossetti, the head of the Pre- Raffaelite school, and the greatest living colourist ; Mr. Dickinson, one of our present teachers of Drawing ; and Mr, Yansittart Neale, whose class for Political Economy was never formed, two men only asking about it, and wanting to know whether it had anything to do with Politics. Our stu&not; dents numbered 155. Among those who joined in the first Term were two of the three whose names are most honoured among us, Mr. Eossiter and Mr. Roebuck ; for the third we had to wait till our second year, and then came Mr. Tansley,&mdash;men fit to stand by the side of Charles Mansfield and Walsh, the best and purest, who presently were taken away from us and from this world.,F. Furnivall.,(To be concluded in our next),THE MUSEUM OF THE LONDON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt;,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,A museum has two components ; an aggregation of specimens and the result of a large amount of labour. The item labour distinguishes a mu&not; seum from a collection. The former is a great power, showing beauty and speaking truth ; but the latter dumb, and, except for what it may become, valueless.,In proportion as a museum epitomizes all knowledge of its own sub&not; jects, so far is it succeeding only in its functions : in proportion however as it only brings together that which requires knowledge to make it avail&not; able, in so much is it failing; to be healthy, it must ever do both.,The use of a museum must depend both on its own organization and on the training those who resort to it have gone through. The modern me&not; thod of reading natural-science subjects admits of a threefold subdivision. Men first get a general idea of the scope of a study, then do a certain amount of general practical work, and conclude by studying in detail both practice and theory. A museum should give direct aid in the first and last of these stages : in the first, one part should illustrate characteristic features ; in the last, another part should illustrate things met with in minute study. It is only when these subdivisions are exhibited that a museum is capable of being wholly useful . A museum should be a student&rsquo;s daily tutor ; in those studies which it illustrates, progress without it is in most cases im&not; possible.,Before the College Museum exhibits these conditions, much must be done. As might have been expected,* the collection has increased with marvellous rapidity. This, the close of its fourth year, finds it with about 6,500 fossils; a year and a half ago it had but 2,000. About 1,300 specimens are mounted, illustrating many hundred species. It is interesting that the bulk of this vast assemblage has been given by students, and the fact may be taken as a pledge that were they only properly directed, we should speedily,* See Report, p. 81, Mag. Voi. I.
have a museum all might be proud of; but very few understand the neces&not; sary conditions. Museums neither grow, nor are they created ; they, like most other things worth having, are only gotten by steady, earnest labour. That a man is immensely the better for doing this kind of work, no one can doubt,&mdash;getting introduced as he does to a new and vaster world, where his sympathies may expand, and working in turns by every method of study. It is moreover a cheerful social labour, which to many would be the hap&not; piest of recreations. There must surely be many in the College who, if asked, would rejoice at the chance of helping in the work of the College Museum. To such the lists of &ldquo; unmounted &rdquo; will be an invitation ; the lists of mounted, a help in their work. The labour that awaits them seems large, but method and industry may in a short time reduce it within reasonable limits. Those who have worked hitherto can assure them that they have passed no happier hours than those devoted to the Museum. This should be an inducement. Time, change, and circumstances have withdrawn several of these from the work. The rifle corps has crippled this as most other departments of the College. Let the Geology Class now starting, furnish a fresh race of curators. SEELEY,,(Late Student of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, now Assistant at the Cambridge Geological Museum.),SOCIAL MEETINGS.,A correspondent from one of the country Colleges inquires how the weekly Social Meetings in the London College are conducted. They would be glad, he says, at his, as also probably at several other Colleges, to set something of the sort on foot. The arrangements at these meetings are of a nature almost too simple to need description ; still, as they have been found to serve an important end, and have been a prominent feature of our College life, it may perhaps be useful to devote a few lines to the subject. The College has always had a coffee-room, intended originally as a conve&not; nience to students coming direct from their work to their class. This, it was found, did not produce so much social intercourse as was expected ; but an agreeable custom sprang up, of having what we called &quot; class-teas,&rdquo; i.e. the members of a class would occasionally agree to spend a social evening together,&mdash;a Lecture or Reading on some miscellaneous subject (open to the whole College) serving to give additional interest to the meeting. This plan was found to be so useful in introducing men to their fellow-students, that it was agreed about a couple of years ago to devote one evening in the week to the special purpose of promoting this friendly intercourse. Mr. Hughes undertook to preside at the meetings, and they were forthwith es&not; tablished. The arrangement is nothing more than that the coffee-room is open on a particular evening to whoever likes to come up and join in friendly talk. Tea and bread-and-butter {ad lib.) are provided at sixpence a head. No attempt is made to turn the meeting into a discussion class. Its object is to enable men to know each other. Two students of like tastes and of tempers favourable to fraternization, might meet week after week in the library or class-room, and would not have much chance of improving their mere acquaintance into a friendship. An evening at the Social Meet&not; ing gives such men an opportunity of coming closer together, and bringing
them in contact with others whom perhaps they would otherwise never see at all. These gatherings have usually been not more numerous than to allow of the conversation being general ; about twelve to twenty being in the room between the hours of eight and ten. After this time, the regular classes for the night having broken up, the party becomes larger ; and it has been an understanding that from ten o&rsquo;clock onwards, any grave dis&not; cussion or general talk should give way to proceedings of a lighter and more free-and-easy character : any one, that is, who likes may then light his pipe, and with this indulgence comes the accompaniment of music. Songs are asked for and given, or in the intervals of these some one recites a bit of Tennyson or Shakspeare, till, about half-way between eleven and twelve o&rsquo;clock, a general chorus concludes the evening&rsquo;s entertainment. Our musical and literary tastes have run chiefly in a patriotic or national groove. This is perhaps partly due to the prevailing sentiment of the day. The songs that have pleased best have been of the old English type.* The poetry, too, most often heard is of the warlike or heroic sort. The &ldquo; Crispin&rdquo; speech of Henry V. on the eve of Agincourt, the Wellington Ode of Tennyson, and Macaulay&rsquo;s &lsquo; Armada,&rsquo; are the greatest favourites. Next to them, the lyrics of Kingsley are most in demand. These gatherings were interrupted by the rifle movement, being for a time merged in the general meetings held during the fonnation of the corps. They are now about to be resumed and placed on their old footing. During the past Vacation Terms similar meetings have been held, with the difference that the reading of some notable piece of prose or poetry from a great author was made a chief feature of the evening&rsquo;s employment. One night we read a chapter from Carlyle s c French Revolution,&rsquo; describing the storming of the Tuileries on the 10th of August ; on another, a chapter from Mill&rsquo;s &lsquo; Liberty ;&rsquo; and on the next, Tennyson&rsquo;s &lsquo; Guinevere.&rsquo; This was found to be very pleasant, and will most likely be continued.,R. B. Swann, Flack, G. Henry W. Henry, Monel.,W. Henry.,Passed &amp;gt; with Credit.,&ldquo;Light, more Light!&rdquo; By James Hole, Hon. Sec. of the Yorkshire Union of Mechanics&rsquo;Institutes. Longmans. 1860.,The little book to which these last words of Goethe serve as a title or motto is a statistical account of the present state of education among the working classes of Leeds. It is interesting as giving an estimate of the sum total of educational effort in a place which may be taken as a speci&not; men (rather a favourable one perhaps) of our great provincial towns. Mr. Hole gives an account of what Day, Sunday, and Evening Schools, Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes, Pauper and Criminal Education, Industrial and Bagged Schools, Savings-Banks and Cheap Concerts, and Popular Lite&not; rature are actually doing, and discusses their various difficulties and defi&not; ciencies. Referring to the valuable measure of last session (then unfor&not; tunately unsuccessful, but all-important as a preliminary to a great legis&not; lative scheme), forbidding the employment of children under twelve who cannot read and write, he quotes the following proof of the effect of an educational test in overcoming the apathy and neglect of parents. A factory inspector says :&mdash;,&ldquo; Having had occasion to request certain certifying surgeons to test the educational powers of the children and young persons of between eight and sixteen years of age, who presented themselves for examination for surgical certificates, and a supposition getting abroad that shortly no child would be allowed to work in the mills that could not read,&mdash; from that moment the applicants for admission to the schools was so great in the district, that within a fortnight all its schools were filled.&rdquo;,Among the many interesting appendices is one showing the circulation of periodicals in Leeds, from which may be gathered an idea of the com&not; parative diffusion of various kinds of light literature. Of 19,727 weekly publications, the &lsquo;London Journal&rsquo; counts for 4,234; &lsquo;Family Herald,&rsquo; 2,834; &lsquo; Cassell&rsquo;s Paper,&rsquo; 3,046; &lsquo;All the Year Round,&rsquo; 439 (and 117 monthly), and the &lsquo;Reas&ouml;ner,&rsquo; 31. Leeds, it appears, takes in 8,000 Re&not; ligious, and 15,000 Temperance monthlies; 119 copies of the Times,,his Three Sons,&rdquo; et hoc genus omne, are always welcomed with applause ; occasionally some one ventures on a sentimental ballad of the period, but such are nearly sure to he received with some sarcastic remark, which shows the hearers, know what good music is.
7 of the &lsquo;Morning Herald,&rsquo; 176 of the &lsquo;Revival,&rsquo; 320 of the &lsquo;Illus&not; trated,&rsquo; 106 of &lsquo;Macmillan&rsquo;s,&rsquo; and 700 of the &lsquo; CornhilL&rsquo; What is there here of the flood of infidel and immoral publications which Exeter Hall denounces so loudly ?,NEWS.,Cambridge .&mdash;The annual gathering of members of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; took place in the Town-hall on Tuesday, October 23.,The chair was taken by the Very Rev. the Dean of Ely (Dr. Goodwin), Principal of the College. Many members of the University were present. After tea, the Chairman proceeded to address the meeting. His speech ranged over a variety of topics connected with the work of the College, its present and past condition, the certificates, examina&not; tions, and the list of classes for the current term, the first of the sixth year. With regard to the volunteer movement, in which the College has taken some part, many of its members belonging to the 8th Cambridgeshire Rifles, the Dean remarked that it was a matter of congratulation that the working men of this country had not been excluded from the movement. It struck him that at the present time there was no country in Europe but our own which would look with any degree of satisfaction on their working men carrying rifles. No one here looked on the movement either with fear or jealousy ; and the fact of the working men taking so prominent a part in it went to show that those working men knew the interest of their country as well and were as proud of her as the highest-horn noble in the land. (Cheers.) The object, he continued, for which their college was established was not merely that they should be taught so much arithmetic, or so much Latin, or so much Euclid, or so much anything else; but also to put on a more sociable footing the several classes of the town, and this it had in some degree effected. Look how the members of the College were regarded, with respect to their social position ; look how they regarded others ; and look how they regarded them&not; selves ! And he fancied that this was brought about in a great measure by such social gatherings as the present. (Cheers.) They were the means of doing a great amount of good. He had great faith in arithmetic, he had great faith in grammar, he had great faith in Euclid, and all the rest of it ; but he had also very great faith in tea. (Laughter.) He did not wish to draw any invidious comparisons between tea and coffee ; but he adopted tea as the most suitable. He believed its social qualities would not be over&not; estimated. It was a matter of great importance that we should be brought together over our tea, that we should look each other in the face, and talk to each other &amp;amp; (Cheers ) The Dean then went on to speak of the condition of the Ely College, and announced that Mr. Clark, the Public Orator, having witnessed Garibaldi&rsquo;s entry into Naples, would give a lecture on that popular subject on November 2nd. He then presented the certificates to the successful candidates. The meeting was afterwards addressed chiefly on matters of business by the Hon. Secretaries, Mr. Gray, Mr. Hadley, and Mr. Macmillan and by Mr. Emery, the successor of Dr. Goodwin as teacher of the Bible Class on the&rsquo;sub,ject of Biblical study.
