Lancaster . &mdash;The &ldquo; Working Men&rsquo;s Mutual Improvement and Recreation Society,&rdquo; having started on an independent footing after the failure of the attempt to consolidate it with the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, continues to flourish as well as possible &ldquo;We of course expected,&rdquo; writes one of its founders, &ldquo; a great falling olf in numbers when the out-door sports were over, but our members are now paying up and coming amongst us in a style that is as gratifying as unexpected. The final &lsquo; split &rsquo; with the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution and the satisfaction of having an honest Society of their own calls forth a great amount of earnestness and brotherly zeal. We have taken a convenient house, and got classes and Lectures in operation. These, as well as the recreation and news-room, are well attended. We teach one another as we have ability, and the best possible feeling pervades the whole Society. We have no paid teachers. The best educated among the working men teach Reading, Writing, and Ciphering five nights in the week. I give a Lecture on English History once a week. The editor of the * Lancaster Guardian &rsquo; is about to do the same, and the Secretary of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, who intends resigning his office there next mouth, will then begin a class for English Literature. A dissenting minister in the town will also probably soon commence a class for English Grammar. We have now about 200 members.&rdquo;,At a meeting of the Society on October 3rd, Mr. Solly brought before the members the question whether they should continue under their present name, or adopt the name of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. He said they were aware he had a strong conviction that the latter was a very valuable name, and one destined to -be of great service in this country. But everything depended on its being thoroughly understood and heartily taken up by the working men themselves, as expressive of their purpose to promote each other&rsquo;s improve&not; ment and happiness, and to seek the instruction of those better educated than themselves. He then read the following letter on the subject from one of the Council of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; in London; but he was anxious they should understand he did not in the least desire to urge on them the adoption of the title in question, unless they felt heartily prepared to adopt it :&mdash;,&ldquo; Dear Mr. Solly,&mdash;I think as time goes on you will be more and more confirmed in your opinion that the name, &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, is of importance. I shall be very sorry to hear that you have been unable to give this title to your institution. None else, I am sure, so well embodies the purpose and principles which are actuating you. The word College has the great merit of being at once old and new. It represents the principle of union which has been at the bottom of all social progress, and at the same time challenges people&rsquo;s attention to the new aspect and application given to the prin&not; ciple. The other title suggested seems to me objectionable on special grounds. For instance, Working Men&rsquo;s Mutual Improvement Society. If a name says as much as that, surely it ought to say more, something to imply that the improvement is to be mutual, not only as between one man and another of the same class, but as between men of different classes. Of the two this is surely the most important kind of mutuality.,It is no affectation to say that in our College, at least, the teachers know that the work does them as much good as it does the students. This recognition of the mutual respon&not; sibilities of various classes, separated as they have hitherto been by mere accidents of occupation and position, is the very most important business of a College. The double title, &lsquo; Mechanics&rsquo; Institute and &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,&rsquo; involves either a redundance or something like a contradiction. If the first half of the title be taken literally, it is in&not; cluded in the second. But custom and experience have made the words &lsquo; Mechanics&rsquo; Institute&rsquo; mean something very different from what the originators of the institutes in&not; tended, and to recover or retain the essence of the thing they aimed at, we are almost obliged to take another name to mark this difference. The good done by the institutes has been immense. The promoters of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; have been the first to acknowledge this. Where they have failed or come short of their original aims, it has been, we believe, from a want of sufficient recognition of the great principle of human fellowship, which must be the ground of all healthy and vigorous action.,&ldquo; We are never tired of reiterating this, because we know that the more men get to believe in it the readier they will be to acknowledge the mutual responsibilities of various classes, fand the equal right of all to whatever intellectual advantages they can get. Of this principle we mean the word College to be a symbol ; and no one can doubt that to have a purpose clearly symbolized in a word is a great aid towards working the pm pose out.&rdquo;
Manchester .&mdash;The usual general public meetiug of the Council and students, pre&not; paratory to the commencement of the new Term, has been held in the rooms of the College, at the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, David-street. It was very numerously attended. Professor Greenwood, who, in the absence of the chairman of the Council (Mr. 0. Heywood), presided, said that during the Long Vacation he and his colleague, Mr. A. Ransome (the Council agreeing with the wish of the students), had inquired in several quarters as to some more suitable locality for the College, but they had not yet been successful. They hoped, however, in the course of the next two months to be more for&not; tunate. The neighbourhood of Grosvenor-street would be preferred. One of the most important measures of last session was the formation of a Students&rsquo; Committee, and one of the last recommendations of that Committee was that there should be French and German classes, but as there were good classes of this kind in connection with that In&not; stitution, the Council could not entertain the subject at present. It was proposed to establish new classes in Geology, Logic, and English Grammar. Several of the teachers afterwards explained the course of study which they purposed to pursue in the ensuing session in their respective classes.,Liverpool .&mdash;A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; has been started in this place. It is at present in connection with the Mersey Docks Literary Institute, a society which has recently established itself in new rooms in Rathbone-street to meet the necessities occasioned by a large increase in the number of its members. At the first meeting held in the new rooms, the arrangements for the College classes were announced by Mr. Bright. He hoped, he said, that it would be w r ell supported, and the hard-handed mechanic of Liver&not; pool placed in as good a position, in reference to educational advantages, as was the me&not; chanic of other towns where similar institutions were in progress. He should be happy to give one night in each week to their service, to teach one of the classes in the pro&not; posed College; and, if the members would attend, he would pledge himself that his post should never be vacant by any neglect on his part. Several other gentlemen, of the highest attainments, had pledged themselves to do the same, and he hoped the members would respond to that generous example, and would fulfil their part of the general duty. In a note addressed, October 17th, to a member of our London Council&rsquo; Mr. Bright says &ldquo;Our W. M. C. fairly starts next week. Mr. Walter Arnold takes a Class&rdquo;in Geography, and Mr. Percy Greg one in Political Economy. We have also classes in History, Arithmetic, and Natural Philosophy. If the thing succeeds, it will be in a great measure due to you, for certainly without having seen and heard from you of the working of your large College, I should never have ventured on our small one.&rdquo;,London&mdash; The usual meeting in inauguration of the College year, the seventh from its foundation, took place on October 25th. There was a large attendance of students and teachers. Every teacher shall examine his class at the end of each Term, in the work of the Term, either in or out of the regular class hours, and either orally or by writing.,2. At the end of every complete course an Examination shall be held in the subject of the course.,3. A part, at least, of every such Examination shall be oral, and the Council shall seek to procure the services of such Examiners as will undertake to attend personally ; the duty of orally examining to be discharged by the last teacher of the class if no other Examiner can be obtained.,4. At each Examination at the end of a course, certificates of competence shall be awarded to those who shall merit them ; and the names of those who, though not re&not; ceiving such certificates, shall have passed the examination with credit, shall be posted in the hall of the College.,5. Every student attending any course is expected to attend the Examination at the close of that course or the next similar Examination ; and any student having passed any such Examination with credit may olier himself at the next following Examination lor a certificate of competence.,6. Special Examinations for certificates of competence shall be discontinued, unless the Council shall at any time order such an Examination after particular consideration of the circumstances under which it shall be applied for.,PRACTICE CLASSES. THE,SH&oslash;rlmtg Ut&oacute; C&oslash;llege Рщш.,No. XXIY. DECEMBER 1, 1860.,ON THE STUDY OE SHAKSPERE.,f,Reading books which are not professedly books of instruction may un&not; doubtedly avail much towards our education, but all depends on what and how we read. Putting aside the quality of the books, and admitting them to be good books of their kind, do we read them hastily, for amusement or excitement, to while away the hour? Then we shall not get much from them. The hour may be whiled away, and some hours may be so inno&not; cently ; but by such voluptuous reading no lasting or valuable impressions are gained, simply because the mind is idle ; and if we read much in this fashion, we positively lose,&mdash;precious time, precious energy, and at last even plain common self-respect.,However needful to many may be this consideration of the pernicious habit of dissipated reading, it is not perhaps the counsel which the stu&not; dents of our College stand most in need of. What they want is encourage&not; ment to read, help in reading. I therefore write this paper on the reading or study of Shakspere ; well knowing the greatness of the subject, but anxious to give a friendly word to beginners.,Now whom do I specially recommend to begin? Not every one, cer&not; tainly. I would not divert those who are inclined to one of the&#39; more definite subjects, wherein the learner can hardly wander, but must needs follow a clear inevitable method with clear inevitable results : such a sub&not; ject is distinctly preferable for class-work, and in many respects for private study also. But there are some who beyond other things like &ldquo; books,&rdquo; especially poetry, and crave to know what wise men have reported of their fellows, what they have thought of this wonderful world. To all such, at least, one may say, &ldquo; Here is Shakspere ! Why not read Shakspere ?&rdquo; But far be it from me to warn off any class of bond fide readers.,. M .Y first advice to any one who will begin, is to start with a real con&not; viction that Shakspere is worthy of serious study, and with a quiet reso&not; lution to study him as a true student. Mere English, you may think, and very easy reading ! But it is not so. There are difficulties on the thresh- old : the book is two hundred and fifty years old, and its language, way о thinking, and even subject-matter, are in many ways strange to us, giown dim to us ; and above all, as you will find continually and always more amd more, to understand Shakspere at all worthily, you must think very considerably youmelf. On the other hand, if you do get to under&not; stand him, and to receive affectionately what he has to give you, you will be well rewarded. In this all the world agrees, or pretends to agree &middot; it is,al T^ S ;; gTea L S 4 S P?&rsquo; e; &rsquo; &ldquo; divine Shaks P ere &amp;gt;&rdquo; etc., yet how few can say, with Milton, my Shakspere ! &rsquo; Now this should be your aim, to make Shakspere your own ; to take into your fellowship, as he took into his the various human characters which live in his pages, to become entirely familiar
with his thoughts and words, understanding them clearly, valuing them truly, and to win as much as you may of his noble, benignant temper. This he&acirc;rt-knowledge cannot be reached without patient, leisurely, thought&not; ful reading, there is so far to go, so much to do. Shakspere&rsquo;s subject is great,&mdash;simply human life ! and his subtlety of workmanship consummate. To working men, however, Shakspere holds out peculiar facilities : Shak- spere needs no library, no long preliminary study, simply quiet steady reading ; and a few minutes snatched from labour may furnish a good lesson for a day.,Aiming to know Shakspere, you will find it a help to leam who the man Shakspere was : so that you may always think of him, not as a book merely, but as a real man. Study his face, as we have it in the various portraits, and read what you can in that, and leam what you can of his actual life. I believe Knight&rsquo;s Biography contains all the facts we know, and it is written in a spirit of genuine love and admiration ; perhaps with almost too much admiration, for you will find he rejects the famous story of poaching Sir Thomas Lucy&rsquo;s deer, the flight to London, and other rumours of youthful dissipation or low estate, and even explains away the evidence which on first impression seems to show that Shakspere married Ann Hathaway to save her character. According to Knight, William Shakspere was from the first an exemplary, prudent person, wise in worldly wisdom as in much better things ; an industrious lad at Stratford school ; married for love ; was a steady, saving workman ; altogether a most well- behaved, thoroughly prosperous man. However the truth of this may be (and you can well afford to give up a good deal of it, if necessary), it will be well for you always to remember that Shakspere came of good yeoman family of Stratford-upon-Avon (bom there April, 1564, the son of &ldquo; Master John Shakspere &rdquo;) ; that at eighteen he married his neighbour, Ann Hathaway, who was some years his elder, and came to London ; that there he made his fortune as actor and play writer, first at the Blackfriars theatre (once the site of the monastery of the Black Briars, now the site of the &lsquo;Times&rsquo; office as near as may be), afterwards at the Globe Theatre, in South&not; wark ; that when still a young man (1597) he bought a capital tenement in native Stratford, the best house in the town, called New Place, adding field to field as the years .went on ; that there in Stratford he retired to live at ease, with his wife and daughters, and there he died (April 23, 1616) at the age , of fifty-two, and there he lies buried in the great parish church. You should go and see Stratford some day by all means. A pilgrimage of that kind sets the imagination at work, and so helps much ; and besides this, it will be interesting to observe, in Shaksperian landscapes, his clear love for his native lowlands,* such scenes as he had seen in his youth by Avon sid&eacute;.,Then you should try to conceive&mdash;all at once you cannot, but gradually, as you can&mdash;Shakspere&rsquo;s England, his panorama ;&mdash;how men looked and lived before him, what they believed, what they thought of continually ;&mdash;■ so like and so different from us. Shakspere&rsquo;s relation to his own age is a matter of great interest ; each illustrates the other in a remarkable manner. How far was Shakspere the spokesman for his contemporaries,&mdash;their re&not; presentative ? How much did he receive, how much withstand, how much,* Illustrated (with other important topics) by Buskin, &#39; Modern Painters,&rsquo; iv. 372-83.
discover and give P These are large and difficult questions, which can only be answered roughly at the best, and that not without visiting and re&not; visiting Shakspere&rsquo;s England, as well as studying Shakspere himself. I will here only ask you to consider well three capital facts of the epoch, and how Shakspere was related to them.,I. The Reformation .&mdash;The Eeformation was not merely a deliverance from ancient superstitions, worship of images, Papal absolution and the like, or even mainly and in essence a theological movement ; it gave the European mind a new&#39; term of youth, and sent it forth to seek, observe, ques&not; tion, and reconsider things generally. Other people than kings and queens, knights and priests, were henceforth to be thought of, in a measure all men ; other things than rigid doctrines and martial feats were to dominate the imaginations of men ; as Columbus crosses the sea, followed by fresh hosts of adventurers, so minds range abroad, with spontaneous energy, seeking and finding. With this spirit, the truest, largest spirit of the Eeformation, Shakspere was entirely one : in almost every page of his works we find proof of a roaming, excursive, very meditative genius. Nothing interested him so much as to watch the secret workings and conflicts of a gifted mind, and ponder over the wonderful laws which sway them : this was his greatest subject, but every aspect of real life and human so&not; ciety touched him, bade him think and* enjoy and reproduce. Indeed, his beautiful creations of fairyland seem to me to owe something to the same all-questioning impulse. Ask yourselves how Shakspere came to write of fairies. The old fantastic beliefs could not lie idle in his mind. He does not believe them, yet they are beautiful to him, worthy of happy musings, capable of taking new and lovely life ; and so we have Ariel and Titania, and all the happy, tricksy crew. I cannot, however, help observing that, however joyous on the surface is Shakspere, and however wide and intense the interest he finds in the world, his last, innermost mood seems to me not only very serious, but sad. The victorious nature of good is not with him a dominant thought ; he dwells more fondly on the grievous tyranny of evil and calamity. Has the restless spirit of Freedom anything to do with this ? Or is it that writers and thinkers, as compared with men of stirrino&middot; action, are for the most part inclined to secret melancholy ?,fehakspere s powers of speculation gives surpassing interest to many of his finei characters, but he is also remarkable for the pure honesty of his regard on all he looks upon. No reader can miss this : how so many kinds of people, of so many difieren! qualities of character, move in his dramas, almost endowed with life, so natural they seem and true. He has limits here too, but within those limits he shines, like the impartial sun, with cheerful light on all, illuminating and quickening. Not that he flatters people, but that he is true to them, and kindly. This sovereign justice is one of Shakspere s most glorious gifts, and, on the whole, entirely his own. His mind was lifted up above the dust and din of his time, so that he could see truly, and, loving men, judge them justly,&mdash;so justly that he could, as we say, create them. His own private thoughts lie deep, serene almost out of sight,&mdash;absorbed in those who speak at his bidding : they speak and act. Cardinals and friars have no cause to complain of his treatment, nor soldiers, nor merchants, nor kings, nor peasants, nor anv- body whom he has treated. In the great conflict that was going on be-,o 2
tween the old and the new, he seems an impartial spectator, reverencing both, delighting in both ; dwelling more with the old, yet unconsciously sharing more deeply the spirit of the new. Partisanship was impossible to him. It is curious to think that the great Puritan movement was ad&not; vancing strenuously in his time, that he himself lived seventeen years into Oliver Cromwell&rsquo;s life. Did Shakspere comprehend an Oliver Cromwell, or did he take no note of such men and what they were doing ? He is simply silent about them.,II. The political condition of England .&mdash;The hundred years of the Wars of the Roses, and the struggle for the emancipation from the Papacy, had brought about an intense national and monarchical feeling. England had had to claim her right to be free, and maintain it against Pope and Spaniard ; she had done so victoriously, and the land was at rest. A great queen was on the throne, and the memory of her father, the great bluff Hany, was still a possession with the people. The nobles, though no longer castled warriors, ruling over provinces, were yet a stately race, living in splendour, and taking a grand part in council, in war, and in all national enterprises :&mdash; still the leaders of the land. You will find this remarkably reflected in Shakspere&rsquo;s works, especially in the series of his English dramas, though he says nothing of Armadas and Atlantic voyages, and other matters of Elizabethan glory. This is the true historical value of the English plays. Shakspere follows history just as he likes, and no further ; some of his cha&not; racters, as Henry VIII., are truly historical, others (as Richard III., pro&not; bably) purely of his own construction. Pacts and dates, too, he turns at his will ; but he records indirectly most truly what he saw and felt in his own time. England is very dear to him, her honour and happiness ; he thinks of his country as the seagirt island, renowned and free amongst the nations ; he is inwardly rejoicing that she is at peace,&mdash;at peace within herself, those frightful civil wars no longer shedding the blood of kindred and marring the labours of quiet industry ; the crimes of ambition, the duty of loyalty, the sacred law of hereditary succession, are to him subjects of the dearest and most constant interest. He loves a lord, but above all a king. I have hinted that the Reformation helped him to care for the poor and humble, and I suppose no English writer before him had ever touched with such brotherly feeling upon peasants, common soldiers, and humble laborious folk : in this respect he is the forerunner of the numerous band of modern artists, who, almost caring for none else, have illustrated the lives of the poor with admirable truth and pathos. But Shakspere cared supremely, which none of, us do, for kings. He can hardly write a play without a king. He had a painter&rsquo;s eye for all the splendours of royalty, the kingly crown, the kingly throne and sceptre, &ldquo; the intertissued robe of gold and pearl,&rdquo; the stately circle of attendant princes, the stately tone of kingly speech ; and he had the poet&rsquo;s eye to see through all these, to feel the high duties of kings and their solemn responsibilities, to see their noble leading, inspiring hosts of men to valour, or, on the other hand, their fan&not; tastic tricks before high heaven and their tragic fate. No man has felt as he that a king was a king, and that a king was a man. The importance to us of this great public spirit in Shakspere will be felt, if it is considered how seldom the higher literature of the present day (with a few exceptions, Carlyle the greatest of them) compasses in any large sense the common
weal ; how it concerns itself chiefly with our private happinesses and private woes,&mdash;leaving the state to party-politicians, newspapers, Radicals who have no soul, dandiacal pseudo-aristocrats,&mdash;or, not to speak too harshly, to more or less well-meaning mediocrities.,III. The condition of English Literature and the English Language .&mdash; Good honest chroniclers there were before Shakspere&rsquo;s time, and dramatic plays were a common recreation among the English people, but in point of intellectual value they were perhaps not much. Shakspere availed himself of these wherever they served his purpose, borrowing freely, making all his own ; but he launched boldly into the wide world of real life, and by his own native power created a dramatic art, the most supreme dramatic art that ever existed. It is not in one thing Shakspere excels as dramatist: it is not too much to say that he excels in all. Management of plot, persons and incidents all working in true harmony, great and little, good and evil ; superb contrast, admirable subordination : and this with richness and variety of detail really beyond measure, eloquence, imagery, wit, pathos, lusty humour, and above all nobleness and beauty of human cha&not; racter. Other writers can claim some of these qualities : it is in the union of them, and the splendour, power, and majestic freedom of their exhibition, that Shakspere is supreme. This you must prove by experience. But how Shakspere accomplished all this as a mere writer for the Globe Theatre must ever be one of the greatest wonders in history. One can only say, Shakspere was Shakspere. He was a great poet, a &pi;&omicron;&upsilon;&eta;&tau;&eta;&sigmaf;, or maker. He came, and saw, and conquered.,As to the language of Shakspere, the important external fact to be re&not; membered is, that at his date there was a revival of classical literature, and all educated persons went mad about Latin. The result was an immense change in the language ; something of the old veracious&#39;simplicity was lost, but great armies of Latin words became naturalized, and the sym&not; bolism of the heathen gods became one of the most popular imageries. We are tired of the heathen gods now, having had far too much of them since Shakspere&rsquo;s time, but to his hearers they were fresh and welcome as they were to him himself, and, considering the temptation, he uses them sparingly. Latin words in Shakspere you see actually a-forming ; taking their English shape, often not the exact shape in which they have finally rested ; and seeking their English sound, as, for instance, words of Latin origin ending in &ldquo; ion,&rdquo; which I believe in Shakspere is invariably treated as a dissyllable, either an emphatic dissyllable or a hurried one, as the metre requires. These Latinisms are very interesting, and confer a peculiar euphonious dignity on his lines ; but his language is most potent when the Latin element is kept subordinate, and the homely English rules the sen&not; tences. I confess Shakspere occasionally offends me with undue richness of rhetoric, but that is rather here and there ; on the whole, his language is magnificently true to the meaning, apt, flexible, searching, irresistibly powerful. J,Shakspere&rsquo;s achievements in metre are another of his miracles. Forms he may have borrowed from his predecessors, but he wrought them alto&not; gether in his own fashion, and his mind was a temple of music. You can hardly pay too much heed to his metre. Compare his songs with his blank verse, and that again with his speeches in prose. I have no space to speak urther on this point. 1
These observations are intended as an introduction in the most compen&not; dious loi m to the study of Shakspere. In no degTee are they or any other observations necessary, but I hope they may be helpful : with or without them the reader can open the book called Shakspere. Now as to the study of the book. Several plays must of course be read carefully before the reader will see well about him, and the comparison of the differ&not; ent characters, subjects, and modes of treatment, will always be very in&not; structive. But there must be a beginning, and the best way is to take one play and work it thoroughly. One of the historical plays will do very well for this purpose ; and 1 propose now to take Kichard the Second (the play my class is reading this Term), and to examine it, by way of a working specimen, as well as 1 am able in these narrow limits,,1. The Theme of the Brama . &mdash;Shakspere clearly aims in this play to give us the origin of the Civil War : the misgovernment of the unworthy Kichard, drawing on the usurpation of the ambitious Bolingbroke, the re&not; sult of which will be England divided, and household war in the land. With subtle touches (regrets of old men remembering) the glorious past is told, the victorious career of &ldquo; grandsire &rdquo; Edward, and his valorous son ; then by a little scene is shown how the fresh murder of Gloucester is rankling in the general mind, the terrible future is prophesied by the wise lips of the aged patriot Gaunt and the brave devout Bishop of Car&not; lisle. The story unfolds itself, coil on coil, like an irresistible fate. First comes the unbridled license of Richard, and the wrong to Bolingbroke ; then, wrong breeding wrong, Bolingbroke appears in arms to recover his own ; for time urges him on, and once Duke of Lancaster he soon aims at being King of England. Richard, deserted by his friends, tortured with remorse and shame, has to bow his neck to his usurping cousin, enter his capital as a captive, and before all men depose himself. Plots and crimes and miseries succeed : the household war is come ! A father is seen en&not; treating for his son&rsquo;s death, brutally mocking the mother&rsquo;s agony and love : a chance word brings murder, and we leave Richard lying in his blood in a dungeon, and the new king, with a burdened soul, vowing a pilgrimage to Jerusalem for his sins.,The particular scenes I have no space to remark upon. The reader must try to conceive them with all the sympathy of imagination he can muster,&mdash;to see the splendid field of Coventry, the deathbed of Gaunt, Richard on the walls of Flint Castle, in Westminster Hall, dashing the glass in shivers before the assembled Parliament, in the dungeon talking with &ldquo; the poor groom of his stable.&rdquo; There is no scene in the play which is not vividly pictorial.,2. The Characters .&mdash;Richard is of course principal throughout, and his character is one of Shakspere&rsquo;s carefully finished portraits. We see him first in prosperity. The son of noble warrior parents, king of England, grave councillors and potent barons around him, how fares he, how does he behave P Too clearly he is all unfit for his great place. He is young, very young (really he was thirty-two years of age, but Shakspere seems to disregard this), weak of will, passionate, and handsome of person (in York&rsquo;s description of him) ; in temper, intoxicated with the giddy hu&not; mours of youth, and the splendid privileges of royalty. We hear of mis&not; tresses, flatterers, proud fashions of Italy, ten thousand men under his house-
hold roof; we see mean favourites, &ldquo;caterpillars of the commonwealth,&rdquo; exalted to undeserved honours and riches, for which the honest poor are taxed. The great king is a spoilt child, and England is his toy. He can&not; not curb his quarrelling nobles ; bids Norfolk, in vain, throw down Henry&rsquo;s gage ; has no ear for the reproof of wise old Gaunt ; is no man of his word. Bolingbroke he sentences to four years&rsquo; exile, meaning perhaps more, and with hasty greed, against his own solemn promise, gripes his property. Thus he has lost his people&rsquo;s hearts. Yet observe, there is no malice in him&mdash;he forgives York&rsquo;s hard words, and immediately afterwards makes him governor of the realm in his absence&mdash;nor any mean duplicity ; and on high occasions, when his temper is not touched, as when sentencing Bolingbroke, he can speak in native kingly style. Then, Bichard in adver&not; sity, He is the same Bichard still, still weak, passionate, apparently even more so than before, because of the strain on him : but adversity has set him thinking ; that is the main difference. His thought is of the noble in tone, and fronts some of the greatest truths, yet all to no purpose ; it drivels away into poor puling sentiment, or explodes in hysterical action : self-mastery he has none. Beturned to England to meet the usurping Bolingbroke, he trusts in the name of king, not in manly and kingly deeds ; staggers and reels between fiery hopes and supine dejection, as an encou&not; raging word from his friends or the advent of ill-tidings sways him ; finally sits down on the ground to &ldquo; to tell sad stories of the death of kings,&rdquo; and disbands all his followers. At Elint he surrenders to Bolingbroke, after another conflict with himself, &ldquo; speaking fondly like a frantic man,&rdquo; in the very presence of his arch-enemy, Northumberland ;&mdash;and sets out for London a prisoner. In the deposition scene it is the same story over again; pride heart-broken, studying its own misery and degradation, storm&not; ing forth into wild reproaches, basting itself with bitter self-mockery, find&not; ing issue only in contemplating its own face,&mdash;even in a looking glass ! The cold moments of sarcasm are singularly telling. This crisis passes, and we have moods of quiet suffering, as in the procession scene, and his touching farewell to his unhappy young queen, daughter of the French king. Lastly, Bichard is in Pomfret, a lonely prisoner; thinking more than ever on his own sorrows, still borne hither and thither by conflicting- impulses ; but we think the passion of them is almost spent now : he knows not whether &rsquo;twere better to be king or beggar. Yet the report of &ldquo; Bo- Imgbroke on roan Barbary&rdquo; brings a sudden flash of wrath; and presently when the keeper enters, and he suspects poison, see what happens,_,&ldquo; The devil take thee, and Henry of Lancaster !,Patience is stale, and I am weary of it!&rdquo; [beats keeper .],Then in comes Exton with his murderers, and poor Bichard dies, fio-ht- ing valiantly. We part with him with something better than pity. We pity his misfortunes, and must needs confess his ill-deservings, but we love пм r&deg;i&deg;&#39; ^ e l&deg; ve ^ or k* 8 gentle heart that knew no malice, for his,childish genuine nature ; we love him because he could think and feel be&not; cause we see he loved others and was loved by them.,John o&iacute; Gaunt is &ldquo;old John of Gaunt, time-honoured Lancaster.&rdquo; 1 he weight of many years and sad watchings over his country press heavily upon him; melancholy, patient, he would leave much to Heaven.
England&rsquo;s woes are his, and he has his own bitter sorrows. He loves his son dearly, but must join in the decree of his banishment, and part from him for ever. The evil day he does not live to see with bodily eyes, but his mind sees it well, and his tongue dares to utter it to the misguided king ;&mdash;a few brave last words for his country, and he dies, Shakspere, I think, made his own Gaunt, added twenty years to his real age, and gave him this noble character, whatever the chronicles might say,,Bolingbroke is contrasted with Richard in another way ; he is young in years, but not in character. Ambitious, wary, bold, politic; in manner gracious and moderate, but secretly altogether regardless of others. He appears first as the challenger of the Lord Marshal, to win popular favour, no doubt ; those grand protestations of loyalty are only skin-deep. His sudden sentence of exile overcomes him for the moment ; the future, full of castles, though perhaps with no throne, is suddenly all blank, and he cannot accept comfort from his kind old father, nor find a tender word for him. Not a very amiable character ! He soon recovers himself, however, and takes leave of the London citizens with telling adroitness. Next he is landed at Ravenspurg, marching towards the Cotswolds, winning (for the time) by honeyed words, the Percies to be his friends. He will be Duke of Lancaster; and presently more. The subtle gradations by which he ad&not; vances from the condition of an armed beggar to the deposing of his so&not; vereign, and taking his seat on the throne, are given in Shakspere&rsquo;s own masterly way. Bolingbroke kneels before York, protesting loyalty, skil&not; fully appealing to private affection ; wins York ; presently suffers the king to be spoken of in his presence as plain &ldquo; Richard.&rdquo; To Richard himself he sends messages, mock-loyal, but leads him prisoner to London. Rude harsh work he leaves to his tool Northumberland, taking the profit of them, however, surely enough. In the deposition scene he is &ldquo; silent,&rdquo; lets Richard have his say, have his little will about the looking-glass : but the end is, &ldquo; Go, some of you, convey him to the Tower.&rdquo; He is now king : such men &ldquo; succeed &rdquo; in the world. He is not, however, a bad or a base man. Not naturally cruel; he pardons the conspiring Aumerle, pardons the brave Bishop of Carlisle, almost gladly. At the murder of Richard he expresses no horror, no deep sorrow, as for a hideous crime, but neither does he show greedy joy. Yet he is no man to be loved ; and the dramas that follow prove it. His son has no fellowship with him, the Percies mutiny, and he leads to the last a troubled life warring with rebellious subjects, never getting to Jerusalem. Probably Henry&rsquo;s character and Richard&rsquo;s also are in the main entirely faithful to actual history, as far as we can ever hope &ldquo;to know&rdquo; them.,York is the cunning old trimmer ; one who holds by the reigning Nabob, as long as he may, and then meets the new-coming Nabob halfway&mdash;a very common character in times of change. Shakspere does him justice as usual, records his one courageous speech, his one thought of pity for his deposed sovereign : but marks him clearly as the ignoble trimmer. Ob&not; serve how York tries to check Gaunt in speaking out so as to keep right with the king, how he deals with Bolingbroke, grand reproofs at first, fol&not; lowed immediately by adherency, keeping up the farce as long as he can ; how he acts as go-between between Bolingbroke and Richard for the crown, returning with &ldquo; Great Duke of Lancaster, I come to thee from plume-
plucked Richard.&rdquo; The final scene in which he appears, when he calls for his boots and posts off to Bolingbroke to betray his son, and then uses so brutally the wife of his bosom, stamps his base stony-hearted character. He lives unscathed, yet not without judgment. No ! what a home he must have had when his duchess and son returned that night to supper !,Northumberland, &ldquo;ladder &rdquo; Northumberland, is Bolingbroke&rsquo;s chief tool and executioner, serving meanwhile with a secret eye only to Northumber&not; land&rsquo;s own profit. First to conspire against Richard, first to rob him of his kingly title ; &ldquo;a haught, insulting man,&rdquo; who can find malicious pleasure in trampling on his fallen master,&mdash;&ldquo; My lord, despatch; read o&rsquo;er these articles &mdash;and commissioned to separate him from his queen, does it in this fashion :&mdash;,&ldquo; Take leave and part ; for you must part forthwith.,Queen. Bauish us both, and send the king with me.,North. That were some love, but little policy.&rdquo;,Bolingbroke he flatters, carries out all his hardest purposes; sees Wilt&not; shire and Green despatched, in the beginning, and in the end comes in to report having sent to London the heads of Salisbury, Spencer, Blunt, and Kent. This is the last we see of him in this Flay ; in the next we have him a rebel against Bolingbroke, and in the hour of peril &ldquo; having leisure to be sick.&rdquo;,In noble contrast to both York and Northumberland is the brave and true Bishop of Carlisle. He appears seldom, but his character is sun- clear, simple, devout, and brave, and true. He is with his master in ad&not; versity, strengthening him with prudent counsel ; before Bolingbroke and all men, under the very frown of power, he will uphold Richard&rsquo;s right to be king, and speak zealously for the banished Norfolk. Even Bolingbroke cannot help regarding him; has he not seen in him &ldquo;high sparks of honour &rdquo; ? ť,The queen is very gentle and loving, and moves us to deep pity. Bushy, Bag&oacute;t, and Green, are &ldquo; creatures &rdquo; of Richard, who fly at the first note of danger; two meet death by execution, the third seems to save his life by turning on his former friends. It is unnecessary to speak of the other characters.,To any one who knows well this grand play, analysis, such as the above, seems quite unnecessary, and indeed a cold-blooded business. The pur&not; pose of it here is to show to the uninitiated how much solid drawing, com&not; position, life, and meaning (apart from eloquence and sentiment and other interest of detail), there is to be found in a play of Shakspere. The student may practise doing&middot; the like, if he will, as a means to ensure his reading carefully, and thinking over what he reads. The main and, indeed, only thing, is to know the text well ; and nothing but reading, and read&not; ing again and again, will do this. Learning by heart (consider what learning hy heart means) is very good, and strangely, out of mere indo&not; lence, neglected by grown-up persons ; but the student should be careful always to remember the connection in which the speech stands, and who speaks,it, and how. Thus the speech, &ldquo; All places that the eye of heaven visits,&rdquo; is comparatively trivial, unless we remember it is spoken by old Gaunt with a gallant effort to comfort the young prince his son, banished
from the Court into exile ; and Eichard&rsquo;s speech, &ldquo; No matter where,&rdquo; takes its proper colour only from Eichard&rsquo;s sentimental character and down-fallen condition.,Here I leave the reader to Shakspere himself. I cannot promise you that you will find in him any decisive impulse to strenuous action, and high self-devotion in practical life. Shakspere&rsquo;s world is too large for us ; but of this I am satisfied, that reading Shakspere may help you (and per&not; haps others through you) to many happy fireside evenings, many happy thoughts, solemn and bright, now and in the years to come; that it may open your eyes to receive joyfully and judge truly works of true imagina&not; tion ; that it may enable you to sustain the troubles of life with more se&not; renity of will, and to front the strange variety of human creatures you must meet, with something of his wise, open-hearted, brotherly spirit. Only remember, without genuine study this cannot be.,Yernon Lushington.,HISTOEY OF THE LONDON WOEKING MEN&rsquo;S COLLEGE.,{Concluded from page 170.),With the new year, 1855, we opened an Adult School at our old Hall of Association in Castle Street, Oxford Street, putting it under the charge of a paid teacher not before connected with the College. The subjects taught were reading, writing, and the first four rules of arithmetic ; and the pupils averaged thirty a night. We had also to discuss the organiza&not; tion of the College, and agreed at last that it should be ultimately divided into five classes -.-&mdash;(1) the merely Matriculated Students ; (2) the Certificated Students, who had gained a Certificate of Competency in any class after four Terms from the entrance ; (3) the Associates, who had shown compe&not; tency in&mdash; a. Bible History ; 6. English History ; c. English Grammar ; d. the elements of some Mathematical or Physical study ; (4) the Fellows, chosen by the Council out of the Associates, for their moral qualities, and their capacity and willingness to help in teaching ; (5) the Council,&mdash;one- third of it to consist, at some future time, of Fellows.,We hoped that the Associates would form a large and permanent body, each member being &ldquo;a man four-square,&rdquo; knowing at least the elements of four great branches of study. The degree of knowledge required in each was defined only by the words, &ldquo; that the Associate was to have the know&not; ledge of those subjects which every person educated in a College ought to have but practically it has been such as would have gained him a Certificate of Competency in each subject. We have been disappointed in the small number of men who have tried to become Associates&mdash;five in all,,I think. The &ldquo; Mathematical or Physical study &rdquo; is the thing that has stopped the way. Few men after a certain time of life will read Mathe&not; matics up to the Elements of Trigonometry, Conic Sections, Optics, and Mechanics, or work at Geology, Physiology, or Botany, so as to stand an examination in them. To meet this, Mr. Lathbury this year (1860) pro&not; posed to add to our divisions of the College a sixth class, of scholars who should be men competent in any one complete branch of study, Art, His-
tory, Language, etc., thus marking men who took up one study only, and went through with it. We adopted his suggestion ; but have yet to see how it will work.,But to return to the year 1855, in the March of which our &ldquo;Live Classes&rdquo; of the College were announced. In our third Term Mr. Grant Duff, now M.P. for the Elgin Burghs, joined us, and took a class in Physical Geography, its students varying from seven to two in number, till it ceased. In the fourth Term, Modelling was introduced among our art-students, and was taught by Mr. Munro and Mr. Woolner, the one as noted for the grace and loveliness of his sculptures as the other for the vigour and cha&not; racter of his. The kitchens at the College were turned into modelling- rooms, men appeared smudged with white clay, and the class went on well for a few Terms ; but it made so many of the Drawing Class pupils lazy at their drawing, that Mr. Buskin told them they must either draw or model, they had shown him they had not time for both ; soon after which the Modelling Class collapsed. In this Term too, M. Jourdain joined M. Talandier as a French teacher. And Mr. Basent, now one of the Latin teachers in the King s College Evening Classes, the brother of the well- known Norse scholar and foreign editor of &lsquo; The Times,&rsquo; came to the Li&not; brary every Tuesday night, to act as a private 4 coach,&rsquo; and help men in their work. And so we were getting to July 28th, 1855, the proposed end of our first College-year ; but we found that carpenters and clerks had not barristers&rsquo; and chaplains&rsquo; Long Vacations, and wanted to go on with work, so we determined to carry on as many of the classes as possible, and issued a short Programme, in which appeared the names of three new teachers,&mdash;Mr. C. H. Pearson, now Professor of English History in Kino-&rsquo;s College (an old pupil there of Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s) ; Mr. Bailey, whose duties as an Hon. Sec. of the ĽeviVs Own Volunteers have since* taken from us ; and Mr. Ludlow, of whom we need only say, that to him all that we have ever done is mainly due.,The latter half of this vacation saw the first commencement of the system which has since been the greatest support to the College and to its teach- ing. Four of om ablest students, fast friends and good workers, Messrs. Chappel, Boebuck, Bossiter, and Thrower, took an Arithmetic Class. At the end of the Term Mr. Bossiter (afterwards the first Fellow of the Col- lege) gained the one certificate of competency granted in Algebra &middot; and so we wound up our first year. Might we hope we had done any work worth doing ? _ This is what the students said to us after a tea at the Hall of Association to which they invited us.,Address of the Students to the Principal, Council of Teachers, and Secretary.,Gentlemen -Conceiving it a duty we owe alike to you and ourselves that your ex&not; ertionsin our behalf should not pass unrecognized, we on this occasion venture an imper&not; fect utterance of the affectionate respect with which we feel inspired towards you At the present time, when men generally appear to have more respect for their own peculiar crotchets than concern for the welfare of their fellows, any effort tending to solve the great educational questions of the day, must be in the highest degree acceptable to the uneducated portion of the community. How much then is this enterprise, having for its operationi intellectual elevation, deserving of our cordial sympathy and co-,The aid you so generously offer to us, the Students of this College, wifi, we trust ena&not; ble us to reach heights and depths of knowledge, before, and without you, inaccessible.
Possessing knowledge, we trust it will make us better as well as wiser men ; for that we know is the desired end of your teaching.,We gratefully accept your aid ; not as a charity, it is not so offered, but as a sacrifice made by you upon the shrine of humanity ; a sacrifice entering into, perhaps, and form&not; ing part of, the duties of your lives.,Gentlemen, you have told us your object is to fulfil your duties towards your fellow- men, and we beg of you to accept our congratidations, on the manner in which you have fulfilled them. We trust we shall sufficiently appreciate your exertions, as to emulate your example, and that, when capability serves, inclination will not be wanting to aid in the good work you have begun. We believe that all conditions of life carry with them duties commensurate with their advantages, and he who most generously construes his duties, best serves the interests of Mankind.,It is because you have interpreted your duties, in a more enlarged and cosmopolitan sense than many of your class, that we especially desire to thank you. We offer our thanks, not as a reward, but as an acknowledgment ; your labours carry with them the only fitting reward they can ever obtain. Thanks are all we have to offer&mdash;as much, perhaps, as you would care to receive.,Gentlemen, in concluding this imperfect expression of our feelings towards you, we would state our regret that greater numbers of our class have not availed themselves of the great advantages afforded by the College ; our hope of an improvement in the future ; and our confidence in the beneficial tendency of your continued exertions.,Signed on behalf of the Students,,July 30, 1855. Rum&not; sey, Phillips, and Townsend (student).,In 1856, Women s Classes were started at the College, taught by ladies, friends of Mr. Maurice, and other members of the Council. The volume of Lectures given to the teachers by Messrs. Kingsley, L&Iacute;. Davies, Tom Taylor, and some of our teachers, was published, and has gone through three editions. The classes have not been numerously attended (see their 1859 Table, p. 38 of this volume), but the teachers are, I believe, satisfied with the results of their work. A Girls&rsquo; School was begun early in 1858, under the control of Miss Woodhouse. At the beginning of our second vear we divided the Council into sections:&mdash;!. Divinity and Humanity (History, Literature, Law) ; 3. Language; 3. Mathematics ; 4. Physics; 5. Art; and then, at three or four subsequent periods, endeavoured to introduce more order into our arrangement of classes and studies ; but still without any very definite result. In the summer of 1856 we began short prayers every night in one of the rooms, but they were given up for want of at&not; tendance ; they have since been revived, and are sometimes attended, other times not At the end of the second year (July 31st) the students gave their teachers a tea, as before. We had had our first excursion to Hampton Court and Bushy Park, and dinner at Teddington on Whit-Monday After wards we were entertained by Mr. Ludlow and Mr. Hughes, at Wimbledon where Lord Goderich (now Earl de Grey, one of our oldest and best mends) coached us m Entomology, and Mr. Kingsley in Botany.,Our third year (November, 1856) opened with a proposal from the three student-leaders Messrs. Rossiter, Roebuck, and Tansley, to form Prepara&not; tory Classes in Elementary Grammar, History, Geography, and Arithmetic, to be taught by Certificated and Senior Students of the College These Classes were^established in January, 1857, and the teachers have been &mdash;in Grammar, Messrs. Cooper, W. Sutton, W. White, W. A.Dalziel, Preston&middot; in History and Geography, Mr. Emslie, Mr. Rawlins, Messrs. Roebuck Giegg, Wilkins, Evans ; in Arithmetic, Messrs. Tansley, W. Sutton.,ihe other great help in teaching given us by the students, was on the establishment (in March, 1859 ) of Tutors (proposed by Mr. Ludlow) to
help men attending the College Classes, The Tutors are, or have been, in Arithmetic, Messrs. T. Fisher and South ; in Algebra, Messrs. Tansley, Thrower, Morgan, Leggatt, and Bromley ; in Geometry, Messrs. Cormack and Wright; in Logic, Mr, Dresser; in Vocal Music, Mr. Townsend; in Latin, Mr, Hurst ; in French, Messrs. W, Sutton, Baguley, and Lawrence ; in English Grammar, Messrs. Gay and Brock ; in Geology, Mr. Seeley. In January, 1857, Mr. Rossiter took the Mastership of our Adult School, and when he left us to be a regular schoolmaster in the country, Mr. Roe* buck took his place, and holds it still, to the satisfaction of all.,As to other events, in December, 1856, we were affiliated to the London University; in January, 1857, we were asked to send Candidates to try for clerkships in the Education-office&mdash;a severe competition, as the other Candidates were also picked men ; we sent one, who, though well up in his subjects, did not succeed. Meantime the lease of our house in Red Lion Square was coming to an end, and we had to look out for a new abode. This was found at 45, Great Ormond Street, the freehold of which, by the help of a magnificent gift of &pound;500 from Mr. Maurice, and other subscriptions, we bought for &pound;1400, and raised the deficit on mort&not; gage, of which we still owe &pound;600.* Our Legal Committee, Messrs. Ludlow, Westlake, and Hughes, advised the Council to register the College as a Joint-Stock Company, in order that the conveyance of the new house and land might be made direct to them without the intervention of trustees. This was done, and it unexpectedly led to the withdrawal of four Oxford men from the Council, who, with other teachers, either not wishing to join the Council, or being probationers, henceforth formed the &ldquo; Teachers not Members of Council &rdquo; on our Terra-programmes.,We moved into our new house in the spring of 1857, and in 1858 moved our Adult School to the basement-floor of it, after the giving up of our Modelling Class. On November 13, 1858, &ldquo;Mr. John Roebuck and Mr. George Tansley were elected Fellows of the College,&rdquo; and on November 34,,&ldquo; Members of the Council,&rdquo; om&#39; first, and still our only ones.,With 1859 began The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Mens&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine. In its pages have been chronicled all subsequent events of interest, the purchase of ad&not; ditional property, the establishment of a Rifle Corps, etc. With this there&not; fore we may close this brief narrative of the first six years of our existence.,F. J. Furnivall.,CORRESPONDENCE.,[Mr. Rossiter, Fellow of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, sends us from the country the following &ldquo; short grumble &rdquo; about our present condition. A more intimate knowledge of the facts of the case than he can obtain at a distance, would probably suggest several pleas in mitigation of his judgment. Some member of the College may probably say something to this effect next month. Meanwhile it may be wholesome to consider whether our old friend&rsquo;s admonition is not somewhat deserved.],This is the commencement of the seventh year of the College in London, and (taking no account of the first year as experimental) it is the first year that has begun without either an advanced mathematical or a Greek Class. Algebra is represented by an ele&not; mentary class, and Geometry extends to Euclid, Book II. The Greek Class, after cul-,* &pound;800, less &pound;300 &pound;3 per Cent. Stock, invested towards reducing the debt.
minating with Plato and Xenophon, has become defunct ; and the Latin Class, after reach&not; ing Tacitus and Terence, has returned to Virgil.,It cannot he said that physical science or art has taken the place of mathematics or classics, for the Modelling and Chemistry classes have been extinct these four years ; the Geology class has dwindled to one class, and Logic, by the bye, has done the same.,Of course it will all be put down to the Ilifle Corps, but, I fear, unjustly. Even if it were so, it ought not to be. It may be said that it is all-important that England should be in a position to command respect, and to repel invasion. So it is ; but our self-respect is more important than the respect of our neighbours, and our own ignorance and our own prejudices are more dangerous invaders than any foreign army.,I fear, however, that the Rifle Corps is not the only preventive to steady progressive study. I have been for years an absentee from the College, and can only speak from the Prospectus now before me : but, from my occasional visits to London, I fear that the &ldquo; social element &rdquo; is the real enemy of the advanced classes. This &ldquo; social element &rdquo; is the one in the College of which I know most, and the one I prize the most, and I fear it is the false position it seems to have acquired, that does the mischief. Play in common, founded upon work in common, is most necessary to the life of a College, especially a &ldquo; Working Man&rsquo;s College but play in common, founded upon nothing but itself, is a deception. And in a College, where the most advanced mathematics are Euclid, Book II,, and Virgil the only classic author read, while physical science is represented only by a Geology class (elementary), much common work cannot be boasted of.,I have no wish to depreciate the Rifle Corps. I am proud of it, and do not believe it is an enemy to College work, or that it is the cause of so low an ebb in the College studies. Let us work first, and then play will be both deserved and valuable.,Suffolk, November 12, 1860. William Rossiteb.,NEWS.,Salford. The notice of the general meeting inaugurating the present Term, held on 24th September, was accidentally omitted from last month&rsquo;s Magazine. An interesting; report of the gathering, with a leading article on the subject, is to be found in the &lsquo; Sal&not; ford Weekly News&rsquo; of September 29th. The proceedings included a tea, a business meet&not; ing, and a dance. The Mayor presided, in the absence of Mr. Langworthy. The state- ment of Mr. Chadwick (treasurer) showed the College to be in a state of undoubted pros&not; perity. Ihe classes had been well attended, and the finances were in a flourishing condi tion. Since the last meeting a Women&rsquo;s Class has been opened, conducted by Mrs.,a ^ d Mrs &middot;,Pochm. But the College had to anticipate a severe loss in the removal of Mr. Parry, their Honorary Secretary, who is about to take an important official posi&not; tion in Liverpool Mr. Parry had been unwearied in the service of the College taking the larger share of the onerous duties of general management ; and the least recognition they could give of their sense of their obligations would be to vote him an honorary Life Member of the College. Mr. Parry (who was warmly received by the meeting) addressed the students on the importance of the system of Examination carried on by the Society of Arts. It was mentioned that two young women, who had made great progress in their own Class, had applied to be admitted into the men&rsquo;s Classes. The application had been lefused; but Mr. Chadwick hoped that some arrangement would be made which would secure to women the advantages occupied by the men. The Elemem,EOad &middot;&ldquo; &ldquo; &raquo;as about,The Working Man&rsquo;s Institute, 45 Wellclose Square (of which Mr. Hansard spoke at the late meeting of the College, asking the students to give their assistance as teachers) is now open. Its planis explained by the following paper put forth by its promoters &middot;&mdash;,To the Working Men of St. George&rsquo;s Bast, Whitechapel. Stępmy, Z црей
Parishes. This Institute is established to provide you after your daily work is done, with a cheap, healthful, and intelligent relaxation. Among the advantages offered, you will find a comfortable Beading-room, well furnished with newspapers (daily and weekly), magazines, chess, draughts, dominoes, etc. A Smoking and Conversation Boom, in which coffee and (in very cold weather) good soup, will be served at cost price. A Library, con&not; sisting of upwards of one thousand volumes (selected to please every taste) which you can take to your own homes. Lectures by some of the best Lecturers of the day will be given during the winter season at fortnightly intervals. A Debating Society will be established shortly. Evening Classes on various subjects (for particulars see posters and notices at the Institute) will^be commenced in November. The Institute is open from 6.30 to 10 p .m. The honorary Secretary or Deputy will be in attendance on Saturdays, Mon&not; days, and Tuesdays, to admit members or receive payments. Terms of admission for working men to Beading and Smoking Booms, Library, and Evening Classes, 1 d. per week, to be paid in advance. An entrance fee of 6 d. will be required from persons wish&not; ing to avail themselves of the Library, and and upwards to a portion of the,Evening Classes.
